Feeling for Two — and More

Emotional intelligence in love, family, and open relationships — without the magic

How to read this book

This book is about what happens between people when feelings show up in a relationship: attraction, tenderness, jealousy, hurt, the fear of losing someone. And about what to do with all of that.

The whole book rests on one simple pattern — the loop. Something happens → your body reacts → your head decides what it means → you do something → that becomes something that happens to the other person. In a couple, two of these loops catch on each other. When there are more than two people, you get a whole web.

Every chapter is built the same way: the main point in short, a scene from real life, what is really going on, how to spot it in yourself and in your partner, what to do, which familiar ideas get in the way, and questions to talk over together. If a chapter has a section called “When it is no longer about skills,” be sure to read it: it marks the line past which you need safety and a professional’s help, not exercises.

As in our earlier books, the body comes first, then what the research shows. There are almost no numbers or names in the text, but every conclusion rests on something. A number in square brackets is a source: tap it and a card opens; the full list is at the end of the book. Where there is little or no data, we say so.

We talk to you directly, as a friend, and without assuming anyone’s gender: this book is for any couple — and for relationships of more than two.

Part I. The lens: how feelings work, and why two makes it harder

01. The emotion loop: what is really happening between you

In short

Emotion between two people works like a loop: something happens → your body reacts → your head decides what it means → you do something → that becomes an event for the other person.

Where the evening goes isn’t decided by the event itself, but by the meaning it gets in a split second.

What to do about it: notice the signal in your body, name the feeling together with the event, and check your guess with your partner instead of acting on it.

Katya is chopping vegetables for dinner when a message from Lin comes in: “Can’t make it, let’s do tomorrow.” Her throat tightens, her breathing goes shallow, her shoulders creep up toward her ears. A ready-made sentence is already in her head: “Once again I come last.” Katya writes: “Ok.” Lin sees the dry “Ok,” and guilt stings her. A couple of seconds later the guilt turns into irritation: “But I warned her.” She answers just as briefly. By evening they’re both sitting in separate rooms, and each one is sure it was the other who pulled away.

Nobody wanted a fight. It was put together by two loops that caught on each other.

What is going on

The body comes first. The body reacts before the words show up. Your heart speeds up, your breathing gets shorter, your muscles tighten, your gaze narrows. This is automatic: nobody switches it on by willpower. In this state it’s harder to listen — your attention shrinks down to whatever stung.

The body doesn’t say what happened. A lump in your chest is just a lump. It might mean “I’m not valued,” or it might mean “my partner just can’t think about dinner right now.” You can’t tell from your pulse and breathing which emotion is running: the same emotion behaves differently in the body in different situations. Your head fills in the meaning — from experience, from habit, from what you expect. And the word “hurt” or “anger” comes last. It’s the end result, not the beginning.

On top of that, there are far more feelings than the familiar short list of “joy, fear, anger, sadness.” Shades flow into each other, and the borders between them are blurry1. That’s why the same state can easily be named in different ways — and what happens next depends on the name.

Meaning decides what you do. “I’m not valued” pushes you toward a cold “Ok” or a reproach. “My partner can’t think about dinner right now” pushes you toward the question “Is everything okay?” One event, different actions. Behind anger there is most often an expectation that didn’t come true (more on that in chapter 16).

Your action is an event for the other person. For Lin, the dry “Ok” is already a new trigger. She has her own body (a sting of guilt), her own meaning (“I’m being blamed”) and her own action (a short reply). And her reply is a new trigger for Katya. That’s how two loops lock together and wind each other up. Partners’ bodies also often tune in to each other — both in a fight and in an ordinary conversation. Tears work the same way: they’re a bodily signal that the other person will read in their own way (chapter 05).

Where emotional intelligence comes in. Behind this big phrase there isn’t a character trait, but several separate skills — one for each link in the loop:

  • notice the signal in your body (chapter 02);
  • name it precisely (chapter 03);
  • understand what expectation is behind it (chapter 16);
  • choose an action and calm yourself and your partner (chapter 04);
  • read the other person’s loop (chapter 06);
  • repair the coupling when the loops wind each other up (chapter 17).

You can be good at one and not good at all at another. That’s why it’s more useful to think about specific skills than about “being emotional in general.”

How to tell it is happening

In yourself:

  • Your body went off before your thoughts did: heat in your face, a lump in your throat, a clenched jaw, shoulders up, shallow breathing.
  • One message or one look, and you instantly have a conclusion about the whole relationship: “my partner doesn’t care,” “once again I come last.”
  • You’re completely sure you know what’s in the other person’s head.
  • Your reply comes out shorter and colder than what’s going on inside: “ok,” “whatever you want,” “fine.”
  • “Always” and “never” have shown up in your thoughts.
  • After the conversation you replay it again and line up arguments.

In your partner:

  • They answer in the same tone — just as dry or just as sharp.
  • They justify themselves, and you can hear irritation in it.
  • They pull away — go quiet, disappear into their phone or into another room. Or, the opposite, they push harder.
  • Their reaction seems out of proportion to what set it off.
  • Later it turns out that each of you thought it was the other who pulled away.

What to do

The first three techniques go in a chain: notice → name → check. This isn’t a tested program, but something put together from what is known in pieces.

1. Notice your body before you answer

When: in the second something stings — a message, a tone, a look — and your hand is already reaching to reply.

What to do: stop and find one or two sensations in your body. Not an emotion, but specifically a sensation: “throat tight,” “shoulders up by my ears,” “breathing shallow.” Don’t look for a word for the feeling yet. Just note to yourself: “My body has switched on.”

How you know it worked: you notice the signal before the reply goes out. And the reply is different from your first impulse.

If paying attention to your body makes the anxiety worse instead — switch outward: feel your feet on the floor, find an object with your eyes, listen to a sound in the room.

2. Name the feeling together with the event

When: once the first wave has passed — a minute later or that evening.

What to do: one sentence on the formula “when this happened, I felt this way.” For example: “When dinner got canceled, I felt hurt.” Not “you don’t value me,” but the event plus your feeling. Then ask yourself: what did I want? What expectation didn’t come true? One sentence — and move on. Naming shouldn’t turn into endlessly replaying your grievances.

How you know it worked: you manage to say what’s going on with you without “you always.” Your partner responds to your feeling instead of defending themselves.

3. Check your guess — ask

When: as soon as you notice you’ve got a ready-made conclusion about what your partner feels or thinks.

What to do: turn the conclusion into a question. Instead of “you don’t give a damn about me” — “It seemed to me you don’t have room for me right now. Is that true, or am I making it up?” Instead of “you’re angry” — “That was a short answer — did something happen, or are you just swamped?” A face, silence and tone are hints, not answers.

If you come from different cultures, they work even worse as proof. Ask each other in what language and in front of whom it’s easier to talk about feelings and family — and treat that as an agreement, not as a conclusion drawn from someone’s nationality. And before you interpret silence or restraint, find out what it does for the person: holding feelings back isn’t always bad (chapters 33, 37 and 38).

How you know it worked: the disagreement clears up — it turns out you each understood the same thing differently. Your partner says: “I felt heard.” You can even keep count: how many times a week a question instead of a conclusion took the tension away.

4. Breathe before you talk about something hard

When: before a conversation you’re dreading, or when a fight has already started to pick up speed.

What to do: sit the way you normally sit. Breathe a little slower than usual, but without effort — so you don’t have to force yourself. For a few minutes. You don’t need to stretch out the exhale on purpose: an even inhale and exhale work just as well2. If you get dizzy, stop. Come back to the topic only if you both agree.

How you know it worked: rate your tension on your own scale before and after — it’s lower. The conversation goes on without threats and pressure.

How this helps couples specifically hasn’t really been tested. But it usually does help you slow yourself down — and it costs nothing.

5. Let them feel that you value them

When: your partner is feeling bad — upset, hurt, tired.

What to do: don’t rush to talk them out of it (“oh, it’s nothing, don’t worry”). Say and show that your partner matters to you: “What’s going on with you matters to me. I’m here.” When researchers compared how people support a partner — talking them out of it, listening, or letting them feel valued — only the last one was linked to relationship quality for both people3. So far this is an isolated finding, but the advice costs nothing.

How you know it worked: your partner softens and starts telling you things instead of getting defensive.

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: you can see on someone’s face what they feel. A frown means anger, a smile means everything’s fine.
In fact: the same facial movement means different things in different situations and cultures4. The same goes for the body: there is no single bodily “fingerprint” for an emotion5. A guess from someone’s face, taken as fact, easily turns into a false accusation.

People think: words mean almost nothing — “93% of communication is nonverbal.”
In fact: that figure comes from experiments where words and tone deliberately contradicted each other and people were asked to guess the speaker’s attitude6. Communication as a whole wasn’t measured there. Words carry a lot — which is why it’s easier to ask what someone meant than to brush off what they said.

People think: emotional intelligence is a special superpower, more important than IQ.
In fact: what emotional intelligence questionnaires usually measure almost coincides with general personality traits7. And “more important than IQ” is a slogan, not a finding: nobody has compared the two that way in relationships. It’s more useful to think about specific skills: notice, name, check, calm down.

People think: take an emotional intelligence training and your relationship will get better.
In fact: trainings raise test scores. But whether there are fewer fights, and whether people start listening to each other better, hasn’t been shown8. A high score and the ability to hear someone are different things.

Talk it over together

  1. What in your body is the first to tell you something’s wrong: your breathing, your stomach, your jaw, your shoulders?
  2. When I answer briefly or go quiet, how do you usually read it? What other readings are possible?
  3. What phrase will we use to check our guesses about each other instead of taking them as fact?
  4. Where, in what language and in front of whom is it easier for you to talk about feelings and family?
  5. What does “I felt heard” mean to you: a question, a pause, a hug, a decision?
  6. If there are more than two of us: what did we agree on, and with whom — and where do my expectations differ from what we agreed?

When it is no longer about skills

Everything above is for ordinary fights where you’re both basically safe. If the relationship involves:

  • violence: pushing, hitting, breaking things, intimidation;
  • control and surveillance: your partner decides who you can see, checks your phone, tracks where you are;
  • constant fear: you weigh every word so as not to set off an outburst;
  • threats of self-harm — yours or your partner’s,

then the exercises in this chapter are not the answer. Safety comes first. If the threat is immediate, call emergency services. After that, see a specialist who works with domestic violence. Don’t do joint exercises in this situation: your partner’s “consent” to them can’t be counted as real. More on this in chapter 23.

02. The body first: signals before words

In short

The body reacts before words do: your heart speeds up, your palms go cold, your jaw clenches. And yet it seems to you that you’re just calmly thinking things through.

A body signal is one observation among others — not a verdict on your partner and not a duty to feel things vividly. And the idea “the better you feel your body, the better your relationship” goes further than what has been tested.

What to do: notice yourself revving up, take a pause and come back to the question itself, not to the accusation. Don’t guess about the other person’s body — ask.

Lena and Wei were planning to go to some friends’ place for the weekend. Wei writes that the plan has changed: Mom is coming. Lena replies: “Fine, makes sense.” If you asked her what she feels, she’d say: “Nothing, I’m just thinking it through.” But she has already reread the message more than once, her palms are cold, her jaw is clenched, and her reply came out shorter than usual. Wei sees only the short “makes sense” and answers even more briefly. For Lena that’s a new trigger, and the circle keeps going.

What is going on

First the autopilot switches on. Any event — being cut off mid-sentence, a cold tone, a plan that fell through — sets off the part of the nervous system that works without your say-so. Your pulse may quicken, you may break into a sweat, your heart rhythm may shift. Each of these signals means something of its own, and none of them on its own tells you which emotion it is. “Revving up” here means the body’s general activation, not sexual arousal (that’s in our book “Alchemy Without Magic”).

Then the body meets meaning. One explanation: the brain brings together the signals from inside with your expectations and the situation and produces a ready-made label — “this is fear,” “this is excitement,” “this is anger.” That’s why the same racing pulse can turn into anxiety, enthusiasm or anger. What decides isn’t the pulse, but how you read the situation.

Meaning pushes you toward action. One person answers more sharply, another goes quiet, a third asks for support or cries (on tears — chapter 05). That action becomes an event for the other person: they have their own body and their own meaning. That’s how two people’s loops lock together.

When words no longer get through. Sometimes the body is so revved up that listening becomes nearly impossible. The studies we looked at give no pulse threshold past which this happens. The guide isn’t a number, but your own signal that you’re revving up.

Feeling your body isn’t just one thing. Researchers separate three things. How accurately you notice a signal, such as your heartbeat. What you say about yourself — how you feel your body. And whether your confidence matches your actual accuracy. These don’t have to line up: someone who says “I don’t feel anything in my body” doesn’t necessarily notice signals poorly. It’s more useful to match faint signals to specific situations, over time. How to find a word for a sensation — chapter 03.

Two bodies in one fight. Partners’ pulse, sweat and other measures often move together. But that’s not a sign of closeness: it also happens in a hostile squabble1. One may have wound up the other, or both reacted to the same sharp remark — you can’t tell from these data. Revving up together in a fight may be mutual escalation, not being in tune with each other. To call such an exchange calming down together, you’d need to see who started it, whether the second person got back to calm, and whether both of you became safer — and there are no such data on couples. A shared pulse did not turn out to be a universal sign of safety in a couple, and the old studies don’t let you predict a breakup from it. Mostly white couples were studied, and there were no same-sex couples in the review. And it hasn’t been shown that this kind of link protects you by itself, either. The practical conclusion: don’t chase a shared pulse and don’t look for who “got wound up” first — stop the escalating exchange and come back to the question (on conflict and the body — chapter 15, on calming down together — chapter 04).

Cortisol and separation. Cortisol is called the stress hormone, but it doesn’t have just one function: it changes with events, with sleep and with how an event is understood. In couples it also often moves together, but not because the substance passes from one person to the other. One person comes home tense from work and speaks curtly, the other takes the tone for anger and tenses up; a shared wake-up time or a separation coming up soon also shift both people on their own. In a good study of couples separated by business trips, mood, sleep, perceived stress, communication and cortisol changed both during the separation and at the reunion. This is about the order of events inside the couple, not about any separation being dangerous.

If there are more than two of you. The experiments on cortisol, hugs and linked pulses were done on spouses and dating couples. For open relationships, swinging, polyamory and relationship anarchy there’s no direct data: not on attention to the body, not on naming feelings, not on repair after a fight. The studies of this kind that were found more often recruited people who signed up on their own, frequently online. A large comparison found no difference in sexual satisfaction between monogamous and non-monogamous people, but it lumped different forms together and didn’t measure the body, so it doesn’t show “healthier” or “worse.” In practice: each partner’s body signal comes out of their own agreement, and the agreements differ from one relationship to another. A broken expectation in one relationship isn’t the same as a breach in another. On jealousy — chapter 25.

If you come from different cultures. There’s no direct data on couples where one partner is from mainland China and the other speaks Russian or lives in Europe. Words for feelings differ in meaning across languages, although their structure is similar. In one comparison of student couples in the US, European Americans showed more positivity in conversation than Chinese Americans. But they are not people living in China, and it doesn’t mean anyone loves less or speaks more directly. And the rule “suppressing emotions is always harmful” was not confirmed when cultures were compared. Don’t predict from nationality — ask: in what language and in whose presence is it more comfortable to discuss feelings and family, and what silence means to each of you (chapter 33).

How to tell it is happening

In yourself:

  • Your body switched on before your thoughts did: heat in your face, cold palms, a clenched jaw, shorter breathing.
  • You ask yourself what you feel, and what comes out is “nothing, I’m just thinking it through.”
  • Your reply came out shorter and drier than usual, and you’ve reread the message more than once.
  • A conclusion about your partner has already grown out of a sensation: “my heart is pounding — so I’m not being respected.”

In your partner:

  • They answer in your tone: just as briefly or more sharply.
  • They go quiet, walk away or, the opposite, push.
  • You both revved up almost at the same moment, and it’s no longer clear who started.

What to do

1. A pause, then the question

When: as soon as you notice yourself revving up — heat, a clenched jaw, faster breathing — especially before a sharp reply.

What to do: say: “I need a pause, let’s come back to this.” Do something calming: take a walk, drink some water, or breathe a little slower than usual, without effort. Then come back to the question itself — to the expectation, not to the accusation. You don’t need to “let out” your anger in a stormy way for this: in a large comparison, activities that bring the revving down reduced anger and aggression2. If you get dizzy, stop the breathing practice. The research doesn’t say how long the pause should last; the main thing is to come back to the topic, not to keep going around it forever.

How you know it worked: the revving up subsides, and the conversation goes on without threats and pressure.

Breathing can help you calm down, but there are many studies, they vary in quality, and the controls are weak in places. In one trial a short breathing practice improved mood and lowered the body’s revving up, and a training where a screen shows your heart signal reduced stress and anxiety according to people’s own reports. Whether this helps couples specifically hasn’t been tested. Nor has anyone tested the “best breathing phase” for a hard conversation.

2. Signal — expectation — request

This isn’t a tested program, but something put together from separate experiments in which putting a feeling into words could change the body’s reaction. It hasn’t been tested in couples.

When: at every flare of anger or hurt, once the first wave has passed.

What to do:

  • Notice one signal in your body.
  • Name the expectation as a guess: “It looks like I was expecting the plan not to change.”
  • Ask yourself and your partner: “What agreement did I think we had?” That can be checked. “My heart is pounding — so I’m being deliberately disrespected” can’t be checked against anything.
  • Ask for something specific: “Give me a heads-up when plans change.”
  • Ask how it sounded to the other person.

If your couple uses two languages, first each of you retells the meaning of what the other said, and only then do you decide what the issue is: language, a family obligation or a broken agreement.

How you know it worked: you end up with more agreements that have been cleared up, and the other person says: “I felt heard.”

3. Two minutes of attention to your body

When: a couple of times a day, at a calm moment. This is a rough guide, not a dose from a study.

What to do: briefly notice your breathing, your posture, the tension in your muscles. Write down in words what you notice and how reliable that sensation seems. Don’t try to produce the “right” sensation and don’t look for an emotion you’re supposed to have. If attention to your body revs up panic, switch outward: look at the objects around you, feel your feet on the floor.

How you know it worked: you notice the revving up before you snap, and your partner, on their own, points out changes in how you act.

This has been tested only weakly: after several months of attention training, people’s reports of shifts in body awareness varied, and these are questionnaire answers, not proven accuracy.

4. About the other person’s body — ask

When: you’ve already formed a conclusion from your partner’s face, tone or silence.

What to do: turn the conclusion into a question: “It seemed to me this is hard for you right now. Is that so, or am I making it up?”, “That was a short answer — did something happen, or are you just swamped?” You can’t tell for sure from a face or a pulse what the other person feels — these are hints, not answers.

How you know it worked: you hear your partner’s own words instead of your guess.

5. Touch — offer it, don’t impose it

When: your partner is having a hard time, there’s tension nearby, and touch won’t be met with objection.

What to do: ask: “Want me to hold your hand?” If yes, just be there with them. If your partner pulls away, no touch for now.

How you know it worked: shoulders drop, breathing evens out, your partner moves closer on their own.

Touch has eased stress only in some individual experiments. After a hug before a stressful task, women had a smaller cortisol release than without a hug, and men didn’t3. Oxytocin, the “love hormone,” hasn’t been shown to be the mechanism here, and the idea of “hugging for oxytocin” wasn’t confirmed in these experiments.

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: if you count your heartbeats without a finger on your pulse, you’ll find out how well you feel your body.
In fact: when the instructions for the task were changed, “accuracy” dropped by about half4. That means a noticeable part of the result is a guess about what your pulse should be and how much time has passed. There are also doubts that a low count points to a weak sense of the body in people who find it hard to name their feelings. A home pulse test is no good for diagnosing either yourself or your partner.

People think: the better you feel your body, the better you do with feelings and intimacy, so it’s something to train.
In fact: the link is weak5. How accurately an adult without any diagnoses notices their own pulse is only slightly linked to how strong an emotion feels. The ability to tell emotions apart, read your partner, empathize and make up after a fight wasn’t measured there. People who meditate rate their accuracy higher, but they don’t feel their heartbeat any better6.

People think: a deep breath fills the brain with oxygen, and the exhale should be longer than the inhale.
In fact: in a healthy person the brain already gets enough oxygen. What helps is a comfortable, slow pace. An exhale longer than the inhale gave no noticeable advantage for anxiety in a weeks-long trial on healthy adults7. And breathing that’s too deep or too fast causes unpleasant symptoms, especially in people who are sensitive to body sensations. Breathe gently and at your own pace.

People think: the nervous system figures out on its own whether it’s safe to be near your partner, and you can see it in the readings on your watch.
In fact: it sounds nice, but it’s poorly tested. Heart rhythm measures depend on breathing, recording conditions and how they’re calculated, and in a fight they won’t tell you which of you is safe. Some scientists seriously dispute the popular theory about this kind of “safety system,” others defend it, and whether talking about it makes a couple safer hasn’t been tested. If breathing helps you calm down, use it even without believing in the theory.

Talk it over together

  1. What is the first thing to change in each of our bodies when we start getting tense: the face, the stomach, the hands, the breathing? If “nothing” — where does it become noticeable later?
  2. What pause signal would work for us so that it doesn’t sound like an accusation or like dodging the topic? How do we come back: who starts the conversation, and when?
  3. In the last tense situation, what expectation or agreement did each of us think was shared? Was it actually shared?
  4. When one of us goes quiet or answers briefly, is it a pause, tiredness, hurt or care? In what language is it easier for us to explain this and describe what we feel in the body?
  5. What kind of touch helps in a tense moment, and when is it better without it? This can differ from partner to partner.
  6. If there are more than two of us: who agreed on what, with whom and when — and where do my expectations differ from the agreement?

When it is no longer about skills

Everything above is for ordinary tense moments when you’re both basically safe. If the relationship involves:

  • violence: pushing, hitting, intimidation;
  • control and surveillance: someone decides who you can see, checks your phone;
  • a threat to harm yourself or someone else,

then breathing, a pause and joint exercises are not the answer. Acting for safety comes first, and an exercise doesn’t replace it. Your partner’s consent to a joint exercise can’t be taken as given in advance. The words “I’m scared” are data: neither watch readings nor talk about the “nervous system” can replace them. More on this in chapter 23.

03. Naming a feeling precisely

In short

The body signals before words do: “pressing,” “tight,” “hot.” Then your head hangs a label on the signal, and what you do next depends on that label. “Bad” doesn’t ask for anything, but “annoyed that the agreement was changed without me” suggests a step.

What to do: speak along the chain “what happened → what feeling this is → what I’m asking for or want to clarify.” Give each other permission to say “my body feels tight, but I can’t tell what it is yet” and come back to the conversation later. Voice a guess about your partner’s feeling as a question, not as a diagnosis. If talking about feelings directly is hard, start with characters from a book or a film.

Mark and Dima agreed to go see friends on Saturday. On Friday, without discussing anything, Dima moves the trip to Sunday: it works better with his job. Mark writes “okay” and puts his cup down a little louder than he needs to. “Is something wrong?” — “It’s fine.” Mark’s chest feels pressed, his jaw is clenched, and the only word he can find is “bad.” Dima hears coldness and goes quiet too. By evening the apartment is silent, and each of them is sure it’s the other who’s taken offense.

What is going on

The body comes first. Pulse, breathing and sweating change before words do. At this point the signal is raw: “pressing,” “hot,” “tight.” It isn’t a feeling yet, but material for one.

If you don’t feel your body well, that’s not a life sentence. The usual test — counting your own heartbeats — largely checks something other than body sensations. “I don’t feel my body well” is a reason to try things out and check (chapter 02).

Then meaning. Your head picks a label for the signal: annoyance, anxiety, hurt, shame. There are far more feelings than “joy, fear, anger, sadness,” and the borders between them are smooth. Here the word chooses the fork in the road. “Bad” doesn’t tell you what to do. “Annoyance: the agreement was changed without me” does — clarify the agreement. And if you read a threat in the signal, you’re more likely to be pulled toward blaming. Anger most often points to a broken expectation, not to your partner being a bad person (chapter 16).

That it’s precisely the nuanced word that turns a feeling into a request is, for now, more of a guess. For couples from different cultures this mechanism hasn’t been proven.

A feeling can be about the event and about yourself. “I’m annoyed that the trip was moved” is about what happened. “Once again nobody takes me into account” is already about how you experience yourself. The second layer can be old: the triggers change, but the feeling stays the same — “nobody sees me,” “everything’s on me,” “it’s all my fault again.” Then the feeling turns out bigger than the trigger, and the conversation about Saturday drags on: it’s no longer only about Saturday. At the same time, the trigger may be real: the agreement really may have been changed without you. Where the second layer comes from and what to do with it — chapters 15 and 30. This is a working distinction; research hasn’t tested it.

Then action — and it becomes an event for the other person. Dima’s body answers the quiet “okay” with tension, his head fills in the meaning (“coldness”), and he chooses his move — silence. Two loops wind each other up. If tears come instead of words, that’s a separate signal (chapter 05).

What a nuanced word gives you. The better a person tells their unpleasant feelings apart, the less often they grab for harmful ways of coping: drinking binges, self-harm, refusing treatment. The link is weak, but it’s the same both in people in treatment and in ordinary people1. People who tell their feelings apart only roughly choose coping strategies no worse than others, but their strategies work worse: more unpleasant feelings are left over. A precise word helps not with choosing a strategy, but with using it.

A caveat: these are associations, not trials. That learning words for feelings reduces violence in a couple or harm from alcohol hasn’t been shown in the studies we looked at.

Saying the word out loud. Naming a feeling and replaying a grievance are different things. A short label (“I’m annoyed”) can gather a blurry state into one word. But if you list over and over again how awful your partner is, you may be rehearsing a threat.

In a lab, one partner in a couple looked at disturbing pictures, and the other picked a name for the feeling. When the partner supplied the name, the person looking felt more relief than when they picked the word on their own — and the more empathy the person naming had, the stronger the relief2. But this is a lab with pictures, not a fight: we didn’t find studies of fights using this technique.

Hence the risk. When you understand your own revved-up state better, it’s easier to take a pause, but it’s also easy to mistake a guess about someone else’s feeling for a fact. What you’ve guessed stays a hypothesis until your partner confirms it. Faces won’t help you out: facial expressions depend on the person, the culture and the situation (chapter 06).

When there are no words at all. Alexithymia is a marked difficulty understanding what you feel and telling others about it. It usually also includes the habit of talking about events rather than states: “my partner was late again,” but not “I felt hurt.” In the loop it looks like this: the body signal is there, but the meaning doesn’t get filled in. Silence can then mean “I don’t know what this is,” not “I don’t care.”

Small studies hint that the difficulty lives not only inside a person, but also between people. In a lab, two women who didn’t know each other talked: if the one telling the story found it hard to name feelings, the listener was worse at telling from the video whether she was feeling good or bad. In diaries of couples living together, the ability to finely tell a partner’s emotions apart turned out to be a separate skill: the higher it was, the less anxiety the partner had. These are all small studies or “snapshots” of a single moment in time: they show where to look, not how much this harms relationships. The practical conclusion: “can’t name it” may mean “doesn’t know what it is,” not “doesn’t care.”

How to tell it is happening

In yourself:

  • Any unpleasant feeling gets one word: “bad,” “it drives me crazy,” “meh.”
  • Your body switched on, but it’s not clear what the feeling is. What comes out is “fine” or silence.
  • You talk about events, but not about what’s going on with you.
  • What grows straight out of the feeling is an accusation, not a request: “you don’t respect me” instead of “don’t change our agreements without me.”
  • The feeling is noticeably bigger than the trigger and familiar from other triggers: “once again nobody asked me.”

In your partner:

  • They answer “fine” or “okay,” but their body says something else: a loud cup, a clenched jaw.
  • Asked “what are you feeling?”, they’re at a loss, answer “bad” or describe what happened. This may mean “I don’t know what it is,” not “I don’t care.”
  • They go quiet in response to your coldness, and you both think it was the other who pulled away.

What to do

The techniques follow the chain “event → feeling → request.” A program like this hasn’t been tested in couples as a whole: it’s put together from what is known in pieces.

1. Event — feeling — request

When: at any noticeable sting during the day. Start with small things, not with a complicated fight.

What to do: four steps. Name the event without judging it: “we agreed on Saturday, the trip got moved.” Find two or three words and choose the closest: “annoyance, tension, a little hurt.” Name the expectation: “for me, Saturday was an agreement.” Make a request or ask: “did we understand it the same way?”

If there are no words, say exactly that: “my body feels tight, but I can’t tell what it is yet, I’ll come back to it later.” And really do come back to the conversation. Agree that this is allowed for both of you.

If the feeling is noticeably bigger than the trigger, also ask yourself: “Who do I feel I am right now?” An answer like “the one who once again wasn’t asked” is the second layer. Talk about it as your own: “this is familiar to me from other situations,” not “you always ignore me.” That doesn’t cancel the request about the trigger itself.

How you know it worked: over the week you have more agreements that have been cleared up, and your partner says: “I felt heard.” Measure that, not the size of your vocabulary.

2. Voice a guess about your partner’s feeling as a question

When: you catch yourself thinking that you already know what your partner feels.

What to do: say your guess as an assumption, as a question: “Does it feel like annoyance?” Not “you’re angry.” Faces and silence are hints, not answers.

How you know it worked: your partner confirms or corrects you (“not quite, more like this”), and the conversation is about the feeling, not about who’s right.

3. Ask what the word means

When: words like “hurt,” “duty,” “respect” sound different to the two of you — especially if you come from different languages and cultures.

What to do: ask: “When you say this word, what happens in your body, and what response do you want to get?” If an expectation didn’t come true, find out whose promise, or whose duty to the family, each of you believed was there. And agree on what language and in front of whom it’s easier for you to talk about feelings and family. These are preferences you talk through, not something you predict from nationality. And if your question is met with silence or “fine,” clarify: is it that the word can’t be found in this language, or that the feeling itself is unclear? These are different difficulties.

How you know it worked: it turns out the same word carried different weight for each of you, and you name what’s behind it.

A comparison of languages found both differences and a shared basis, but that’s not a forecast for your couple. A study of young American couples of Chinese and European descent showed that in a fight, cultural background affected how feelings were expressed and how the body responded — but these weren’t mixed marriages, and it wasn’t mainland China. And the rule “suppressing feelings is always harmful” isn’t confirmed by a review across different countries. For a couple where one person speaks Russian and the other is from mainland China, there’s no tested protocol. What’s left is to ask (chapters 33, 37, 38).

4. A diary of shades

When: you notice a shift in your body or your mood.

What to do: write down the event and rate several different feelings on your own scale, not just one: anger, annoyance, hurt, anxiety, shame, loneliness, confusion. In the evening reread it: has your explanation of what happened changed? It’s good to start with a short list of what unpleasant feelings there are and how they differ: this kind of learning improved people’s ability to tell feelings apart in an experiment that compared them with a group without the learning3. You can split an entry into four parts: what’s in your body, what word fits, how you interpret it, what you feel pulled toward. That way you can separate the sensation from the guess (this breakdown is a working one; it hasn’t been tested).

How you know it worked: your words have become more distinct, and your requests clearer. Check whether the diary helps you notice things, rather than just adding more picking apart.

This hasn’t been tested on what matters most: in healthy adults, the ability to tell feelings apart grew over a period of frequent check-ins, but that’s an observation, not a trial. It showed no effect on aggression, drinking or fights in a couple.

5. Attention to your body, for several months

When: during the day, briefly.

What to do: note your breathing, your posture, where you’re tense, and write down what you notice. Don’t come up with a name for the feeling right away. If looking inward revs up panic, switch outward: the objects around you, sounds. Breathing techniques are in chapter 04.

How you know it worked: more often you manage to say “something’s tight in my chest” without filling in a reason.

In a study, this kind of training changed how people themselves rated their awareness of body sensations. Mindfulness programs increased clarity about feelings, but the conclusion is preliminary. They don’t automatically improve heartbeat-counting accuracy or satisfaction with the relationship.

6. Talk through characters

When: talking about feelings directly is hard — for you or for your partner. “What are you feeling?” is met with emptiness, “fine” or a retelling of events.

What to do: discuss a film, a book or a story about people you know: who was hurt by what, why the character acted that way. You can follow the chain: what the character noticed, how they understood it, what they did. And then, if you feel like it, ask yourself: “Do I recognize something of my own in this?”

How you know it worked: there are more words for feelings — first about the character, then sometimes about yourself too: “that happens to me as well.”

Whether these conversations teach people to name their own feelings hasn’t been tested. Something else was studied: reading fiction, on average, slightly improves performance on tasks about understanding other people4. But that’s about other people’s feelings, not your own, and not about conversation in a couple. So this is a workaround, not a treatment. Watching films about couples together as a separate technique — chapter 35.

What isn’t known yet. Whether better differentiation of feelings helps both partners repair the relationship is unknown. Who attention to the body is harmful for hasn’t been worked out either: with strong bodily distress it can intensify panic. For open and polyamorous relationships, same-sex couples and nonbinary people, no tested effects of these techniques were found in the studies we looked at. In practice: the same questions “body → meaning → request,” without roles tied to gender. And if there are several partners, ask what agreement was made with whom and whom it concerns.

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: say a feeling out loud and it will weaken right away.
In fact: not necessarily. In an experiment with spiders, people who put their feelings into words had a weaker bodily reaction to the spider a week later than the others did, and were a bit more willing to approach it than those who were distracted. But the fear they reported themselves didn’t differ from the other groups5. A word may change the body and behavior later on, but immediate relief isn’t promised. And a spider isn’t a fight: this hasn’t been tested on couples. So “name it and calm down” is too strong a promise.

People think: men go quiet because they don’t feel anything.
In fact: the data paint a different picture — one about learned rules and early experience. The more strictly a man follows “masculine” norms, the worse his mental health tends to be on average and the less willing he is to seek psychological help, and things are worse still with relationships to other people. It’s specific norms that are linked: “do everything yourself,” “power over women,” “playboy.” The norm “work comes first” doesn’t belong here6. The second layer is childhood: maltreatment is linked to difficulty naming feelings in adulthood, and the link ranges from weak to moderate7. These are associations, not proven causes, and “male muteness” is a mindset, not a diagnosis and not a matter of ability.

People think: women are more emotional, and men are worse at empathy.
In fact: this phrase lumps different things together: experiencing, expressing, talking about yourself, reading other people’s feelings and the body’s response. Each has its own difference, and there’s no single number for “more emotional.” Men have, on average, slightly more difficulty naming feelings, most noticeably in the habit of talking about events rather than states8. Women, on average, read emotional signals more accurately, but an earlier review of brain studies didn’t find a consistently stronger response to other people’s pain in women. An average doesn’t describe a specific person.

People think: alexithymia is a trait, and there’s nothing you can do about it.
In fact: it’s a difficulty, not an unchangeable essence. Clarity about your own feelings grows after mindfulness programs — both compared with before and compared with people who didn’t take the program9. A short training (what feelings there are and how they differ) improved the ability to tell unpleasant emotions apart, while the group without training showed no growth3. Caveats: the studies vary in quality, and the conclusion is preliminary; this is clarity, not treatment; it doesn’t prove that people started feeling their bodies more accurately; no lasting rise in partners’ satisfaction has been shown.

Talk it over together

  1. Which words for anger, hurt and anxiety do you use most often, and how do they differ in your body?
  2. When you answer “fine” or go quiet, what’s going on inside, and what response from me would help?
  3. What agreement do you mean when you say “but we decided”?
  4. How is it easier for you to talk about something unpleasant: right away, an hour later, in writing?
  5. When I guess your feeling out loud, what’s better: to ask, to stay quiet, or to offer two options?
  6. If there are more than two of us: with which of us is it easier for you to name feelings, and what helps with that?
  7. Is there a feeling about yourself that switches on for you over different things — for example, “once again nobody sees me”? How can I tell that it’s this one that got hit right now?

When it is no longer about skills

The techniques in this chapter are meant for relationships where it’s safe for both of you to talk. If there is:

  • violence;
  • coercive control and surveillance;
  • a threat to harm yourself or someone else,

then naming feelings and breathing don’t replace safety. A joint exercise is possible only with the free consent of both of you: nobody is forced into it. If there’s immediate danger, call your local emergency services. More on violence and control — chapter 23; on when you need a specialist — chapter 35.

04. How to calm down: on your own, together, and when you’re helping someone else

In short

You can calm an emotion at different points of the loop “event → body → meaning → action”: in experiments, looking at the situation differently worked best, while trying not to feel barely helped. “Keeping up a front” isn’t free for the person next to you.

What to do about it: notice the body signal and name the feeling; when you need a pause, say so and say when you’ll come back; before helping, ask what’s needed — to listen, to figure something out together or to do something practical; acknowledge your partner’s feeling without agreeing in advance with their version of the facts; touch only with consent.

Small observations suggest that after a hard day, silence can be a way to come back to yourself, not a closed door: sometimes helping means giving a little quiet.

These techniques haven’t been tested separately on couples from different cultures or on relationships with several partners, so treat them as hypotheses you test on yourself.

Lena came home from work after a hard conversation with her boss. When Marina asked “how was your day?”, she answered “fine” and started scrubbing a cup that had been clean for a long time. Marina can see that not everything is fine. She asks again, then suggests writing her boss an email, then brings up looking for a new job. Lena hears her confusion being turned into a task and shuts down even tighter. Marina hears a closed door in the silence and starts talking louder. By night both of them are tense — and both are sure the other one did everything wrong.

Both of them meant well.

What is going on

The body comes first. The boss’s words, silence at dinner, a message with no reply — and the body rearranges itself: the heart speeds up, breathing gets shorter, the skin sweats a little, blood pressure rises, the stress hormone goes up. You can’t tell from the hormone what feeling it is: the body responds this way to anger, to fear and to excitement alike.

Then meaning and action. The meaning gets filled in: “I’m not respected,” “my partner doesn’t care,” “I’m failing again.” It suggests an action: speak up, stay quiet, walk away, fix it. And the action is already an event for someone else’s body.

Who calms whom. You can calm yourself (you change a link in your own loop), calm down together (you affect each other with a hand, a voice, your presence) and calm the other person (with advice, a joke, comfort). This is a working scheme, not a proven law: it only shows at which point you step in.

What changes the feeling, and what changes only the picture. You can step in in different ways: distract yourself, rethink the meaning, accept the feeling (let it be there), hide its expression. In lab experiments, looking at the situation differently helped most, especially through another person’s eyes. Distraction helped less. You can manage to hide the expression, but the feeling barely changes, and trying not to feel at all gave almost nothing1. These are lab tasks, not a ranking of techniques for couples. The conclusion is simple: a calm face doesn’t yet mean a calm body.

Looking at things differently was tested on married couples too. In one trial, couples spent a year briefly describing their fights through the eyes of a well-meaning outsider, and the drop in marriage quality that the other couples went through over that year didn’t show up in theirs2. How to do it — chapter 17, the technique “The view of a neutral third party.”

If anger grew out of a broken expectation, first find out which expectation was shared, and only then argue with the feeling. This is an idea to test, not a trial result (chapter 16). How to notice a body signal and name it — chapters 02 and 03.

“Keeping up a front” isn’t free for the other person. Someone who stays quiet and keeps up a front is hard to read. Their partner searches for cues, spends more effort and gets tense too. That’s how researchers explain it. In experiments, women who didn’t know each other were assigned to suppress their emotions during a hard conversation: the connection broke down, and the other woman’s blood pressure went up3. These aren’t couples, so read this as a direction, not a size. In couples it looks similar: when a person hides their feelings while sacrificing something for their partner, both tend to feel worse. But nobody assigned suppression at random there, so it’s an association, not a proven cause. On tears — chapter 05.

Support. When someone feels bad, it’s a request for contact, and your response is the next event. If it matches the request, “no one’s there for me” can turn into “I’m being helped.” If it misses the feeling, it reads as “I’ve been brushed off.” Couples who cope with stress together have, on average, better relationships, and what matters is the quality of that coping, not the number of conversations and pieces of advice. Couples’ diaries turned up something unexpected: sometimes help was given in a way that went unnoticed, and this “invisible” help went along with better adjustment to stress. And in same-sex couples in new relationships, one partner’s stress was linked to the other’s relationship satisfaction, and the cushioning effect of coping together showed up only later. These are associations and self-reports; the cause hasn’t been established.

Touch. Light stroking activates nerve fibers linked to pleasantness, and a hug adds a social signal. But after that, appraisal does the work: is it the right person, the right moment, a safe place? Welcome touch can lower the sense of threat; unwanted touch flips the meaning. In experiments where people were waiting for an electric shock, a close person’s hand weakened the brain’s response to the threat more than a stranger’s hand, which on the whole didn’t help. The authors found no differences between spouses, people who were dating and friends4. This is the brain’s response, not a guarantee of relief in a fight. There’s no single “hand rule.”

Do partners catch each other’s stress? One person’s stress shows in their speech, attention, sleep and behavior in a fight, and for the other person that’s an event too. In newlyweds, the spouses’ hormonal stress responses became more alike over time. But stress isn’t “transmitted” by molecules: shared schedules, conditions and conflicts also produce a match, and the match doesn’t always calm things down — it also shows up in fights. Perhaps the second person doesn’t copy the stress, but helps with it. No such studies on same-sex couples turned up in the review.

When the load is brought in from outside. Answering calmly when you’ve been hurt takes effort. It seems that stress from outside uses up the same strength: in newlyweds’ diaries, on tense days spouses had less strength left to respond calmly to difficulties in the couple5. And when there was more stress from outside, how people rated the relationship that day depended more strongly on individual small things with their partner: one failure colored their opinion of the whole relationship6.

What does a person do at home on an evening like that? In a small observation of air traffic controllers’ families, after overloaded shifts the controllers withdrew into themselves at home more often; if they were supported on such an evening, there was more withdrawal and less anger7. The authors read it this way: quiet helps an overloaded person get back to their normal state, and a supportive partner, by giving that quiet, softens the spillover of tension into the couple. These are all associations, seen in newlyweds and in a small group of controllers’ families, not trial results; they shouldn’t be applied to everyone. Perhaps what Lena with her cup needed wasn’t a task, but a little quiet. Taking quiet time and saying so isn’t the same as silently keeping up a front in the middle of a conversation. How to talk about a hard day as soon as you walk in the door — chapter 15, the technique “When the stress was brought in from outside.”

How to tell it is happening

In yourself:

  • Your body revs up (heat, heartbeat, a clenched jaw), but there are no words yet.
  • You say “fine” or stay quiet, but inside it isn’t fine.
  • You want to fix things and talk the other person out of it right away, even though nobody asked.
  • The conversation goes in circles: the same grievance retold again.
  • After a hard day, a small thing from your partner seems like a sign that everything is bad.

In your partner:

  • They say “fine,” but their body shows it isn’t — like Lena with the cup.
  • After your help or advice, they shut down even tighter.
  • While your partner stays quiet and keeps up a front, you notice tension growing in you too.
  • From something you did or a failure of their own, they draw a conclusion about everything: “you don’t care about me,” “I’m no good for anything.”

What to do

Everything below is for ordinary fights. If your partner coerces or controls you, these techniques don’t fit (chapter 23).

1. Notice your body and name the feeling without arguing with it

When: when it hits you and your hand is already reaching to reply or to walk away.

What to do: find one place of tension in your body: jaw, chest, stomach, breathing. Name the sensation and the feeling without arguing with it: “tightness in my chest, feels like hurt.” Only then choose an action: speak, ask for a pause, step out to breathe. If your reasoning runs ahead of your body, add a quick check before any explanations: tension, breathing, feeling. This is an adaptation; it hasn’t been tested on its own.

How you know it worked: you keep a short log: body signals, how hard it was before and after (on your own scale), whether you did what you’d agreed on. There’s no tested “dose”; to start, once a day at a calm moment and every time things get tense.

A short training in “observe and accept” lowered the body’s stress reaction in the lab; it hasn’t been tested in couples’ fights. Accepting a feeling doesn’t mean accepting unfair treatment (chapter 23).

2. A pause with a return

When: you notice heat, a faster pulse, a clenched jaw, and the conversation is still going.

What to do: say out loud: “I need a pause, I’ll be back at such-and-such time.” Do something that calms you and is within your strength. Come back and this time talk about the expectation: note the body signal, name the broken expectation as a guess, ask whether it was shared, ask for a specific change, check how your partner heard you. In a bilingual couple, first each of you retells the meaning of what the other said, and only then do you decide what the issue is: language, a family obligation or a broken agreement.

How you know it worked: you came back to the conversation, and you both feel calmer. A pause works if the return actually happens. If it drags on into long avoidance or drowns out a safety issue, it’s no use. Trials with couples haven’t compared this kind of pause.

3. Acknowledge the feeling and ask what kind of help is needed

When: every time the other person feels bad.

What to do: repeat the feeling and the context in your own words: “it hurt that you were interrupted in front of everyone.” Ask: “Am I getting the feeling right?” Then: “Should I listen, help figure it out together, or help with something practical?” An honest answer can also be: “Give me a little quiet.” That can be help too (see above, “When the load is brought in from outside”); agree on when you’ll come back to the conversation. Advice is good when it’s asked for and can actually be carried out, but it hasn’t been proven to be the best default. Reading feelings without mistakes isn’t a magic button either: the link between guessing accuracy and satisfaction is weak8, so check your guess out loud (chapter 06). There’s no tested sequence of phrases, and models about stages of acknowledgment are still theory.

How you know it worked: you hear “yes, exactly,” and the tension is lower.

Acknowledging a feeling and being there isn’t the same as sorting out your partner’s emotions every time. “I can see this is hard for you, let’s just be with it for a bit” is a conversation between equals. But if one person habitually solves the other’s emotional problems, the roles can freeze: caretaker and cared-for. This is a working distinction; it hasn’t been tested. How the caretaker role is connected to desire — chapter 18.

4. Touch with consent

When: the other person is having a hard time, and you both want it.

What to do: ask before touching, or offer a hand or a hug, and accept a “no” without taking offense: unwanted touch can itself be a threat. If someone else’s touch isn’t available or feels unpleasant, a hand on your own chest can help: in one experiment it also lowered the hormonal response to stress.

How you know it worked: the person feels lighter: shoulders drop, breathing evens out.

5. Check how the conversation ended

When: you keep coming back to the same unpleasant topic over and over.

What to do: before and after the conversation, ask each of you how you’re feeling. And honestly: did the conversation end with a decision or with a retelling of the same grievance? Chewing over the same thing together isn’t the same as telling each other how your day went. That this kind of circle causes depression hasn’t been shown: being low itself changes energy and communication.

How you know it worked: after the conversation you’re calmer and there’s a decision, not another lap around the circle.

6. Accept the feeling, but not the other person’s conclusion

When: your partner draws a conclusion about everything from something you did or a failure of their own: “no call means you don’t care about me,” “I’m no good for anything.”

What to do: separate three things: the feeling, the facts and the conclusion. First the feeling: “I can see you were anxious, and that matters.” Then the facts from your side, without arguing and without taking on blame that isn’t there: “Here’s what was going on with me that day. I do care.” At “I’m no good for anything,” don’t rush to rescue, and don’t agree either: “I can see how much it hurts right now. That’s not how I see you.” If this kind of pain doesn’t let go, suggest thinking together about who else they could talk to about it: that’s an addition to your support, not a replacement for it. Acknowledging a feeling doesn’t mean agreeing with the conclusion (section “What people usually think — and what is true”).

The danger of this technique is brushing someone off under the guise of calm. If promises really are being broken, your partner’s anxiety isn’t a whim, and it’s the actions that need sorting out. And if the same request for reassurance keeps coming around again and again, don’t cut back on warmth: at a calm moment, talk about the circle itself (chapter 10).

How you know it worked: your partner hears that the feeling has been accepted, and the conversation is about what actually happened, not about who doesn’t value whom. If the feeling stayed unheard, go back to it rather than repeating the facts.

In people who felt understood, fights had a weaker effect on relationship satisfaction9. The technique itself hasn’t been tested.

If there are more than two of you. There are no experiments on these techniques in relationships with several partners: they were tested on women who didn’t know each other, married women, spouses and newlyweds. A comparison of monogamous and non-monogamous people found no noticeable difference in sexual satisfaction. But different formats were lumped together there, and studies like this often rely on volunteers, so the result can’t be carried over to everyone, and it says nothing about stress and touch. What follows is a suggestion, not a tested protocol: figure out in advance who agreed to share what information with whom; say which partner is being asked for support; check whether a response breaks the others’ privacy. The cost of staying silent about agreements in a network is different from what it is in a couple (chapters 27 and 29).

If you come from different cultures. There are no direct studies of bodily reactions in couples of “a person from mainland China and a Russian-speaking or European partner.” The closest data are student couples in the US: European Americans showed more positive emotions in conversation than Chinese Americans. But these aren’t mixed couples, and it doesn’t mean anyone is less caring. There’s no data that a person from mainland China prefers indirect communication, that the family will run the relationship, or that a Russian-speaking partner is blunt. Ask the person themselves what a pause, consulting with relatives and translated words about feelings mean: is it politeness, time to calm down, disagreement or leaving the conversation? Agree in advance who gets personal information. What counts as affectionate and appropriate touch is for the two of you to decide (chapters 33, 37 and 38).

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: if you don’t show your emotions, your partner and the relationship are protected.
In fact: partly. You can hide the expression, but the feeling barely changes1, and it costs the listener3. Among US students of European descent, a habit of hiding emotions went along with worse mood; among students from Hong Kong, no such link was found10. But this is about the person’s own well-being, not about any benefit to the partner, and not about mainland China. A short silence can be appropriate. The risk is habitual shutting down, especially if your partner reads silence as withdrawal. There’s no need to demand opening up in the middle of danger or overload: agree on when you’ll come back to the conversation.

People think: acknowledging your partner’s feeling means agreeing with it.
In fact: acknowledgment says “from where you stand, this feeling makes sense.” It confirms neither your conclusion about the facts nor your request. It has been tested on its own, but in the lab and on students, not on couples11. If an expectation was broken, acknowledge the disappointment and separately find out whether you’d actually agreed on it. Acknowledgment doesn’t justify control or violence (chapter 23).

People think: a 20-second hug releases oxytocin (the “closeness hormone”) and relieves stress.
In fact: in an experiment with heterosexual couples, a hug like this before a stressful task reduced the release of the stress hormone in women, but not in men12. That oxytocin was the cause hasn’t been shown. Its level in the blood, judging by a review, doesn’t consistently track attachment. There’s no universal rule.

People think: anger has to be let out, otherwise it stays inside.
In fact: anger and aggression were noticeably reduced by activities that lower arousal13. There’s no single dose there for fights over broken expectations. There’s no physiological need to vent anger energetically.

Talk it over together

  1. When I’m having a hard time, what do I need more: for you to listen, to figure it out together or to do something practical? How will you tell if I’m quiet?
  2. What does my “fine” or my silence mean to you: politeness, time to calm down, disagreement, leaving the conversation?
  3. What kind of pause in a fight works for both of us, and how will we agree on when to come back?
  4. When I touch you at a hard moment, does it help, get in the way or depend on the situation? How should I ask?
  5. What expectation of you did I think was shared, even though we never talked it through?
  6. If there are more than two of us: which of us can be asked for support in which situations, and what of what’s said can be passed on to others?
  7. What feelings and states can’t I bring into our relationship: anger, tiredness, tears, idleness, a bad mood? And which of them do I find hard to bear in myself — or in you?

05. Tears: what happens in the body and what the other person hears

In short

Tears are both an event in the body and a signal to another person. That they “cleanse” the body hasn’t been confirmed. But as a signal they do work: in experiments, visible tears more often made people want to support the person crying.

Whether the person crying feels better depends on the reason, the moment and the response of whoever is nearby. Dry eyes are no proof that you can’t feel.

What to do: don’t interpret the tears — ask what’s needed right now, then come back to the reason, and by evening check whether the response you chose helped. This order is a proposal, not something that’s been tested.

Yaroslav and Denis are arguing about the weekend: go to Denis’s mom’s place or stay home. Yaroslav lays out his arguments evenly, point by point. Behind the arguments is an expectation he never actually says out loud: that sometimes the weekend would be just theirs. Then he breaks off mid-sentence: his throat has tightened, his eyes have filled up, his voice won’t obey him. “It’s allergies,” he says, and turns away toward the window.

Denis immediately has several versions in his head: “he’s really hurting,” “he’s playing on my sympathy,” “I need to wrap this up before it gets worse.” Denis picks the fastest one: “Fine, we’ll go.” Yaroslav nods. By evening they both have the same feeling: they talked, and nothing got decided.

What is going on

Before the tears. They can be set off by an expectation that’s been broken or is under threat — like Yaroslav’s with the weekend. The body responds with arousal: the throat tightens, breathing gets knocked off rhythm, the eyes get wet. This is a raw signal; it doesn’t say what exactly happened.

During. Tears come in different kinds. Basal tears moisten and protect the eye all the time. Reflex tears come in response to an irritant, like onions. Emotional tears follow an appraisal of an event and bodily arousal. The gland is switched on by the part of the nervous system that runs the glands and internal organs without the will getting involved. The rest of the body goes its own way: pulse and breathing can change along with the experience. There’s no single pattern — the picture differs from person to person.

People say that crying releases “feel-good hormones” — oxytocin and endorphins. It sounds nice, but it’s poorly tested: no confirming measurements in humans were found. Tears themselves say nothing about how strong a feeling is, about honesty or about hormone levels.

After. You don’t always feel better, and not always right away. A painful reason brings on tears and may at first make you feel worse; then support or solving the problem changes how you see the event and your mood. In people who recalled their own tears, a better mood went together with support, solving the problem or a sudden insight, while shame and holding back tears went with a worse mood after crying. But from memories you can’t tell whether it was the tears that helped or the fact that someone was there. So the question isn’t “did enough tears come out,” but “did the episode itself and what came after it help this person.”

Meaning. The body gives a raw signal, and the head fills in the meaning: “loss,” “relief,” “closeness,” “I can’t cope,” “ashamed that people can see.” Then comes the action: stay, turn away, say “allergies.”

The other person. For the other person, the tears and this action are a new trigger, and their own loop starts up. Tears get read as a request for help, a sign of closeness, pressure or weakness — sometimes in several ways at once: “they need help,” “this person is close to me,” but also “can they handle this,” “is this pressure.” The other person’s response — coming closer, asking, steering away from the topic — in turn becomes a trigger for the person crying: a warm response can change how they see the event and their mood, while hostility and shame work in the opposite direction. That’s how two loops lock together. If there are several partners, there are more onlookers, and each one’s response is a separate trigger. The skills are the same: notice the body signal (chapter 02), calm yourself and your partner (chapter 04), read the other person’s loop (chapter 06).

What tears do to the onlooker. The strictest test: people from different countries were shown people with tears and without them. Visible tears noticeably increased the desire to offer support, and the boost came specifically from the tears, not just from the facial expression1. But people were rating strangers in pictures, and they were asked about intentions, not about actual help. Whether this works in established relationships, and whether the person crying feels better because of it, the experiment didn’t test. That tears end an argument hasn’t been tested either.

The smell of tears. In one experiment, men who sniffed women’s tears reported less sexual arousal; another group didn’t replicate this. In one game such men were less aggressive, but there’s no independent confirmation, and the active substance hasn’t been found. It’s too early to build advice for couples on this.

Who cries, and in front of whom. The same feeling in the throat reads sometimes as “it’s safe to show this,” sometimes as “I’ll lose respect.” The data were collected by country, not by couple: for a couple like “one from Russia, the other from China” there are no measurements. The Chinese saying “a man doesn’t shed tears lightly” is a possible norm, but not a forecast for a specific person. For same-sex, transgender and nonbinary partners, and for different agreements about the relationship, there are no ready-made rules either. There’s one working question: what does this particular person risk by showing tears in front of others? More in chapters 32 and 33.

How to tell it is happening

In yourself:

  • Your throat tightens, your eyes get wet, your voice won’t obey you, your breathing gets knocked off rhythm — before any words.
  • You want to hide the tears: turn away, put it all down to “allergies.”
  • Behind your even arguments is an expectation that never got said out loud.
  • Along with the tears comes a wave of shame that you’re being seen.
  • There are no tears, but something has shifted in your body, and you can’t find words for it. A feeling can be there without tears.
  • After consensual intimacy, for no visible reason, you feel bad and tears come. The authors of a clinical review call this relatively common but easy for doctors to miss; it’s a review, not a measurement, and there are no figures (chapter 11; on the physiology of arousal and orgasm — in the book “Alchemy Without Magic”).

In your partner:

  • Several versions come to mind at once: “my partner is hurting,” “they’re playing on my sympathy,” “I need to wrap up this conversation.”
  • You’re pulled to give in quickly or change the subject.
  • A suspicion shows up: “this is manipulation.” It’s one of the versions, not a fact.
  • Afterward: you talked, and nothing got decided.

What to do

1. Ask, don’t interpret

When: whenever someone near you is crying, especially in the middle of an argument.

What to do:

  • Ask: “What would be better right now: me staying close, giving you space or touching you?”
  • Name the topic or the expectation without diagnosing the tears. Not “you’re playing on my sympathy,” but “we stopped at the weekend, and it matters to me.”
  • Agree on when to come back to what’s unresolved: in an hour or tomorrow evening. A pause is normal if you do come back to the topic: the expectation doesn’t go anywhere, even when the conversation is cut off.
  • By evening, each of you briefly notes how hard it was and whether the response you chose helped.

How you know it worked: the topic really did get discussed, by evening you both feel less tension, and you both say the response you chose helped. Honestly: this order is a proposal, not something that’s been tested. There’s no “how much to cry” dose.

2. If you’re the one crying: body first, then the request

When: your throat has already tightened, and you’re pulled to turn away or say “allergies.”

What to do: ask yourself: “What has changed in my breathing, my throat, my face, my posture?” (chapter 02) — especially if you’re stronger with words than with sensations. Then say in one sentence what you need: for them to stay close, to give you space or to touch you. And name the expectation behind the tears: “It matters to me that sometimes the weekend is just ours.”

How you know it worked: the topic really did get discussed instead of getting lost behind the word “allergies,” and you can say that the response you chose helped.

3. A long hug, only with consent

When: when you both want it and it’s appropriate.

What to do: hug not for a second, but for longer. In an experiment with couples, a hug like this before lab stress reduced the release of the stress hormone in women, but not in men2. What was tested was a hug before stress, not comforting someone who’s crying, and only in opposite-sex couples; real fights and same-sex and non-monogamous relationships haven’t been tested. Pay attention not to the hormone, but to whether the touch is wanted.

How you know it worked: the person themselves says the touch was what they needed.

4. Writing about a breakup: keep an eye on how you feel

When: you want to write out on paper what happened.

What to do: in a trial with people who had separated, writing about what happened could make recovery worse — for those who were searching it for meaning3. Giving a feeling room and prescribing how it should be expressed are different things. If you write, notice how you feel, and drop the kind of writing after which you consistently feel worse (chapter 22).

How you know it worked: you don’t end up feeling worse after writing, time after time.

5. A plan for when a baby is crying

When: in advance, before your patience runs out, and again whenever a new adult is going to be with the child.

What to do: agree on where the baby can safely be put down, who will take over and how to step out into another room. Go over it with every adult who stays with the child. Educational materials about babies’ crying have been tested: mothers’ knowledge grew, and more of them advised stepping away when irritation builds. This didn’t show a drop in injuries, and nobody has tested the plan itself. If the baby has trouble falling asleep: in a trial, behavioral sleep programs for babies from six months to a little over a year improved sleep measures and found no differences in attachment, and later no broad harm either, but that doesn’t prove zero risk; there were no newborns in the trials (chapter 31).

How you know it worked: every adult can describe the plan and use it.

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: you need to have a good cry: stress hormones and toxins come out with tears, and you’ll feel better.
In fact: the composition of emotional tears is slightly different, but no removal of stress hormones or toxins in any noticeable amount has been shown4. And you don’t always feel better — far from it: in a diary study of women, only in about a third of cases5; a study of adults found no overall improvement right after crying — everything depended on the reason, and recovery could come later6. If someone feels worse, it’s better to offer support or a change of scene, not more tears.

People think: tears are manipulation, a way of playing on sympathy.
In fact: in experiments with pictures, tears made a person seem more helpless and increased the desire to help7. But this shows how tears are perceived, not why a person cries; neither this experiment nor the international one showed that tears serve a calculation. And the onlooker’s sympathy doesn’t prove the person crying is sincere: suspicion is only a version, and you can’t check it from the tear itself. Check the facts, ask what’s needed, respond like a human being. An accusation right off the bat can kill the wish to open up; how often that happens is unknown.

People think: women cry more often because of hormones, and a man who cries looks weak.
In fact: in a survey across many countries, women reported crying more often, but the difference varied a lot from country to country, so hormones alone can’t explain it (the survey was mostly of students). In an experiment with photos, a person with tears seemed warmer but less competent; in the multi-country experiment, tears increased willingness to offer support. “Weak” depends on who’s looking, and it doesn’t predict how your partner will react.

People think: if someone doesn’t cry, they’re closed off and cold.
