In fact: tears are one channel, not a gauge of feeling. One person finds it hard to identify and describe their feelings; another names a feeling precisely, but has no tears. Depression, a habit of holding back in front of others and medications can all play a part. A review of clinical observations notes blunted feelings in a noticeable share of people being treated for depression with antidepressants8, but there the drug isn’t separated from the illness itself, and this isn’t the share of people who can’t cry. Instead of a label, ask: “What’s going on with you, in your body and in your thoughts?” And if a medication was recently started or changed: “Did this change after it?” Anger over a broken expectation can be named separately from the question of whether the other person cried (chapter 16).

Talk it over together

The questions also work for a conversation among several partners: each person answers for themselves.

  1. What comes to your mind first when you see my tears, and what do you feel like doing?
  2. What are your own tears for you: a request for help, tiredness, anger, shame, a way to take a pause?
  3. What’s needed at a moment like that: staying close, giving space, touching, asking or staying quiet?
  4. How can we agree on a pause in an argument: for how long, and who comes back to the topic first?
  5. How did your family treat tears — men’s, women’s, in front of guests? In what language is it easier for you to say “I feel bad”?
  6. What helped not in that moment, but an hour later or by evening?

When it is no longer about skills

Everything above is for ordinary arguments where you’re both basically safe. In three cases, these techniques are not the answer.

  • Aggression in the couple. Your partner responds to tears or disagreement with aggression: shouting with threats, hitting, not letting you leave; there’s violence or coercion in the relationship. Tears are not a way to protect yourself: the experiments on the smell of tears are a lab game, not violence in a couple. Safety matters more than any conversation format, and the format in this chapter doesn’t replace a safety plan. More in chapter 23.
  • A crying baby and rage. If the crying drives you to rage, put the baby down in a safe place and step out until it passes. Never shake a baby.
  • Medications. If tears and other feelings have become blunted after starting or changing a medication and it weighs on you, talk it over with the doctor who prescribed it. Don’t stop treatment abruptly.

06. Reading your partner: what it gives you and where the line is

In short

Guessing what your partner feels is useful, but the payoff is modest: how accurate your guesses are is only slightly linked to how satisfied you are with the relationship1. Another habit shows a clearer link — holding your thought about the other person’s intentions as a version, not as a fact2. And one more thing: being able to read feelings is not the same as being kind. The same knowledge serves both care and pressure3.

What to do: check your guess out loud instead of acting on it as if it were a fact. And judge people by their behavior — how they handle consent, truth and the word “no” — not by how beautifully they talk about feelings.

If a refusal is followed by revenge, isolation or surveillance, what you need is not the exercises in this chapter but the section “When it is no longer about skills.”

Artyom and Igor have lived together for a long time. Igor comes home from work, sees Artyom’s clenched jaw and a table that was set and has gone cold — and decides: he’s hurt that I’m late again. “Well, sorry for working,” he throws out from the doorway. Artyom has spent the whole evening replaying a conversation with his mother; he hears a reproach that wasn’t there and answers: “Do whatever you want.” Igor goes into the bathroom and doesn’t come out for a long time.

Each of them responded not to what happened but to his own guess about what happened. And each guess became a new trigger for the other.

What is going on

You have no direct access to your partner. You see behavior: a pause, a tone of voice, a face. Meanwhile, inside you there’s something of your own: tension in your body and, as a rule, anger or anxiety. From all this your mind builds a meaning — what your partner feels and what they want. Then you do something, and your partner is now responding not to the event but to your guess. That’s how your loop becomes the trigger for your partner’s loop.

Another person’s body is a noisy source. The same facial expression can mean different things in different situations and cultures: a smile or a frown doesn’t reveal a hidden feeling unambiguously4. A single heart rate variability reading on your watch says nothing about either safety or trauma, and experts dispute the trendy explanations built on the “vagus nerve”5. Your own signals aren’t read perfectly either: how accurately you sense your heartbeat is only weakly linked to how strong the experience is. How to learn to hear your own signals — chapter 02. Trendy labels — “oxytocin makes you sensitive,” “the ideal ratio of positive to negative,” percentages about body language — have not been tested for couples.

The most vulnerable step is meaning. A sensation and someone else’s behavior turn into a version: “my partner doesn’t care,” “they’re trying to hurt me.” The most defensible skill is calling a version a version, keeping it temporary and checking it with the people it concerns. This skill is moderately linked to satisfaction with the relationship (the skill itself is measured with questionnaires and tasks, and that’s not the same as reading minds correctly), but the link is only observational: it doesn’t prove that training this will improve relationships or stop violence, and interventions for couples haven’t yet shown a reliable effect. A partner’s tears are a separate strong signal (chapter 05). How to cope with a wave that has rolled over you — chapter 04.

Guessing right doesn’t yet mean helping. Couples who guess each other’s feelings more accurately are, on average, slightly more satisfied with the relationship. The link is weak, and it’s only a link, not a cause: it’s more noticeable for hard feelings and almost invisible for joyful ones1. There’s also a reverse hypothesis: sometimes people avoid accuracy where a thought threatens the relationship — the head protects the heart. It grew out of small studies in a narrow context and doesn’t prove that deliberate blindness helps a couple. Besides, deliberately not understanding can hide an unsafe situation.

There are three skills here: understanding (“my partner is having a hard time”), resonating (it’s hard for you too) and compassion — adding care and the wish to help. Compassion is told apart from the state in which someone else’s pain overloads the listener. This is a framework, not the result of trials: that training empathy will improve couples has not been proven.

A skill is not kindness. You can read someone’s fear accurately to support them — or to pick the most effective way to pressure them. In studies, the same knowledge about how to manage emotions went along sometimes with helping others and sometimes with harming them — depending on how the person relates to morality and how inclined they are toward calculated manipulation3. The data are modest: these are social dilemmas and the workplace, not romantic couples, and they don’t show that emotionally skilled partners hurt others more often. Beautiful talk about feelings can hide coercion, so judge consent, honesty and behavior separately from eloquence. And coercive control isn’t simply a lack of empathy: a controlling person can understand someone else’s fear well and use it on purpose.

When your anxiety is declared unreliable. This is called gaslighting. It means a pattern of pressure, not any argument. You notice something, you get anxious and you say so. The other person, who holds more power, time after time presents your observations as proof that you’re being unreasonable, narrows your independent sources for checking reality and uses your uncertainty to get you to comply. Your own signal has been declared unreliable, and there’s nothing left to lean on. Among survivors of domestic violence interviewed by sociologists, most described exactly this, and many named it as an obstacle on their way to getting help6. But this isn’t a share of all couples, and it isn’t a test: there is no general scale that would fit any gender and a non-monogamous family.

Dazzling self-confidence. At first it attracts. Later, criticism can trigger a defensive fight for status and a hostile reading of the partner’s intentions. This is a model of a process, not a diagnosis based on one fight. “All self-absorbed people hurt their partners” is a mistake, and the “vulnerable” variant wasn’t examined separately in these studies. No tested screen was found that could predict violence from a first impression; it hasn’t been shown that a dazzling start foreshadows it. What matters more is a repeating pattern: a sense of entitlement and revenge in response to disagreement. A short empathy exercise as a way to soften this kind of rivalry and keep the partner safe has not been tested in either non-monogamous or Chinese-European couples. A partner of any gender can set boundaries without waiting for a diagnosis.

What science doesn’t know yet. Whether a finer connection with the body or a richer language of feelings helps both people make up after fights. Whether checking a broken expectation (“what did we actually promise each other?”) works better than the usual “look at the situation differently.” For now the conclusion is cautious: signals of your own tension in the body can help you stop, or they can push you to mistake a guess for certainty. For Russian-Chinese couples, non-monogamous (open, swinging, polyamorous, relationship-anarchist) and same-sex couples, and for transgender and non-binary people, there’s no tested data, either on the accuracy of guesses or on the techniques in this chapter. For a network of relationships it makes sense to ask everyone affected, not just the two of you, but that’s logic, not a finding.

How to tell it is happening

In yourself:

  • You already “know” what’s in your partner’s head and don’t check.
  • You respond not to what happened but to your version: “Well, sorry for working.”
  • Smooth talk about feelings convinces you more than what the person actually does.

In your partner:

  • They respond to a reproach that wasn’t there.
  • What you get back is a cold “do whatever you want,” a pause or them leaving for another room.

If the other person denies things that can be checked, cuts you off from people, watches you, takes revenge for a “no” — see the section “When it is no longer about skills.”

What to do

1. Check your guess out loud

When: in any tense conversation, when you notice you already have a ready-made conclusion about what your partner feels. There’s no tested dose: do it until it becomes a habit.

What to do:

  1. Notice what exactly your partner did or said and what’s going on in your body.
  2. Name what you saw without a conclusion: “when the conversation broke off.”
  3. Say your guess as a guess: “It seems to me you’re hurt. Am I hearing you right?” Or: “Maybe I’m wrong: is it fear, anger or something else?”
  4. If your partner corrects you, accept the correction, don’t argue with it.
  5. Ask: “Do you need advice right now, or do you just want to be heard?”

Validating a feeling and agreeing with the facts are different things.

How you know it worked: your partner answers “yes, exactly” or calmly corrects you. When you ask “do you feel heard?” you hear “yes” more often than before.

This was tested modestly — a lab experiment with students compared validating and invalidating responses. It’s an “ingredient,” not a ready-made sequence of phrases for couples.

2. Four questions instead of a verdict

When: when something has stung you, before a conversation and every time you feel hurt. If your body is wound up, take a pause first (chapter 04).

What to do: answer, to yourself or in writing. What is happening in my body? What am I deciding about my partner’s intention? What other explanation is possible — at least two others? What can I ask about without accusing? Then ask that question.

How you know it worked: you manage to come up with other explanations, the question sounds like a question, and your partner confirms it didn’t come across as an interrogation.

As a set of questions for couples this hasn’t been evaluated; it rests on a moderate but only observational link2. The exercise isn’t suitable when there are threats and coercion (see the section “When it is no longer about skills”).

3. Compassion, carried through to one action

When: when there’s someone near you who is having a hard time right now. Not necessarily your partner.

What to do: stay with the thought of this suffering and with the wish for it to ease. Then do one freely chosen helpful action. In the experiment people practiced with guidance for about half an hour a day for a couple of weeks; short home versions weren’t tested.

How you know it worked: write down which action you did voluntarily and how the person responded.

This hasn’t been tested in couples, including non-monogamous and intercultural ones. In a small experiment, people trained in compassion were more willing to spend their own money to correct an unfairness in a stranger’s favor7. So compassion can be trained; it isn’t fixed once and for all. But generosity before the start wasn’t measured, the recipient was a stranger, and relationships and the long term weren’t tested. For dangerous relationships this says nothing: compassion doesn’t cancel self-protection. It is not an obligation to give to someone who hurts you.

4. Agree on language and on who’s present when you talk

When: if you come from different cultures or speak different languages.

What to do: instead of guessing about “culture,” ask each other: in which language and in front of whom is it easier for each of you to talk about feelings and family? What do a pause, consulting with relatives and a translated word for a feeling mean to each of you? Who gets personal information? Words for feelings differ across languages, so one label can mean different things. More on this in chapters 33, 37 and 38.

How you know it worked: you have agreements made by the two of you, not assumptions about “how they do things.”

For a couple made up of a person from mainland China and a Russian-speaking or European partner, there’s no tested data on the role of family, on how partners agree on the meanings of words in Mandarin and Russian, or on whether the exercises in this chapter work here.

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: a sensitive person is always a good partner.
In fact: being able to guess is not kindness. It hasn’t been shown that very high sensitivity creates good relationships, and when it uncovers a threat, it can be hard to live with.

People think: emotionally developed people don’t manipulate.
In fact: the same knowledge about how to manage emotions went along with both helping and harming others3. Skill and ethics are different dimensions, not necessarily opposites. The risk is mistaking good talk for reliability.

People think: holding back feelings is always harmful.
In fact: in one comparison of Hong Kong Chinese and European Americans, the link between holding back feelings and well-being was different (not couples and not mainland China), and pooled data don’t support a single rule that “suppression is always harmful”8.

People think: charm and a dazzling start show what a person is up to.
In fact: “love bombing” is the name for an avalanche of attention followed by pressure. There’s no validated test for it. Look at what you can see: violations of consent, repeated testing of boundaries, revenge for a refusal. Charm doesn’t reveal intentions — not in men, not in women, not in any form of relationship.

Talk it over together

  1. When I go quiet, what do you usually decide is going on with me? How often is that spot on?
  2. Which of my reactions most often looks like “I’m angry,” when actually something else is behind it?
  3. How can I tell you what I need right now: to be heard, practical help or just having you close?
  4. What does a pause in a conversation mean to each of us: a breather, hurt, respect? How will we let each other know it’s temporary?
  5. What looks, from the outside, as if I’m taking revenge or pressuring when I hear “no”? How can we notice it earlier?

When it is no longer about skills

Red flags: the person time after time denies things that can be checked; cuts you off from people or from records and messages; monitors your messages and movements; threatens; restricts money and contact; punishes refusal; coerces you into sex; doesn’t let you safely object, leave or consult independent people. Repeated testing of boundaries and revenge after a “no” are also signs. But the feeling of being “dismissed” in an argument isn’t a sign on its own.

What to do: if there are threats, stalking, sexual coercion or violence, what comes first is a confidential safety plan and help from local specialists in such situations. The joint exercises in this chapter don’t replace that, and in such relationships your partner’s free consent to them can’t be taken for granted. More in chapter 23.

Part II. Attraction and beginnings

07. What attracts us and what a “match” is made of

In short

A questionnaire filled out before meeting lets you guess, slightly, who gets attracted easily and who many people like. But who will appeal to you in particular, and to whom you will appeal, the questionnaire says almost nothing about: that arises between two people, in a live conversation.

Similarity usually goes along with liking, looks are more strongly linked to attractiveness than earnings are, and the pretty stories about hormones, scent and “pheromones” haven’t held up in rigorous data.

What to do about it: treat a first impression as a hypothesis, not a verdict; after a meeting, write down both the sensation in your body and your interpretation of it; ask the person themselves about language, family and agreements; state interest and refusal directly.

Vika and Lin matched on an app on almost every point: the same age, they love the same books, both can’t stand noisy bars. At the café the conversation goes smoothly, and yet Vika catches herself glancing at the clock. “Everything’s right, but inside it’s quiet,” she texts a friend on the way home. A few days later, at someone else’s exhibition, she talks for a long time with a person she wouldn’t have picked from a profile, and notices that she’s talking faster than usual and doesn’t want it to end. Lin, meanwhile, rereads Vika’s short reply after their meeting and decides: she isn’t wanted.

What is going on

First, the body. Attraction and wariness can echo in the body. Which specific bodily changes accompany attraction wasn’t measured in the dating studies reviewed, and no single bodily sign that says “this is a match” was found there. Hormones work here as a background, not as a button (see below). On the physiology of arousal — our book “Alchemy Without Magic.”

Then meaning. The sensation is ambiguous, and you’re the one who builds the meaning: “the interest is mutual,” “I’m being pushed away,” “this is my person.” Behind the meaning is an expectation: what you expect from the person and what the two of you actually agreed on. If it’s broken, anger rises. This is a signal of a mismatch, not a verdict of “wrong person.”

Then action. Approach, reply, go silent, suggest meeting. For the other person this is a new trigger, and their own chain starts. That’s why mutual interest is where two decisions intersect, not one person’s skill at “convincing” the other.

Why a pair is harder to predict than a person. Attraction can be broken down into three layers: how inclined a person is to get attracted; what many people notice in them; and what arises only between two people, out of their timing, their interpretations and their responses to each other. The first two are partly predictable. The third belongs to the pair: among students at speed dating, everything measured about the people before they met explained almost nothing about attraction within a pair1. This is a test on students, not proof that “chemistry” can never be predicted. Pairs like a person from mainland China and a Russian-speaking partner, queer dating and non-monogamous unions weren’t tested in such studies — there’s no ready-made “compatibility index” for them.

The ideal and your “type.” A stated ideal can steer your attention toward a trait, but a live conversation brings something else, sometimes something unexpected. Still, “ideals predict nothing” is too strong: in one study some of the stated preferences matched attraction. Your “type” can be revised; it’s not an oracle.

Five things worth telling apart. This is this book’s working map, not a tested model. It helps you see at which step the story got stuck.

  • Attraction — you want to get to know the person better.
  • Compatibility — shared values and habits that will hold up in everyday life.
  • Availability — whether the person is really free: previous relationships, agreements, time.
  • Safety and trust — you feel calm around them, and their behavior confirms it: a “no” is accepted, their word is kept.
  • Timing — readiness for a relationship now and the ability to agree together on meeting up.

Compatibility felt in one meeting and compatibility tested by behavior over time are different things: a profile shows stated intentions, a meeting shows interaction, and reliability becomes visible only over time. This is a reasonable distinction, but not a measured one. Whether these five things are independent of each other, nobody knows.

Attraction is the best studied: first impressions partly predicted whether there would be interest and dates later. But this says nothing about the other four things, and the participants were mostly students and people at community events; whether this holds for other cultures and non-monogamous unions is unknown.

Timing is less solid. Readiness for a relationship is linked to whether one starts and whether it lasts. Fear of being single is linked to more interest in less responsive people and to less willingness to end unsatisfying relationships. But this is a measured trait, not proof that “the right time” decides your fate. Short pauses between turns in a conversation are linked to a sense of connection, but you can’t derive a threshold for texting from this (chapter 09). Safety as a separate step toward a first meeting wasn’t tested in these studies either.

If there are more than two of you. Here availability is above all about agreements. Before the first meeting it’s worth clarifying: is each person allowed to see others, how will current partners find out, what is the new person agreeing to. These are agreements, not an indicator of which form of relationship is healthier. There are no rigorous “from match to first meeting” comparisons for this kind of dating, and many studies involved people who signed up online on their own. On forms of relationships — chapter 24, on agreements — chapter 27.

If you come from different cultures. There’s no direct data on attraction or texting for a couple made up of a person from mainland China and a Russian-speaking partner. The nearest studies describe something else: interviews with unmarried working women from Beijing showed pressure from parents and different responses to it, not a rule that “the family always decides.” Among couples in Australia — Western, Chinese and mixed — dissatisfaction was linked to negative communication; avoidance was more common where the female partner was Chinese. But this isn’t dating in China, and these studies don’t confirm a rule of “the direct West, the indirect China.”

How to tell it is happening

In yourself:

  • On paper everything fits, but inside it’s quiet. Or the other way around: on paper the person isn’t for you, but you don’t want the conversation to end.
  • The person replies vaguely, and you think about them more than usual. This may be uncertainty at work, not a sign that the person is “the one.”
  • You immediately read a short reply after a meeting as “they don’t want me” or “they’re deliberately rejecting me.”
  • You explain the person’s behavior by their country or group instead of asking the person themselves.

In the person across from you:

  • Instead of a clear “yes” or “no” — evasive answers or silence; you agreed on something, and they don’t keep their word.
  • It’s unclear what their reserve means: interest, language or a way of communicating they’re used to.
  • After a clear “no” they keep texting (in that case — the section “When it is no longer about skills”).

What to do

1. Write four lines after a meeting

When: within an hour after the meeting.

What to do: what you notice in your body — the place and the sensation, not “I liked it”; what tentative name to give the feeling, as a hypothesis; what expectation stood before it — what you were expecting and what you had really agreed on; what you want to ask or do next.

How you know it worked: after a few weeks your notes show the difference between sensation, feeling, explanation and request. This is self-observation for people who sort out feelings in words but hear their body poorly; it hasn’t been tested as a way to improve dating. The closest known finding: naming a feeling was linked to a calmer bodily reaction, but that was with fear, not attraction. The notes show your chain, not someone else’s: don’t infer the other person’s intentions from them.

2. Separate two questions

When: after writing, before answering “so, shall we see each other again?”

What to do: ask yourself separately: “Do I want this person?” and “Can I free up room for the next meeting and keep my word?” If you’ve already met, ask each other whether you’d like another meeting.

How you know it worked: the answers can differ — “I want to, but there’s no room” or “there’s room, but the pull is weak” — and you decide consciously, not out of inertia. This is a suggestion, not a tested questionnaire.

3. Ask the person, not the stereotype

When: as soon as you find yourself explaining the person’s behavior by their country, language or family.

What to do: find out from the person themselves: in which language it’s easier to talk about important things, how much family involvement they want, how disagreement gets expressed and what silence means. Don’t read reserve as a lack of interest until you find out what’s behind it — language or their usual way of communicating. This is a safeguard for the conversation, not a tested cross-cultural technique. More in chapters 33 and 37; on love and family in China — chapter 32.

How you know it worked: instead of a guess, you have the person’s own answer.

4. State interest and refusal directly

When: when you realize that you’re interested, or that there won’t be a next step.

What to do: say it clearly and without games. In scenario experiments, a clear refusal left more clarity and less urge to keep checking on the person and texting again afterward than a vague one did, though it didn’t take away the unpleasant feeling right away2. These were scenarios, not real chats. If silence makes you angry, separate what happened (“no reply yet”) from the meaning (“I’m being deliberately rejected”) and name the expectation behind the anger. This is a practical conclusion, not a tested protocol.

How you know it worked: the other person understands where you stand, and both of you have more clarity than after a vague answer; the unpleasant feeling may still remain. If a clear refusal could provoke violence or stalking, a direct conversation isn’t required — see the section “When it is no longer about skills.”

5. Questions that build up

When: once, while getting to know each other, if both of you are comfortable.

What to do: less than an hour. Agree on a language and on the right to skip any question without explanation. Take turns answering more and more personal questions — from “what would a perfect day look like for you” to “what’s hardest for you right now.” Before and after, rate your closeness on the same scale.

How you know it worked: closeness grew, and both of you are comfortable. In an experiment with pairs of strangers, questions like these produced more immediate closeness than small talk3. But what was measured was closeness in that moment, not falling in love: “the questions that make you fall in love” is a popular retelling, not a finding. The questions don’t replace consent to intimacy, and there’s no point squeezing personal answers out of anyone. If your languages differ, agree on the wording in advance. There’s no testing for non-monogamous and queer dating. There’s no point repeating it regularly: nobody has tested how often.

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: opposites attract.
In fact: liking goes along with similarity to a moderate degree4, and on live dates the similarity a person felt mattered more than the actual similarity. In established different-sex couples it all depends on the trait: in political views partners are very alike, in sociability almost not at all. This doesn’t prove that similarity is what brought this particular couple together. That complementary characters work better than similar ones hasn’t been shown either. A cultural difference is not psychological incompatibility.

People think: men care about looks, women about status.
In fact: in real ratings, looks are moderately linked to attractiveness and earnings only weakly, for both men and women5. These are general links, not how accurately someone is matched to one person’s ideal.

People think: you have to play hard to get — then the other person will be drawn in more.
In fact: in one experiment, female students looked at fictional profiles of men who were uncertain about them — and thought about those men more and reported stronger attraction6. But these weren’t real couples or on-and-off warmth, there’s no large independent replication, and there’s no basis for carrying the result over to men or to other kinds of dating. Getting stuck in your thoughts is an unreliable sign of a healthy match. It’s better to be honest about your interest: deliberate uncertainty can create unnecessary suffering; how often it does, nobody knows.

People think: at the peak of her cycle a woman is drawn to more masculine men, and the nose chooses by genes, pheromones and a kiss.
In fact: in a large study with measured hormones, the taste for “masculine” faces didn’t track the hormones7; another found shifts in attraction and desire in general, but not toward any particular type of man. Earlier studies reported shifts in taste for certain male features, but that interpretation is disputed. You can’t infer a particular woman’s tastes and intentions from a calendar. It’s the same with scent: some early experiments found a link, but an overall review of the link between immune genes and scent ratings and partner choice didn’t find it8. A human sex pheromone that reliably triggers attraction hasn’t been found. A scent you liked and a kiss you wanted are information about the two of you, not a genetic verdict.

Talk it over together

  1. What do you notice in your body when we’re together, and what does it mean to you?
  2. What agreements do you have right now — with past partners, with other people, with your time — and which of them concern us?
  3. What does the word “serious” mean to you, and in which language is it easier for you to talk about it?
  4. How can I tell that you want another meeting, and how can I tell that you don’t? What’s the easiest way for you to say “no”?
  5. What does silence after a message or a meeting mean to you, and what helps when you need time?
  6. Which of the people close to you should know about us, and how involved in this do you want them to be?

When it is no longer about skills

Everything above is for ordinary dating, where it’s safe to say “no.” Warning signs:

  • after a clear “no” the person keeps texting, calling, showing up where you go;
  • they threaten or push, demanding that you explain yourself in person.

A clear refusal as a technique isn’t designed for this: its advantage was tested in safe situations. If a refusal could provoke violence or stalking, a direct conversation isn’t required. On dating apps, young women and sexual minorities face harassment more often (the samples in the review were mostly Western). For threats, surveillance and coercion — chapter 23; on the risks of online dating — chapter 08.

08. Where people meet: apps, the feed and silence in reply

In short

In the US, couples used to come together more often through friends; now it’s more and more often online. But the data don’t let you tell from where people met how the relationship will turn out: judge by the relationship itself.

A feed of profiles changes how you choose: in experiments, the longer people swiped, the less often they said yes. Silence after a message is ambiguous: it may be a refusal, or it may be something else entirely. Looking again and again at the page of someone who didn’t reply or who said no gives you no answer, and for the other person it becomes a new event.

What to do: notice your body before you hit “send”; count the conversations you want, not the matches; if you don’t want to continue and it’s safe, say so in one clear message. Both people have the same right to choose, to reply and to say no.

Timur lies in the dark, the screen lighting up his face. Profiles come one after another, and his thumb moves faster and faster: left, left, left. Yesterday he wrote to Anton, and the conversation flowed easily. Today there’s no reply. His chest has tightened, his breathing has gone shallow, and a story is putting itself together in his head: “so I wasn’t right for him.” His finger opens Anton’s page: a fresh story, Anton sitting with friends in a café. Timur sends another message, right after the first one. Anton sees them back to back, feels awkward and puts his phone down: he’ll decide what to reply later, not now.

For Timur this is rejection. For Anton, it’s pressure.

What is going on

First, about what we don’t know. How the body reacts to a feed of profiles and to silence in reply wasn’t measured in the studies found. Here’s what seems plausible: the outcome is unknown, and uncertainty can switch on a threat response. The chest tightens, breathing gets shorter, you feel a pull to check your phone. But this is a guess, not a measured fact. From neighboring fields there are two findings, neither of them about dating. People who put their reaction to a spider into words had a weaker skin response to it a week later (less sweating), even though, by their own account, they weren’t any less afraid. And daily practices of paying attention to the body and naming feelings as a couple, carried out over several weeks, helped adults notice body signals better and find words for them. More on this — chapters 02 and 03.

Then the loop kicks in. Your body sends a signal, and you build the meaning: “I’ve been rejected” or “something happened.” The action depends on the meaning: write again, check their page, delete the chat, reply sharply, close the app. For the other person this is an event: two messages in a row read as pressure, silence reads as an answer. Every quick “no” in the feed is also someone’s event: the person who got swiped past has less chance of getting a reply.

Both people have the right to decide. The one who chooses and the one being chosen are both entitled to reply, not to reply, or to stop. Nobody’s attention is owed to anyone by default. If the screen, a refusal or silence makes you want to cry, that’s also a body signal; on tears — chapter 05.

Where couples come from. An app changes whom you meet and what you see first: a profile invites you to show yourself selectively, and the feed gives you many options in a row. But this doesn’t explain what a couple’s happiness depends on. In the US, friends have stepped back, online has moved ahead, and for same-sex couples it has become the path most of them take. These are couples that formed, not the odds for everyone who’s looking. For heterosexual couples, once the decade was taken into account, how they met didn’t predict breaking up1. Meeting through friends doesn’t guarantee safety, and the data found contain no direct comparisons of friends, work, communities and apps in terms of how safe and mutual the relationships are.

The attention market. The screen ranks faces: whom to show first, who got a reply. On one American platform, both men and women on average wrote first to people who got noticeably more attention than they themselves did; the bigger the gap, the less often a reply came2. This is about other people’s attention on one platform, not about a person’s worth and not about attachment. In the feed you can see that some people get written to more often, and a feeling of “nobody sees me” is born. But that is not what you’re worth. There’s no tested data on paid boosts and secret ratings.

The profile feed. A long stream of profiles is the same thing over and over: interest can turn into dissatisfaction, and rejections can come faster and faster. In several experiments, young adults, students and online volunteers went through profiles one at a time, accepting or rejecting each, and acceptance dropped noticeably as the feed went on; in one experiment, only among women3. This is behavior inside a task, not “dating fatigue” in real users. But the longer the feed, the more often the question “what do I want?” can get replaced by the feeling “none of them fits.”

Silence without explanation. There’s no reply, and the question comes up: “was I rejected, or did something happen?” Quite a few of the people surveyed said they themselves had disappeared from romantic relationships, and about as many said it had been done to them. But the respondents were people who volunteered, and this study didn’t determine attachment style. In experiments with imagined situations, people were asked to picture being accepted, being clearly turned down, or the other person simply disappearing. Rejection and disappearance upset them about equally, but after a rejection things were clearer, and after a disappearance the pull to keep watching and to write again was stronger4. These are imagined scenes, not long-term harm.

The person’s page. A visible story or a late reply is incomplete information. Jealousy or uncertainty gives it meaning, and checking, demanding access or sending another message becomes a new event for the other person. If the refusal has already been said, the person’s page isn’t a source of an answer but a continuation of a question that has already been answered. The data don’t show how often this goes as far as surveillance: those experiments only measured how strong the pull to watch and write again was, not whether people actually do it. A large survey in the US: more than half of younger American women who had used online dating said they had received unwanted explicit content; prolonged unwanted contact was more common among LGB people than among heterosexual people. But this is about online dating, not about surveilling partners.

If there are more than two of you. There’s now a lot of research on open relationships, swinging, polyamory and other forms where everyone knows and agrees, but it doesn’t test app dating for these people: there are no comparisons across forms. What you gain by disclosing it in your profile hasn’t been measured, and shares, risks and the “best” profile text can’t be derived from the many online surveys answered by people who volunteered. In practice: make clear whether you can see others, how your current partners will find out, and what the new person is agreeing to. These are agreements, not proof that some form is better. More in chapters 24, 27 and 28.

If you come from different cultures. The studies found have no direct data on a Russian-speaking person dating a partner from mainland China. The closest thing is observations of couples in Australia. Dissatisfaction with the relationship went along with negative communication in Western, Chinese and mixed couples alike. Indirect communication was rare, and avoidance was more common where the female partner was Chinese5 (the Chinese participants had lived there for a long time). This doesn’t confirm the scheme “Westerners speak directly, Chinese people indirectly.” Interviews with unmarried women in Beijing show pressure from parents, but the women respond to it in different ways: the family doesn’t decide for everyone. Ask the person themselves: in which language is it easier to talk about important things, how far is family let into conversations, and what does silence mean. More in chapters 32, 33 and 37.

How to tell it is happening

In yourself:

  • Your body reacted before your thoughts did: chest tight, breathing shallow, a pull to check your phone.
  • There’s no reply, and the story is already written: “so I’m not the right fit.”
  • Your finger opens the person’s page by itself while there’s still no reply.
  • You want to write again without waiting for an answer.
  • You swipe faster and faster and say “no” more and more often.
  • You see that others get written to more often and think: “nobody sees me.”

In the person you’re writing to:

  • They get message after message and feel awkward or pressured.
  • They put the phone down: they’ll decide what to reply later.
  • Their silence gets read as an answer, though there can be many reasons for it.
  • The refusal has already been said, and someone still writes or looks at their page: that’s a continuation of a question that has already been answered.

What to do

1. Stop before you react

When: every time you feel the pull to write again, check their page or delete the chat.

What to do: a minute or two.

  1. Find the place in your body where it has tightened, gone hot, empty or heavy: chest, throat, stomach, shoulders.
  2. Name the feeling tentatively, in one or two words: “feels like hurt,” “feels like fear of being left with nothing,” “just tired.” Check that the word is connected to the sensation and hasn’t turned into a theory.
  3. Write down which expectation was hit (“the reply will come by evening”) and what you actually agreed on.
  4. Ask yourself what you want: to clarify, to wait or to close the app. Do it.

How you know it worked: after a couple of weeks your notes show whether the share of times when you first named the feeling and then chose an action is growing. Noticing your own body better doesn’t mean understanding other people’s intentions better.

It sounds reasonable, but it hasn’t been tested for dating: the parts come from lab studies, and nobody has tried them on dates.

2. Say it once and clearly

When: after a conversation or a meeting, if you don’t want to continue and it’s safe.

What to do: send one short message: “Thanks for meeting. I don’t want to continue.” No judgments, excuses or promises to come back. Don’t negotiate: if pressure comes, don’t reply. If a refusal could provoke violence or stalking, a direct conversation isn’t needed.

How you know it worked: the answer is clear, and the contact has ended. In experiments with imagined situations, a clear refusal gave more clarity and less urge to keep watching than disappearing did, though it didn’t take away the unpleasant feelings4. How to choose the words, how safe it is and how it works in real life hasn’t been tested.

3. If you were the one turned down, consider the question closed

When: a clear refusal has come, and your hand reaches for the person’s page.

What to do: consider the question closed: the answer has been given, and the page won’t change it. Use the urge to look as a signal for technique 1.

How you know it worked: the urge came and went, and the page stayed closed.

4. Count the conversations you want, not the matches

As a personal protocol this hasn’t been tested: the data have no norms for the number of profiles per day, waiting times or “compatibility scores,” and nothing tests whether the number of matches gives you a chance at a good relationship.

When: after each session in the app.

What to do: write down three things: how many conversations you wanted to continue, how your sleep is, what your attention and mood are like. Every two or three weeks, look through your notes. If using the app coincides with worse sleep, attention or mood, change how you use it or take a break.

How you know it worked: what’s growing is the number of conversations and meetings you want, not the number of matches.

5. After a meeting: first separately, then together

When: after a meeting.

What to do: each of you separately notes interest, comfort and pressure, without confusing “I’m used to it” with “I want it.” Then ask each other whether you’d like more, and accept the answer. In a small experiment, repeated meetings increased liking, but it was measured right after the conversation; whether couples form from this wasn’t tested. The wish to continue depends on both people, and after a refusal there’s no reason to repeat contact.

How you know it worked: both of you were able to answer, and the answer was accepted.

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: couples who started online are less happy and less stable.
In fact: two large surveys gave opposite results. In the American one, people who met online were slightly more satisfied and broke up less often; in a survey from many countries, those who met offline turned out to be more satisfied6, 7. The people surveyed in these studies differ, and no one randomly assigned them a way of meeting. The data support neither “online is worse” nor “online is better.”

People think: apps harm your mental health.
In fact: on average, users’ psychological well-being is worse than other people’s8. But most studies simply compare people without changing anything: you can’t determine the cause from them, and the sadness may have come before the app. This isn’t a verdict on the app: look at your own sleep, attention and mood (technique 4).

People think: there’s an “80/20” rule: almost all women chase a handful of the most attractive men.
In fact: there’s no tested data behind such a share: in real messaging, both men and women wrote “up”2. Turning an untested share into a rule harms both sides.

People think: if a person disappeared without a word, that’s just how they’re wired: they avoid closeness.
In fact: the data don’t confirm this. Disappearing is a behavior, not a diagnosis: surveys found several different motives for it. Describe the behavior, not the person. On attachment — chapter 13.

Talk it over together

  1. How did we meet, and what did each of us feel while waiting for a reply?
  2. What expectation did each of us have at the start, and what did we actually agree on?
  3. What’s normal for each of us, and what stings, when it comes to other people’s pages, likes and activity history?
  4. What happens in your body when I take a long time to reply, and what helps then?
  5. If one of us wants to stop the contact, how do we want to hear about it?
  6. Who knows about whom and about which dating, and what does each of us need to know?

When it is no longer about skills

Red flags:

  • threats;
  • surveillance;
  • pretending to be someone else;
  • pressure to hand over access to accounts;
  • stalking after a refusal.

In such cases what matters most is safety, not a quick reply and not persuasion. No one is obliged to stay in contact with someone who threatens them. Cut off contact in any safe way, and don’t explain the refusal face to face. If there’s a direct threat to life, contact emergency services. On control and violence in a couple — chapter 23.

09. Flirting, texting and the first meeting

In short

Flirting is read from the situation, not from a code: in one experiment, outsiders watching recordings of speed dates guessed women’s romantic interest only slightly better than chance1. Your own desire gets mixed into the reading, and your body responds on its own while you wait for a reply.

Provoked jealousy, deliberate pauses, “hot and cold” and “negging” haven’t proven useful, and some of the data point to harm.

What to do: ask instead of guessing, accept “no” as the end, give yourself a minute before you hit send. A date is getting to know someone, not a verdict.

Mark wrote to Ildar on Friday evening: a photo from an exhibition and a couple of lines about what it reminded him of. No reply. By midnight Mark has reread his message more than once, found an unnecessary joke in it, and come up with several explanations at once: Ildar isn’t interested, Ildar is playing “take it slow,” Ildar is just on a train with no signal. His fingers are already typing a second message, and his chest is tight, like before going on stage. Ildar, meanwhile, is standing in line at the train station, trying to find a reply that would sound neither too cold nor too hot.

What is going on

First, the body. A message came or didn’t — and the body responds before words do: pulse and breathing can go off rhythm, the chest or stomach can tighten. The studies this chapter rests on didn’t measure the body; the closest support comes from the lab: when people afraid of spiders were asked to find words for their feeling, their skin reacted less. Noticing body signals, judging by the descriptions, can be trained (chapter 02). Hormones are a background, not a button: no recognized pheromone that would affect attraction has been found in humans2, and there’s no convincing evidence either that women’s taste in male faces follows the hormones of their cycle.

Then meaning. The signal is ambiguous, and you fill in the rest: “they want me,” “they’re rejecting me,” “they’re playing.” How you fill it in depends on experience, the situation and desire. In experiments, heterosexual men on average overestimated other people’s sexual interest, and women underestimated it; once their own desire, self-esteem and openness to short-term flings were taken into account, only a small part of this difference remained3. So it’s worth taking seriously that your own desire gets mixed into how you read someone else’s interest. But this is a conclusion about a group, not about every individual. It hasn’t been proven that this bias is innate and present in every man, or that it leads to violence by itself. There are no estimates for queer, non-monogamous and intercultural couples.

There is no code. There’s no universal code of flirting, let alone a tested trick. Changing your “flirting style” doesn’t promise success. There’s no basis for deliberately copying someone’s posture and words: experiments with mimicry gave mixed results. How flirting is read in mainland China, among Russian speakers, in queer settings and in non-monogamous communities hasn’t been tested directly. This doesn’t mean the rules are different there — it’s simply more reliable to ask than to decode.

Then action. The meaning grows into an act: a third message, a direct question, or a technique — a deliberate pause, jealousy “as a test,” a show of being busy, “hot and cold,” “negging” (disguised put-downs meant to make the person lose confidence). These techniques deliberately create uncertainty or pain to get the desired reaction, bypassing a direct request. It sounds like tactics, but it’s poorly tested. It hasn’t been shown that provoked jealousy increases involvement in the relationship. In a survey, “negging” was judged more harmful than other approaches, and whether it works remains untested4. In experiments with imagined situations, uncertainty kept up the urge to check and keep writing, and the feelings were unpleasant; but more attention is not trust and not tenderness. There’s no experiment in which a pause of several days led to liking; no safe “dose” of provoked jealousy was found either.

For the other person this is a new stimulus. Their reaction is set off by your act, not by your intention. The other person may take uncertainty as a threat, reach for reassurance or pull away, and the chase easily passes for growing involvement. In texting, between a message and a reply there are schedules, time zones, translation and notification habits, so you can’t tell the cause from the delay. In a live conversation, the faster the other person responds, the stronger the sense of connection5, but these are pauses in conversation, not hours between messages: nobody has set a threshold of “how long to wait” for early texting.

Experiments with disappearing without explanation (“ghosting”) show only how silence affects the person waiting for a reply; they don’t explain why a particular person vanished. One unanswered message doesn’t prove any motives, and anxiously expecting rejection isn’t a diagnosis (on attachment — chapter 13). Online, an impression can get inflated: the person shows a selection of highlights, and you fill in the rest. But that’s a theory, not a tested recipe.

A joke is an invitation to interpret. Laughing together with someone shows shared understanding; a joke at someone’s expense can sound like contempt. In couples, warm humor goes along with better relationships and negative humor with worse ones; this is a link, not a cause, and it hasn’t been tested on first dates. A joke that doesn’t translate may be misunderstood — a likely mechanism, but not measured in Russian-Chinese couples.

Rejection and silence. Rejection stings: the thought “something is wrong with me” shows up. Unclear silence adds uncertainty. On tears — chapter 05.

How to tell it is happening

In yourself:

  • You reread your message, looking for what’s wrong with it.
  • You have several explanations for the silence right away, almost all of them about you or about “a game.”
  • Your body feels the way it does before going on stage: tight chest, shallow breathing.
  • Your hand reaches for a second message, though the first one hasn’t been answered.
  • You want to hold a pause “to build intrigue” or to make them jealous.
  • Your conclusion about the other person’s interest is already made, and you haven’t asked a single question yet.

In the other person:

  • They reply late or briefly — there are many possible reasons.
  • They choose their words so as to sound neither too cold nor too hot.
  • Under pressure, they pull away or reach for reassurance.
  • They say a clear “no” — that’s an answer, not a code.

What to do

1. A minute for your body before sending, and a stop rule

When: your hand reaches for a second or third message in a row.

What to do:

  1. Stop for a minute or two. Find where the tension is: chest, throat, stomach, breathing.
  2. Name the feeling with a tentative word: “feels like anxiety,” “feels like hurt.”
  3. Name the expectation: “I wanted a reply today.” If you’re angry over an expectation you never voiced, check that expectation instead of accusing the other person of breaking an agreement (chapter 16).
  4. Come up with two reasons for the silence that have nothing to do with you: lots to do, a different time zone, language.
  5. Choose an action: nothing, or one direct question — “How do you like to text?” Reply when you have the energy, and if you can’t, let them know your reply will be late.
  6. Stop rule: after a clear “no,” make no more offers; after silence or vagueness — at most one gentle reminder in the same channel, then stop. If it’s safe, you can give a clear refusal in one sentence: “I don’t want another date.”

How you know it worked: after a couple of weeks there are fewer follow-up messages and more direct questions. A short log helps: sensation — expectation — action.

This hasn’t been tested for dating: the basis is a lab experiment in which naming a feeling went along with a weaker skin reaction (though people rated the fear itself the same), and a trial in which weeks of mindfulness practice helped people notice their body and name feelings. The refusal sentence isn’t a tested script, and body awareness doesn’t reveal the other person’s intentions. On naming feelings precisely — chapter 03.

2. Ask instead of guessing

When: you notice a ready-made conclusion — “there’s interest,” “it’s a game,” “it means ‘no.’”

What to do: turn the conclusion into a question: about interest, about what “let’s meet” means. Liking is not consent to a specific action, so ask in words what the person wants, and treat a missing or withdrawn “yes” as a refusal (chapter 11). An invitation, the bill, a goodbye can mean politeness, attraction, obligation or refusal; the pattern “the man initiates, the woman responds” comes from an old survey of students in a wealthy country and isn’t a rule today, let alone for same-sex couples. Ask before interpreting the bill or a kiss. If you come from different cultures, ask in which language it’s easier to discuss important things, how much family involvement is wanted and what silence means (chapters 37, 38, 39). Something unfamiliar is a reason to check the meaning, not to fill in the family’s expectations and consent on your own.

How you know it worked: the disagreement gets cleared up, and you don’t have to search a “no” for a hidden “yes.”

3. Say the important things about yourself in advance

When: you’re writing your profile, moving from texting to meeting, or getting to know someone whose agreements differ from yours.

What to do: separate what matters from what’s harmless. What matters: intentions, existing commitments, important practical limits. What’s harmless: small differences in how you describe yourself. In one American study, discrepancies in height, weight and age in profiles were common, but mostly small, and how many of them were deliberate is unknown. It hasn’t been shown that an honest profile gets you more dates. If a term in another language is unclear, ask what it means instead of treating the mismatch as deception.

If you have several relationships, say so right away: whether you can see others, how your current partners know about you, what the new person is agreeing to. The gain in the number of dates hasn’t been measured: there’s no data on flirting in exactly these kinds of relationships, and research on non-monogamy is more often about other things and often relies on online volunteers. Deliberate rivalry when agreements differ is especially likely to mislead, and whether the harm differs across configurations is unknown.

How you know it worked: at the meeting there’s nothing to “uncover,” and questions about agreements sound calm.

4. Make the meeting easy to say no to

When: going from “we matched” to “let’s meet.”

What to do: a match isn’t a meeting yet: each person decides separately, and you need to agree on the details and weigh the risk. Suggest an option that’s easy to turn down, like an hour over coffee. Don’t treat a phone number, address or location as the price of proving interest — neither yours nor the other person’s. Background: about half of American adults consider dating apps unsafe, and among women it’s the majority. For same-sex couples, online was the most common way to meet in the American data, and LGB users more often receive unwanted sexual content and contact after a refusal. On attraction — chapter 07, on the app market — chapter 08.

How you know it worked: both of you came because you wanted to, and saying no was possible without any explanation.

5. Opening up in steps

When: at a meeting or in a long conversation, when both of you want to go deeper.

What to do:

  1. Agree that each of you has the right to skip any question without explanation.
  2. Take turns answering questions, each one a little more personal than the last: from “what day this month stuck with you” to “what’s been hard for you lately.”
  3. If a question turns out to be too sharp, go back to an easier one.
  4. About forty-five minutes, once per meeting.

How you know it worked: before and after, rate on a scale how close the other person feels, and ask whether both of you want to continue. In the lab, pairs of strangers felt closer after a conversation like this than after ordinary small talk, but closeness was measured in that moment, not the outcome of relationships. After a refusal there’s no reason to continue; opening up is not consent to sex. If your languages differ, agree on the words in advance (chapter 38).

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: if you keep at it after a “no,” the person will warm up, which means the interest is real.
In fact: in experiments with imagined situations, a clear refusal and disappearing without explanation were about equally unpleasant, but a clear refusal gave more clarity and less urge to check and keep writing6. What was measured was intentions, not whether persistence works. “No” is a boundary, not a hidden “yes”; persistence easily turns into stalking (chapter 23).

People think: the first date decides everything.
In fact: at speed dating, first impressions of compatibility predicted later interest7, but only as a probability: an impression can change. And “chemistry” can’t be calculated in advance: in other speed-dating data, information about people collected before they met explained almost nothing about desire for one particular person8. Speed dating is different from getting to know someone over a long time. A date is information, not a verdict.

People think: Westerners speak directly, Chinese people speak in hints.
In fact: observations of couples in Australia didn’t support this scheme: there was little indirect behavior (though avoidance was more common where the female partner was Chinese), and dissatisfaction with the relationship went along with negative communication everywhere. But the Chinese participants there had lived in the country for a long time, and this isn’t Russian-speaking people dating people from mainland China. Interviews with unmarried professional women in Beijing showed pressure from parents, but not a rule that the family decides for everyone.

Talk it over together

  1. How do both of us like to text: how often, in which channel, in which language? What does it mean to you if a reply comes later?
  2. What do you hear in the words “let’s meet”: an hour over coffee, dinner, a night? How can we say it so that it’s easy to say no?
  3. Which of my messages came across differently from how I meant it?
  4. What happens in your body while you’re waiting for a reply or deciding what to write?
  5. How do you prefer to hear “no,” and how do you want to say it?
  6. What are our agreements about other people, and what does each of us want to share about it?

When it is no longer about skills

Everything above is about ordinary dating, where things are generally safe for both people. If there is:

  • continuing to write, show up or threaten after a refusal;
  • surveillance: the person knows where you are, though nobody told them;
  • demands for a phone number, address or location as “proof of interest”;
  • intimidation or pressure to make the meeting happen,

this is no longer about reading flirting; it’s a threat to safety. Calling it “misread flirting” means missing the danger.

Don’t hold “sympathetic” negotiations, and don’t explain the refusal in detail. If it’s safe, state the boundary once and end contact. Tell people close to you and turn to whatever protection is available, including support services and law enforcement. More in chapter 23.

10. Falling in love: the pull, waiting for a reply and what to do about it

In short

The start of love often takes up a lot of attention: you want to think about the person, check whether a reply has come and wait for reassurance.

The pretty “chemical” explanations — the love hormone, passion’s expiration date, “love as a drug” — are poorly tested or haven’t held up.

What to do about it: when there’s no reply, separate four things — what happened, what’s in your body, what interpretation you’ve built and what you want to do. First bring the intensity down, then come back to the conversation — about the expectation, not with an accusation.

And if the feeling already has hold of you, learn to come back to your own life and ask the people close to you to tell you what they see.

Lena looks at the screen: next to her message it says “read,” and there’s been no reply for several hours. She and Mei met very recently, and Lena knows Mei has a project deadline today. Still, she opens the chat for the umpteenth time, checks when Mei was last online, types “just wanted to see how you are” — and deletes it. Her stomach is clenched, she barely slept last night, and at work her thoughts go round in circles. The pause doesn’t worry Mei: she’ll reply in the evening. In the evening a short “tired, let’s talk tomorrow” arrives — for Mei it’s just tiredness. Lena reads a verdict in it, and her next message comes out sharper than she meant it to.

No one here is doing anything out of spite. Two loops simply got caught on each other.

What is going on

First the body, then the story about it. Lena’s stomach is clenched, her sleep is off. What exactly happens in the body at the start of falling in love has been measured very little: there’s no reliable data on pulse, breathing and sleep. How to catch such signals in yourself — chapter 02.

What shows up in the brain. In a small study, people who were intensely in love were shown a photo of their loved one, and areas rich in dopamine — the ones linked to reward — responded especially strongly1. But the scanner shows only where oxygenated blood flows. It doesn’t measure dopamine release, just as it measures neither how pleasant love is nor how exceptional, and it says nothing about the couple’s future.

It’s useful to tell “wanting” apart from “liking”: the dopamine areas are linked more to the pull toward a goal, while pleasure is handled by other systems. This is a working frame, not a chemical analysis: you can ask yourself what there’s more of right now — pull or pleasure.

Hormones are not a button. In a small comparison, people who had recently fallen in love had higher levels of a stress hormone than single people and people in long-term couples; when they were examined again later, the difference was gone. But this hormone changes over the course of the day and with exertion — it’s not a measure of feeling. In another small study, people recently in love differed on one serotonin measure — but in the blood, not in the brain, so it says nothing about obsessive thoughts. There are no large independent replications.

How this adds up to a loop. What follows is a hypothesis, not a measurement. A message or a pause before a reply is the trigger. The body presumably responds with attention and arousal, and, when the outcome is unclear, with wariness. Meaning gets built: “maybe it’s mutual” raises hope, “maybe not” raises a sense of threat. Then comes action: check their status, write again, ask for reassurance. The other person’s reply is a new trigger, and they have their own loop, hooked into yours. Anger can be read the same way: behind it, most often, is a broken expectation (“reply the same day”) that may never have been agreed on.

For Lena: “read” is the trigger, the clenched stomach is the body, the “verdict” is the meaning, the sharp message is the action. For Mei this is a new trigger, her next reply may come out drier, and Lena will again read it as coldness.

Limerence. This is the name for an obsessive preoccupation with a person, together with a craving for the feeling to be returned. A short questionnaire that makes it possible to measure this has appeared recently2, but it didn’t find out how often this happens or how long it lasts. The loop is the same, only the stakes are higher: an ambiguous message, hope and threat taking turns, checking and asking for reassurance, and the reply is ambiguous again. A link with an anxious attachment style (chapter 13) is plausible, but how strong it is remains unknown. Unrequited love is always a story of two people; on tears — chapter 05, on breakups — chapter 22.

Reassurance in circles. An ordinary request for comfort when you’re anxious is one thing. It’s another matter when the same questions (“are you sure you’re not mad?”, “do you still love me?”) come up again and again. In observational studies, such repeated requests for reassurance are linked to depression and to the people around rejecting the person who keeps asking3. The link isn’t strong, and it doesn’t prove cause.

Intensity is not a measure of compatibility. Sometimes the pull is strongest when you’re anxious: the person is close one moment and gone the next, warm, then cold. On a swing like that, what feels intense is the relief after tension — like when a long-lasting pain lets go — not the fact that you’re especially right for each other. The sign: when things become calm and reliable, the intensity fades. This is a working guess, it hasn’t been tested, and there are other explanations. But you can’t conclude “the pull is so strong, so this must be my person” from intensity alone. On deliberate swings in texting — chapter 09.

From falling in love to lasting love. Early infatuation often comes with obsessiveness, while in lasting love desire and involvement can remain with less obsessiveness. The data show no “passion → attachment” switch at a particular anniversary. Presumably the partner remains a reward, while predictable responsiveness and shared experience change the meaning of bodily arousal — but that’s an interpretation, not a measurement. There’s no tested recipe for keeping passion alive across different cultures, genders and relationship setups. The first months — chapter 12, desire over the years — chapter 18, how a couple changes over time — chapter 40.

When your partner has a new person. In open-relationship circles, the surge of excitement about a new partner is called “new relationship energy”; the term is loose, and it’s about attention, desire and joy. For one person, their partner’s date with someone new is a reward (“my partner is happy”); for another, a threat (“our time will disappear”). Then come a request for reassurance, bargaining, jealousy or joy for the other person, and each person’s reaction becomes a new trigger for the others. These are possible loops, not a measured sequence. In one online survey of people in polyamory, being mindful of one’s own state was linked to less jealousy and more joy for one’s partner, but this is only a statistical link; the survey didn’t measure how long the surge lasts or how the new connection affects the long-standing one. So there’s no tested time frame for when it “passes” — and no grounds to consider it unreliable in advance either. A “quarantine” on a new connection hasn’t been tested either. On jealousy — chapter 25, on joy for your partner — 26, on agreements — 27.

If there are more than two of you. Everything said above about the brain, marriages and lasting love was obtained mostly from couples, and it can’t be silently carried over to open couples, swingers, polyamory and relationship anarchy. The samples in such studies are often narrow. At the same time, sexual satisfaction among monogamous and non-monogamous people hardly differs on average, although the comparison lumps different setups together. In a network of several people the question “what was agreed and what was broken” matters more than “is this normal.” More in chapters 24 and 28.

If you come from different cultures. In several studies, Chinese respondents preferred indirect communication more often than European American ones did, especially in pleasant situations (the description doesn’t say how many of them lived in mainland China). But average differences don’t tell you how a particular person will behave. For the loop, one thing matters: an ambiguous reply gets read by familiar rules, and a pause, a polite “we’ll see” or quiet agreement can mean different things. For a couple where one person is Russian-speaking or European and the other is from mainland China, there are no direct studies — what’s left is to ask: is this agreement, politeness or a request to wait; how does the family’s opinion enter into decisions. These are cautious adaptations, not tested techniques. Chapters 33, 37, 38.

How to tell it is happening

In yourself:

  • You open the chat for the umpteenth time, check when the person was online, type a message and delete it.
  • A short reply reads as a verdict.
  • Your stomach is clenched, your sleep is off, and at work your thoughts go round in circles.
  • Hope and threat take turns, and you feel a pull to ask for reassurance.
  • The pull is strongest when the person disappears or is cold, and when things with them are calm, your interest fades.
  • Your next message comes out sharper than you wanted.

In your partner:

  • For your partner a pause is just a pause: things to do, tiredness, they’ll reply in the evening. Your partner may not notice your anxiety.
  • They reply briefly — for your partner it’s tiredness, not rejection. Under the pressure of your checking, their replies can get drier, and that reads as coldness.

What to do

1. Separate four things when there’s no reply

When: you’re in the thick of waiting, and your hand reaches to check the chat or write again.

What to do: write down four lines. What happened (just the fact: “read,” no reply). What responded in your body (if nothing, write that). What interpretation came up. What you want to do. Don’t pass off “it seems to me they meant X” as “I feel X.” If the interpretation is a grim prediction (“they’re leaving,” “it’s all over”), label it: “this is an anxious thought.” Don’t take it on faith and don’t chase it away; instead, ask yourself what support you’re lacking right now: sleep and energy, support from others, confidence in yourself. This is self-observation, not a tested remedy for obsessive infatuation. More in chapters 02 and 03.

How you know it worked: you can see where the fact ends and the made-up story begins.

2. A pause for the body, then a conversation about the expectation

When: anger or anxiety has washed over you because of silence, a broken promise or unexpected coldness.

What to do: notice one sensation in your body (the answer “I can’t find anything” works too). Do whatever brings your arousal down — whatever works for you specifically — until things feel more bearable. Then go back to the conversation — to the expectation itself, not to an accusation. Name it as a guess: “It looks like I had an expectation that…” Ask whether it was shared, ask for one specific change and check how the other person understood what you said. If there are two languages, each of you first retells the meaning of what you heard, and only then do you figure out what it was: language, an obligation to family or a broken agreement.

How you know it worked: you managed to go back to the conversation instead of putting it off for good. Endless avoidance and suppressing worries about safety are not what was studied.

Activities that lower arousal noticeably reduce anger and aggression4. But that’s about anger in general; we put this sequence of steps together ourselves, and no one has tested it in this form.

3. Questions that build up

When: you want to get closer, and both of you agree to try.

What to do: take three sets of questions — from impersonal to more personal. Answer in turns; either of you can skip a question. In the lab this took a fair amount of time. A lighter version: take turns telling about one meaningful experience, while the listener retells it and asks: “Do I understand you right?”

How you know it worked: after the conversation, each of you separately rates closeness, sense of safety and wish to do it again. If the sense of safety drops, stop. Don’t go deeper with someone who punishes vulnerability; don’t demand openness across a language barrier.

In the first experiments, after this procedure people rated closeness higher than after an ordinary conversation5 — but that’s closeness, not romantic love. A home version hasn’t been tested as a program.

4. If your partner has a new person

When: you notice jealousy, a need for reassurance, bargaining — or, on the contrary, joy for your partner.

What to do: keep your agreements and your shared time with long-standing partners in view. Don’t devalue the new connection. Ask each other directly: what would be a visible sign for each of you that the existing agreements are still in place?

How you know it worked: there’s no tested protocol. The guideline is simple: each of you can name such a sign for yourself, and it can be discussed calmly.

5. You have a feeling — or the feeling has you

When: being in love has taken over almost everything: sleep, work, seeing friends.

What to do: ask yourself two questions. Can I switch to something else for a while — work, health, friends? Was there a moment when I made my own decision: “I’m going for it, I’ll try”? If you can’t switch and there was no decision, it looks like the feeling has hold of you. Then learn to come back to your life through small things: put off a texting spat until morning, go out for a walk, call a friend. Big steps — moving in together, sharing money, agreeing to see only each other, meeting the family — don’t rush them while you still know little about the person (chapter 12).

How you know it worked: you’re able to step into your own life for a while, and the feeling stays a joy rather than a takeover.

These are working questions; they haven’t been tested.

6. Ask the people close to you to keep an eye out

When: at the start of an intense infatuation, while your head is still clear.

What to do: choose one or two people you trust. Tell them what to watch for: you stop seeing friends, drop things, barely sleep, make big decisions within a few days. Ask them to talk about what they see — what exactly, how often, in what situation — rather than pass a verdict on your partner.

Be careful with people who don’t accept your choice itself — a partner from another culture or of the same gender, an open relationship. Their worry may be about disapproval, or it may be about real harm; facts help tell one from the other. If it’s about pressure, surveillance or threats — chapter 23. The decision is still yours.

How you know it worked: the people close to you name specific things that can be checked.

The technique itself hasn’t been tested, and it hasn’t been proven that the people close to you predict how a relationship will turn out better than the people in love do.

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: oxytocin is the hormone of love and trust: the more of it, the stronger the bond.
In fact: the idea came from experiments on prairie voles, and carrying it over to humans remains a hypothesis. Voles that were genetically insensitive to oxytocin still bonded with their partner6. In humans, attempts to replicate the effect on trust failed7, and a pre-registered test also found nothing. Oxytocin in the blood wasn’t linked to attachment ratings; a nasal spray reduced insecure attachment behavior, but that’s not a measure of natural closeness. A blood test, a spray or a response to touch doesn’t confirm consent or attachment, and pressuring a partner into contact doesn’t create trust.
