People think: love is literally an addiction, like a drug.
In fact: in a small study, reward areas switched on in people in love1, but a shared brain area doesn’t make love a drug addiction. The comparison with addiction is a pretty theory, not a diagnosis. The guideline for harm is loss of control, damage to your life, coercion and danger — not the strength of the feeling. An intense feeling for a new partner in non-monogamous relationships isn’t by itself an illness or cheating either. And sticking the “addiction” label on a healthy feeling can stigmatize it.

People think: passion inevitably fades after a few years.
In fact: no expiration date for intensity has been established. In a survey in one country, about four in ten people married for more than ten years described themselves as “very intensely in love”8. This is self-report and a share of a subgroup. Long-married people selected for intense love showed activity in reward and attachment areas when looking at a photo of their spouse — this shows that it happens, not how often.

People think: family prohibitions only fan the flames of love — like with Romeo and Juliet.
In fact: when people were surveyed over several months in a row, interference from family and friends added neither love nor commitment9. On the other hand, in a summary of many studies, approval from the people around them went along with love and commitment9. This is a link, not a cause: approval may simply have reflected that things were already going well for the couple. People were recruited online, and each had one partner, so the conclusion hasn’t been tested for open unions.

Talk it over together

  1. What happens in your body while you’re waiting for my reply, and what do you want to do then?
  2. What pause in texting is normal for you, and what pause is worrying? How can I let you know that a pause is just a pause?
  3. What expectations of each other do we already have that we’ve never once agreed on out loud?
  4. How do you signal “yes,” “wait” and “no” — and how can I tell them apart?
  5. What about this feeling helps you live right now, and what gets in the way — of sleep, work, other people, someone’s boundaries?
  6. For those who are more than two: what changes for you when there’s a new person in my life, and what would be a visible sign that our existing agreements are still in place?
  7. Did you have a moment of your own decision — “yes, I’m going for it, I’ll try”? What was it like?

When it is no longer about skills

A strong feeling doesn’t justify pressure, and hormones don’t justify coercion, cheating or violence. Signs:

  • a “no” or silence is answered with more calls, messages, “accidental” appearances and surveillance;
  • the feeling is used as an argument: “I love you so much I can’t stop”;
  • self-control is lost, sleep and work suffer, and violated boundaries pile up all around;
  • a request to slow down is met with resentment, pressure, attempts to cut you off from the people close to you, or punishment. It’s exactly this reaction that’s worrying, not just how fast the big declarations come (chapters 06, 12).

When a pursuit ignores a refusal, it’s a matter of safety and unwanted contact, not of how intense the passion is.

What to do. If a refusal has come, stop trying to get in touch. Anger, guilt and humiliation after unrequited love are described in research10, and you can get through them without contact. If contact is being forced on you, a refusal is enough — it doesn’t require explanations. On surveillance, pressure and threats — chapter 23, on when to see a specialist — chapter 35.

11. First Intimacy: Consent, Sex, and What Comes After

In short

Sex adds new information to a couple about the two of you: how each of you handles a “no,” awkwardness, the morning after. It is not a contract and not proof of love.

Consent is what is said right now: specific, voluntary, reversible. Hesitation, freezing, pulling away are a reason to stop immediately.

What to do: ask about the specific step, leave an easy way out, and afterward ask what each of you wants. Research hasn’t found a right time for first sex: what decides it is consent, readiness, and protection.

Dima and Mark are on their third date. At the door, Dima asks: “Staying?” Mark answers: “Well… okay, I guess.” Dima hears “yes.” In the morning Mark is quiet and stares out the window, and Dima already has his verdict ready: he regrets it, it was a mistake, wrong person. Meanwhile Mark is remembering that he meant to say “let’s do this another time,” but what came out was “okay, I guess” — he didn’t want to hurt Dima’s feelings, and now it feels awkward to bring it back up. Each of them filled in the meaning for the other, and every “so that means” became a new trigger.

What is going on

The body comes first. A look, a hand on a knee, the question “staying?” — and the body answers: with arousal, interest, fear, uncertainty, an inner argument. From the outside, all of this looks alike: a smile, silence, a nod, stillness. That’s why it’s hard to reliably tell from the outside what a person wants right now. This is a takeaway for practice, not an algorithm.

Consent is what’s said now. It is specific, voluntary, reversible, and renewed at every step. A “yes” to a kiss doesn’t cover the rest, and yesterday’s “yes” doesn’t cover tonight. Hesitation, freezing, pulling away are a signal to stop immediately. Asking only makes sense if the question can be answered without consequences.

When the choice isn’t free:

  • Alcohol, threats, depending on someone else’s approval. These can shift attention and make “no” more costly. According to young people’s own accounts, alcohol could make misunderstandings about consent worse; there’s no exact threshold. With alcohol and unequal power, you need to check more often. If your partner can’t engage, freezes, or changes their mind, everything stops.
  • Freezing. This is an involuntary reaction to threat, not consent. Among survivors of sexual assault, it is linked to severe stress and depression after what happened1.
  • Unequal power. For example, one person reports to the other at work, and saying no would cost a lot. This holds for any genders and any combination. A nod or an unsure phrase in a foreign language is no substitute for desire.
  • Contraception. Secretly taking off a condom, tampering with birth control, pushing toward pregnancy — this means changing the terms the person agreed to. Fear and anger when it comes out are not “oversensitivity.” In the groups studied, pressure toward pregnancy and contraceptive sabotage went hand in hand with unplanned pregnancy.
  • Photos. There are three permissions: to create and send, to keep, to show others. Consent to the first doesn’t include the others. Sharing intimate images without consent, and threatening to share them, are described as a distinct form of abuse. Among students, there was also sexting that was unwanted but formally agreed to: a “yes” on record isn’t the same as wanting it. The responsibility lies with the person who abused it.

With a new person, uncertainty is added. It’s unclear what the other person likes and whether things will work out. Attention moves from sensations to monitoring yourself, a pause reads as rejection, and wariness grows. This is a plausible scheme, not a measured fact. If there’s no erection, no orgasm, or you want to stop, that’s a situation, not a diagnosis: one night says nothing about an anxiety disorder or about how your partner feels about you. What helps more is room to ask questions and to stop, not a test. The orgasm gap between heterosexual men and women with a partner shows up in an American survey, but it’s about the past month, not the first night, and the popular “percentage” for the first time can’t be pinned on that data.

Meaning, action, response. Next, the mind fills in a meaning: pleasure, commitment, uncertainty, “nothing has changed.” The action depends on the meaning — texting in the morning, disappearing, hugging, going quiet. For the other person, this is a new event; if the meanings don’t match, the loop spins up on its own. The sequence “notice the body → check the meaning → choose an action by consent → ask how it affected the other person” is this book’s framework, not a tested protocol. On the body, see chapters 02 and 04; on the physiology of arousal and orgasm, see the book “Alchemy Without Magic.”

Attraction is not a forecast. Hormones here are background, not buttons; brain scans of people in love don’t show a couple’s future compatibility either. You can strongly want a new person and still not know whether everyday life, your agreements, and safety will hold up. No single night promises a future or proves love. What comes next is in chapter 12.

Afterward. The body wants different things: contact, from pleasure; distance, from tiredness, vulnerability, a broken expectation, or a reminder of trauma. One person’s need for space can sound like rejection to the other; the question “what do you want — to be close, to be apart, to be touched?” cuts the guessing short. These are possible explanations, not diagnoses. In newlyweds, sex went along with greater sexual satisfaction for the next few days as well — according to their own records; this isn’t a hormonal window and not a guarantee of attachment. Affection after sex also goes along with satisfaction, but what is cause and what is effect is unclear.

Sadness and tears. Sadness or irritation after consensual sex is familiar to many: nearly half of the female students surveyed, and slightly fewer of the men surveyed, had felt it at least once in their lives (these are different surveys and can’t be compared directly)2, 3. Sadness doesn’t necessarily point to a bad relationship. According to a review, this state is underestimated in clinical practice, and it doesn’t exist among formal diagnoses. If the heaviness keeps coming back or the sex was unwanted, pay attention. Tears aren’t one diagnosis either: sadness, irritation, relief, or an overwhelming good feeling; tears of joy during sex haven’t been studied directly. Don’t decide for the person “it hurts because of me” or “you regret it” — ask what happened and what they want right now (chapter 05).

Morning and attachment. No one has tested whether behavior the next morning reveals attachment style: silence has many explanations — anxiety, tiredness, pleasure, expectations. Ask instead of putting a label on it (chapter 13).

“Friends with benefits.” One person expects friendship, the other a relationship, and both stay silent. Most people who looked back on such a friendship stayed friends, and for about half it became just as close or closer; those who didn’t keep the friendship more often talked about deception and loneliness. The data don’t support “it always ends badly,” but the success rate is unknown. It hasn’t been shown that talking about rules protects against things going wrong. The data are mostly from students and from the West.

If there are more than two of you. There’s little data, and the samples are narrow. Among people with several partners, attachment differed from one relationship to another. Findings about monogamous couples and student consent norms don’t carry over directly to such networks: open couples, swinging, and polyamory can rest on different agreements. So consent for each action is asked of each person separately, and before first intimacy it’s worth finding out which agreements with other partners affect it and what gets told to whom (chapters 27, 10, and 26).

If you come from different cultures. Research describes average differences, not rules for an individual. In several studies, Chinese respondents preferred indirect communication more often than European Americans did; how many of them lived in mainland China is unknown. For a couple where one person is from mainland China and the other is Russian-speaking or European, there’s no direct data on consent, first sex, or breakups. So ask this particular person: “Is this consent, politeness, or a request to wait?”, which language makes the words clearer, how family figures into decisions. A nod or an unsure phrase is no substitute for desire in any culture. These are cautious hints (chapters 33, 37, and 38).

How to tell it is happening

In yourself:

  • You say “okay, I guess” or “whatever you want,” but inside it’s “I don’t know” or “I don’t want to.”
  • Your body has frozen or is pulling you away, even though out loud it’s still “yes.”
  • In the morning, you fill in the meaning for both of you: “so they regret it,” “so it was a mistake.”
  • You want some time apart, but your partner reaches for a hug (or the other way around), and it reads as rejection.

In your partner (this is a reason to ask, not proof: you can’t reliably read it from the outside):

  • They answer evasively: “okay, I guess,” “maybe,” “whatever you say.”
  • They smile and nod but don’t move toward you.
  • Afterward, they go quiet, retreat into their phone, the shower, another room.
  • In the morning, they send a short text or disappear — and you already have your conclusion ready.

What to do

None of these techniques has been tested as a method: they’re pieced together from what is known in bits and pieces. The closest known finding: couples who can talk about sex are, on average, more satisfied; the link is moderate. But that’s about talking in general, not about asking “do you want to?” at a specific moment.

1. Agree before you start

When: before anything has started, and before each new time together — last time doesn’t count.

What to do: say what you want and what you don’t; ask what kinds of touch they like, and ask about contraception. Name the way out loud: “We can stop at any moment, no explanations needed.”

How you know it worked: both of you can say “stop” and “I don’t know” without consequences.

2. Ask at every new step

When: when moving on to something new.

What to do: keep it short and specific: “does this feel good?”, “keep going?” “I don’t know” is a pause, not a “yes.” If there’s hesitation, freezing, pulling away, or a change of mind, stop and come back to the conversation later, without blame. It hasn’t been shown that asking kills the mood, so there’s no reason to skip a check you need.

How you know it worked: afterward, both of you say you wanted it and felt comfortable.

3. Ask afterward

When: after every time you’re intimate, until it becomes a habit.

What to do: “What would be better right now — staying close, some space, or a hug?” If it helps, name what you see without a diagnosis: “I can see things got quiet.” If an unspoken expectation came up, agree on when you’ll come back to it. After unwanted sex, don’t push touch. This format was designed, not tested — neither for tears nor for silence.

How you know it worked: less guessing, and the same misunderstanding comes up less often.

4. Four lines for a hard moment

When: tension is left over after being intimate, you can’t make sense of what your body is feeling, or anger is rising that an expectation was broken.

What to do: two or three minutes in your notes app, four lines; once every day or two, until the tension goes away. What happened, as an event. What you notice in your body — a sensation or “nothing in particular,” both answers are fine. What meaning you filled in (“so it was a mistake”). What you did or want to do. If anger showed up, add a question: was my expectation said out loud and accepted by the other person? Stop if paying attention to your body gets overwhelming. There are no trials of this; the closest thing is group sessions on attention to sensations, which increased desire in women.

How you know it worked: the tension has gone, or a reason to talk has come up.

No technique cancels out a refusal, a safety boundary, or an agreement.

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: early sex ruins relationships.
In fact: no study has shown this. A survey of married people linked the timing of first sex to the quality of the marriage, but people were asked in hindsight, only those who had stayed married were asked, and no one was randomly assigned a timing4. Shame and pressure may do harm — how often is unknown.

People think: consent can be read without words, and people read it accurately.
In fact: in month-long diaries, as sex with a partner added up, people’s sense of consent shifted, and they named fewer and fewer signals. The last time becomes an observation, not permission in advance5. The data didn’t show a point after which you no longer need to ask, or that people read signals accurately. A smile, a nod, and silence don’t answer the question “do you want to right now.”

People think: casual sex harms women.
In fact: the motive matters more than the casualness itself. In a year-long study of students, hookups under pressure and for reasons that weren’t really their own predicted more anxiety and depression and lower self-esteem; hookups out of their own desire did not6. Women weren’t analyzed separately, and selection effects can’t be fully ruled out. Among heterosexual students, women regretted their last casual encounter more often than men did, but this is a group average, not a forecast for an individual: a survey like this can’t separate biology, norms, safety, and unequal pleasure. The question isn’t the stereotype but whether you freely want it.

People think: after sex, oxytocin, the “attachment hormone,” bonds women but not men.
In fact: it rises during arousal and orgasm in both. Experiments with the hormone given as a nasal spray don’t carry over to real-life sex7, and a single measurement in saliva or blood doesn’t show lasting attachment. So the hormone story shouldn’t be used to predict faithfulness, justify pressure, or dismiss men’s feelings.

Talk it over together

  1. What does “yes” mean for each of us? How do we say “no” or “wait” — with words, gestures, silence?
  2. What do we do when it’s unclear whether the other wants to keep going? What signal stops everything at once?
  3. What do we want after being intimate — to be close, to be apart, to talk, to sleep? How do we talk about it?
  4. What does this intimacy mean to us: it changes nothing, it’s the start of something, it’s not clear yet? What expectations have gone unspoken?
  5. What do we decide about messages and photos: what’s okay to create, what to keep, what to show others?
  6. If there are more than two of us: which agreements with other partners affect this intimacy, and what gets told to whom?

When it is no longer about skills

The techniques above work where both of you are, overall, safe. If there is:

  • a partner secretly taking off a condom, tampering with or hiding contraception, pushing toward pregnancy;
  • a threat to share intimate photos, or sharing them;
  • continuing after “stop,” freezing, or pulling away;
  • punishment for saying no;
  • using dependence, a position, or someone else’s intoxication to make saying no costly,

then the priority is safety and control over your own records and your decisions about pregnancy, not a couples’ communication exercise. In family planning clinics, people are asked about this in private, and hidden methods of contraception and safety resources are discussed; among women who had recently experienced violence from a partner, the odds of being coerced into pregnancy were lower after this approach8. Don’t show your answers to a partner who may be controlling. More on this in chapter 23.

12. The First Months: How People Grow Close and Decide to Be Together

In short

In the first months, closeness doesn’t grow from personal stories as such, but from the response to them: the listener lets you know they understood, accepted, and value you.

Staying together isn’t a single feeling but more like a decision you keep making: it rests on whether you’re good together, whether there are attractive alternatives around, and how much has been invested. And each person hears the word “together” their own way until it’s been talked through. Irritation from a shared life is a reason to figure things out, not a verdict.

What to do: open up in portions and check the meaning; ask with real interest about the good things; find out, out loud, what “we’re together” means. It’s best to name the limits for fights, and your “yes” with its scope and time frame, right away. If your plans differ, there are three moves: negotiate, change the form of the relationship, or leave clearly. Threats, coercion, and surveillance are a question of safety, not compatibility.

Katya and Mei have been together for a few weeks. One evening, Katya tells her for the first time how her father didn’t show up to her graduation. Mei listens, nods, says “that’s awful,” and suggests ordering dinner: feeding someone is her way of being there. Something in Katya’s chest tightens and quietly shuts; she changes the subject to a TV show. At night she wants to ask something else entirely: “Are we together? Are you seeing anyone else?” She doesn’t ask: she’s scared of the answer. In the morning, Mei sees a closed-off face and stays silent too. Each reads the other’s silence her own way, and both silences become triggers for the next ones.

What is going on

The loop. Someone takes the risk of saying something personal. The speaker’s chest, throat, and stomach tighten; the listener has their own reaction to someone else’s vulnerability. Each fills in the meaning: “I’m being heard” or “no one’s there.” And each does something: asks a follow-up, hugs, jokes, offers food, looks away. That becomes an event for the other person, and the two loops lock together.

In the studies this chapter relies on, the body wasn’t measured — only people’s accounts and behavior — so the body link is a working guess. If you hear your body’s signals poorly, you can train that separately: in a trial where people were randomly split into groups, a daily practice over several weeks improved the ability to notice them, though its effect on relationships wasn’t tested (chapters 02 and 03). Tears in a moment of openness are also a choice point for the listener (chapter 05).

The body and the “chemistry of love.” In a small experiment, a brain area linked to reward responded more strongly to the image of a loved one, but this says nothing about dopamine or about the couple’s future. Oxytocin, the “love hormone,” isn’t a button either: a single measurement poorly shows what a person’s usual level is (this was tested on a small group of healthy men). Whether biology changes before people get close or goes along with it is unknown (on falling in love, see chapter 10).

Opening up and the response. The explanation goes like this: a story becomes closeness when the listener seems understanding, accepting, and caring. You don’t have to open up when it isn’t safe. In diary records of each interaction, the more responsive the partner seemed, the closer people felt; in one study, talking about feelings was more strongly linked to closeness than talking about facts. Among strangers, liking grew from taking turns sharing: what matters is the exchange itself, not a magic list of questions.

Responsiveness, judging by data that are still modest, is also linked to desire: in these studies, more strongly in women, but this isn’t a rule for all women, let alone for same-sex couples. A long-term study looked at how it’s linked to how hard a person takes everyday setbacks, and to mortality, but “responsiveness makes you live longer” hasn’t been proven. The listener can ask what kind of support is needed rather than assume that direct comfort works for everyone. See also: “Alchemy Without Magic” (the physiology of arousal), chapters 14 (bids for connection) and 18 (desire over the years).

Good news. When a person shares something good, the other responds in different ways: with lively interest, quiet approval, criticism (“what about the risks?”), or changing the subject. This is a theory: it doesn’t prove the rule “always choose the first one.” All you can see is that the quality of the response matters, not that any kind of enthusiasm helps. It’s worth responding to what the news means to this particular person, including when family or other partners find out about it.

What holds a couple together. Commitment grows when you’re good together, weakens when there are attractive alternatives around, and gets stronger with investments: shared money, friends, time, a home. In a large review, all three supports were linked to commitment, and commitment predicted breakups1. These are associations, not causes: it doesn’t follow that the more you’ve invested together, the healthier the love. All three change week by week, so commitment looks less like an event and more like a decision you confirm again: with words, with actions, by turning down what gets in its way. This is a practical reading, not a tested fact.

When things decide themselves. Sometimes a couple moves in together and sets up a shared household without deciding anything out loud. Then the constraints grow before the intentions are clear. This is an idea; there’s no experiment behind it. The takeaway: make decisions about exclusivity, housing, money, and leaving the relationship separately. And remember your own moment of “yes, I’m giving this a try”: if there wasn’t one and your life is already shared, it’s time to say the choice out loud. The research here is almost all about heterosexual marriages.

When things start to get on your nerves. As lives intertwine, one person’s actions can get in the way of the other’s plans: running late ruins dinner. The scheme goes like this: the body tenses, the mind fills in “I’m not a priority,” then comes a reproach, a withdrawal, or a request to confirm love — and that’s already an event for the partner. It’s easy to decide: “now I’m seeing their true face.” But something else is possible: in a survey of people in relationships, small things felt more irritating where the partner more often disrupted the usual routine and it was unclear what you are to each other2. The scheme is still a theory; whether irritation grows with closeness and dies down later, a snapshot of one moment can’t show (on years together, see chapter 40). So irritation is a reason to ask yourself: is it the action itself that makes me angry, the lack of clarity between us, or the unresolved question of how to fit our days together?

What “together” means. Science doesn’t say when it’s time to figure out “what are we to each other”; it also hasn’t been proven that the conversation itself protects against a breakup. Uncertainty starts its own loop: a message is late, the body gets worked up, the mind asks “what are we?”, then comes checking up or pulling away, and the other person’s reply or silence spins the circle up again. As a general explanation, this is a theory, though observations support it. Clarifying can remove the uncertainty about the facts; whether it removes the anxiety, and whether “relationships without a label” are linked to anxious attachment, there’s no data. If anger flared after a disappointed expectation, separate what was promised out loud from your own unspoken forecast before seeing bad intent (chapter 16). This is practical advice, not a tested technique. And silence in response to a message is not yet “avoidant attachment” (chapter 13).

In experiments with written scenarios, an explicit rejection gave more clarity and less intention to keep tabs on the person than disappearing did, though it didn’t immediately take away the unpleasant feelings3. These were not real breakups.

If there are more than two of you. Little is known about the first months in several relationships at once. A survey about the lift at the start of a new relationship (chapter 26) recruited people who signed up on their own and asked them once: it doesn’t represent polyamorous people in general or other open arrangements. Conclusions about questions for getting close, about comparing cultures, and about moving in don’t carry over to such arrangements without being tested: in the descriptions of these studies, non-monogamous participants weren’t singled out. Explicit agreements with everyone involved are needed here, but there are no trials of this practice. In consensual non-monogamy, unlike cheating, people distinguish agreements, disclosing them, and breaking them. Based on these data, no configuration is “more normal.”

How to tell it is happening

In yourself:

  • You want to share something personal, but your chest tightens and the conversation drifts elsewhere.
  • You want to ask “are we together?” but you’re scared to hear the answer.
  • You read silence or a late reply as a verdict.
  • You can’t say what counts as “together” for the two of you.

In your partner:

  • After you’ve opened up, they switch to food, a joke, or a TV show.
  • They respond to good news flatly, with criticism, or by turning the conversation to themselves.
  • To “what are we to each other?” they answer “later.”
  • They close off and go quiet, and you don’t know why.

What else to notice:

  • Worrying: fog around their past and what they do; confessions that are too heavy already on the first date; vows like “you’re the best thing in my life,” “forever”; contempt for all their exes, or “all men (women) are like that”; demands that you drop your friends or change jobs; disappearing, then being intensely close again.
  • Reassuring: modest promises get kept; you can get tired of each other, spend time apart, and calmly meet up again; both of you are ready to introduce each other to the people close to you.

This is a working list, not a validated test: a reason to ask and not to rush big decisions, not a diagnosis. Whether loud early declarations lead to pressure hasn’t really been tested4. What matters more is how the person reacts when you slow down: is “not so fast” respected — or met with pressure, punishment, cutting you off from friends?

What to do

1. Open up in portions and check the meaning

When: you want to get closer, and both of you agree. Once, for no longer than forty-five minutes.

What to do: take three blocks of questions, going from impersonal to personal. Answer in turns; either of you can skip a question without explaining. After each answer, briefly check whether what was said got through. At the end, each of you separately rates, from one to ten, closeness, safety, and wanting to do it again.

How you know it worked: safety and wanting to do it again feel no weaker than closeness. Whether such ratings predict anything about future love hasn’t been tested.

Don’t do this with someone who punishes vulnerability, and don’t ramp up openness under pressure. Only something narrow has been tested: among strangers in a lab, this increases the feeling of closeness; the home version with a safety rating hasn’t been tried. If you have different native languages, check the meaning especially carefully: there’s no version of the questions that has been tested across cultures.

2. Ask about the good news

When: something good has happened.

What to do: one of you talks about a real joy. The other first asks how the teller would like the news to be received, then asks: what it means, what the hardest part was, what the teller is proud of, what comes next. The listener doesn’t turn the conversation to themselves. Then the teller says whether they felt understood.

How you know it worked: the teller feels understood, not just heard out. You can keep a short log: did you share good things this week? The technique itself hasn’t been tested.

3. Talk through what “together” means

When: when you’re ready, not in the middle of a fight.

What to do: first, each of you separately answers four questions: what was expected; what you actually agreed on; what you felt in your body (if nothing, write that down); what you want to ask. Then take turns saying what you want on five topics, while the other repeats back what they heard:

  • other people: is it okay right now to date and sleep with someone else, and what happens if interest in someone comes up;
  • sex and safety: what kind of protection, and how to tell each other about it;
  • disclosure: what to tell each other, and whom to tell about the relationship;
  • pace: how often to see each other, when to introduce each other to the people close to you, to share a home and money;
  • status: what to call each other, and what counts as a change to the agreement.

Write down the result: for each topic, “yes,” “no,” or “not decided yet,” plus the date when you’ll revisit it.

How you know it worked: both of you retell the agreement the same way.

Neither this script nor the four questions before it have been tested: it’s a list of topics, not a protocol. For non-monogamous arrangements, “exclusivity” may not fit: what’s needed is specific agreements (chapter 27). A refusal, a safety boundary, and existing agreements with other partners come before any exercise.

4. If your answers differ, pick one of three moves

When: it turns out you want different things.

What to do: negotiate — figure out what exactly differs and what each of you is willing to change. Or change the form of the relationship: go slower, without exclusivity, or as a friendship with intimacy (chapters 24 and 28). Or leave: directly and briefly, not by disappearing (chapter 22). The data don’t say which move is right; the three moves themselves are a practical conclusion that hasn’t been tested.

How you know it worked: no one is guessing from silence, and it’s clear what’s been decided.

5. If you come from different cultures, ask what an answer means

When: an answer is ambiguous: “we’ll see,” “it’s awkward,” silence.

What to do: ask what it is: consent, politeness, or a request to wait. What role the family’s opinion plays. Which language is easier for discussing important things.

How you know it worked: you can retell what the person meant and have them confirm it (chapters 33, 37, and 38).

In surveys, Chinese respondents preferred an indirect communication style more often than European Americans did, but this is about the group, not the individual: observations of couples don’t support the rule “Chinese couples speak indirectly.” There’s no data on Russian-speaking and Chinese partners.

6. Name the limits for fights from the very start

When: at a calm moment, ideally in the first months.

What to do: each of you says what must never happen in your fights. For example: no hitting, no yelling, no mockery, no disappearing for days without a word. If yelling, mockery, or disappearing has already happened, say it as soon as you’ve both cooled down: “We’re not doing that,” — without putting it off until “if it happens again.” If there’s hitting, threats, surveillance, or coercion, what’s needed isn’t this conversation but the section “When it is no longer about skills.”

How you know it worked: both of you can name the limits, and after they’ve been said, the behavior changes instead of repeating.

You don’t have to wait for someone to hit you to call humiliation and intimidation harmful: in couples before their wedding, where there had been no physical violence yet, these predicted its first occurrence in the early years of marriage5. This is a risk, not a verdict; the data are only on male-female couples, and the conversation about limits itself hasn’t been tested.

7. Your “yes” — with a scope and a time frame

When: you’re asked to take on more “just for the time being”: pay for both of you, move, run the whole household.

What to do: name what you definitely have a “yes” for: what, how much, until when — and when you’ll revisit it. For example: “I can pay the rent for six months, but not after that.” Ask yourself what you’re agreeing for: for the sake of what you want to build together, or to avoid a fight and guilt.

How you know it worked: the concession feels like a choice, not like a payment for peace and quiet; the other person notices it and thanks you; on the set date, you come back to the conversation.

In the diaries of people who were dating, concessions made for the sake of something good together went along with better days, and those made to avoid something bad went along with worse ones6. This is an association, not a tested technique.

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: there’s a set of questions after which strangers fall in love.
In fact: after a long conversation taking turns, going from impersonal to personal, people rated their closeness higher than after an ordinary chat7. Love and how lasting the couple is weren’t measured. The questions can be offered as a conversation with no strings attached, but not as a technique that orders you to feel.

People think: the main thing is to support someone when things go wrong, and celebrating their successes is a nice extra.
In fact: in recorded conversations of couples, responses to good news were more closely linked to how well the relationship was doing, and to breaking up soon after, than responses to bad news were8. This is an observation and doesn’t prove the cause, and helping in hard times matters too.

People think: moving in together is the best way to test a relationship.
In fact: in American data on first marriages, divorce happened more often among those who moved in together before getting engaged9. This is an association, not proof that moving in leads to divorce: selection is possible, and the rising cost of leaving also explains the picture. Whether moving in will harm a particular couple is unknown; first agree on expectations, money, and a way out.

People think: early signals — how fast someone replies, “avoidant attachment” — show how it will all end.
In fact: “red flag” lists have no tested accuracy. You can’t tell a new acquaintance’s tendency toward violence from a single trait10. Loud percentages of accuracy in predicting divorce are misleading until you take into account how common divorce is in general11; this is about the method, not about a person with a “red flag.” For same-sex, non-monogamous, and Russian-Chinese couples, risk and accuracy haven’t been established.

Talk it over together

  1. What does “we’re together” mean to you: what’s included, and what isn’t?
  2. What do we currently consider okay when it comes to other people, at what pace are we moving, and how will we know if that changes?
  3. When I share something personal, what helps you listen, and what lands as “you’re not with me”?
  4. How do you like people to react to your good news: with questions, with joy, with a quiet smile?
  5. What would be a signal for you to revisit our agreement?
  6. What do you want this relationship for — and is that here now, not just in the plans?
  7. What do I bring out in you: calm, anxiety, care? Which of these do you like?

When it is no longer about skills

Threats, physical or sexual violence, coercion, surveillance, and forbidding someone to leave or to see people are a question of safety, whatever the chemistry and the structure of the relationship. Signs:

  • boundaries are met with threats or punishment;
  • you’re forced into sex after saying no;
  • your movements and messages are monitored;
  • you’re not allowed to leave or to meet friends;
  • humiliation and intimidation to the point where you’re scared: this is not “getting used to each other,” even without hitting.

This is what a loop of coercive control can look like: a boundary under threat, fear, the thought “it’s not safe to say no,” giving in or hiding things, and the controlling partner takes that as permission. This is a description of a possible loop, not a test.

What to do: don’t try to fix this with the conversations and exercises from this chapter. Tell someone you trust. Think through how to end contact safely. If your life is in danger, call emergency services. If saying no could set off violence or surveillance, a direct face-to-face conversation isn’t required: it’s enough to end things in a safe way. More on this in chapter 23.

If there’s no fear, but jabs and contempt have become routine, that’s what chapter 41 is about.

13. Attachment: Why We Love the Way We Do

In short

Attachment isn’t a label on a person but what happens to you when closeness feels uncertain: you’re pulled to get closer, to check, to text again — or to pull back, go quiet, bury yourself in work.

It’s more accurate to describe this with two scales than with the four “types” from tests, and you can act differently with different people. Childhood matters, but it isn’t a life sentence.

What to do: look at a specific episode — what was going on in your body, what story got filled in, what you did in response; ask for one change, naming the behavior rather than the character; don’t expect one partner to “heal” the other.

Artyom texts Dima at lunch: “Are we seeing each other tonight?” No answer. By four o’clock he’s gone through every explanation — busy, cooled off, offended, someone else has come along — and he texts twice more. Dima is in a meeting at the time, and message after message on the screen looks like a bill to be paid. Something inside him tightens, and he wants to put the phone far away. He answers with one word: “Tonight.” Artyom reads coldness in it; Dima reads pressure in the messages. In the evening they meet, and each already has his own story about the other.

Nobody meant to hurt anyone. Two habits of coping with uncertainty got caught on each other.

What is going on

A pause with no answer can start a cycle. An unread message, silence at dinner, “we’ll talk later” — any unclear pause. The body may respond: arousal, attention latching onto every unclear sign, an urge to move toward the person or away from them. But to be honest: the studies reviewed have no measurements of heart rate or hormones that reliably tell the two styles apart. And looking calm on the outside doesn’t mean being calm inside. So the body part is possible but not measured, and you can’t classify a person from one episode.

Then a meaning gets filled in. For some, it sounds like “I’m being left” — and they’re pulled to seek contact, double-check, text again. For others, it’s “I’m being pressured” — and they’re pulled to keep any visible need to a minimum: go quiet, bury themselves in work. These are ways of coping in a specific moment, not labels.

Your move is an event for the other person. One more message reads as pressure and increases the pulling away. Pulling away reads as rejection and increases the chasing. How to notice and name a signal in the body — chapters 02 and 03; how to calm down together — 04; where tears fit into this cycle — 05; how to read anger — 16.

Two scales, not four types. Anxiety is how much your search for contact ramps up when closeness feels uncertain. Avoidance is how much you’re pulled to pull back when closeness feels like an intrusion. The four “types” from popular tests are just four combinations of high and low on these scales, and the data fit a smooth scale better than separate boxes1. The practical takeaway: one person’s result can differ with different people. The question “which of the four am I?” doesn’t fit well; the question “what happens to me with this person when closeness is unclear?” fits better.

It’s unknown whether test cutoffs work the same way in different languages and with different kinds of couples. For Chinese-Russian and Chinese-European couples, for two men or two women, for non-monogamous arrangements, these data don’t give universal “norms.” It’s more useful to ask each other which language makes it easier to talk about a need (on feelings in a foreign language, see chapter 33) and which relatives take part in the couple’s decisions.

Childhood matters, but it isn’t a life sentence. Repeated experiences of care shape expectations: how available others are when you need them. Later friendships, partners, and losses can refine those expectations. A familiar tightness in the chest can trigger an old prediction, but it doesn’t make it inevitable. In a long study that followed people from birth to adolescence, attachment turned out to be linked to the quality of care and to close friendship, while most of the temperament traits and genes studied were barely linked to it. There is some continuity, but it isn’t a fixed trait: even in toddlers, categories matched poorly when retested2 — though that’s about early childhood, not the path to adult love. In a higher-risk group followed for many years, adults who looked back and described “earned” security hadn’t always been insecurely attached as infants: some had supportive care later, some had depressive symptoms. “Earned” security is a working concept for researchers, not a diagnosis of childhood trauma and not a promise of healing. What will change attachment for a particular person — non-monogamy, a move, a child — these studies don’t say (chapters 28, 31, 36).

A gentle response softens the moment, but doesn’t heal. A request to change a behavior is heard as criticism; anger and pulling away follow. In recorded conversations of male-female couples where one asked the other to change something, avoidant partners showed less anger and pulling away when the other person spoke more gently3. This is an observation, not an experiment where the behavior was randomly assigned; and no one was a “secure healer” all the time — moments were compared. That gentleness changes the person themselves in the long run is still only a hypothesis: there’s no confirmation for Chinese-European, same-sex, or non-monogamous couples. The phrase “first love yourself, then you’ll be able to love someone else” wasn’t tested in these studies either: no one has measured a threshold of self-love below which you can’t love.

The “anxious + avoidant” couple. The mechanics are plausible: asking for reassurance feels like pressure to the one who guards their independence, and stepping back looks like rejection. But this explains why things can be bad between the two, not why these people chose each other. In a large review, both anxiety and avoidance were linked to lower satisfaction — both for the person themselves and for their partner. And in a long study of male-female couples, couples with an avoidant man and an anxious woman stayed together surprisingly steadily, even though they initially rated their relationship lower4. “Together” and “satisfied” are different things.

What’s known about outside help. Couples therapy that works with vulnerable feelings tries to make needs a topic for conversation rather than a reason for attacking and withdrawing. According to pooled data, couples improve compared to where they started; compared to other methods, the gain is more modest and less certain; after some time, the result held5. But the pooled data also included student theses and studies without random assignment, and this is the outcome of treating a couple, not proof that attachment has been “cured.” There’s also a short technique: briefly recall a person you felt secure with. Right after it, feelings, thoughts, and behavior shift moderately, and this has been replicated in many studies6 — though there’s no certainty that all the failures were published. It doesn’t show lasting changes in a couple. Whether this suits everyone — same-sex, non-monogamous, Chinese-European couples — hasn’t been established. Whom to see for therapy — chapter 35.

If there are more than two of you. A common belief: people who choose consensual non-monogamy are avoidant. The data don’t support it. In an online survey, among people with no such experience, avoidance was linked to being willing to try it, but not to actually trying it7. In another study of people with two or more concurrent romantic partners, anxiety and avoidance were measured separately with each partner: on average, insecurity with both was low, and insecurity with a particular person went along with lower satisfaction in that specific relationship, but not in the other one8. Both studies used volunteers, and in the second the measurements were taken once, so the evidence is weak. So people in polyamory aren’t one attachment type, and you can’t say it makes people more secure. Open relationships, swinging, polyamory, relationship anarchy are different forms; you can’t carry data over from one to another. On forms — chapter 24, on jealousy — 25, on agreements — 27, on networks of relationships — 29.

How to tell it is happening

In yourself:

  • Closeness feels unclear — and there’s a strong urge: to check, to text again, to get confirmation. Or the opposite one: to switch off, answer with one word, bury yourself in work.
  • Out of a pause, a story about the other person is instantly ready: “I’m being left” or “I’m being pressured.”
  • Your body reacted before your thoughts did.
  • If anger flared over an unmet expectation, “the agreement was broken” and “they wanted to reject me” stick together into one, even though they’re different things.
  • You want to explain everything with “your type” rather than describe what happened this time.

In your partner:

  • They text again, ask for clarification, double-check — or go silent, answer briefly, go into another room.
  • Your moves wind each other up: one gets closer — the other moves away; the other moves away — the first gets even closer.
  • A quiet reply isn’t necessarily a calm one.

What to do

These techniques aren’t a tested program but an assembly of what’s known in bits and pieces.

1. Ask for one thing — gently

When: when you want your partner to change something, especially if your partner tends to pull away.

What to do: name one specific behavior, not a character trait: “at dinner, the phone stays in your hand,” not “you’re always on your phone.” Ask for one change that can be agreed on. After the request, pause and let them answer. To start, one request per conversation is enough: no one has determined the optimal frequency. This is a conclusion from observing couples, not a tested exercise.

How you know it worked: less pulling away (silence, changing the subject, leaving the room), less anger. After the conversation, rate separately how well it went for each of you.

Don’t use this where there’s violence and control (see “When it is no longer about skills”).

2. Three lines: body, story, urge

When: after an episode when closeness felt uncertain. Especially if analyzing comes more naturally to you than feeling.

What to do: write down three lines. The first — what you felt in your body: where, how strongly, how it changed. The second — what story got filled in about the other person, in one sentence. The third — what urge came up and what you ended up doing. If there was anger, add a fourth: what agreement or expectation was broken — separately from any conclusion about the other person’s intentions. A separate entry for each partner.

How you know it worked: the gap between the signal and becoming aware of it gets shorter; instead of “they wanted to reject me,” you get “the agreement was broken.”

This specific kind of entry hasn’t been tested — it’s a way of observing yourself. If you can’t name a sensation, that doesn’t yet mean you’re one of the “avoidant.” It’s better to collect several episodes with one person.

3. Something to lean on before a hard conversation

When: before a conversation you’re a bit afraid of.

What to do: recall one specific time when someone was with you who was available and supportive (not necessarily a partner). Recall what you could see, hear, and how it felt in your body. A minute or two is our suggestion, not a tested dose. Shifts show up in the moment; a lasting improvement in attachment hasn’t been shown this way.

How you know it worked: rate separately how it felt right away and how the conversation went, and later, how the relationship changed. If the memory brings pain or anxiety instead of support, stop.

4. Facts first, then “style”

When: when you want to explain an unpleasant feeling with your “attachment style” — especially if there are more than two of you.

What to do: find out what has been agreed on, how much time each person has, how disclosure is going, and which expectation exactly wasn’t met. Look at each relationship separately: you may act differently with different people.

How you know it worked: instead of “that’s just how I am,” a specific question comes up — about the situation or for your partner.

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: there are four attachment types, and each of us is one of them.
In fact: these are four combinations of two scales, and the data fit a smooth scale better.

People think: your type is set in childhood and doesn’t change.
In fact: there is some continuity, but attachment isn’t carved in stone. It’s more reliable to look at how a person behaves with the same partner time after time than at memories of childhood.

People think: anxious and avoidant people are drawn to each other — and get stuck.
In fact: these studies don’t give a rule that “such couples attract each other,” and explaining a painful situation by attraction means risking dragging it out. The deciding question is a different one: can each of you make a request and get a response without chasing, pressure, or disappearing?

People think: a secure partner will heal an insecure one.
In fact: a gentle response softens the moment, but lasting change hasn’t been shown this way. By assigning your partner the “healing,” you can end up with pressure and self-blame. It’s better to offer secure responses by your own choice and keep an eye on how both of you are doing.

Talk it over together

  1. When closeness feels uncertain, what happens in each of our bodies, what story do we fill in, and what do we do?
  2. What’s the first sign that we’ve gotten into the cycle — one reaching closer, the other pulling away? What does it look like from the outside?
  3. What expectation did each of us have before our last fight — and was it ever said out loud?
  4. How can a request be made so it doesn’t sound like criticism? What can each of us say instead of going silent or attacking?
  5. In which language is it easier for us to talk about needs? Which relatives take part in our decisions — and what do we both agree on?
  6. If there are more than two of us: what can each person count on in response to reaching out — separately in each relationship?

When it is no longer about skills

Everything above about gentle requests and mutual disclosure assumes that each person can answer freely and leave the conversation. If the relationship has:

  • fear of your partner’s reaction;
  • forbidding or checking messages, phone, social media, location (tech-based surveillance is also a distinct form of partner abuse);
  • threats;
  • violence;
  • a demand to “fix” or “heal” the other person,

then the techniques in this chapter aren’t the answer. Don’t do mutual disclosure or joint conversation exercises with someone who controls or uses violence: a serious threat to safety needs a safety plan, not a joint communication workshop. No one is obligated to stay in an unsafe relationship to test out “softening.” More on this in chapter 23.

Part III. The long haul

14. Everyday Life: Small Bids, “Thank You,” Being Happy for the Other, Sleep

In short

The famous “everyday numbers” — response percentages, a “magic” good-to-bad threshold, the “five to one” rule — don’t hold up well under scrutiny. The modest findings are more reliable: after a bad night there are more fights, and less warmth in them; a partner’s wanted touch softens the body’s response to stress (in small experiments); in the lab, healthy young couples got slightly more REM sleep when they slept together.

What to do: notice small bids you make to each other, give thanks for specific things, ask about good news and linger on the good, ask what kind of care is needed right now, postpone hard conversations after a sleepless night, and choose your sleeping arrangement based on the quality of sleep, not on a rule.

No fights doesn’t yet mean there’s something good: warmth, interest, and being happy for the other need looking after separately.

Artyom is finishing the dishes; Kirill is lying on the couch with his phone. “Hey, the neighbors’ elevator is broken again,” Artyom says without turning around. Kirill grunts and scrolls his feed. What Artyom actually wanted was to ask whether they’re going to visit friends for the weekend, but starting with the elevator was easier. Kirill tossed and turned all night, his head is buzzing, and any remark sounds like a demand. He didn’t notice that the question wasn’t about the elevator. Artyom didn’t notice that Kirill hadn’t slept. The evening passes in a silence that each of them reads his own way.

Neither of them wanted this silence. A question that was “not about that” and a sleepless night came together in it.

What is going on

A small thing is a bid. A question across the room, a joke, a gesture, a “look at this” — any small attempt to make contact. The body responds before words do: attention, heart rate, breathing, and sweating change. Then a meaning gets filled in — “they’re glad to see me” or “nobody cares about me” — and an action gets chosen: respond, brush it off, say nothing. For the other person, this is a new trigger, and the two loops lock together. The scheme is plausible but hasn’t been tested as a whole: it hasn’t been shown that every missed bid wounds attachment.

What’s known about the body. In the studies reviewed, the body wasn’t measured right at the moment of a missed bid; there are only the closest small experiments. When a partner is nearby — holding a hand, stroking, or just sitting there — the body responds less strongly to pain (sweating was measured), and no difference was found between these ways. A short hug from a partner before a stressful task lowered the stress hormone in women, but not in men1; this is one small trial, and it didn’t show a long-term effect or the role of oxytocin. Oxytocin isn’t the “love hormone”: attachment can’t be consistently tracked by its level in the blood.

Then expectations kick in. You’re waiting for a response, you see the back of a head — the mind fills in “they don’t notice me,” and your cold reply becomes a new trigger for the other person. But the back of the head could have meant tiredness, concentration, or searching for a word in a second language. A question checks this most accurately, not a guess. On body signals — chapter 02; on calming down together — chapter 04; on tears (they can be a bid too) — chapter 05.

Silence in response is not a verdict. The “one demands, the other withdraws” pattern changes depending on the topic and on what’s being asked for. Silence can mean overload or an attempt to stay safe, not “stonewalling.” The label doesn’t reveal the motive (on conflict, see chapter 15).

No bad doesn’t yet mean there’s good. “We don’t fight, we’re not rude, we don’t cheat” is the absence of harm, not warmth. The good and the bad in a couple aren’t one scale but two2: you can almost never fight and still almost never enjoy each other. So it isn’t enough to remove what hurts. Whether there’s interest, shared joy, and a lively response needs looking at separately.

Good news is a bid too. A promotion, a good day, a little find you want to share. What happens when one person shares something good and the other responds has been studied3, but it hasn’t been tested as an exercise for couples. The assumption goes like this: an attentive response shows that both the event and the person matter. Alongside this are observations of laughter and play: couples who spent longer laughing at the same time had higher scores on relationship quality, and partners’ playfulness went along with greater satisfaction with the relationship4. But laughter and play were observed, not assigned; it doesn’t follow that “scheduled laughter” heals fights.

Gratitude works through care that gets noticed. One person does something thoughtful, the other notices and says “thank you,” the first feels seen — and becomes more responsive. This is a description of a path, not a proven effect of an exercise. In observations of couples, those who appreciated their partner more stood out for their responsiveness and commitment to the relationship — both by their own account and in the eyes of outside observers — and such couples more often stayed together over time5. Appreciation goes along with caring for the relationship, but it can’t be separated from the relationship’s quality: in a good relationship it’s easier both to give thanks and to notice care. Gratitude exercises have been studied more widely, but on individuals, not couples, so a mandatory daily “thank you” as a way to fix a relationship is no better grounded than any other “dose.”

Touch, “we,” and rituals. The assumption goes like this: a wanted touch or a shared joke gives the body a sense of ease, the mind reads it as “safe, I’m understood” — and closeness grows. An unwanted touch runs the chain backward. The data don’t give a universal “dose” of touch: the kinds of support differed little, and the result depended on who was receiving it and on the stress. Rituals like an agreed-on “good morning” text can make being available predictable, but the benefit of that particular ritual hasn’t been measured. “We-talk” (“we had a fight,” not “you ruin everything”) goes along with a couple’s well-being, but swapping “I” for “we” hasn’t been tested as a treatment: language more likely reflects the relationship. In an arrangement of several people, “we” can mean the couple or the whole network — say which out loud.

Sleep is an invisible participant. The other person’s sleep is changed by movement, noise, affection, a sense of safety. And bad sleep changes how ready the body is to react: a small remark reads more harshly, there are fewer sympathetic responses, the reply is sharper. The trigger is the same, but the body and the meaning are already different. Here the data are better than for the loud numbers: after a bad night, couples’ diaries showed more conflicts, and emotions and the accuracy of empathy during a fight were worse6. Lack of sleep before a discussion went along with less warmth, and in the conflict itself, with a higher stress hormone (the participants were mostly couples happy with each other). It can’t be proven that lack of sleep caused each fight. Activities that lowered arousal reduced anger, while those that raised it barely did; but this isn’t treatment for couples (on anger, see chapter 16).

What we don’t know yet. It’s unclear whether gratitude, wanted touch, and listening improve a relationship beyond what the couple already has, or simply go along with good ones: what’s needed are experiments where couples are randomly split into groups. Male-female couples have been studied the most; for other combinations no replications were found, so it’s better to name who made the bid and who responded, rather than hand out the roles of “wife” and “husband.” No direct studies were found on a couple of a Russian-speaking or European man and a woman from mainland China. The closest things are therapists’ accounts (they note attention to the vocabulary of feelings, family roles, and harmony) and a comparison of students in the US. These aren’t data on Chinese women’s preferences, and they don’t prove that a Chinese partner is less caring or that a Russian-speaking one is more direct. Arrangements of several people are studied mostly through those who volunteered for an online survey; there are no tested equivalents of the percentages or the “five to one” for them, and the experiments with the hug and the hand were done with male-female couples.

How to tell it is happening

In yourself:

  • You say something in passing, you get an “uh-huh” or the back of a head, and inside it’s already decided: “they don’t notice me.”
  • You want attention, but it’s easier to start with something unrelated, like the elevator.
  • You had a bad night, any remark sounds like a demand, and you answer more sharply than you’d like.
  • There are almost no fights, but it’s hard to remember the last time you had a good time together.
  • You can only describe your partner through responsibilities — who pays, who cooks — and it’s hard to say what kind of person your partner is in their own right.

In your partner:

  • They grunt, don’t look up from the screen, answer briefly or go quiet. This could be tiredness, overload, searching for a word in a second language, or a way to stay safe, not indifference.
  • They don’t notice that the question wasn’t about what was actually said.
  • After a bad night, they’re sharper and less sympathetic over the same trigger.
  • They share good news less and less often, or only in passing.
  • Later it turns out that each of you read the silence your own way.

What to do

None of these techniques has been tested as a program for couples: it’s an assembly of what’s known in bits and pieces.

1. A week of bids and “thank yous”

When: every evening for a week.

What to do: write down a couple of moments from the day when there was a small bid — yours or toward you — and whether a response came. And one where you managed to notice care and say “thank you” specifically: “Thanks for the package: I managed to finish the report on time.” What matters isn’t the word itself but what exactly touched you. If you don’t understand whether the person needs your response, ask: “Do you need me to respond to this right now?” At the end of the week, go over the notes together.

If fairness matters to you, a “thank you” for an ordinary responsibility doesn’t cancel a request for a fair division (chapter 20). If you hear your body’s signals poorly, start with the action: what exactly turned out to be helpful (chapter 03). You don’t have to give thanks on command.

How you know it worked: you find more specific examples, and the other person says they understood what you said the way it was meant.

This is self-observation, not a worked-out method: the data don’t confirm how many “thank yous” are needed. If there are more than two of you, name which relationship you’re talking about: a bid might have been meant for another partner or run into the schedule — then missing it doesn’t mean there’s no interest (chapters 29 and 27). If you speak different languages, a missed reply might be the effort of translating (chapter 33).

2. Ask what kind of care is needed right now

When: your partner is having a hard time, or you want to support them with a touch, a joke, a word.

What to do: ask: “What kind of care do you need right now?” and “What does this gesture mean to you?” If it’s hard to answer, offer options: to listen, to give advice, to help with something practical, or to leave them in quiet. Offer touch, don’t impose it: presumably, wanted touch helps, and unwanted touch does the opposite. Don’t guess based on gender or nationality, don’t assign your partner a “type,” and come back to the question from time to time: the answer changes.

How you know it worked: instead of a guess, you get a specific answer, and what you offer is gladly accepted.

3. Eight minutes of listening

When: at a calm moment; how often hasn’t been studied, you can start with once a week.

What to do: take turns talking about what’s weighing on you, for about eight minutes each. The listener first clarifies what was meant, and only then offers solutions.

How you know it worked: both of you say you were understood, and the real request got an answer.

This has been tested weakly: in an observation of male-female couples, attentive listening went along with coping better with stress, but the couples were observed, not trained. If it isn’t safe to answer freely, don’t push the conversation (chapter 23).

4. After a bad night, say it out loud and postpone

When: someone didn’t get enough sleep, and there’s an important conversation ahead.

What to do: say: “I had a bad night, it’ll be hard for me to listen” — and agree on when to come back to the topic.

How you know it worked: you come back to the topic without things escalating.

5. Three weeks of sleep

When: three weeks in a row, every morning.

What to do: write down how you slept, whether you felt safe and comfortable, and whether there were conflicts. By agreement, try different things for a week each: together, apart, separate blankets.

How you know it worked: nights are more stable, and you can come back to the topic without things escalating. You don’t need hormone tests for this.

This is a self-observation plan, not a tested protocol.

6. Responding to good news

When: your partner shares something good — a success at work, a nice encounter, a little find.

What to do: put aside your phone and what you’re doing. Ask: what happened, what was the nicest part, why it matters. Show that you’re interested. Then switch: tell your own.

How you know it worked: the one who shared feels that their story was heard, and sharing good things gets easier.

How much this helps and how often it’s needed is unknown. And it isn’t a substitute for talking about what hurts.

7. Linger on the good

When: you’re having a good time together — at dinner, on a walk, on a lazy morning.

What to do: don’t jump straight to the next thing. Stay in it and say out loud: “We’re good right now.” Sometimes recall such moments together: what happened and what you felt. Take care of your pleasant shared rituals — a walk, cooking together, a favorite café: after a fight or during a hard stretch, they can become a way back to each other.

How you know it worked: good things are easier to remember, and after a fight there’s somewhere to come back to.

The technique itself hasn’t been tested. A similar exercise — recalling good moments from your relationship with the help of prompts — was studied in an experiment with people living far from their partners7, but with each person separately, not with couples, and how much it gave is unknown (on distance, see chapter 36).

8. Look at your partner as a separate person

When: your partner has become familiar, and you mostly see responsibilities: who pays, who cooks, who takes out the trash.

What to do:

  1. For a few minutes a day, while your partner is busy with their own things — working, washing dishes, getting dressed — just watch. With no goal and no expectation that they’ll notice or say “thank you.” Notice what it brings up in you.
  2. From time to time, name one trait that describes your partner in their own right, not how your partner treats you. “Caring” doesn’t work — that’s about how they treat you. “Loves animals” doesn’t either: that’s what matters to your partner, not what this person is like.
  3. Answer for yourself: what do you love this particular person for, rather than anyone else?

How you know it worked: you can describe your partner as a person, not as a list of chores, and your partner makes you curious again.

This hasn’t been tested as an exercise. And admiring someone doesn’t mean closing your eyes to broken promises, pressure, or neglect (chapters 23 and 41).

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: happy couples respond to almost all of each other’s small bids, while those who later divorce respond to about one in three.
In fact: these percentages came from a practice center’s web page, not from a paper where you can see how they were calculated8, 9. The original observation was a small group of newlyweds, and no one set a response rate there. No independent replication of these percentages was found, and predicting divorce without testing on a new group is risky in general. What matters isn’t hitting a percentage but noticing that someone has reached out to you.

People think: there’s an exact ratio of good to bad — “five to one” or some other threshold — that shows whether a couple is thriving or withering.
In fact: “five to one” is a practitioners’ rule about behavior around a fight, not about every hour of life, and no one has proven a common threshold for all couples. Everything depends on what counts as “one interaction” and which couples were studied. And the “exact scientific threshold” of good to bad, which was also written about a lot, has been debunked: flaws were found in its calculations and data10, 11. Good experiences matter, but this doesn’t bring the threshold back, and you shouldn’t make diagnoses by it. If you want to count something, count opportunities for warmth and repair, not points (chapter 17; on falling apart, see chapter 15).

People think: if you speak your partner’s “love language,” the relationship will get better.
In fact: many forms of care help. But a review disputes that there are several separate “primary languages” and a special benefit from matching your partner’s “native” one12. This is still preliminary; it’s consistent with the experiment where holding a hand, stroking, and simply being present during pain didn’t differ in effect. It’s better to ask what kind of care is needed right now.

People think: for a relationship to be good, you have to sleep in the same bed.
In fact: in a lab, healthy young couples slept one night apart and one night together; on the shared night there was slightly more REM sleep (the phase with the most vivid dreams), and fewer interruptions of this phase13. This is a small comparison: couples weren’t randomly assigned to bedrooms for a long period, and it says nothing about how lasting the relationship is. It makes sense to choose your sleeping arrangement based on uninterrupted sleep, alertness during the day, and the closeness you want. There’s no ban on separate beds in the data.

Talk it over together

  1. Which of my small bids to you slip past — and what does “I was heard” look like for you?
  2. When I say something in passing, what do I want: attention right away, later, or just presence? How can I tell you?
  3. What would you have liked to hear “thank you” for over the past week, and what kind of care do you need right now?
  4. How do you like to get my response to your good news? What does it mean to you when I’m sincerely happy for you?
  5. What does a shared bed mean for each of us — and what do we feel when we sleep apart?
  6. If we speak different languages: which of my words or silences might you read differently than I meant? If there are more than two of us: which relationship does this small thing belong to?
  7. What’s good about us right now — not “we don’t fight,” but what exactly makes us happy? When was the last time we had a really good time together?

15. The Fight: What’s Really Dangerous About It

In short

A fight by itself isn’t a verdict. What’s dangerous is the loop: the body revs up, the mind fills in “I’m under threat,” you attack or withdraw, and your partner answers in kind. Sometimes the argument isn’t about the topic: stress from somewhere else spills over, or an old sense of yourself kicks in.

The promise to predict from a single fight whether a couple will break up hasn’t passed independent testing, however confident it sounds. Constant hostility shows up in the body too, but the link with health overall is weak.

What to do: agree on a pause in advance, check that the core of what was said has been heard, and come back to the topic when both of you can talk again. If your fights involve fear, threats, or control, the techniques in this chapter don’t fit — see the section “When it is no longer about skills.”

Vika brings up the vacation again. Her voice rises, her sentences get longer and sharper: “You keep putting it off.” Dina’s answers get shorter and shorter, then she gets up and goes to the sink. Her chest feels tight, her ears are ringing, the words won’t come together, and she knows: if she answers now, she’ll say something nasty. Vika sees her back and hears one thing: she doesn’t care. Then she follows her and asks louder.

Dina walked away so as not to say something nasty. Vika saw indifference in it. That’s how two loops get caught on each other.

What is going on

The body comes first. When an accusation is made, many people’s heart rate goes up, their muscles tense, their attention narrows: the eyes lock onto the threat, nuances get lost. This is a working picture, not a sequence measured in every couple. Two people’s bodies can change in sync during an argument, but the research doesn’t show who’s leading whom.

Then the meaning. The body needs an explanation: “I’m under threat,” “I’m being shamed,” “I don’t count.” It’s useful to read anger as a signal that an expectation has been broken, not as a verdict on your partner. This is a theoretical framework, not a measured share of cases (chapter 16).

Then the action. The meaning picks a move: counterattack or withdrawal. And that move becomes an event for the other person. The one who criticizes sees the retreat and fills in: so they don’t care. The one who retreated hears pressure in the repeated requests and withdraws further. The two loops rev each other up. On overload — chapter 02; on calming down together — chapter 04.

A familiar pairing: one pushes, the other withdraws. The change that’s needed doesn’t happen, urgency and anger grow, the requests get repeated. The other person feels pressure and retreats, and the retreat adds urgency for the one who’s asking. Silence here can mean overload, disagreement, refusal, or seeking safety, and you can’t read the motive from the silence itself. Who pushes depends not only on gender but also on the topic: when the change being discussed is one the other person wants, the roles can switch.

The same argument going around in circles. A topic keeps coming back, for example time together. Each time, the same irritation in the body, the same interpretation (“I come last”), and the same habitual tactic. It helps to distinguish what can be negotiated (a schedule) from a lasting need (how much closeness someone needs). The first gets discussed; with the second, you learn to live. Accepting a difference doesn’t mean putting up with coercion.

The good news: in a comparison of two approaches to therapy for distressed married couples, recurring differences didn’t prevent noticeable improvement, and the emphasis on acceptance worked no worse than the traditional approach several years later. The sample was clinical, made up of married Western couples; it doesn’t prove that acceptance is better.

Not every fight is about what’s being argued over. It helps to gauge its depth, step by step. This sorting is a working one: no one has tested whether it helps.

  • Stress from elsewhere. The trigger is random: traffic, “we should have left earlier.” The load was brought home from work or from family: by a plausible picture, the body is already revved up, and an ordinary phrase sounds like a reproach. An argument like this isn’t worked through but stopped (technique 5).
  • A clash of habits and values. The shared task: name what matters to you, ask what matters to the other person, combine them, accept what’s left over. The popular claim that “most of a couple’s arguments are perpetual” hasn’t been tested: repetition isn’t a verdict yet.
  • An old sense of yourself. The triggers differ, but the feeling is the same: “nobody sees me,” “it’s my fault again.” The feeling is bigger than the trigger, the conversations hit a dead end, and it’s happened in past relationships too. There’s most likely no shared task here: an argument about the dishes is unlikely to help, and each person has their own work to do on themselves (chapter 30).
