What’s known about the first type comes from small diary studies of spouses (newlyweds, families of air traffic controllers): after hard workdays, people more often withdrew at home1 and got angry2, and under heavy stress, a small setback did more damage to their opinion of the relationship3. These are associations in small groups, not trials: it hasn’t been shown that such stress is the main cause of fights for most couples.

The meaning of an argument can differ between the two: in surveys of spouses, two concerns were found behind it — “I’m being blamed and controlled” and “my partner isn’t putting in effort, I’m being neglected”4. It makes sense to ask “what did this mean for each of us,” but whether it helps hasn’t been tested.

Avoidance brings instant relief: say nothing — and the tension drops. The partner reads this as care or as distance. “Arguing” and “avoiding” couples are descriptions of patterns, not inborn types; there are stable couples who don’t tackle every disagreement head-on. Neither “we never fight” nor “we talk everything through to the end” is a rule; the data don’t confirm that couples without fights are the happiest. The test question is a different one: are you able to make important decisions and come back to hurt feelings?

When people stay silent out of fear of conflict. The explanations are reasonable: “I don’t want to ruin the evening,” “not in front of the kid,” “if it happens again, then I’ll say something,” “my partner is going through a hard time.” But what goes unsaid can pile up, and then closeness becomes scary: get closer — and everything bursts out. How often this happens hasn’t been measured. Whether it’s better to speak up right away or later hasn’t been compared, but putting something off until a calm moment and staying silent for years are different things. Fear for your safety is something else; it’s covered in the section “When it is no longer about skills.”

What a fight says about the future. In an observation of married couples, how people argued and how their bodies reacted were linked to whether the couple later broke up. This matters, but the study is small and describes that group, not any future couple. An association isn’t a cause: it doesn’t follow that this particular remark destroyed the relationship. “Criticism, contempt, defensiveness, stonewalling” are handy labels, but they’re links in the same loop, not separately proven mechanisms. If it’s hard to tell what’s happening in your body, the phrase “this is too much for me, I’m going quiet” will say more than the label “I’m a stone wall.”

What a fight does to the body. In an experiment with married couples, participants were given standard wounds on the skin. After a conversation with a disagreement, the wounds healed worse than after a supportive one5. The conditions were assigned by the researchers, so the effect is confirmed in the lab. In couples with consistently high hostility, the wounds healed noticeably more slowly, but this is a difference between groups, not a measure of the harm from any fight. The study doesn’t give a simple chain of “fight → inflammation → slow healing.”

The scale is more modest: the link between marital quality and health overall is weak6, and these data don’t measure the harm of one hostile conversation.

How to tell it is happening

In yourself:

  • Your body has revved up: heat, a lump in your throat, clenched jaw, ringing in your ears, shorter breaths.
  • Your attention has narrowed: the words won’t come together, it’s hard to follow what the other person is saying, you want to interrupt.
  • You’re afraid you’ll say something nasty if you answer right now.
  • A topic keeps coming back with the same irritation, the same “I come last,” and the same tactic: push or withdraw.
  • You have a label ready for your partner: “always putting things off,” “doesn’t care about me.”

In your partner:

  • Their answers get shorter and shorter, they go into another room or fall silent. Or, the other way around, they repeat the request louder and follow you.
  • You can hear defensiveness or contempt in their words: not just disagreement, but putting you down.
  • They’re crying, and it’s unclear what to do about it.
  • Both of you read each other’s behavior as indifference or attack.

Is it worth working on this further — these are questions for yourself, not a tested forecast. On safety, see the section “When it is no longer about skills.”

  • Worrying: the fights are constant, from the very start; talking changes nothing, and the same fight goes around in circles for years; instead of talking about what matters, there’s just letting off steam; there’s almost nothing good.
  • Reassuring: the fights come in phases; after talking, something really changes; the topics vary; even in a fight, you can lean on each other.

What to do

These are techniques, not a tested program. Where the support for them is weak, it says so directly.

1. A pause by agreement

When: agree on it in advance, outside of a fight. Use it when an argument is revving up.

What to do:

  1. Choose a word for the pause and a reason in one sentence: “This is too much for me, I need a pause.”
  2. Say when you’ll come back. The person who’s still overwhelmed has the right to push back the time. There’s no “timer” deadline.
  3. During the pause, do something calming. Don’t rehearse your reply and don’t plan revenge. The data don’t support letting off steam through vigorous “venting”: calming activities reduce anger, while activities that rev you up do next to nothing7. The study wasn’t about couples.
  4. Come back and start with the specific expectation, not with an assessment of character.

If your connection to your body is weak, go by your breathing, your voice, the urge to interrupt, and how hard it is to follow the words.

How you know it worked: you come back without a new explosion and discuss what you were arguing about, rather than avoiding the topic forever.

A pause at home hasn’t been tested as a couples’ technique — only lab studies on short interruptions and cooling down.

2. Checking that the core was heard

When: on a charged topic, before any decisions.

What to do:

  1. One person spends a few minutes describing the situation, without demanding anything.
  2. The other retells the core of it in their own words and asks: “Did I get the core of it right?”
  3. The first person corrects them.
  4. Only then move on to decisions.

If the listener has trouble with their body, it’s worth noticing the urge to object before answering. If your languages differ, you can retell the core and invite corrections. A memorized “I hear you saying…” without getting into the substance doesn’t work.

How you know it worked: both of you say you were understood, and the conversation got as far as the actual request.

In an observation of couples, attentive listening went along with the couple coping better with stress and being more satisfied, but the technique wasn’t assigned to couples, so the effect of the format itself hasn’t been tested. There are no trials for couples with different languages.

3. Body, expectation, request

When: in every fight, whenever you can.

What to do:

  1. Notice one signal in your body: heat, clenched jaw, a lump in your throat.
  2. Name the broken expectation as a guess: “It looks like I’m counting on…”
  3. Ask whether this was agreed on.
  4. Ask for a specific change.
  5. Check how the other person understood what you said.

In a bilingual couple, each person first retells the other’s meaning and only then decides what the issue is: the language, obligations to family, or a broken agreement.

How you know it worked: more and more often, an argument ends with a request that has been put into words and heard.

This is an adaptation: no trial has tested this chain. How to repair a relationship after a fight — chapter 17.

4. When someone cries

When: someone started crying during a fight.

Tears change the loop: one person hears pain, the other hears an accusation or an escape from the topic. In photo experiments, viewers were more willing to support the person crying, and the person crying seemed warmer but less competent. Real fights weren’t filmed in these studies, so neither “tears calm an argument” nor “tears derail a decision” can be called a general rule.

What to do: ask: “Do you need me here, some space, or a touch right now?” Don’t touch without permission. When you both can, come back to the broken expectation (chapter 05).

How you know it worked: the person names what they need, and later you come back to the topic without reproach.

This is a suggestion based on consent, not a tested scheme.

5. When the stress was brought in from outside

When: the day was hard, and on the way home you notice the weight in your body.

What to do:

  1. Say it right at the door: “Hard day, it’s not about you.”
  2. Name what happened, what you’re feeling, and what you want: to be listened to, to get advice, to get practical help, or to be left in quiet.
  3. Your partner retells what they heard. Then say whether that response helped.
  4. If you snapped, apologize, explain: “It’s not about you, it built up” — and start over.

A hard day explains snapping (in newlyweds’ diaries, on such days there was less energy left for a calm response8), but it doesn’t excuse it. Your partner has the right not to take it personally and to say: “We’re not doing that.”

If quiet is needed, it’s worth letting the person be in it without taking offense: in a small observation of air traffic controllers’ families, when they were supported at home, the overloaded ones more often withdrew into themselves and less often got angry1; this is a small observation, not a trial.

How you know it worked: after hard days, there’s a sense of support, and the fights after them are milder.

Couples who cope with stress together are, on average, more satisfied with their relationship9, but this is an association: this particular conversation hasn’t been tested.

6. In your partner’s shoes — with me

When: outside of a fight, especially if an argument keeps going around in circles.

What to do:

  1. Imagine yourself in your partner’s place and answer by going on feelings, not arguments: what about me is hardest to live with? Describe it in detail, for example: “I’m always busy with something, and it’s hard to relax around me.”
  2. Tell your partner about it and ask whether that’s true and how they cope with it.
  3. Decide what to soften or do differently. Offer your partner the chance to go through the same steps.

The goal is to see your own part in the pairing, not to guess the other person’s thoughts: those you only find out by asking (chapter 38).

How you know it worked: there’s less blaming, and each of you names and changes your own part in the pairing.

This exercise hasn’t been tested.

If you come from different cultures

There’s no direct data on fights in couples where one person is Russian-speaking or European and the other is from mainland China. The closest thing is heterosexual student couples in the US, European American and Chinese American: the former showed more positive and less negative behavior in an argument. These are preliminary data: there were no mixed couples, and they don’t say that a Chinese partner is less caring or that a Russian-speaking one is more direct. Ask directly what counts as a request and a refusal between you, what a pause means (respect, overload, disagreement, or avoiding the topic), what role parents play, and which language to argue in. Agree on meanings rather than putting behavior down to nationality (chapter 33).

If there are more than two of you

For open, swinging, polyamorous, and anarchic networks, no tested forecasts of breakups or results of exercises were found. Many studies rely on people who volunteered online, and lab experiments, such as the one with wounds, were done with married or heterosexual couples. Be clear about which couple and which agreement the argument is about, and whose change is being discussed. No relationship structure is the gold standard of health.

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: divorce can be predicted almost for certain from a short conversation.
In fact: the high numbers were obtained on small groups and with models fitted to the same data10: on the same observations, accuracy looks inflated, and when the outcome is rare, few of the “worrying” forecasts turn out right. For a new couple after one conversation, there’s no independent check. It also hasn’t been confirmed that contempt is the best single predictor, that it causes infections, or that a harsh start to a conversation decides a couple’s fate. For couples where one person is from Russia or Europe and the other from mainland China, for same-sex couples, and for open relationships, there’s no tested forecast. A false forecast can frighten people for nothing or push them toward a premature breakup, and how often that happens is unknown.

People think: women demand and men go silent — that’s biology.
In fact: the “she pushes, he withdraws” pattern and the reverse one showed an almost identical moderate link with worse outcomes11. In an observation of married couples, the roles changed along with the topic12. This doesn’t prove that biology plays no part, but the roles can’t be called “inevitable.” For two women or two men, it’s better to name each person’s role in the conversation.

People think: there’s a heart rate above which a person can’t think straight, and a break has to last at least twenty minutes.
In fact: high intensity gets in the way of a conversation, but there’s no single threshold after which a person “stops being able to think”: it’s shifted by a person’s usual heart rate, exercise, illness, and medication. Twenty minutes is popular advice, not a tested minimum. In the lab, even very short interruptions affected aggression in an argument13, but that’s a reason to study short pauses, not a recipe for home; they haven’t been tested as a safety measure. Setting a timer for coming back can be dangerous for the person who’s afraid.

People think: a bad relationship harms your health like smoking does.
In fact: there’s a link, but it’s weak; the data don’t show it’s equal to smoking6. The often-repeated number about better survival refers to strong ties with people in general, not to the harm of a bad marriage14. Chronic conflict is worth taking seriously, without counting “cigarettes a day.”

Talk it over together

  1. What does each of us notice first in our body when an argument revs up, and how much time do we need for the words to come together again?
  2. What does a pause mean for each of us: care, respect, rejection, or running away?
  3. What topic keeps coming back for us? What in it is a request we can negotiate, and what is a difference we’ll have to live with?
  4. Whose changes do we discuss more often, and do the roles of “asks” and “retreats” switch when the topic is different?
  5. How can we check that we understood the core, not just the words?
  6. What do we leave unresolved for the sake of peace, and when do we come back to it?
  7. When we fight over something small, what’s behind it for each of us: “I’m being blamed,” “I’m being neglected,” or just a hard day?

When it is no longer about skills

This chapter is for arguments where both of you can speak freely and without fear. If your relationship has:

  • violence during fights;
  • coercive control: behavior is driven by fear of your partner’s reaction;
  • surveillance or stalking;
  • threats of self-harm,

then the techniques in this chapter don’t fit. A pause under these conditions means going to a safe place without any obligation to come back by a deadline. The silence of someone who is afraid can be a form of protection. A call like “don’t shut down, let’s talk” can increase the danger. Tears don’t prove there’s no danger. Make a safety plan together with a domestic violence specialist. More on this in chapter 23.

If there’s no fear, but jabs, contempt, and outbursts have become the norm, that’s what chapter 41 is about.

16. Anger, Hurt, and Broken Expectations

In short

Anger says: what you expected didn’t happen, and the way to something important is blocked. It is a signal about an obstacle, not a verdict on your partner. It pushes you not away from the problem but toward it1.

“Letting off steam” won’t work: what helps is whatever lowers the intensity, not whatever winds it up.

What to do about it: first cool down, then separate three things — what you expected, what you agreed on, what happened — ask for a specific change, and check how your request was heard. If the conversation isn’t going anywhere, set a time and come back to it. Talk about what you are protecting, not about how bad your partner is; and say goodbye to an expectation that won’t come true here.

Much of this comes from labs and from opposite-sex couples. For open relationships and cross-cultural couples, no direct tests were found.

Anton came back from a trip and expected Sasha to meet him at the station. Sasha figured they would “figure it out on the way home” and stayed at work. Anton stands on the platform with his suitcase and feels his jaw clench and his pace pick up. At home he says: “You don’t care at all.” Sasha answers with a long explanation of why meeting him wasn’t necessary — and Anton hears in it confirmation of what he just said. By evening they are no longer arguing about the station but about who doesn’t respect whom.

What is going on

First, the body. What you expected didn’t happen — and the body gears up: the heart speeds up, the jaw clenches, the pace quickens. There’s an urge to move toward the goal, not away from it. That is how anger is described for emotions in general; it hasn’t been tested on couples specifically.

Then the meaning. Your head quickly fills in the rest: “this is unfair,” “I’m not respected,” “trust has been broken.” Here comes a fork: either a specific request for change, or an attack on the person. “You don’t care at all” is a verdict: all the other person can do is defend against it. “It matters to me to be met after a trip; let’s agree on how it’ll go next time” is a request: it can be answered with “yes,” “no,” or a counteroffer. Either way, anger stays information: something important didn’t happen.

Then the other person’s response. The other person responds to both the form and the content. A defensive explanation, like Sasha’s, hits a different expectation in the first person — to be heard and acknowledged. That’s how one person’s action becomes a trigger for the other, and two loops wind each other up. In a relationship of several people, the loops link into a network: one person’s action hits several people at once. How the body gets overloaded in a fight — chapter 02; how to calm yourself and your partner — chapter 04; if tears are coming — chapter 05.

An expectation is not an agreement. An expectation can be understandable and still not be a shared commitment. Anton had a hope, Sasha had his own reading of the phrase “we’ll figure it out on the way,” and neither of them had an agreement. How much of the anger in couples grows precisely out of unmet expectations, no one has reliably measured, so this is a working frame, not a proven fact. And it doesn’t mean every disappointment has to be swallowed: what matters is telling a hope from an agreement.

What lies under anger. Anger often covers something else. Guilt (“it turned out badly, I need to fix it”) nudges you to make amends. Shame (“something is wrong with me”) nudges you to hide, justify yourself, or attack. Hurt is the feeling that you now mean less to the other person2. These ideas were tested mostly not on couples; whether they help couples make up after a fight hasn’t been tested in couples. Not everyone who feels ashamed wants to attack, and not every “I’m sorry” repairs the harm done. The idea that shame and rage wind each other up in a circle remains a hypothesis; it hasn’t been measured as a cause of divorce.

What anger protects. Under anger there is often something important that you’ve been blocked from: a loved one’s health, time with the kids, the relationship itself. If you name it out loud, the argument can be about what’s valuable rather than about who’s bad. “Your health matters to me, I’m scared for you” instead of “You smoke, you don’t care about anyone”: the complaint is the same, but this one can be answered. And in every “no” there’s a “yes”: “I won’t go on Saturday — it’s our only day with the kids.” Whether naming it out loud helps hasn’t been tested separately.

Hurt you keep carrying. If a complaint goes unanswered, it gets replayed over and over: the anger and the body’s revving up renew themselves, your partner runs into a reaction that seems out of proportion, and the original issue never gets sorted out. This is a hypothesis, not a measured pathway. The fact that the body reacts to a fight doesn’t mean a single hurt will cause heart disease.

An expectation that won’t come true here. Sometimes you’re waiting for something that won’t happen in this relationship: another child, talks about feelings, a youth spent together. “I accept it, but my partner will change” is accepting a dream of change, not what is. It’s more honest to grieve and make decisions based on reality (technique 6). How a couple changes over the years — chapter 40.

Anger is not the only signal, and not a diagnosis. It’s just one of the signals in the overall feedback between partners. Next to it are bids for connection and being happy for the other person (chapter 14), gratitude, withdrawal, hurt. Neither the ratio of good to bad, nor heart rate, nor the role in the couple, nor the style of fighting makes a diagnosis on its own. What actually predicts a breakup — chapter 15.

Roles, not gender. Many of the studies this chapter relies on were done on opposite-sex couples; for same-sex and gender-diverse couples there are no direct tests in this material. It’s more useful to name the role in a specific fight — who is asking, who is pulling away, whose change is needed — rather than “wife” and “husband.”

If you come from different cultures. There is no direct research on couples made up of “a Russian-speaking or European partner and a woman from mainland China.” The closest thing is student couples in the US: in a conversation about a conflict, European American participants showed more positive emotional behavior and less negative behavior than Chinese American participants. But these were students in the US; the study says nothing about your couple. Surveyed therapists who work with couples in China, Taiwan, and Hong Kong point to attention to words for feelings, family roles, and harmony; these are observations from a small group of practitioners, not tested frequencies. Money for relatives, visits, and family decisions may need an explicit agreement, but nationality by itself doesn’t predict any of these preferences: agree on meanings rather than attributing behavior to a country (chapters 33 and 37).

How to tell it is happening

In yourself:

  • Your body is geared up: your heart is racing, your jaw is clenched, your pace is quickening.
  • There’s a ready-made meaning in your head: “unfair,” “not respected,” “trust broken.”
  • You want to attack the person (“you don’t care”) rather than ask for something specific.
  • A complaint went unanswered, and you replay it over and over.
  • Under the anger, something else stirs: “I mean nothing to the other person” or “something is wrong with me.”
  • Being angry is scary: when someone else yells, you freeze; dissatisfaction stays unspoken for years.

In your partner:

  • They answer with a long justification — and you hear in it confirmation of your words.
  • Their reaction seems out of proportion to what caused it.
  • They go silent or walk away.
  • The argument is no longer about the event but about who doesn’t respect whom.

What to do

1. Cool down first, then ask

When: as soon as you notice anger, before discussing anything.

What to do:

  1. Notice that your body is geared up: what has tensed, what has sped up.
  2. Stop mentally judging the guilty party and rehearsing your counterattack.
  3. Do something calm that soothes you until the intensity drops. Research doesn’t name a single length of time; find what works for you.
  4. Come back and name a specific request in one sentence.

How you know it worked: the anger is lower than before the pause; there are no flare-ups of aggression; the heart of the issue got discussed, not shelved forever. Calm shouldn’t turn into a demand that the hurt person go quiet. That lowering the intensity helps was tested on all sorts of people, not on couples.

2. Three lines about an expectation

This is not a tested protocol: this exercise hasn’t been tested on couples.

When: after a significant disappointment, once the intensity has dropped.

What to do:

  1. Notice the signal in your body and write it down. If thinking in words comes more easily to you than noticing sensations, still start with the body signal, and only then argue about whether the hope was fair (how to name a feeling precisely — chapter 03). The benefit of this order hasn’t been tested.
  2. Write three lines: “Expectation: …”, “Agreement (what was said out loud): …”, “What happened: …”. Phrase the expectation as a hypothesis.
  3. Ask yourself: was this said out loud and accepted by both of you? Is it doable in your life — with your schedules, money, distance? Did language or context change the meaning? If you didn’t agree on it, don’t accuse — ask: “Next time it matters to me that…”. If there are several partners, clarify what the boundary is: a shared rule, a request, or an assumption about the other person’s relationship with someone else (chapter 27).
  4. Ask the other person to retell the request in their own words. In a couple with different languages, each person retells the other’s meaning and only then decides what it was: language, an obligation to family, or a broken agreement. In an observational study of opposite-sex couples, those who listened to each other attentively coped better with stress together, but this was observed, not assigned.
  5. A few days later, check: does the same disappointment come back?

How you know it worked: the expectation turns either into an agreement or into a conscious decision to let it go; both of you understand what was asked for.

3. Find what’s under the anger

When: the anger keeps coming back or seems bigger than what caused it.

What to do: ask yourself, then your partner: what event hurt, and what meaning got built on top of it? “Plans changed” and “I don’t mean anything to you anymore” call for different responses. If there’s no precise word for the feeling, start with the impulse: “I want to disappear” or “I want to prove I’m okay” — this is often clearer than a label. With a partner who speaks another language, first clarify the meaning, then argue about the tone. In a relationship of several people, the feeling “I wasn’t invited” doesn’t by itself prove that a rule was broken: what matters is what you actually agreed on.

If the anger is carrying you away, answer these in order, out loud or on paper:

  1. What happened: who said or did what.
  2. What I feel.
  3. What I’m losing and how important it is to me.
  4. How I see myself right now: “nothing depends on me,” “I’m not seen”?
  5. What part of this to tell the other person.

How you know it worked: the conversation moves from “you’re like this” to “this is what hurt me.”

It hasn’t been tested whether reconciliation is helped by sorting through body signals, by these questions, or by separating “an assumption didn’t come true” from “an agreement was broken” — or whether apologizing for a specific action works better than general self-flagellation. On apology and trust — chapter 17.

4. A pause with a promise to come back

When: the fight is escalating and it’s clear it will only get worse.

What to do: say out loud that you’re taking a pause, and name when you’ll come back: “Saturday morning, after breakfast.” Agree in advance on how you each read a pause: for some people it’s care, for others it’s rejection. A single length hasn’t been tested: in a lab model of conflict, even a very short pause affected the level of aggression3, but these experiments don’t give a ready-made recipe, so find the length that suits you. If the other person has gone silent, that’s not necessarily stonewalling: it can be overload or a search for safety, and you can’t read the motive from silence.

How you know it worked: both of you come back to the conversation without escalating.

5. A recurring pattern: change it or accept it

When: the same fight keeps coming back.

What to do:

  1. Together, describe the script: expectation — anger — defense.
  2. Separate what in it can be changed in behavior from the differences you’ll have to accept.
  3. Pick one changeable step for each of you: what exactly you do differently when the script kicks in.
  4. Agree on how you’ll track it: the pattern itself, satisfaction, following through on what you agreed.
  5. Come back to the conversation at the set time and compare notes: what worked, what remained a difference.

How you know it worked: look not at whether the fights have disappeared, but at whether the pattern is changing. For scale: in a trial with distressed couples, years after several months of couple therapy, about half had a noticeably better relationship, and about a quarter of the couples had split up. That is the result of work with a psychologist, not of a home exercise.

6. Say goodbye to an expectation

When: it’s clear that what you want won’t happen here, and the anger or longing keeps coming back. This is not about humiliation and fear: those you don’t need to accept (chapters 23 and 41).

What to do:

  1. Name the fact in the present tense, without “but,” “for now,” “soon”: “My partner doesn’t want another child,” not “they’ll change their mind later.”
  2. Let yourself be sad: “It’s a pity we won’t have this.”
  3. Ask yourself: can I take this into my life — today, not forever? For the sake of what?
  4. If yes — remember what it’s for, and give what matters to you a place outside the couple: in your own pursuits, among friends. If no — admit it: “This won’t be in my life” — and decide from there (chapter 22).
  5. Tell your partner honestly: “Our relationship is precious to me, and today I’m choosing this; I can’t vouch for a whole lifetime” — and stop waiting for your partner to fix everything.

How you know it worked: things get easier, even if the fact is unpleasant; you can see again what is valuable in the relationship.

In the trial from technique 5, acceptance was part of one of the two therapies, and the outcomes of both were similar4: the benefit of acceptance, or of this technique on its own, has not been shown.

7. If being angry is scary

When: your own or someone else’s anger frightens you, and dissatisfaction piles up in silence.

What to do:

  1. Recall old scenes of three kinds: someone was angry at you; someone was angry in front of you; the anger was yours. What did your body remember? Tell yourself: “This is a different situation now.” If the scenes are painful, don’t go through them alone (see “When it is no longer about skills”).
  2. Alone, out loud, in a calm voice, rehearse the boundary phrase “Don’t treat me like that,” and while you do, think not about the person who hurt you but about what you’re protecting.
  3. If a fight drags on, ask yourself every day: how much does this hurt me, what am I losing, for the sake of what am I coping.

How you know it worked: starting a hard conversation isn’t as scary; you manage to name a boundary without yelling and without going silent.

This technique hasn’t been tested. How fear of fighting piles up unspoken things — chapter 15; if jabs and blowups have become the norm — chapter 41.

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: let your anger out and it’ll go away.
In fact: activities that lower the intensity reduce anger, and those that wind you up don’t5. Yelling and going over grievances while thinking about the person to blame seem to train aggressive readiness rather than let it fade. In one lab experiment, hitting a punching bag while thinking about the offender later increased aggression — compared with distraction6. A rush of energy after yelling doesn’t mean the aggression went down.

People think: venting anger means protecting your heart.
In fact: people with persistently high anger and hostility later had more cardiac events7. But this is a link for a stable trait, not the effect of a single outburst; publication bias is also possible. Against anger, what helps is lowering the intensity, not vigorous release. About a single fight and the heart, the data say nothing definite.

People think: never go to bed angry.
In fact: no one has convincingly tested that a fight needs to be resolved before sleep. A forced late-night talk between tired people can backfire, though by how much hasn’t been measured: in a small experiment, sleep deprivation worsened some of the reactions linked to conflict8. It’s wiser to set a safe time and come back to it.

People think: anger destroys a couple, so it’s better not to show it.
In fact: the data are more complicated. In an observational study of newlyweds, direct “negative” behaviors worked differently depending on how serious the problem was, while indirect ones worked worse under any conditions9. This doesn’t mean criticism, threats, or violence are constructive: the outcome depends on how exactly you act and how serious the problem is. The working takeaway: describe the complaint and the change you want without attacking the person. The research looked at early marriages of opposite-sex couples.

People think: a well-matched couple has the same values; if we fight about them, we’re incompatible.
In fact: in established relationships, partners’ actual similarity is only weakly and unreliably linked to how much they like each other; the similarity they themselves perceive is linked noticeably more strongly10. In one study of newlyweds, the spouses’ views largely matched, but the quality of the marriage was more closely tied to similarity in personality11. This doesn’t prove cause and effect. And even similar people have to negotiate in a specific argument.

Talk it over together

  1. What exactly happened, and what did it mean to you?
  2. What was expected, and was it said out loud? What did we actually agree on?
  3. How did my reaction sound to you: like a request or like an accusation?
  4. What helps you cool down, and after how long can you come back to the conversation?
  5. When you go silent — is it overload, protection, or something else?
  6. What’s the difference for us between a request, a wish, and a commitment — in each of our languages?
  7. What does our most frequent argument protect? How do we respect what matters to the other person when we don’t share it?
  8. What might each of us not get here — and what remains valuable to us?

When it is no longer about skills

Everything above is for relationships where you can answer freely. If there is:

  • violence or coercive control in fights;
  • danger in answering freely;
  • silence or agreement out of fear,

the usual couple protocol doesn’t fit. Safety comes before conversation, and “coming back in ten minutes” is not protection: lab pauses were studied as a model of aggression, not as a way to protect yourself. Silence out of fear doesn’t mean anger has been successfully regulated. Asking the person who was harmed to calm the aggressive partner out of shame means putting the responsibility on the wrong person. More in chapter 23.

You don’t have to accept violence and fear: this isn’t a difference you have to live with. If being angry is scary because of the past — beatings, abuse, fights between parents — such memories are better worked through with a professional.

17. Repair After Hurt: Apology, Forgiveness, Trust

In short

After a hurt, what works is not “the right phrase” but whatever changes the other person’s next move. The hurt person’s main question is “Will this happen again?” An apology answers it only if it carries new information.

Apology, forgiveness, and trust are three different things, and one doesn’t guarantee another. And forgiveness after which the other person changes nothing can damage the self-respect of the one who forgave.

What to do: apologize in parts — what happened, how it hit the other person, whose responsibility it is, what will change — and don’t ask for forgiveness in the same breath. Judge the repair by what happens next, not by your intentions.

If the hurts come with aggression or control, this chapter is not the answer: see the section “When it is no longer about skills” and chapter 23.

Vera promised to pick up Katya’s documents from the copy center and forgot. Vera can tell that Katya is upset from a single word in the messenger: “Got it.” In the evening Vera starts explaining why it happened: a hard day, a mixed-up schedule — and with every sentence Katya’s answers get shorter. Vera hears an excuse in her own words before Katya gets a chance to call it that, and she falls silent. By night they both lie with their backs to each other, and each one waits for the other to speak first.

What is going on

First, the body. When someone lets you down or answers sharply, the body switches on before words do: the heart speeds up, breathing gets shorter, the face muscles tense, your gaze narrows. At that moment you hear worse. In one experiment, people were asked to imagine someone who had hurt them: sometimes with thoughts of revenge, sometimes with thoughts of forgiveness. With vengeful thoughts, the body and the person’s sense of well-being responded worse. A caveat: the experiment was short; it shows the body’s response to a thought, not health over the years, and not whether it’s safe to go back to the person who hurt you.

Then the meaning. The main question after a blow is “will this happen again?” That’s why “I had a hard day” easily becomes a new threat: the hurt person hears not information but a brush-off.

Then the action, and it is an event for the other person. Pull away, answer sharply, go silent — and now the first person has something to react to. The loops wind each other up (on fights — chapter 15).

A repair attempt is a move that should break the chain: acknowledge what hurt; take back an unfair accusation; offer a pause. Whether it works depends on what the other person heard and what you do next: it’s not your own assessment of your sincerity but the next move that separates real soothing from a phrase that only sounds conciliatory. In the scene, repair would not be a story about a hard day, but: “I forgot, and you had to wait for nothing. Tomorrow I’ll pick them up before lunch and text you when they’re ready.” This is a description of a process, not a tested clinical formula.

Perfection isn’t needed. In observations of mothers and infants, what turned out to matter more was not the absence of glitches but the fact that they get noticed and the two tune back in to each other. For adult couples this is only an analogy, but it changes the question: not “how do we never fight” but “what do we do after a misunderstanding.” From psychotherapy: when a rupture with the therapist gets repaired, the outcome of treatment is usually better. The link is moderate, and it’s not a couple of partners, so it doesn’t prove that “one apology will keep you from divorce.”

Trust works the same way. A broken promise is also an event: the body notices that it’s unclear what to expect, the meaning shifts toward “unreliable,” and checking up and pulling away become more likely. And checking up is again an event, now for the person being checked. Trust is broken down into predictability, dependability, and faith that things will stay that way; it is most visible where someone could have chosen their own gain and chose the partner’s interest instead. These are ways of describing trust, not experiments in which some step brought it back.

Forgiveness and self-respect. Forgiveness can take away the desire for revenge. But if the person who hurt you doesn’t admit guilt and keeps doing the same thing, your own yielding can start to feel like losing yourself. In a multi-year study of newlyweds and in experiments, the link between forgiveness and self-respect depended on whether the partner made amends and behaved kindly: if they did, self-respect held; if they didn’t, it was undermined1. These are data on newlyweds, couples of a man and a woman; it hasn’t been shown for other unions. So the question isn’t “whether to forgive at all” but what the other person does: forgiveness doesn’t cancel boundaries (technique 3).

If you come from different cultures. Among Chinese newlyweds, warm and moderately restrained communication turned out better, by the couples’ own ratings, than chronic conflict2. This is one type of couple. The study warns against “restrained means doesn’t love,” but it doesn’t say what a particular person feels, and it doesn’t set a norm for a partner from Russia or Europe. How an apology, a pause, a promise said out loud, and the involvement of relatives are read in a “China — Russia/Europe” couple, there are no direct data. So what’s needed is an agreement, not a method: let each of you say in your own words what an apology, a pause, a firm commitment, and telling family about what happened mean, and write down what both of you understood the promise to be. Don’t assume anyone is “indirect by nature” or “direct by nature” (chapters 33 and 38).

If there are more than two of you. The form of the relationship and the breaking of an agreement are different things: if an agreement broke, what’s at the center is the agreement itself and the consent of the people it concerned, not the fact that the relationship is open. “Fidelity” there may be about disclosure, safer sex, time, or consent, rather than exclusivity. Say out loud: who agreed to what, whose private life the apology touches, what keeping the promise will look like and in front of whom. There are no direct tests of apologies, forgiveness, or this chapter’s exercises in such unions, and data from opposite-sex couples can’t be passed off as data on non-monogamy (chapters 27 and 29).

How to tell it is happening

In yourself:

  • After a mistake you immediately want to explain why it happened — before saying how it hit the other person.
  • “But” and “if you were hurt” show up in your apology.
  • After a broken promise you’re pulled toward checking up and pulling away.
  • You’ve said you forgive, but the hurt comes back: you replay it again, you feel uneasy.
  • You forgive the same thing again and again, and you respect yourself less and less afterward.

In your partner:

  • Their answers get shorter; they pull away or go silent while you explain.
  • They check whether you keep your word.
  • Each of you waits for the other to speak first.
  • They expect forgiveness but don’t admit guilt and keep behaving the same way.

What to do

1. Body first, then explanations

When: right after a sharp remark or a forgotten promise, while you want to justify yourself or fire off a message right away. Especially if explaining is easier for you than feeling.

What to do: notice a sign in your body: your breathing, tension, the urge to answer immediately. Only then sort out what happened. If you need a pause, name it: “I need to catch my breath; I’m not walking away.” A pause and a restrained answer can also be repair, but they get read differently. Ask each other: “How will each of us know the misunderstanding has really been repaired?” Agreeing on what was heard and what will change matters more than demanding that it feel good right away.

How you know it worked: your response is different from your first impulse, and the pause isn’t read as walking away. The “body first” order is a suggestion for self-observation, not a tested method (chapters 02 and 04).

2. A four-part apology

When: once the first wave has passed and it’s clear the other person was hurt.

What to do:

  • Say what happened: specifically, without “if you were hurt.”
  • Take responsibility: without “but” and without an explanation that sounds like an excuse.
  • Name how it affected the other person, and ask whether that’s right.
  • Offer a doable fix that can be seen.

The question “is that right?” is there so the person feels understood: when that feeling is present, fights affect relationship satisfaction less3.

Don’t ask for forgiveness in the same breath: “forgive me” without these parts shifts the work onto the hurt person, who now also has to decide whether to forgive. There’s no tested single formula for different languages, cultures, and forms of union, so ask: “What would real repair be for you?”

How you know it worked: a few days later, ask whether the person felt that responsibility had been taken and that what was promised actually happened. At the same time, the person isn’t obliged to accept the apology.

These parts come from lab experiments with hypothetical offenses4; they haven’t been tested as a tool for couples. In other lab experiments, the apology came out better when the “offender” first boosted their own sense of self-worth and defended their self-image less. This hasn’t been tested in marriages over years.

3. Separate forgiveness, contact, and trust

When: when you’ve been hurt and people are rushing you to “forgive and forget.” Or when you’re waiting for forgiveness from the other person.

What to do: keep three things separate: an inner decision not to take revenge and not to store up bitterness (the return of warm feelings is something else); a new agreement about contact; evidence that the other person’s behavior has changed. You can choose the first without giving the second and third: forgiveness by itself doesn’t mean the danger is over, trust is back, or the relationship will go on. And the other way around: you can stay without having forgiven yet, and keep working through the pain. Out loud: “I don’t want to take revenge on you, but until I see changes, I’ll keep my distance.” The decision to forgive stays with the person who was hurt. On infidelity — chapter 21; on the choice to “stay or leave” — chapter 22.

For trust to come back, by the very logic of the concepts, you need an accurate account of what was broken, proportionate amends, and reliable behavior in places where there is again a real chance to break it. Surveillance or a forced confession is not the same as voluntary reliability. No one can guarantee “this will never happen again.” After an ordinary disappointment, trust is more an agreement to build the relationship knowing the person is capable of doing it again, plus each person’s decision to do everything they can. This is a conclusion drawn from the concepts, not a result of trials.

To understand what exactly was broken, tell apart a promise, an explicit shared rule, and something one of you only assumed: anger most often signals a broken expectation (chapter 16). You can keep notes: the event; the signal in your body; the behavior you expected; whether it was explicitly agreed on; your request; your partner’s response; how you were the next day. Sharing the notes is up to you, not a form of access to devices. The technique is practical but not tested. Also untested, each on its own: “observation, feeling, need, request,” a gratitude journal, “repair phrases,” a body check-in.

How you know it worked: you can name which of the three things are already there and which aren’t yet. You judge trust by behavior that repeats and that can be checked voluntarily, not by a promise.

4. Forgiveness from the inside: start with a small hurt

When: a hurt won’t go away, even though not much happened: someone promised and didn’t follow through, or answered in a sharp tone. Start with something like that, small and recent, not with infidelity or old family wounds. And only when you have the strength: there’s no crisis, you feel calm and safe, and you can stop at any moment.

What to do: in writing or in your head.

  1. Describe what happened in dry facts: “Vera promised to pick up the documents and didn’t.” No guesses about why the other person did it or what they were thinking.
  2. Ask yourself what it cost you: time, energy, trust, support, a sense of your own importance. Behind a hurt there’s usually a loss, even in small things.
  3. Choose the most important loss. Remember how things were before it, and let the feeling — anger, sadness — run its full course without rushing it. If there are too many feelings, take one, for example just the anger.
  4. Notice how your place is experienced here: you’re not valued, not noticed, treated as if you count for nothing. That is painful and unfair.
  5. Give yourself some sympathy: it’s a pity it turned out this way and that you had to feel like that.
  6. Finish the sentence “The right thing for me would be…”: to say something about it, to let it go, to never again ask this person for something like this.
  7. Imagine doing exactly that, and check: did a calm “yes” appear, a feeling that this is the right way to act? If not, look for another option.

You can’t force yourself to forgive: forgiveness comes at the end of this kind of work, and sometimes it doesn’t come at all. The exercise is for a hurt that’s already behind you: if the harm is still going on, it is not the answer (see the section “When it is no longer about skills”).

How you know it worked: things get easier, and you can come back to the situation without a new wave of anger and the urge to prove something. This is a working order of steps, not a tested program.

5. The view of a neutral third party

When: when the fight has already been talked through, but it still gets to you.

What to do: recall a real disagreement. For about twenty minutes, as the study participants did, write how a neutral person who wishes you both well would describe it. Then, separately, divide it up: what was the event, and what was the worst possible interpretation.

How you know it worked: before you start and a few months later, write down how hard your fights are and how you rate the relationship. In a trial with married couples, for those who did the writing, the usual decline in marital quality over that year stopped5. How strong the effect was and how often to repeat the exercise at home isn’t stated in the description. Stop if the reappraisal starts to erase the question of safety: this is not a way to justify violence.

6. A relationship check-in in calm times

When: not in the heat of a fight, but ahead of time.

What to do: once a year, go through a structured checkup: each of you rates the relationship separately, then comes feedback and a conversation. In a trial with married couples, a checkup like this improved intimacy; on the other outcomes the picture was mixed. A homemade questionnaire is not what was tested.

Instead of a questionnaire, you can simply talk through some questions: this is a prompt for conversation, not an evaluation. What do you actually rely on in each other, and who gives and takes how much? When was the last moment of intimacy — exactly when? What feelings and states can’t you bring into this relationship? What is your partner like on their own, outside their relationship with you? What are we together for — and at what point in the day does that happen?

How you know it worked: each of you, separately, rates tension, intimacy, and acceptance as better. Where being open could bring revenge, don’t share your answers with each other online, at a distance. This is a supplement, not an answer to aggression or broken agreements.

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: it’s enough to say “I’m sorry” sincerely.
In fact: in experiments with hypothetical offenses, the parts of an apology that were rated included not only regret but also responsibility, an offer to fix things, and a promise to change4. These were not experiments on long-term couples. Sincerity matters, but one “I’m sorry” won’t do it. The link between apology and forgiveness is moderate overall — an average across different situations, not a recipe.

People think: forgiving is always good for the relationship.
In fact: among newlyweds, the link between forgiveness and later marital satisfaction depended on the partner’s behavior: with frequent negative talk, forgiveness was not a reliable protection6. In another study, spouses’ tendency to forgive predicted continued aggression, psychological and physical7. These are links based on self-reports: they don’t show that forgiveness “breeds” aggression, and they don’t say how often harm is possible here. The takeaway: allow for forgiveness without reconciliation, and watch whether the harmful behavior stops.

People think: once trust is broken, it can’t be restored.
In fact: science hasn’t tested this: that would take a definition, a measurement, and long “before and after” observation. A small, selected group of couples who went through therapy after infidelity doesn’t tell us whether trust always returns to its former level. Interviews with people who went through the discovery of infidelity describe stages: an “emotional roller coaster,” then a “moratorium” — and only after that do the partner’s efforts to rebuild trust start to be noticed; how many participants there were is unknown.

People think: if a couples program has trials behind it, it will save the relationship.
In fact: a large skills program for unmarried parents produced no overall favorable effects on relationships8. A few years after therapy for spouses with severe, long-standing distress, about half the couples showed clinical improvement, and about a quarter had separated or divorced. A program can be a support, but judge the result by your own measures: each person’s satisfaction, behavior, safety.

Talk it over together

  1. What would real repair after a hurt be for you: words, an action, time, a pause?
  2. How will we know a misunderstanding has been repaired, and hasn’t just gone quiet?
  3. What in my apology sounds like an excuse, and what sounds like taking responsibility?
  4. What happens to you in the first minutes after a sharp remark, and what do you need then: a pause, words, distance?
  5. What does forgiveness mean for each of us: to stop being angry, to trust again, to go back to how things were? Which of these is possible now?
  6. What exactly was promised, agreed on, or only assumed — and which of us did it concern?
  7. What does trusting again after a disappointment mean to you: being sure it won’t happen again — or knowing that we both do everything we can?

When it is no longer about skills

Everything above assumes the harm isn’t ongoing. Signs that this is not about skills:

  • psychological or physical aggression keeps repeating despite apologies and forgiveness;
  • one partner coerces, controls, monitors the other’s devices;
  • there are grounds to expect revenge for being open.

In that case, exercises in joint disclosure and “reappraisal” are dangerous: they can erase the question of safety. Talk about safety separately with each person, not in front of the partner. Coercive control calls for an individual assessment, not guesses about whose gender is “more dangerous”9, 10. No one is obliged to forgive, go back, or trust. More in chapter 23; on when professional help is needed — chapter 35.

If it doesn’t go as far as violence and control, but time after time you have to forgive jabs, contempt, or blowups, and nothing changes — that is what chapter 41 is about.

18. Desire Over the Years

In short

Desire in a long-term couple changes on average, but “fading” is not a law: the decline doesn’t happen to everyone, and not forever. One possible reason for the decline is not closeness itself but a role that has set in: one partner has become the other’s caretaker. For now this is only a link, and it has been tested only in some couples.

Don’t read the length of the relationship or how often you have sex as a verdict: break it down into parts — interest, pleasure, tiredness, and what you think about all of it.

What to do: invite in a way that makes “no” cost nothing, try new things together, and check whether both of you liked it. If “yes” is said out of fear, it’s no longer about desire — see the section “When it is no longer about skills.”

Katya sits down next to Yulia and puts a hand on her knee. Yulia flinches: her shoulders feel like lead, her head is buzzing, her body is asking for quiet. But within a second another meaning builds itself: “Again. Now I have to.” Katya feels the leg go still and builds her own meaning: “I’m not wanted anymore.” She takes her hand away, says “fine, got it,” and goes to wash the dishes. Yulia hears a reproach in that. A few days later both of them steer clear of the couch, and each is sure she’s protecting the other.

No one meant to hurt anyone. Each of them had her own loop kick in, and the loops caught on each other.

What is going on

First, the body. Desire is not a button and not a single hormone. It’s affected all at once by tiredness, pain, sleep, medications, the phase of the cycle, stress, and how your body responds to the specific person beside you. So look at age, pain, medications, and sleep separately rather than writing everything off as “we’ve cooled off.” Antidepressants, for example, often change desire, orgasm, and ejaculation1. Chronic pain with touch in the vulvar area is not rare either.

Hormones have an influence, but they don’t decide for you. They can change how you feel, your sleep, and your desire, but they don’t choose your partner. A severe premenstrual disorder is a real diagnosis, but it affects a small share of women; ordinary arguments on different days of the cycle don’t make it a hormonal illness.

A calm body. Wanted touch changes the body’s response to stress, but a “love hormone” role here hasn’t been shown. Among the studies reviewed, there are none in which couples had their heart rate, breathing, and desire measured at the same time, so “a calmer body means more desire” is, for now, the logic of the loop, not a proven result. On arousal and “flooding” — chapter 02; on the physiology of arousal and orgasm — our book “Alchemy Without Magic.”

Feeling yourself or judging yourself. During intimacy, attention easily slides into evaluating: how do I look, am I doing everything right, what are they thinking of me. The body tenses, there’s no room left to explore, and your partner easily reads this as “doesn’t want to.” That such thoughts get in the way is plausible: they were studied in women2. How much they get in the way hasn’t been established. At the same time, paying attention to yourself comes in different kinds: watching yourself from the outside is one thing, noticing warmth, breathing, and touch is another. In women with low desire who went through a group program of mindfulness and education, desire grew along with attention to the body3. A working distinction: being sexy means being relaxed and alive in your own body, not following someone else’s picture of seductiveness.

Then the meaning — and the action. The same tension can be read as “I’m out of strength” or as “I have to hold my defenses.” A partner’s silence — as “things are hard right now” or as “I’m not wanted anymore.” What you do depends on the meaning: you move away, push, joke, ask. And your action becomes a new trigger for the other person, and the loops lock together. If the two of you need different amounts of closeness, you can easily end up in a cycle of “request — refusal — another request”: one reads the refusal as rejection, the other reads the repeated requests as pressure. The scheme is plausible, but it hasn’t been tested as a stable trait of people. Tears during intimacy are a separate body signal (chapter 05).

Fading is not a law. Separate the average curve for a group from the fate of a particular couple. A survey of students in steady relationships shows: the longer they’d been together, the less often they had sex and the lower their satisfaction, while desire was lower only among women. But this is a snapshot: it compares different people rather than following one couple over time, and “every year takes away desire” doesn’t follow from it. How a couple changes over the years in general — chapter 40.

Warm-up and the gap. The familiar picture is “you felt desire, so it started.” But it can go differently: a warm touch first brings neutral or pleasant sensations, you appraise them as “I like this” — and only then does interest show up. If the same invitation sounds like “you’re expected to want this,” wariness and avoidance kick in. This picture is built around women’s experience; that everyone goes through exactly this chain hasn’t been shown. And a desire gap — when partners want different amounts of sex — is not a diagnosis in itself, and there’s no single percentage of such couples: definitions differ.

“Yes” out of care and “yes” out of duty. Caring about your partner’s sexual needs and forgetting about your own are different things: the first went along with more satisfied partners. Giving in “to get closer” went with better outcomes, giving in “to avoid something bad” with worse ones. Exaggerating your desire went with lower sexual satisfaction, especially on days when desire was low; hiding that you don’t feel like it didn’t turn out to be clearly harmful — sometimes the partner was even more satisfied with the relationship. Both “any holding back does harm right away” and “faking it is harmless” are wrong. A motive doesn’t replace consent: “I want us both to feel good” and “I have to give in so they won’t be hurt” look the same from the outside.

Closeness and distance. “Distance feeds eroticism” is a good question about personal space, but not a proven law. Responsiveness and autonomy are different things, not two ends of one scale (chapter 19). A partner’s responsiveness went along with growing desire. In long-term couples, a rise in intimacy over the course of a day predicted stronger passion and more satisfying sex4. But these are diary-based links, and no one was assigned long-term distance. “Tell me everything” can come across as surveillance — this is a mechanism, not a measured result. If your head feels close but your body pulls away, write those observations down separately. And if distance does help, it’s not through mystery and unavailability, but through each of you having your own life and being interested in the other as a separate person. This is a working idea; it hasn’t been tested.

When one partner has become the caretaker. Sometimes one person gradually takes on everything: planning, cleaning, reminding, calming the other down and solving their problems. Tiredness builds up, and a meaning takes shape: “I’ve got one more dependent, not an equal partner.” Desire retreats. The other person feels rejected and asks for attention more insistently — and the load gets even heavier.

One link shows up in surveys of women with children who are partnered with men: the larger the share of housework a woman carried, the less she desired her partner, and the link partly ran through her seeing her partner as a dependent5. This is a link, not a proven cause; the whole chain hasn’t been traced, and no such data were found for other couples. This doesn’t cancel care: responsiveness, as mentioned above, went along with greater desire — apparently through the sense that you are someone special to your partner and that your partner seems more valuable6. So what’s worth working on is unequal responsibility, not tenderness.

If your couple isn’t the kind that gets studied. Almost everything above is about opposite-sex couples, more often newlyweds and students. Desire trajectories for same-sex couples, open unions, and “China — Europe” couples aren’t in the data reviewed. There are few studies on non-monogamy, mostly from North America. A pooled analysis found no noticeable difference in sexual satisfaction between monogamous and non-monogamous people7, but it lumps together different forms of union and says nothing about each one separately. There are no tested protocols for networks, so work through desire and agreements for each pair separately (chapters 27 and 29).

For cross-cultural couples there’s little data too. In Brisbane, couples with a Chinese woman went into conflict topics less often, but talked about them in a way similar to Western couples. However, language and culture can’t be separated there, and this isn’t a result for mainland China, nor the experience of “Russian-speaking or European man — Chinese woman” couples. In a small experiment, Chinese couples who did online step-by-step touch exercises improved their scores compared with a waitlist; by how much, the available description doesn’t say. How family, scheduling intimacy, and shared money affect such couples is unknown. Instead of rules by nationality, you’re left with questions: in which language can you talk precisely about the body and desire, what role does each of you want for your family, what does “honest” mean to each of you (chapters 33 and 37).

How to tell it is happening

In yourself:

  • Your body answers before your thoughts do: your shoulders are heavy, your head is buzzing, you want quiet.
  • A meaning builds itself right away: “again,” “now I have to,” or “I’m not wanted anymore.”
  • Tiredness, pain, or medications get written off as “we’ve cooled off” and “the love is gone.”
  • You say “yes” so they won’t be hurt, not because you want to.
  • After a refusal, anger rises.
  • You feel like your partner’s parent: you plan, remind, manage their moods.
  • During intimacy you watch yourself from the outside: how do I look, is everything right.

In your partner:

  • They flinch or freeze at an invitation.
  • They take their hand away, say “fine, got it,” and go off to do chores.
  • A cycle of “request — refusal — another request” is running: one chases, the other retreats.

What to do

1. The “pleasant — neutral — unpleasant” pause

When: when you want to suggest intimacy or when it’s being suggested to you.

What to do: the invitation is warm, with a clear right to say “no.” Pause for a minute or two: find what’s there in your body — warmth, tension, breathing, heartbeat. Name it in one word: pleasant, neutral, or unpleasant. Answer: “I want to,” “neutral for now, let’s try something gentle and see,” or “no, thanks” — any answer is accepted. With “unpleasant,” stop, no explanations needed.

How you know it worked: once a week, for five minutes, check in together: did you want to, was it pleasant, was there pressure. It’s a good sign if “no” can be said without fear, and moving from “neutral” to “pleasant” happens by your own choice. If the exercise turns into squeezing out consent, stop.

This is a suggested way to observe the body, not a validated test. What was tested was the whole group program (mindfulness, thinking techniques, education): it improved desire in women with low interest, but the role of any single exercise is unknown.

2. Check your motive — and invite, don’t demand

When: when you’re about to agree but there’s no “I want to” inside. Or when you’re the one inviting.

What to do: ask yourself: “Would this be my choice if saying no cost nothing?” If yes, it’s your choice. If not, it’s giving in “so they won’t be hurt,” not desire. When you say no, say “no” to sex, not to the person — and “yes” to what you value: “Not tonight, I have no energy. Let’s lie in bed together tomorrow morning.” This is advice, not a tested technique. For the one who’s inviting: if interest hasn’t shown up, stop. If anger rises after a refusal, that’s a reason to look at your own expectation — what exactly you expected and whether it had been discussed (chapter 16) — not proof that your partner is obliged to give in.

How you know it worked: both “yes” and “no” come out honest, without fear of consequences.

3. A conversation about desire: four questions and three words

When: once every week or two, for twenty to thirty minutes, not in bed and not right after a refusal.

What to do: four questions, each one on its own: is there interest, is there pleasure, is there tiredness, what do I think about it. Three words sort out expectations: a wish (“I’d like it to be like this”), an agreement (“we settled on this”), and an entitlement (“I’m owed this”). Each of you answers the four questions about the last two weeks, names one wish, and says what part of it is already an agreement. You decide what to do when one of you has no interest. And you agree on a no-obligation evening — time together with no obligation to have sex and with the right to stop.

How you know it worked: after a month, there’s less pressure and it’s clearer what each of you wants.

The script hasn’t been tested as a protocol, and neither has scheduling: both “scheduling kills passion” and “scheduling saves passion” remain guesses. If the conversation starts with anger, bring the intensity down first — that makes it easier to come back to the expectation instead of pushing through the other person’s refusal.

4. Something new together, chosen by both

When: when both of you want something fresh, not when one of you is pushing.

What to do: each of you separately writes down two or three optional activities you haven’t done before and that don’t scare you. You pick one that both of you agree to. About an hour and a half a week, for ten weeks or for as long as you both want. After each time, both of you give two ratings from 0 to 10 — “how it was for me” and “how I feel in the relationship.” Nothing frightening, nothing imposed, and no surprises without consent — a jolt is easily read as stress.

How you know it worked: the ratings hold or go up for both of you, not just for the one who started it. If they drop for one of you, change the activity.

The evidence is modest: in an experiment where couples were assigned by lot, couples doing “exciting” activities became more satisfied with their marriage than couples doing “pleasant” ones, but compared with couples who did no special activities, the gain wasn’t significant. The available description says nothing about desire — this is a possibility, not a recipe.

5. Different roles, not just one

When: one of you more and more often feels like the other’s parent or caretaker.

What to do:

  1. Together, write down who plans what, who does it, who reminds, and who calms whom down. Thinking ahead is work too.
  2. Hand over one area entirely — both the thinking-through and the doing, not “help me” (chapter 20).
  3. Spend time in other roles too: “two kids” — goofing around, dancing, watching cartoons; two adults — calmly agreeing on something practical. Taking care of each other is fine too, as long as that role isn’t the only one.

How you know it worked: no one feels permanently like the older one or the younger one, and there’s time when you’re simply the two of you, not busy with household stuff.

Whether this brings desire back, no one has tested. And it’s not a reason to deny care to someone who is ill: how necessary care during illness or disability differs from a role that has set in hasn’t been studied.

6. A slow ladder: first for yourself, then together

When: desire has been gone for a long time, and you want to bring it back without sudden leaps.

What to do: one step at a time, about a week for each. The first two are for yourself; the rest are together, and only while it’s pleasant for both of you. Don’t start with toys or sharp changes.

  1. Sleep and energy. While you’re running on empty, don’t go further.
  2. Pleasure without eroticism. In the evening, recall a few vivid impressions for each sense: sight, hearing, smell, taste, touch. Notice, don’t evaluate.
  3. For a few minutes a day, watch your partner busy with their own thing — with no goal, as a separate person.
  4. Conversations and activities that aren’t about the household: a movie, a walk, cooking for the fun of it.
  5. Touch without eroticism: hugging, stroking, lingering close.
  6. Flirting: jokes, messages, playful touches. Then — something new (technique 4).

How you know it worked: a step comes easily, and you want the next one. If it gets tense, go back a step. If the ladder has turned into an obligation to “feel the right way,” stop.

Neither the order of the steps nor the benefit of admiring your partner has been tested. Similar touch without a goal is used in sex therapy, but it has only been tested together with other techniques8.

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: desire in long relationships inevitably dies.
In fact: an average decline does happen, but not for everyone and not forever. Among newlyweds, wives’ desire fell more steeply, while husbands’ desire on average didn’t fall (part of the drop was explained by childbirth)9 — but that’s an average across groups, not a property of women; in marriages that were followed up many times, the declines in satisfaction and frequency of sex flattened out over time10; in a large online survey, the types of boredom and desire didn’t depend on how long people had been together11 (also a snapshot). And the thought “desire is gone, so love is over” adds pressure and resignation, while the data don’t support that conclusion.

People think: healthy desire comes on its own, and if you need warming up, something is wrong.
In fact: desire that comes after pleasant touch is not a sign of illness but one of the possible paths. Consent under pressure is a different matter: among women with low interest in sex, caring for the partner while forgetting their own needs went along with greater sexual distress (a clinical sample, not a frequency in the population).

People think: the more sex, the happier the couple.
In fact: couples were told to double their usual frequency of sex for a few months. They didn’t get happier: there was less enjoyment, and mood shifted toward the negative12. Caveats: not everyone wanted to take part, the groups differed slightly from the start, and the instruction “do it more often” may itself have worked as pressure. The guide is wanting it, pleasure, and consent — not a tally.

Talk it over together

  1. What’s closest to you right now: interest, pleasure, tiredness, or something else?
  2. When I suggest intimacy, what do you notice in your body in the first second, and what do you tell yourself about it?
  3. What counts as a wish for you, what counts as an agreement, and what do you consider owed to you even though we haven’t discussed it?
  4. How should I say no so that it doesn’t sound like “you’re not wanted anymore”?
  5. What new activity together would both of us want, and what about it needs to feel safe?
  6. What do we do when one of us has no interest and the other does — and in which language is it easier for us to talk about it?
  7. Which role are we in most often with each other — caretaker, dependent, two kids, two adults — and which one are we missing?

When it is no longer about skills

Everything said above is about free choice. If there are signs in your couple that there is no choice, this is no longer about optimizing desire but a question of safety:

  • fear of refusing sex because of the consequences;
  • no access to your own money;
  • threats;
  • violence;
  • coercive control and surveillance.

In that case, don’t use this chapter’s exercises, don’t “warm up” for the sake of consent, and don’t lean on average data about satisfaction to justify giving in. Don’t merge accounts if your partner restricts your access to money or makes threats. Safety and freedom of choice come first; more on this in chapter 23. If your life is in danger, call your country’s emergency services.

19. Closeness and Autonomy

In short

Closeness and independence in a couple are not enemies, but they don’t carry the same weight: satisfaction with the relationship is much more strongly linked to closeness than to independence.

This doesn’t make the “I” unnecessary: according to some of the data, with more independence, closeness more often led to saying things out loud rather than backing off; how big the difference is, these data don’t say.

What to do: ask for a pause together with a time to come back, separate “I want” from “we agreed,” listen for what stands behind “we,” and choose new things together and voluntarily.

Vera comes home from work and wants quiet: headphones, the kitchen, no decisions. Nika has been waiting a long time for this evening, to have dinner together and talk about the trip. “I need some time alone,” Vera says. Nika hears: “You’re a burden to me.” She goes into the other room and closes the door a little louder than she meant to. Vera hears a reproach in the slam, and the quiet she wanted turns into a tense wait for a fight.

Neither of them wanted an evening like this.

What is going on

First, a caveat about the body. The studies this chapter rests on measured what people report about themselves, their diaries, and their conversations. Heart rate, breathing, and hormones weren’t measured in these studies, so everything about the body below is a guess, not a result.

The body. Vera has tiredness and a need for quiet. Nika has the tension of a dashed expectation: the evening together didn’t happen, even though she’d been counting on it. If you’re not good at telling body signals apart, it’s easy to mistake tiredness for “I’m not loved anymore,” and a missed connection for intent (chapter 02; on calming down together — chapter 04).

Meaning and action. A request for quiet can be read as “I need to recharge,” or as “you’re a burden to me.” “Let’s decide together” — as “I’ve been taken into account” or as “it’s as if my wishes don’t exist.” According to one explanation, the more you expect your partner to provide both companionship and support for your growth, the bigger any missed connection can seem. Then comes a choice: say it out loud or back off. Backing off is read as abandonment, and a cold response as confirmation. Anger here is most often a signal of a dashed expectation, not a verdict on your partner (chapter 16). If tears come — from hurt, or from relief when one of you comes back — see chapter 05.

Two needs, not one. Satisfaction with the relationship is strongly linked to how well the need for closeness is met, and weakly to how well the need for independence is met1. These are links, not causes, and a weak link doesn’t mean independence isn’t needed. Relying on another person and having a will of your own don’t get in each other’s way: a partner who calmly accepts the other’s dependence and is sensitive when the other is struggling helps them become more independent2. And the more your reasons for being together feel like “your own” (“I want to,” not “I have to”), the less defensiveness there is in an argument.

There’s no record to set in closeness. In a survey of couples in Germany, too little closeness turned out to be noticeably worse for the relationship than too much3. But this measured how much a person feels their partner to be part of themselves; whether complete merging is good, it doesn’t say.
