Imbalance in either direction makes itself felt: that day the person is less satisfied with life, the next day they invest in whatever they shortchanged, and six months later such people have lower well-being on average4. For those who strongly lean toward independence and achievement, the benefit of closeness is weaker. You can’t always squeeze out the maximum for yourself and the maximum for the two of you at the same time: it’s a seesaw, not a race for a record.

Staying yourself up close. In family therapy, this is the name for the ability to hold on to your own opinions and feelings next to someone close, without cutting off contact and without dissolving. In couples from Spain and the US who were followed over time, the higher this ability, the better the relationship and the less anxious attachment — that is, constant worry that the loved one will drift away (chapter 13). But long-term and causal studies are few, and the effect of therapy has barely been tested. Whether time apart protects against sexual difficulties, and whether this ability treats them, hasn’t been tested; for now these are therapists’ observations. And this is not permission for one-sided distancing, nor a reason to skip over your partner’s needs.

Where “stifling” comes from. One explanation is historical, and it hasn’t been tested as a “right dose of closeness”: people began to expect self-expression and growth from marriage, while time together shrank. It describes American marriage in general, not the reason a particular person’s desire faded; it says nothing about mainland China or non-monogamous unions. A working question from it: which expectations — emotional, family-related, growth-related — belong to the couple, and which you can get elsewhere (chapter 34).

Growth: together, apart, outside the couple. Among Chinese newlyweds, growth “together with the partner” predicted satisfaction with marriage and with life for both partners a year later. Growth “from other sources” went along with greater marital satisfaction when it was moving in the same direction for both. In couples’ diaries, on days with more personal growth than usual, passion is higher. But among those who chronically grew separately from their partner, passion is lower: closeness drops, and neither shared growth nor time together explains this5. Growth outside the couple cuts both ways: it adds a sense of “I can,” and it can pull you away from your partner (chapter 18).

Apart and at a distance. Living apart isn’t necessarily a bad sign. Among older Britons, living apart while in a couple was linked to better mental health than being single, and differed little from living together. In a survey in four countries, satisfaction was highest among the married; among those living apart, it was higher if there were plans to move in together or marry, and lowest without such plans. This doesn’t show that apart is better than together. Nor does it answer what it’s like with the load of travel, visas, time zones, duty to families, and translation that a Russian–Chinese or European–Chinese couple lives with (chapter 36).

If there are more than two of you. All of this was tested on couples. For networks of several partners, the conclusion about balancing closeness and independence hasn’t been tested, and neither has a shared “we” for three or more. There is a fair amount of research on polyamory and other negotiated forms, but most of it comes from North America, open data are almost nonexistent, and the samples are often white, educated people with middle and high incomes. An American study of single people measured familiarity with polyamory, not satisfaction in established unions. Results from people who signed up for an online survey on their own should be labeled exactly that. On top of that comes the question of how to divide time and trips among several relationships. Before comparing, decide what you count as the unit: a person, a pair, or the whole network (chapters 24 and 29).

If you come from different cultures. The closest thing to this topic is an observational study of conversations among Chinese, Western, and mixed couples in Australia. Couples with a Chinese wife avoided conflict topics more often than couples with a Western wife. But when Chinese couples did discuss a problem, their communication resembled Western communication, and no benefit of “indirect” communication was found. At home the groups spoke different languages, so culture and language can’t be separated. These aren’t results for mainland China, and they’re not about couples of a Russian-speaking or European man and a woman from mainland China; there are no estimates of family involvement or shared accounts in such a couple. The data on Chinese newlyweds above aren’t about mixed couples either. So what’s needed is questions, not rules by nationality: in which language can you speak precisely, what role does each of you want for your family, what does “honest” mean to each of you. Counting “we” in a Russian–Chinese couple is especially questionable — the languages and their rules for pronouns differ — but that’s a guess (chapters 33, 37, and 38).

How to tell it is happening

In yourself:

  • Asking for time alone feels dangerous: you expect in advance that it’ll be read as “you’re a burden to me.”
  • Tiredness comes out as “I’m fed up” — or doesn’t come out at all.
  • “Let’s decide together” leaves you feeling as if your wishes don’t exist.
  • A missed connection — an evening that fell through, a message with no reply — looks deliberate.
  • “I need some time” has become a way to get out of any conversation.

In your partner:

  • They walk off and close the door, go silent, or respond to your pause with coldness.
  • They back off instead of saying out loud what mattered.
  • They agree with everything and stop saying what they want for themselves.
  • “We” sounds like duty, not like “we’re in this together.”

What to do

1. Asking for time alone — with a return

When: when you need a pause. Practice on calm days first.

What to do:

  1. Ask yourself what exactly you want: to rest, to think, to be without conversation. If it’s tiredness, call it tiredness, not “I’m fed up.”
  2. Say it in three parts: what you need (“an hour of quiet with no decisions”), what it isn’t (“it’s not about you”), when you’ll come back (“at eight — dinner together”).
  3. The other person repeats back what they heard and names their own expectation (“the evening together mattered to me”) — without accusations.

How you know it worked: after you come back, each of you says in one sentence how it landed. The request stops setting off a fight, and the return happens at the promised time.

This technique hasn’t been tested: there are no trials in which people were taught to ask for time alone this way. It’s put together from the mechanics above and from the data on accepted dependence and “your own” reasons to be together. If “I need some time” turns into a way to dodge any conversation, the agreement isn’t working — revisit it together.

2. Say what kind of expectation it was

When: when a missed connection has stung — the evening fell through, a reply didn’t come.

What to do: before reproaching, say what it was: an agreement (“we arranged to have dinner”) or a wish (“I would have liked to”). Then the miss isn’t read as a broken promise.

How you know it worked: a missed connection stops being perceived as a broken promise.

This technique hasn’t been tested in trials either; it’s a conclusion drawn from the mechanics above.

3. Pre-agreed windows for staying in touch

When: you’re at a distance — in advance, before a miss has happened.

What to do: agree on when you’ll be in touch. A missed window is a reason to ask what happened, not proof of intent.

How you know it worked: a miss prompts a question, not hurt. This is a guess without trials.

4. Something new, chosen together

When: you want to try something new together.

What to do:

  1. Each of you separately suggests a few optional activities that are unfamiliar to both of you.
  2. Together you choose one that’s acceptable to both. Anything frightening, or surprises your partner didn’t want, won’t do.
  3. Do it for about an hour and a half a week, several weeks in a row.
  4. Before you start and afterward, each of you rates your enjoyment and your satisfaction with the relationship. Both people’s ratings count, not just those of the one who suggested it.

How you know it worked: neither person’s ratings drop, and the activity has stayed voluntary. If one of you agrees out of duty, it’s a different experiment.

Caveats. In a trial with spouses, activities that stir you up gave more satisfaction than merely pleasant ones, but compared with the “nothing special” option, no overall benefit was found; there are no data on long-term effects on desire6. What was measured was the couples’ answers, not hormones, so explaining the effect with a “rush” would be made up. For networks and same-sex couples there are no estimates.

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: long-distance couples are doomed.
In fact: in a large sample of Americans, those who lived far apart rated their relationship and their commitment higher, and by the follow-up survey they had broken up just as often as couples living close by7. But the people who enter and stay in such unions aren’t random, so you can’t conclude from this that distance has a benefit. The conclusion is more modest: build the connection around real constraints, not around expecting it to crash.

People think: the more often a couple says “we,” the happier it is.
In fact: overall, “we” in a couple’s speech is linked to well-being in a positive direction, and often more strongly when it’s your partner who says it, not you. But in some studies the link goes the other way: “we” can be a word of duty rather than solidarity8. Whether asking a couple to say “we” makes them happier wasn’t tested in these studies. In one one-time survey, relationship quality was most strongly linked to the feeling of “we” itself — more strongly than to liking the partner or to similarity. Listen to what stands behind the word, don’t count pronouns, and don’t erase the other person’s “I.”

Talk it over together

  1. What do you hear when I say “I need some time alone”? What stands behind those words for me?
  2. If we marked on a scale “how much we’re included in each other,” where are we now, and where does each of us want to be? Do they match?
  3. What do we expect from each other — companionship, emotional support, support for growth? Which of these can we also get elsewhere, without hurt feelings?
  4. When we say “we decided” — did both of us decide, or one? Where does each of us still have an “I want”?
  5. Which of my activities outside the couple nourish me, and which pull me away from us? What new thing would we like to try together?
  6. Which windows for staying in touch suit us, in which language is it easier for us to speak precisely, and what does “honest” mean to each of us? (If there are several of you, each pair in the network may have different answers.)

When it is no longer about skills

If any of these signs shows up in a conversation about closeness and independence, it’s no longer about balance:

  • fear of refusing sex;
  • being unable to use your own money;
  • threats;
  • violence;
  • coercive control, surveillance.

Then the question is safety and freedom of choice, and this chapter’s conclusions are no reason to put up with it. If it’s about money: a joint account works only as a voluntary choice. Where one person restricts the other’s access to money or makes threats, or where you need a personal reserve in case you leave, it’s better to keep accounts separate (on money in a couple — chapter 20). More in chapter 23, and on when outside help is needed — chapter 35.

20. Chores, Money, and Power: Who Remembers, Who Decides, Who Can Say No

In short

Fights about chores, money, and “who decides” are rarely about the trash can itself or the number in the account. They’re about who remembers, who chooses, and who can say no.

What to do: divide not only the tasks but also the remembering, the choosing, and the oversight; agree in advance on what “done” means and whose job it is; talk about money in terms of shared obligations, personal access, and the right to say no.

And don’t expect everything at once from one person: some of what you need can come from friends, family, community.

Tell support (what will get done if trouble hits) apart from closeness, and “I’m putting up with it” apart from “I’m bearing it.”

Mark remembers that the insurance runs out on Friday, that the printer is almost out of paper, and that Dima’s sister has a birthday coming up. Dima knows this about himself: any task that’s named, he’ll do. In the evening Mark sees the trash bag by the door and says: “Do you even notice that anything goes on in this house?” Dima hears a reproach for being lazy: if he’d been asked, he would have taken it out. Mark hears in that “if I’d been asked” a confirmation: once again it’s on him alone to keep everything in his head.

Each of them is right about his own thing. One about the bag, the other about the remembering.

What is going on

About the body — honestly. The studies this chapter rests on didn’t measure the body: they’re surveys, diaries, interviews. There’s nothing to say about the body in a moment like this (on the body in a fight — chapters 02 and 15). What’s left is what you notice in yourself: anxiety at an unexpected charge on the account, irritation that you’re keeping everything in your head again, fear that saying no will cost you dearly, willingness or resentment when you give in. And sometimes — almost nothing.

The bag isn’t just a bag. The one who plans and reminds also sees that it will soon fill up again. The meaning gets built like this: “I alone am responsible for the home.” Anger signals a broken expectation: “it went without saying” — but was there an agreement about it? The action may turn into criticism. The other person hears not the task hidden in it but a reproach, and defends themselves or walks away. That defense becomes a new trigger for the first person.

It’s the same with money. An unexpected expense causes anxiety: one person calls the purchase affordable, the other calls it a broken promise. Silence or accusation changes the other person’s sense of safety. The number on the statement doesn’t say what it means to each of you.

With power it’s subtler. A request sets off an assessment: “my no counts” or “I can’t say no without losing my home, money, or the relationship.” In the second case, consent looks like a joint decision, even though there’s no choice. It’s commonly thought that power belongs to whoever earns more. But in opposite-sex couples, relationship quality was linked to a personal sense of influence — whether you’re taken into account — and no link was found with how evenly influence was balanced in the couple1. These are one-time surveys: they don’t prove that a formal title changes anything.

Giving in. From the outside, giving in out of love and giving in “just to avoid a fight” look the same; the price is different. The one on the receiving end responds with gratitude, expectation, or pressure. Tears — chapter 05; anger from broken expectations — chapter 16.

Putting up with and bearing. These look similar from the outside too. You put up with things by default: silently, at the cost of your strength. You bear things by decision: you know what it costs, for how long, and for the sake of what. When you have to put up with a lot, anger can flare over small things. This is a working distinction, not a research finding.

Invisible work — noticing that a task is about to come up, going through the options, deciding, following up. It’s divided unevenly and goes along with dissatisfaction: in interviews with couples, women more often took on several of these parts, and in a survey of parents, those whose “mental” tasks were split more evenly were more satisfied with their relationship. What’s the cause and what’s the effect is unknown. Sometimes the one who remembers takes the task over themselves: they know “the right way” to do it. Perhaps this affects who does what — but it’s not a reason to blame the one who carries more. Whether anything helps against this kind of taking over hasn’t been tested.

A joint account. How accounts are set up doesn’t by itself say how people handle money. But there was an experiment: engaged and newlywed couples were randomly split into three groups — merge accounts, keep them separate, no instructions. For those who merged, relationship quality held steady; for the others it declined2. Why, the authors can only speculate. It’s a single trial; it hasn’t been tested for non-monogamous unions, same-sex couples, couples with cross-border obligations, or relationships with coercion. Mandatory merging can become a tool of control.

Support and closeness are different things. Support is what you can actually rely on when it comes to money, illness, kids, repairs, paperwork, a crisis. Closeness is listening, hugging, understanding. In trouble you often need both. Trust researchers single out dependability — whether you can count on a person when it’s needed — as a separate component3. The trap is thinking that one replaces the other: staying because of care and understanding when there’s no one to catch you. People say a couple is worse off without support than without closeness. This hasn’t been tested: the data don’t allow ranking one above the other.

One person isn’t the whole set. When you expect everything from one person — comfort, practical help, company, sex, arguments about ideas — what starts to matter is that person’s strength and whether there are other people around. Friends and family don’t fully replace a partner, and a partner doesn’t replace them. That consciously distributing your needs among friends saves a couple hasn’t been proven. A different arrangement of closeness says nothing by itself about either health or pathology.

Where these data come from. Almost everything above is about opposite-sex couples in Western countries; for same-sex couples, non-monogamous unions, and “Chinese woman and European man” couples there are no comparable estimates. For open unions, neither joint accounts, nor an even division of tasks, nor written agreements have been tested; research on such unions comes mostly from North America, and the samples are often skewed toward white, educated people (chapters 27 and 29).

The closest thing is recordings of conversations of Chinese, Western, and mixed couples in Australia. Couples with a Chinese female partner avoided talking about conflict more often than couples with a Western female partner, but Chinese couples, once they started, communicated in a way similar to Western couples; no particular benefit of indirect communication was found for Chinese couples4. Language and culture can’t be separated there: at home the couples spoke different languages. Couples with a female partner from mainland China and a Russian or European male partner aren’t in these studies. So instead of forecasts by nationality, there are questions (there are no tested rules): in which language can both of you speak precisely, whose family helps out, who handles paperwork in a language that’s foreign to one of you, who sends money to relatives (chapters 33, 37, and 38).

How to tell it is happening

In yourself:

  • You’re angry at the bag, but really at keeping everything in your head again.
  • You say “yes,” but inside there’s resentment or fear.
  • It’s unclear whether you can say no — to an expense, to relatives coming to stay, to sex tonight — without losing money, your home, or the relationship.
  • You expect everything at once from one person.
  • There’s plenty of care and understanding, but if you got sick or lost your job, it’s unclear who would catch you.
  • Things get done, but the feeling of being heard doesn’t come.
  • It’s easier to be the support than to lean on someone: asking for help feels awkward.
  • You put up with a lot in silence and notice it only when you ask yourself.

In your partner:

  • They hear a reproach instead of a task and justify themselves: you should have asked.
  • By “fair” they may mean something else: equal hours, equal effort, or according to ability.
  • They say “you can count on me,” but when the moment comes, the thing isn’t done.

What to do

1. Divide not only the tasks but the remembering

When: the same arguments about chores keep coming back.

What to do: write out the areas: food, cleaning, documents and deadlines, money, health, relatives, kids, leisure. For each one, write down four roles: who anticipates that a task will come up; who chooses and decides; who does it; who checks and reminds. Just write it down, without judgments. Then assign one owner to each area and describe “done” in one sentence. The owner holds all four roles; the other person reminds only if the owner has asked. You can hand over an area in one conversation: “This is entirely yours now, and I’ll take something of yours.” If you have to ask again and again, the issue isn’t memory but agreement: is the other person really taking the area on, and can you live with how things are (chapter 16). For the first month — once a week for 15–20 minutes: what has moved, where is there overload. This hasn’t been tested as a method: the areas come from interviews, the steps are ours.

How you know it worked: fewer “but you were reminded,” each area has one owner, and at the end of the month both of you consider the division fair.

2. Agree on money in advance

When: before an argument about money, or when the same one keeps repeating.

What to do: discuss an account for shared obligations, each person’s personal access, and how to check that both of you consider the system fair and have the right to change it or leave it. Don’t merge accounts if someone restricts the other’s access, makes threats, or needs a private safety reserve. If you want to make it firm, write down on one page what’s expected, who’s responsible, how to exit, and when you’ll review it. Sign only if both of you can freely say “no”: a paper signed under unequal power will lock in the inequality (on agreements — chapter 27). That such an agreement improves relationships hasn’t been tested.

How you know it worked: both of you can say what’s shared and what’s personal; you argue less often about what was promised to whom.

3. Check whether “no” is possible

When: you’re deciding about an expense, relatives coming to stay, or sex — and after a concession that left a bad aftertaste.

What to do: “Who pays” doesn’t replace the question “who can safely say no.” Instead of “do you feel your power,” ask about specifics: can you disagree with this expense, with relatives coming to stay, with sex tonight — and what will happen afterward? Immigration status, language, access to accounts, family support — these are reasons to ask such questions; how they play out hasn’t been measured, and there’s no tested technique. Before making a concession yourself, ask yourself: am I choosing this freely, can I manage it, is it mutual?

How you know it worked: “no” isn’t followed by punishment, and both of you can name where it’s safe to say no and where it’s hard.

4. Lay out expectations across nine functions

This is the book’s working scheme; it hasn’t been tested in research. Not every couple needs all nine: the scheme helps you see who expects what, from whom, and whether it’s doable.

The functions: co-strategist (shared goals), friend (being together without an agenda), decision coordination (who decides what), lover (sex and physical intimacy), co-builder of the family (home, kids, relatives), social ally (on the same side in front of others), inspirer (growth and new ideas), repair partner (fixing things after fights — chapter 17), guardian of autonomy (each person’s right to a separate life — chapter 19). Go through support as a separate line (technique 5).

When: expectations diverge and resentment builds up. Revisit it after two or three months (the timeframe is our choice, not research’s) and after changes: a move, a child, an illness, a job change.

What to do:

  1. Each of you separately, without discussing it, gives each function four ratings from 0 to 10: how important it is to me; whether I want it to be mutual; how much I need exclusivity (only with this person, or with others too); how much I can actually give right now, and at what cost: an hour of sleep, a sum of money, an evening, sex without desire.
  2. Mark each line: “agreement” or “my expectation” — that makes it easier to spot a future resentment before it turns into anger.
  3. Compare your sheets. Look not for right answers but for gaps: it matters to you — the other person can’t manage it; you need exclusivity — the other wants to share.
  4. For each gap, name the sources: the two of you, friends, family, community, paid help with the household. If there are more than two of you, rate each person separately: people lean on different loved ones in different ways.

How you know it worked: after a month, each of you can say who covers the important functions; you hear “you know what I expect” less often; there’s no line where you silently carry more than you can. If it turns into keeping score and pressure — stop.

A separate point, for when you ask for support: say what you need — to be listened to or to be advised. In lab observations of married couples, a response that matched the request made people feel their partner was responsive, while criticism and sarcasm in response lowered that feeling. When the request was for advice, the match didn’t make a difference.

5. Separate support from closeness

When: the question “if something happens, will anyone catch me?” creeps in, or you’re revisiting expectations (technique 4).

What to do:

  1. Each of you separately writes down where you rely on each other: repairs, kids, parents, money, health, paperwork. Not “would catch me if something happened,” but actual cases: when it happened and in what situation.
  2. Ask yourself: if I get sick or lose my job, what will still get done — and by whom?
  3. Finish the sentence “I can lean on you when…” several times and sort the answers into two piles: practical help and warmth. If one pile is empty and the other is full, that’s a reason to discuss what’s missing.
  4. Compare your sheets. For each gap, say what help you expect and whether it was there last time.

If checking feels scary, start with support in general: where you already lean on friends and family. If there are more than two of you, go through each person separately.

How you know it worked: each of you can name which things you don’t have to think about because the other person holds them, and what will get done if one of you goes down.

This hasn’t been tested as a method.

6. Separate “putting up with” from “bearing”

When: your strength keeps shrinking, anger flares over small things, concessions are piling up.

What to do:

  1. Write down everything you’re putting up with, from big to small: a job you don’t like, the commute, a squeaky door.
  2. Find a few items you can remove today — and remove them.
  3. If you can’t remove something — for example, you’re caring for a sick relative — decide to bear it: name what it costs, for how long, and for the sake of what. Cut other drains on your energy, hand off some tasks to others, add good things for yourself: sleep, food.
  4. If the list includes jabs, contempt, or someone blowing up at you — that’s no longer about chores (chapter 41).

How you know it worked: the list is shorter, and about what’s left, you can say what you’re carrying it for.

This technique hasn’t been tested either.

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: chores have to be split exactly in half, or there will be no happiness.
In fact: the data don’t show a universal “half.” In a large survey, satisfaction was lower both for those who believed they did more than their fair share and for those who believed they did less5. An equal number of tasks and a sense of fairness are different things. Strict bookkeeping can hide an unequal load.

People think: money is the number one cause of divorce.
In fact: in diaries of arguments at home in families with children, money wasn’t the most frequent topic, but money arguments ended worse and more often stayed unresolved6. Among the topics measured, financial disagreements were among the strongest predictors of divorce, while the financial situation itself, once the arguments were taken into account, was not7. But a predictor is not a cause, and the slogan about “the number one cause” can serve as cover for intimidation and control.

People think: sacrificing for your partner always strengthens the relationship.
In fact: what matters is whether you’re moving toward something or running away from something. In diaries of student couples, concessions made “for the sake of what’s valuable” went with better well-being and better relationships, and concessions made “just to avoid conflict” went with worse ones8. Narrow studies on sex show something similar: putting your partner’s needs ahead of your own went with more distressing feelings about sex, and a hidden lack of interest went with a sense of inauthenticity. “Duty” in sex and erasing yourself can harm the one who gives in (on desire — chapter 18).

People think: one partner should cover all the important functions of a relationship.
In fact: support from other people for autonomy, for the feeling “I can handle it,” and for closeness is linked to better well-being, and this broadly holds across cultures9. This is about people in general, not about couples; no rule was found under which one partner is supposed to cover everything. A demand like that can push toward isolation.

Talk it over together

  1. What do you anticipate and remember for both of us, in a way that lets me not notice it?
  2. What is “fair” to you: equal hours, equal effort, or according to ability?
  3. What is money to you — safety, freedom, care, control? What’s shared, and what’s yours?
  4. On which questions can you say “no” to me without fearing the consequences? On which is it hard?
  5. Which of your recent concessions were chosen willingly, and which were made to avoid a fight?
  6. Which of the nine functions do I expect only from you, even though others could provide part of it? What can you actually give right now?
  7. If I get sick or lose my job, what will you take on? And what will I take on if it happens to you?

When it is no longer about skills

If even one of these is present in the couple, it’s no longer about optimizing the relationship but about safety and freedom of choice.

  • fear of refusing sex;
  • no access to your own money;
  • threats, violence, coercive control, surveillance;
  • one partner controls access to income or makes threats over spending.

What to do: don’t use this chapter’s average results as an argument for putting up with things or giving in. Don’t merge accounts, and keep a private safety reserve. Don’t do this chapter’s exercises together, and keep your answers where the person who frightens you won’t find them. Talk about safety with someone you trust or with a domestic violence specialist; more in chapters 23 and 35.

Part IV. Crises

21. Infidelity: what it is, why it happens, and what comes after

In short

The word “infidelity” lumps together different acts, which is why the numbers on it vary so much and why couples understand what happened in different ways.

The body most likely responds to the threat before words do, and the conclusion “they did it on purpose” gets built on afterward. Two people read the same episode differently and act on their own reading.

Being attracted to someone else is not yet infidelity. Seeking out meetings, secrecy, and breaking an agreement are separate steps, and before them you can work out what is pulling you.

What to do: name the act and the agreement, not the general word; first find out what you had agreed on; check safety before any joint conversation; give the other person what they need to make decisions about their health and the relationship, without graphic details.

The rule “always confess” has barely been tested.

Artyom happens to see a notification on Kirill’s phone screen: “Thanks for tonight, it felt so easy.” The name belongs to a coworker Kirill had mentioned in passing. Artyom’s heart pounds, his mouth goes dry, and in his head a story is already taking shape in which every late Thursday finds its explanation. For several evenings Artyom says nothing, then, as if in passing, asks to see the messages. Kirill, caught off guard, snaps back and puts the phone away. For Artyom this is one more piece of proof. For Kirill it is confirmation that he has already been judged guilty.

Each of them is sure the other knows what he is doing.

What is going on

First the body. The heart pounds, the mouth goes dry. A sense of threat and shame may show up. What exactly goes on in the body in the first days after discovery has hardly been measured, so this whole chain is a reasonable picture, not a measurement.

Then the meaning. The body doesn’t say what happened. The head fills in the meaning: “this is no accident,” “something is wrong with me,” “they already see me as a criminal.” Anger most often signals a broken expectation (chapter 16). Whether it turns into a verdict depends on whether the expectation has been checked: was there really such an agreement? Between the signal and the conclusion “the other person knew they were breaking it” there is a step, and you can notice it (chapters 02 and 04).

Then the action. Interrogating, checking, going silent, walking away. Each person’s action becomes a trigger for the other. Two people read the same contact differently — and behave according to their reading, not according to what actually happened.

Secrecy spins the cycle faster. It follows the same pattern: while something is being hidden, the other person lives in a picture where something is missing. When an affair surfaces unexpectedly, past episodes — a late night, a curt reply, an unfamiliar name — turn into ambiguous evidence. When the secrecy goes on, the hurt partner experiences it as danger and searches for information again and again. Intrusive thoughts and anxiety in those days are not yet a diagnosis. Tears at the moment of discovery are a separate signal to the other person (chapter 05). On repairing the interlocked loops, see chapter 17.

“Infidelity” is a bag of different acts. A kiss, a long emotional closeness, secret texting, sex — any of these can break an agreement, yet surveys often ask only about sex with someone other than the partner. Estimates depend heavily on how infidelity was defined, how anonymous the survey was, and who was recruited, so there is no single reliable “worldwide” figure1.

Where it comes from. The reasons depend on who was asked and how. Much of the data comes from “right now” surveys and accounts given after the fact, and the link runs both ways: dissatisfied partners may look for others, and infidelity itself lowers satisfaction. In one small online survey, where most respondents were young volunteers, people named attraction, but alongside it they named neglect, anger, a lack of love, self-esteem, circumstances. The split “men do it for sex, women for feelings” isn’t there2. A motive named after the fact was not necessarily the cause. Ask about a specific person’s motives instead of assigning them by gender.

Before the act: attraction is not yet infidelity. The body responds to living people, and that is true in a couple too. Someone attractive is nearby — and there’s a flutter inside. From there the meaning can go two ways. The first: “this person sees in me what no one at home has noticed in a long time.” Then you’re pulled to meet again, the fantasies keep spinning, and you get drawn in deeper and deeper. The second: “a nice person — and that’s all.” And it goes no further. This is a possible picture, not a measurement. Attraction, seeking out meetings, secrecy, and breaking an agreement are different things, and each is worth judging on its own.

If you learn that your partner feels this kind of attraction, check the expectation, as with anger above: was it “you won’t be drawn to anyone” or “we agreed not to seek that kind of contact”? These are different expectations, and before treating what happened as a violation, find out which one you had. In open relationships, attraction to one more person does not in itself break the agreement: what matters is what exactly you agreed on.

After discovery: telling and repairing are different things. For some couples, things get better after discovery. In a small observational study of couples in therapy, satisfaction rose where the affair had been disclosed, and where it stayed secret, things went worse. Over the years, couples with an affair divorced more often than the rest, and within this tiny group they divorced more often when it stayed secret3. But no one assigned disclosure at random, and these data don’t compare a voluntary confession with being found out. They don’t prove that the decision to confess changed anything, and they don’t confirm the phrase “a couple can’t recover after an affair.” Recovery programs have so far been tried on only a handful of people: many felt better, but the benefit hasn’t been proven.

Online life and jealousy. An ambiguous like or contact with an ex also prompts checking. Perhaps the checking creates new ambiguous signals and the cycle spins faster, but this hasn’t been proven. These data also don’t show that social media causes infidelity, that jealousy proves an affair, or that looking through a phone brings calm. There is no tested threshold for “micro-cheating” in the available data: a one-on-one friendship you’ve agreed on and a hidden romantic exchange are different things.

If there are more than two of you. Open relationships, swinging, and polyamory are not the same thing, and a survey about sex with someone other than the partner does not count broken agreements in them. Whether a meeting counts as betrayal depends on consent and on the specific boundary you talked about. You can break a boundary there too: what changes is the agreement itself, not the possibility of breaking it. How often violations happen isn’t reliably known: when people in such relationships are recruited online, the ones who respond are those who chose to — you can’t judge everyone by them. Sometimes a partner’s interest in others brings not only jealousy but also joy for someone you love. According to the available data, neither monogamy nor non-monogamy is healthier in itself (agreements — chapters 27 and 28, jealousy — 25, joy for a partner — 26).

If you come from different cultures. American surveys and trials give American numbers, not Chinese ones. There are data from China, but no single “Chinese norm” comes out of them. Therapists who work with Chinese couples describe the involvement of the spouses’ relatives, and that some clients explain events instead of naming the feeling right away. But these are therapists’ accounts, not a census: you can’t derive a norm from them, or gender differences, or a specific person’s feelings. For couples where one partner is Russian-speaking or from Europe and the other is from mainland China, no outcome studies were found. So ask each other what you agreed on, how family is involved, and in which language it’s easier to talk about hard things. Don’t lump the mainland, Taiwan, and Hong Kong together, and don’t take indirect speech for dishonesty (chapters 32, 33, and 37).

How to tell it is happening

In yourself:

  • Your body reacted before your thoughts did: your heart is pounding, your mouth is dry.
  • From one message or one name, a whole story came together in seconds, with every past late night and curt reply fitting into it.
  • You’re sure the other person knew what they were doing and did it on purpose.
  • You feel pulled to check again and again: the phone, the messages, where the other person is right now.
  • Your questions sound like an interrogation. Or the opposite: you stay silent and let it pile up.

In your partner:

  • They answer sharply, shut down, put the phone away. Shame may be behind the sharpness.
  • They feel the question of guilt has already been decided and defend themselves before anything has been cleared up.
  • They dodge questions. For the other person, that’s a new trigger.

In both of you: you understand differently what you agreed on, or you never agreed on it at all.

Before the act, in yourself:

  • You’re drawn to a specific person, but you can still switch back to your own life and your relationship.
  • Or you’ve already been swept away: imaginary conversations with this person, thoughts about “fate” and “we were made for each other,” an urge to find reasons to write or meet, a wish to hide the messages (on this kind of obsessive preoccupation with a person, see chapter 10).

What to do

This isn’t a tested program but an assembly of what is known in pieces.

1. Name the act and the agreement, not the word

When: you want to say “you’re cheating on me,” or you hear back “this isn’t cheating.”

What to do: replace the general word with two specific things: what exactly happened (a kiss, texting, a meeting, sex) and what you had agreed on. For example: “I happened to see a message from your coworker. What was that, and what do the two of us consider okay?”

How you know it worked: you describe the same thing in the same words, and you can see where your readings went apart. The argument is about something specific, not about “you always.”

2. Five lines before the conversation

When: before a hard conversation, while your body is still buzzing.

What to do: write down five lines. What exactly was on the screen or what was said. What you feel in your body. What meaning is being built on top of it. What urge you have: to interrogate, to check, to walk away. What you’re about to do. Then write out the expectation in words and ask yourself: was it shared and agreed on, or only mine? Start the conversation with “what did we agree on?” rather than “you did it on purpose.”

How you know it worked: you can name the sensation separately from the conclusion, and the conversation opens with a question, not an accusation. This hasn’t been tested — it’s just a sensible habit that takes a few minutes. If you’re being intimidated, writing things down doesn’t replace taking safety steps.

3. Ask what you’ve agreed on instead of checking

When: you see an ambiguous signal — a like, a private message, contact with an ex — and you’re tempted to go through the other person’s phone.

What to do: ask what you have agreed on: what’s okay, what isn’t, what’s private. While you’re at it, look at whether one of you is trying to control the other. Forcing access to devices, threats, and surveillance are already a safety issue.

How you know it worked: you have specific rules about phones and messages, and asking about them no longer sounds like an interrogation.

4. The conversation after discovery — in order

When: the affair has already come out, and you’re about to talk.

What to do: four steps. First, safety: no threats, no surveillance, no coercion. If there are any, don’t start a joint process (see the section “When it is no longer about skills”). Then, what happened: an honest account of what the other person needs to know to make decisions about their health and the relationship, without graphic details and without forced meetings. Then, what it meant for each of you. And finally, whether you want to rebuild trust, and with what concrete steps. How many conversations or sessions this takes is unknown.

How you know it worked: look separately at safety, unwanted contacts, agreements that can be checked, and how each of you is doing. Improvement over months doesn’t guarantee things will be the same years later. If one of you is being coerced, don’t start a joint process.

5. Drawn to someone else, but nothing has happened

When: you’re drawn to a specific person, but you haven’t broken your agreement so far.

What to do:

  1. Don’t chase the thoughts away, but don’t act on them either. You can let yourself fantasize: that makes it easier to look at the attraction from the outside.
  2. Answer three questions for yourself. What valuable experience could you have with this person — what would you do together? What is special about this person? What comes alive in you around someone like this — and is there room for it in your life as it is now? Sometimes there’s nothing special about the person: what matters is not the person but what you could experience with someone like that.
  3. Then think about your relationship. Is what came up in these answers present in it? Was it never there, or did it disappear — when, and after what? Can you bring it back into the relationship, or live it somewhere else in your life, without a second relationship? The answer “we have everything, I’m just a living human being” is also an answer: attraction in itself doesn’t prove that something is missing in the relationship.
  4. Check whether you can turn away. Shift your attention to your relationship and ask honestly: “Are things okay with us?” If you can think about your relationship, you have the attraction, but it doesn’t rule you. If you can’t, and your thoughts keep drifting back to that person on their own, work through it with someone outside, preferably a professional (chapter 35).
  5. You can choose to cut back on contact with this person.
  6. If you want to tell your partner, first figure out why: to share, to ask for reassurance, to revisit the agreement, or to offload your own anxiety. These are different conversations. If there are threats or coercive control around you, don’t tell (section “When it is no longer about skills”).

How you know it worked: you can name what exactly is pulling you and bring your attention back to your own life. Your actions follow your agreement, not the impulse.

This hasn’t been tested: neither the questions, nor boundaries, nor telling your partner have been tested as protection against infidelity.

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: most married people cheat sooner or later.
In fact: different surveys give anything from a small share of spouses in a year to about a quarter of marriages over their whole course4. The counting differs, and many online forms of infidelity and infidelity without sex didn’t make it into the numbers. But it doesn’t come out as “most.”

People think: once a cheater, always a cheater.
In fact: the risk of doing it again is higher, but it isn’t destiny. In a survey of young Americans, more than half of those who cheated in their first relationship did not report doing it again in their second5. Look at actions and agreements, not at the label “that kind of person.”

People think: men are hurt by the sex, women by the feelings.
In fact: if you ask people to choose which is worse, the difference comes out sharp: in student experiments, most men and a minority of women named sex on the side as worse6. But that’s students reacting to an imagined scene, not to real infidelity. On average the difference is small, and any kind of infidelity can hurt anyone7. Where the difference comes from is still debated. Better to ask what exactly took the blow: trust, sexual health, commitment, or standing in other people’s eyes.

People think: an honest person should always confess.
In fact: this rule hasn’t been properly tested, so it’s unknown whether it helps3. When there’s a threat of revenge or coercive control, confessing can lead to harm (how often is unknown). But a secret that puts the other person’s sexual health at risk or violates their consent doesn’t become a trifle just because there are no trials. Tell an honest account and agreed boundaries apart from graphic details and a forced meeting.

People think: if you’re drawn to someone else, something must be missing in the relationship.
In fact: in one small survey of people in relationships, a strong crush on someone else turned out to be fairly common8. It went along with several things at once — both the person’s own traits and how things were going in the relationship — rather than with one cause. Something missing is one possible explanation, not the meaning of every attraction: if you take it as a verdict, it’s easy to blame yourself or your partner for nothing. But that doesn’t make every such pull harmless either: look separately at actions and agreements.

Talk it over together

  1. What did we actually agree on — and where did our readings go apart?
  2. What felt threatened for each of us: trust, health, commitment, our place in other people’s eyes?
  3. What sensation showed up in the body first — even before words?
  4. What do you need to know to make decisions about health and the relationship, and which details can you do without?
  5. How will we agree on privacy, phones, and messages so that this doesn’t turn into an interrogation?
  6. Who else does this concern — other partners, family — and which of them needs to know what? In which language is it easier for us to talk about this?
  7. If one of us is drawn to another person, what do we expect: that it won’t happen at all — or that we won’t seek out meetings? Do we want to know about such an attraction — and why?

When it is no longer about skills

Everything above is for cases where both of you are generally safe. If the relationship includes:

  • threats of retaliation in response to discovery or questions;
  • demands to hand over the phone, passwords, or location right away;
  • surveillance;
  • coercion into meeting a third person or into giving a detailed account;
  • infidelity or jealousy used to justify humiliation, intimidation, shoving, and hitting,

then the exercises in this chapter are not the answer. According to a review of studies, infidelity and romantic jealousy are often named as reasons for violence against women9.

Put safety before any joint conversation. Make a safety plan confidentially, not in front of your partner: for example, with a domestic violence hotline or with someone close who won’t pass anything on. Don’t treat the promise “I’ll change” or anger management classes as a guarantee: programs for people who use violence mostly have a small effect — it shows up in official records, but not in what the victims themselves report. Demanding that the victim “watch their tone” doesn’t change the behavior of the person who controls. Don’t use the joint exercises from this chapter where there is intimidation and coercion. More in chapter 23; on whether to leave or stay, see chapter 22.

22. Leave or stay: breakups, divorce, a broken heart

In short

The pain after a breakup is real, but it has no calendar: the rule “you need half the length of the relationship” sounds like a law, but in the studies we found, nobody tested it.

The wish to stay and the wish to leave live side by side in the same person, and one doubt is not enough to make a decision. Staying is also a decision with a loss, and that loss is worth grieving.

What to do about it: watch your own signs rather than the date (sleep, intrusive thoughts, energy for work, the urge to text your ex), bring the heat down first before a hard conversation, and end the relationship clearly and safely.

If there is violence, surveillance, or threats, safety matters more than any conversation (chapter 23). Chest pain or shortness of breath after a loss is a reason to call an ambulance.

Liza recently moved out of Dina’s place. In the evening a photo from their last vacation pops up on her phone, and Liza notices: her chest feels squeezed, her breathing is short, her palms are damp. A thought comes on its own: “If she really didn’t care, she’d have deleted everything by now.” Liza writes: “How are you?” — and stares at the screen until morning. Dina answers an hour later with one word: “Fine.” For Dina it’s a polite full stop. For Liza it’s a puzzle for the whole night: does “fine” mean “we can still go back” or “leave me alone”? Her fingers are already typing the next message.

What is going on

First the body. A breakup knocks out several supports at once: the shared routine, the person next to you before sleep, the one you can text on a bad day. It is plausible that sleep, eating, and the sense of safety suffer from this, although the data give no precise picture for an ordinary breakup. The brain takes rejection as a threat: “I’m no longer among my own.” You feel it in the body: a lump in the throat, a tight chest, emptiness. In lab experiments, rejection partly touched the same brain areas as physical pain, although a finer analysis tells these two kinds of pain apart. But the experiments are small, and they don’t describe illness after a divorce. In other experiments, an ordinary painkiller lowered people’s ratings of the pain of rejection, but the studies we found contain no large test on real breakups, so there are no grounds to recommend a pill for missing your ex.

Then the meaning. A reminder — a photo, a song, a shared courtyard — triggers longing and anxiety, and the head fills in the rest: “we’ll get back together,” “something is wrong with me,” “my ex doesn’t care.” The meaning suggests an action: write, look at their page, “accidentally” end up nearby. Tears here are not a measure of love, and their absence says nothing about how deep the feeling is; relief after crying is not guaranteed (chapter 05).

Then the other person’s response. For the other person, this is a new trigger. Their answer or their silence starts the loop again in the first person. The chain looks plausible, but nobody has measured a universal schedule for its turns.

Before the breakup, the same loop is at work. A caring act gives reasons to stay, a broken promise gives reasons to leave1. Fear, financial dependence, hope for change, and the thought of how this will hit the other person all live in someone at the same time. Anger here is most often a signal of a broken expectation (chapter 16). Before you count an act as deliberate harm, ask: did we ever agree on this expectation out loud? It’s a working hint; nobody has tested it on its own.

The thought of leaving is not yet a decision. In a large survey of married people, about one in four had recently thought about divorce. A year later, some of those who had been thinking about it no longer were, and some who hadn’t been now were2. The survey was about marriages in one country, and it doesn’t prove such thoughts are harmless. What matters more is whether the thought keeps coming back, whether there’s already a plan, whether you’re safe, and whether the problem itself is changing. And when you compare “now” with “how it used to be,” remember: memory is not a recording but a retelling, and what you remember may depend on how you feel right now3, 4.

Staying is also a loss. Grief can happen without a breakup: you stay — and realize that something here is never going to be. Another child, the gentleness you’re waiting for, a youth spent together. If the loss isn’t named, the decision to stay rests on the hope “once things change.” Accepting doesn’t mean liking it, and it doesn’t mean staying or putting up with humiliation and fear: it means agreeing to deal with what is, and you can still decide later in either direction. The distinction is a working one; it hasn’t been tested. How to accept — chapter 16; how a couple changes over the years — chapter 40.

There is no deadline. In young people who were followed after a breakup, mood jumped around from day to day, especially soon after it. In one student survey, women on average took the breakup harder, but the way participants were selected and the questions themselves could have affected that, and you can’t predict from an average how you will cope. As for the “fatal year”: according to statistics, divorces are most common in the first years of marriage, then the risk drops, and the “seven-year itch” is still debated. This is a picture of the population, not a schedule for your marriage.

The heart can break literally. “Broken heart syndrome” exists: it’s a distinct heart condition, sometimes severe but passing. It can be set off by strong emotions, and also by a serious illness or surgery5. How often it happens in people going through a loss hasn’t been measured. An expert report says only that it makes up a small share of cases first suspected to be a heart attack — not a share of everyone with a broken heart. There is also a longer-range link: divorced and widowed people on average have a higher risk of death. These are observations of huge groups: they don’t prove that the breakup itself is the cause, and they aren’t a forecast for you. Strong ties with people are also linked to a longer life, and they matter in their own right (chapter 34).

If there are more than two of you. Sexual satisfaction showed no difference on average between monogamous and non-monogamous relationships; different forms of non-monogamy were lumped together here. The studies we found contain no representative data on how often open relationships and polyamorous ones break up: volunteers from the internet aren’t good enough for that. About children: in one district of Tanzania, researchers found almost no signs of harm from non-monogamous families, but that was about polygyny, not modern polyamory, and carrying it over to polyamory hasn’t been tested.

If you come from different cultures. The studies we found include no direct research on breakups in “Russia or Europe — mainland China” couples. The closest are studies of conversations in Chinese, Western, and mixed couples (there, the cultural makeup of the couple was linked to how the couple talks and how satisfied they are with the relationship) and a few interviews with therapists about Chinese couples: they describe interference from relatives and cases where a person explains events instead of naming a feeling. These are group averages; they aren’t about any specific person. The mainland, Taiwan, and Hong Kong are different, and indirect speech is not the same as insincerity. American estimates can’t be taken as the norm for Chinese couples. A practical question when deciding “stay or leave”: have you talked about family involvement, privacy, the language for hard conversations, and what it means to tell other people (chapters 32, 33, and 37)?

How to tell it is happening

In yourself:

  • Your chest is tight, your breathing short, your palms damp when a reminder pops up.
  • Your hand reaches on its own to text, to look at their page, to “accidentally” walk past.
  • Sleep, eating, and work have gone off track, your thoughts go in circles, and a whole story gets built out of one word from your ex (“fine”).
  • The wish to stay and the wish to leave live in you at the same time, and from day to day first one, then the other wins out.
  • The thought of leaving disappears, then comes back more and more often and gathers details: when, where to, how to say it.

In your partner or ex:

  • They answer briefly and with long pauses, and you read that as a sign.
  • The one who leaves silently is avoiding conflict, while the other piles up questions: “is this the end or a pause?”
  • While the relationship is still alive: after an act of care, you’re pulled to stay; after a broken promise, to leave.

What to do

The first technique is the best supported. The rest are working schemes, not tested programs.

1. A pause before a hard conversation

When: you notice heat in your face, a clenched jaw, a racing heart, and the conversation about breaking up, belongings, or the kids is already underway or about to start.

What to do: say: “I need a pause, let’s come back to this at such-and-such time.” Do whatever specifically calms you down. Then come back and discuss the expectation or the decision itself. Research doesn’t say how long the pause should last: it ends when your body has calmed down, but you must come back. Activities that lower the heat noticeably reduce anger and aggression.

How you know it worked: the conversation really did resume. If the topic just keeps getting avoided, or you’re muting anxiety about your own safety, it’s no longer a pause. And if you’re being pressured and controlled, a request to “watch your tone” doesn’t change the behavior of the person applying the pressure.

2. Watch the signs, not the calendar

When: once a week, and in the moment a reminder hits you.

What to do: rate your sleep, intrusive thoughts, energy for work, and the urge to text your ex on your own scale. If things don’t get better for months, rethink your approach. When it hits: write down the urge and your mood, then for about five minutes (that’s a rough guide, not a tested dose) either go over the downsides of the past relationship or shift your attention elsewhere, and rate again. In the lab, going over the downsides reduced the feeling of love but immediately made mood worse; distraction lifted mood and didn’t touch love. Love, your mood right now, symptoms, and a decision about the future are different things.

How you know it worked: the numbers slowly improve. If the method mostly sends your thoughts spinning in circles, drop it. It hasn’t been tested at home, day after day. If your ex threatens or stalks you, this technique isn’t for you.

About journaling: writing out your feelings doesn’t help everyone. In people who had recently separated, writing about emotions could get in the way of recovery, especially for those who were already actively searching for the meaning of what happened6.

3. A “stay or leave” map

When: the doubt has dragged on and keeps going in circles.

What to do: each of you writes four lists separately. What is unsafe right now. What is valuable right now. What can realistically change, and by what signs that will be visible. What support you have outside the couple. You can compare the lists together only where there is no violence and no coercive control. Set a date and come back to the third list.

Then ask yourself two questions. The first, instead of the label “abuser” or “narcissist”: what is this behavior doing to my life now, and what will it do further down the line? It’s more useful to name the problem as your own — “I have a problem with how my partner drinks” — and honestly decide whether you can live with it. If you’re scared, the question is already about safety (chapter 23); if there’s no fear, but jabs and contempt have become the norm, see chapter 41. The second: am I willing to invest in what is here now, and not in what will be once my partner changes?

How you know it worked: expectations, behavior, and agreements have changed, not just the promises. “Will change” counts if you can see it. The scheme is a working one, not a tested one.

4. A clear ending

When: the decision has been made.

What to do: state the decision directly. Give a short reason. Lay out specific boundaries: contact, belongings, shared matters, logistics with the kids. Leave room for a reaction, but don’t bargain for agreement to the breakup. One conversation, and a separate one about logistics. A clear ending hurts, while disappearing adds uncertainty: the person doesn’t know whether it’s all over and checks again and again. This is a proposed mechanism, not a proven advantage of the approach; nor has it been shown that a face-to-face conversation is always better. If there’s a threat or surveillance, write instead, or keep your distance.

How you know it worked: the other person is no longer left with the question “is this the end or a pause?”

Two caveats. In separated spouses, contact with the ex is linked to feeling worse. That is an observation, not a tested rule of “not a single message”; texting about the kids and logistics is a separate question. And friendship afterward: after a friends-with-benefits arrangement, most people kept the friendship, and those who reported being deceived coped worse. This is about one kind of relationship; there are no grounds to insist on staying friends after every breakup.

5. A snapshot for the future

When: you have doubts about a long relationship and want to decide right now.

What to do: record a voice memo on your phone or write down how you feel in the relationship and what your complaints are. Set a date — a few months or a year from now — and on that day listen to it or read it again. Long relationships aren’t decided in a hurry. If your partner checks your phone, don’t keep the recording there (chapter 23).

How you know it worked: you can see what has changed and what keeps going in circles, and your decision rests on that, not on the mood of one evening. But the recording is the view of a single day, not an objective measure, and the technique itself hasn’t been tested.

6. Grieve from the outside

When: after a breakup, or when you’re staying but it’s clear that something in this relationship won’t be there. This technique is about loss, not about putting up with humiliation, fear, or control: in those cases, boundaries and safety are what matter (chapters 41 and 23).

What to do: out loud or on paper, as if you were looking at yourself from the outside, but without turning away from the loss:

  1. What happened, and when did the wave rise? Only facts.
  2. What do I feel? Different shades: pain, disappointment, shame, anger at myself.
  3. How do I experience myself here? For example: “something is wrong with me,” “nobody cares about me.”
  4. Look at yourself in all this. Usually what comes up is “I’m sorry it’s like this.” If there’s no compassion for yourself, go back to the first steps.

Then ask: what small thing would do me good right now — a run, sleep, a call to a friend?

How you know it worked: the wave has receded, compassion for yourself has appeared, the next step is clear. If your thoughts only spin harder, drop it, just as with writing out feelings in technique 2.

Looking at their fights from the outside helped spouses stop their marriage from getting worse7 (chapter 17); there is no such test for grieving or for this order of steps.

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: to get over a breakup, you need half the length of the relationship.
In fact: the studies we found didn’t test any such rule. When young people were asked how they felt over several weeks after a breakup, what stood out was not the recovery time but how strongly their mood jumped from day to day8. It’s a small observation: it shows how wide the swings are, not a norm.

People think: if you have doubts, it’s time to leave.
In fact: mixed wishes, to stay and to leave, are common, and there’s no tested rule that “doubt means leave”1. In student couples, what set apart those who stayed from those who left wasn’t doubt but how important the things the relationship gave them were, and whether there were alternatives9. These are associations in groups, not a calculator for your couple; for married couples, open relationships, and intercultural couples, the dividing line hasn’t been tested. Holding on only because of what you’ve already invested is costly too. For an orderly way to decide, use the map from technique 3. Why people stay in violent relationships is a separate topic (chapter 23).

People think: rebound relationships are a bad idea.
In fact: in a couple of studies, new relationships after a breakup weren’t linked to feeling worse; sometimes people in them felt better10. But that doesn’t mean a new relationship heals grief, and these studies don’t set a minimum waiting period. Questions to ask: do both people understand the current commitments, and are the dates filled with reminders of the past? This is reasoning, not a tested questionnaire. In people with several partners at once, attachment to each one turned out to be its own, so a new person doesn’t have to repeat the old script. Growth after a breakup is possible but not guaranteed, and it hasn’t been shown that suffering is necessary for growth. If you go back to your ex, ask what has changed in expectations, behavior, and agreements (chapter 17; on infidelity — chapter 21).

People think: staying together for the kids is better for the kids.
In fact: that’s only partly true. Children of divorced parents on average lag behind children from families where the parents stayed together: in school, behavior, and well-being. But after a high-conflict marriage the picture is different: divorce should be compared not with an imaginary conflict-free family but with the real home, where conflict could have been there both before and after11. The outcome depends on conflict, parental care, and resources. Keeping a child amid intimidation or violence “on principle” risks harm. Guideposts: how much fear and aggression the child sees, whether stable care continues, whether there are enough resources (chapter 31). For open relationships, Chinese-Western families, and same-sex parents, these conclusions haven’t been tested separately.

Talk it over together

Only if it is safe for both of you to talk about this.

  1. What in our relationship is unsafe for one of us right now, and what is simply hard?
  2. What do you value here that nobody else gives you?
  3. Which expectation you had of me has been broken — and was it ever said out loud and agreed on?
  4. Which of the things tormenting us can actually change, and by what signs will we notice it?
  5. If we break up, what does a clear and caring ending look like: who says what, in what way, how often we stay in touch afterward?
  6. Who else needs to learn about the decision — family, other partners, friends — and in which language, in what order?
  7. What did we expect that, it seems, we won’t have — and how do we live with that without ceasing to value each other?

When it is no longer about skills

  • Violence, coercive control, surveillance, threats. For example, an ex stalks you, checks your messages and movements, makes threats. Strangulation, even without serious consequences, is an important risk marker for the murder of women12. In this situation, a joint map and the usual conflict advice can put the victim in danger. What to do: make a safety plan confidentially (a promise to change doesn’t replace it), and end the relationship from a distance or in writing; more in chapter 23.
  • Chest pain, shortness of breath, or fainting after a loss. Call an ambulance: the same signs also occur with other heart emergencies.
  • Grief that stays unbearable for a long time and gets in the way of living, and thoughts of suicide. This is a matter for a professional; there’s no need to wait for some deadline.

23. Violence and control: when skills don’t help

In short

What separates a fight from violence is not loudness but freedom. In a fight, each person can leave the room, say no, and keep their own view. When one person is afraid of the other — of surveillance, threats, punishment for saying “no” — the choices narrow, and quiet compliance can be a way of adapting to danger.

Then the skills from the neighboring chapters (naming a feeling, taking a pause, apologizing) don’t work as repair: they’re designed for two free people.

What to do: ask not “who shouted louder” but “who is afraid of whom, and what happens after a ‘no’” — and if there is fear, turn not to an exercise but to a support service.

Every evening Marta asks Ira to show her messages: “Just so I don’t work myself up.” At first Ira shows them with a smile. Then she starts deleting anything extra in advance. Then she writes to her friends less often — it’s easier that way. One day Ira covers the screen with her palm, and for three days Marta answers in one-word replies. Ira tells an acquaintance: “We just fight a lot. We need to learn to talk calmly.” No one shouts, no one raises a hand, so from the outside it looks like a fight between two people who lack skills. But less and less, Ira gets to decide for herself what to show and whom to talk to.

What is going on

A caveat: the studies we reviewed don’t show that control in a couple can be recognized by a bodily sign — not by pulse, not by breathing, not by tears. But there is a description of how a person may appraise what is happening, and it adds up to a familiar loop.

In a fight, the loop speeds up in both people. An expectation is broken, the body switches on alarm, the head fills in a meaning (“I’m being ignored”), the person raises their voice or goes quiet — and this becomes a trigger for the other (chapters 02, 15, and 16). But each of them has a way out: to leave, catch their breath, disagree — and not be punished for it.

Under control, the loop is different. When one person repeatedly monitors, isolates, and threatens, ordinary choices become unsafe. The other starts anticipating punishment: the body stays on guard, the meaning is “a mistake will cost me dearly,” the action is to guess ahead, give in, hide. This is how a victim may appraise danger, but it isn’t a diagnosis, and it isn’t a reaction everyone shares. From the outside, this kind of compliance looks like agreement, or like “we’ve started arguing less.”

Sometimes, for one of them, it’s not losing control but a choice. Domination holds up even without anger. The skills from chapters 03 and 04 — naming your arousal, taking a pause — help the person who blows up. If the goal is power, working only on anger leaves untouched the beliefs, the access to the other person, the retaliation, and the control tactics. This isn’t true of everyone who uses violence: the mechanisms differ (on the dark side of emotional intelligence, see chapter 06).

That’s why repair doesn’t work here. Repair (chapter 17) assumes two free people with a shared goal. If one can punish the other for honesty, a “conversation by the rules” falls into the same loop. Anger as a signal of a broken expectation (chapter 16) explains a fight, but not surveillance and threats: those are behavior, not an unmet expectation. Tears and remorse after an episode (chapter 05) are not a measure of change.

Three different pictures. The phrase “intimate partner violence” covers different things: violence as a system of domination — surveillance, isolation, threats, fear (this is called coercive control); outbursts in individual fights; and resistance to the person who controls. This isn’t a tested model but a way to organize observations. Its value is that it tells behavior and power apart, rather than loudness of voice1.

This explains part of the dispute over whether men and women are equally violent: population surveys more often capture outbursts, while support services see control and self-defense. Victims can be of any gender, in any kind of couple — both different-sex and same-sex.

How control works. Isolation removes the people who could check reality and help. Surveillance turns any message into a possible reason for punishment. The person complies to prevent the next episode. A fight becomes violence when a stable pattern takes away the ability to safely refuse and to communicate independently — whatever it’s called, “love” included.

Phone monitoring is one method of control, but the studies we reviewed have no data on how often it’s done in secret. One jealous remark doesn’t count as violence, while stalking is serious even without a single blow. What matters is not “is there monitoring” but how it’s set up: a shared calendar by agreement and a password handed over under pressure are different things.

Two signals of risk to life. The first is strangulation. Among women who had experienced violence, those who were killed or survived an attempt on their life had been strangled before several times more often2. The second is leaving a highly controlling partner: around the time of leaving, the risk is noticeably higher3. These are associations, not exact probabilities and not proven causes: it doesn’t mean that everyone who has been strangled will be killed, and not every breakup is dangerous. Both studies are solid, but they’re about women; for other genders and countries, the studies we reviewed contain no estimates of the same quality.

The conclusion: threats, strangulation, and escalating control are a reason for a confidential risk assessment and an individual safety plan, not for communication training.

Consensual choking during sex is a separate topic. The authors of a study of young women write that it’s common among young adults4. But the numbers above come from situations of violence and don’t carry over to consent; the studies we reviewed have no risk estimates for this kind of choking — neither in the direction of danger nor in the direction of safety.

Why people don’t leave right away. The decision to stay or leave is made up of constraints: children, housing, social circle, attachment, and fear pull in different directions. If leaving itself threatens retaliation, staying for a while may be a safety calculation, not consent to violence. (Attachment and violence — chapter 13; leaving in general — chapter 22.)

Programs for people who use violence. The results are modest: the effect on repeat violence is small overall, and where people were assigned at random, it’s even weaker5. By police records, those who completed a program have fewer repeat incidents, but by the victims’ own accounts there’s no visible improvement6. This gap is the main point: a police record is not the same as the safety the person next to them talks about.

“Completed the program” doesn’t mean “it’s safe now.” Couples therapy, if it helps at all, does so in carefully selected cases of outbursts during fights; this doesn’t carry over to control. While the person is attending a program, the victim has no reason to loosen their precautions.

If there are more than two of you. The studies we reviewed have no data on violence in consensual open relationships and in polyamory: neither how often it happens nor comparisons with monogamous couples. The questions “who is afraid of whom” and “can you say no” can be asked in a triad and in a network too, but the studies we reviewed contain no tests of such a transfer. The closest is a survey of women after childbirth in Thailand about unions where the husband has several wives: the authors write that there is more violence there than in monogamous ones, but that’s a different structure, and the survey says nothing about which form of relationship is more dangerous.

If you come from different cultures. In a small study, therapists who work with Chinese couples described the involvement of the partner’s relatives and clients who explain events instead of naming the feeling right away. These are therapists’ reports, not a survey: they say nothing about a specific person. The mainland, Taiwan, and Hong Kong are different contexts, and indirect speech is not dishonesty. No studies were found for “Russia or Europe — mainland China” couples, so ask each other: how is each of your families involved, what stays private, in which language do your hard conversations happen, and what does “telling someone” mean.

How to tell it is happening

In you, if you are the one being controlled:

  • You pick every word so as not to set off an outburst, and adjust to the other person’s mood in advance.
  • You delete, hide, cover the screen — not because of secrets, but so there won’t be consequences.
