  • You see and text the people close to you less often: “it’s easier that way.”
  • There are fewer arguments, but not because the two of you reached an agreement.
  • You’re afraid that being sincere will cost you.

In your partner:

  • They demand to see your messages, passwords, where you are — even if it’s “out of love.”
  • They decide whom you see and whom you call.
  • They punish you for refusing: with coldness, bans, threats.
  • They stalk you after a breakup.
  • There has been strangulation at least once.

What to do

There are no tested procedures here — this is an order of steps put together from what is known in pieces.

1. Questions about freedom, not loudness

When: after a hard conflict, when it’s unclear whether it was a fight or something else.

What to do: recall the last such conflict and answer in writing, separately from your partner; if you could be punished for being frank, don’t discuss it together. What happened — in actions, without judgments. Who was afraid of whom. Could you say “no,” leave the room, text a friend — and what used to happen after a refusal? Were there injuries, threats, punishment? Repeat this after noticeable conflicts until the picture becomes clear.

How you know it worked: your answers become more specific, and you can see where there’s a fight and where there’s fear and limits on freedom. Any “yes” about fear, threats, or punishment for refusing is a reason to go to the section “When it is no longer about skills,” not to conversation practice.

2. Access only by agreement

When: someone asks for access to a phone, passwords, location — or jealousy pulls you to check.

What to do: list the kinds of access: passwords, location, a shared calendar, reading messages. For each one, answer: did both of you decide on it yourselves; can it be withdrawn without consequences; what will happen after a refusal; is there a ban on contacts behind it? If the answers everywhere are “ourselves,” “yes,” “nothing will happen,” and there are no bans on contacts — it’s an agreement. Otherwise it’s control, whatever it’s called. If jealousy is looking for ways to check, talk about the anxiety and the boundaries in words, without demanding surveillance.

How you know it worked: any access can be withdrawn, and nothing happens.

3. If the control comes from you

When: as soon as you suspect the other person is afraid of you.

What to do: ask yourself whether the other person can refuse, and what happens after a refusal. Look not for an anger worksheet to work through on your own, but for a program with structure, accountability, and independent safety checks.

How you know it worked: not by attendance and not by your own rating of your anger, but by the other person’s account of their freedom and safety — given through an independent professional, not through you. And by the fact that there’s no new coercion. Words about improvement don’t replace the other person’s safety plan.

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: women and men are equally violent in couples.
In fact: that’s only partly true. In an American national survey, the shares of men and women who reported physical violence from a partner turned out almost equal7 (the studies we reviewed have no such data for China, Russia, and Europe). But a “yes” there doesn’t distinguish severity, injuries, sexual violence, or context. Victims of any gender need to be heard, and calling control a “symmetrical fight” puts a person at risk.

People think: jealousy and checking the phone are signs of love.
In fact: no. Jealousy shows that something has been hurt, not how much love there is. In one student survey, the more time people spent on social media, the more jealous they got over what they saw there8. Secret checks can speed up conflict, and for a victim of violence, forced access and surveillance are a threat.

People think: violence in a couple is just poorly controlled anger.
In fact: not only. Some outbursts grow out of a fight that keeps escalating, but surveillance, cutting off the ways out, and threats can’t be explained by anger1. “Working on anger together” when there is intimidation can expose the victim to retaliation — how often hasn’t been measured. First you need an assessment of control and safety.

Talk it over together

For couples where it’s safe for both of you to answer honestly. If you could be punished for being frank, don’t discuss this together — read the section below first.

  1. What happens between us when one of us says “no”? What happens after that?
  2. Which kinds of access to each other did we choose ourselves, and which appeared out of fear or pressure? Can each of us withdraw any of them without consequences?
  3. Which friends, relatives, and activities have been showing up less often in our life — and whose decision was that?
  4. What do I do when I’m angry, and what is safe for me to say if I’m scared?
  5. In which language is it easier for you to say “I’m scared” and “no” — and how can I tell it has been said, if words aren’t enough?
  6. If there are more than two of us: which of the other people in our system can each of us tell that they’re not doing well, without consequences?

When it is no longer about skills

It is no longer a question of communication if there are:

  • threats — to you or to people close to you;
  • strangulation, even once;
  • forced access to your phone, passwords, location; surveillance and stalking, including after a breakup;
  • bans and punishment for refusing, for meetings and calls; isolation from people close to you;
  • fear that sincerity will cost you, and a habit of adjusting to the other person’s mood in advance;
  • thoughts of suicide, in you or in your partner.

One sign is enough; there’s no need to wait for a blow. This applies to people of any gender and any form of relationship. What to do:

  • Don’t discuss the plan with a partner who controls you, and don’t demand that the person being monitored reveal their plan to the one controlling them.
  • Prepare privately: a safe device your partner has no access to; documents; trusted people; a local specialized intimate partner violence service. It will assess the risk and draw up an individual plan. These are safety recommendations, not a tested sequence.
  • The risk is higher around leaving a highly controlling partner, so leaving is planned together with the service, not decided in one sudden move.
  • Until the risk has been assessed, don’t go into joint therapy, “fair fights,” or the exercises from this book.
  • In immediate danger — threats, stalking, strangulation, a suicidal crisis — call your local emergency service or a protection hotline.
  • If the control comes from you: stop, and look for an accountable program with independent safety checks. A program by itself doesn’t make the relationship safe.

Part V. More than two: ethical non-monogamy

24. A map of forms: one word, different agreements

In short

The words “open relationship,” “polyamory,” and “swinging” name different agreements, and for two people the same word can easily mean different things.

The form of a relationship is not a type of person but a set of rules: who, what, how much time, and what gets told to others. Neither monogamy nor non-monogamy is the “norm” by default.

What to do about it: talk about the agreement, not the label; look at how each participant is doing; and read a strong emotion as a signal about an expectation it’s time to name out loud — not as an order to stay or to leave.

Timur and Sergei have been together for several years. Over dinner Sergei says: “Let’s be open.” Timur nods: what he hears is “we can go to parties separately, no names and no follow-up.” Sergei meant someone from work he’d like to spend evenings with and talk to late into the night. A month later they’re sitting at the same table again. Timur’s mouth has gone dry, Sergei is talking faster than usual, and each of them is sure the other one broke the agreement.

The word was the same, but the maps in their heads were different.

What is going on

First the body. Any proposal to change exclusivity (“only the two of us”) stirs something in the body: tension or excitement may come. What exactly pulse, breathing, and hormones do during the conversation itself, or during a partner’s real date with someone else, wasn’t measured in the studies we reviewed. So only general things can be said about the body in such moments. These data contain no lever that would set the form of a relationship: men in couples and fathers on average have lower testosterone than single and childless men, but that’s about being in a relationship at all, not about closed versus open; and for the most studied variants of the “attachment gene,” no noticeable effect on behavior in humans was found.

Then the meaning. The same tension can be read as an opportunity, a loss, a relief, or uncertainty. What you do depends on the reading: agree, refuse, go quiet, hide.

Your action is an event for the other person. It starts your partner’s own chain: their own body, their own meaning, their own response. In a couple, two such chains are hooked into each other. When there are several partners, you get a network: one person’s act lands in several chains at once, and each person has their own reading. Tears in such a conversation are a separate topic (chapter 05).

The main mistake is thinking a word means the same thing to both people. A form is an agreement: what has been agreed on, whom it concerns, how time and priority are divided, what is told and to whom.

The map

The umbrella term is consensual non-monogamy: departures from exclusivity that have been agreed on and that everyone they concern knows about. Within it, there are different things:

  • Monogamy — sex and romance only with each other.
  • “Monogamish” — a mostly closed relationship with agreed-on exceptions.
  • Open relationships — most often, sex with other people.
  • Swinging — most often, joint or “social” sexual encounters.
  • Polyamory — several close relationships at once, including romantic ones. It can be hierarchical (one relationship has priority agreed on in advance) or non-hierarchical (no priority is assumed in advance).
  • Solo polyamory — autonomy, without a partner you’re assumed in advance to live with.
  • Relationship anarchy — questioning hierarchies between relationships that are set in advance.

These are working descriptions, not diagnoses: in practice they overlap. There isn’t enough data on solo polyamory, non-hierarchical polyamory, and relationship anarchy: not on how many people live this way, not on long-term outcomes, not on tested ways of coping with jealousy. The map describes; it doesn’t rank.

Infidelity is not a square on the map but a violation. The difference from agreed non-monogamy is whether the people it concerns know what’s happening and agree to it. Consent and enjoying every event are not the same thing, and whether consent is free can’t be determined from the word “polyamory.” A violation is possible inside an agreed form too, if something is done beyond what was agreed on (chapter 21).

Who lives this way

A noticeable share of single Americans have at some point lived in some form of consensual non-monogamy1. That’s “ever,” not “now,” and it covers any non-monogamy, not only polyamory; how many people live this way today is less well known. Among single Americans, the wish to try polyamory was more common in men and in sexual minorities, but that’s an association across groups, not everyone’s need. Research doesn’t show a single “non-monogamous personality,” or a shared motive among swingers and people in solo polyamory: it’s more reliable to ask the person than to work out their motive from gender or orientation.

How people do in different forms

Satisfaction. On average, people in non-monogamous and monogamous relationships are about equally satisfied with the relationship and with sex2. But the studies compared groups, not couples before and after opening up; the samples are mostly Western, many were recruited online, and relationships that ended didn’t make it into them. The average also hides the network: additional relationships change time, priority, and novelty, and each person judges the change through their own agreement, so look at each participant.

Stigma. If the people around you see the arrangement as a flaw, a partner may expect rejection, hide the relationships, and lose candid support. The path is plausible but not proven. In several experiments with descriptions of couples, people rated non-monogamous relationships worse than monogamous ones3; this is preliminary. There is little data on discrimination and health.

Swinging. A joint encounter can bring novelty and closeness, and mismatched expectations can bring anxiety and resentment; the other person’s reaction decides whether you move toward a conversation or toward secrecy. These are hypotheses. Studies only see those who stayed, so “swingers have more stable relationships” is untested, and nobody has tested swinging as a way to strengthen a marriage. The samples there are heterosexual; there are no conclusions for same-sex couples.

If there are more than two of you. In an online survey of people in polyamory who chose to respond, primary partners got higher ratings for commitment, satisfaction, and communication. But these are people’s own ratings; it hasn’t been proven that the hierarchy produces this. Everyone’s consent doesn’t even out resources: time, attention, and priority are divided unevenly, and a partner’s other relationships bring both benefits and costs. Time, families, and children — chapter 29; jealousy — 25; joy at another’s romance — 26.

If you come from different cultures. There are no tested scripts for bilingual negotiations about the form of a relationship, just as there are no data on couples where one person is from Russia or Europe and the other from mainland China. Surveys on China show norms and infidelity, not agreed non-monogamy: in one of them, most people in couples considered sex outside marriage unacceptable, while among married people extramarital sex became noticeably more common over time. A study of heterosexual spouses from northern China linked similar expectations and the couple’s communication to marital satisfaction; the practical takeaway: let each person name their expectations directly. A “Chinese style” of communication, attitudes toward non-monogamy, or the role of family can’t be derived from this (chapters 33, 37, 38).

How to tell it is happening

In yourself:

  • You’re sure “but we agreed,” yet you can’t retell the agreement in your own words.
  • Your body switched on during the conversation about other people, and you want to go quiet, argue, or leave.
  • It’s unclear who gets how much time and who has what priority.
  • You hide the relationships from relatives or coworkers out of fear of judgment.

In your partner:

  • They hear something else in the same words, like Timur.
  • They believe you’re the one who broke the agreement.
  • They go quiet, avoid the topic, or, on the contrary, talk faster than usual.
  • In a network, one person is sagging: this participant gets less time, or their priority is unclear.

What to do

1. Talk about the agreement, not the label

When: someone says “let’s be open” or suggests changing a rule — before you agree or refuse.

What to do: instead of the word, ask about the content: who, what, how much time, what gets told to others. Ask each person separately what “relationship,” “open,” “family,” and “faithfulness” mean, where you should live, and in which language it’s easier for you to talk about this. Don’t put anger down to nationality: find out what exactly was agreed on.

How you know it worked: each of you retells the agreement in your own words, and the retellings match.

2. Three columns

When: when changing a rule, or after an event that made someone angry — before calling the reaction jealousy.

What to do: each of you, separately and in writing, takes one incident or rule and fills in three columns: “what happened,” “what was expected,” “what it would take for this kind of change to become acceptable.” Swap sheets and read them silently, without objecting. Mark where they differ and write down one sentence of agreement that each of you can retell in your own words. If there are more than two of you, each person fills in a sheet for every connection they’re part of. If you can’t make out your body’s signals, start with events and actions.

How you know it worked: before and after the conversation, each of you separately retells the agreement, and the retellings match.

Honestly: this technique was built from logic; it hasn’t been tested on people in non-monogamy. The closest study only linked similar expectations and the couple’s communication to marital satisfaction, and that’s not proof that retelling helps.

3. A check-in on each participant

When: every few weeks; pick the rhythm yourselves.

What to do: each person separately answers the same five questions on a scale from one to five: satisfaction with the relationship with each of their partners; trust; sexual well-being; whether there’s enough time and whether priority is clear; whether the agreement is clear. Don’t discuss the answers until everyone has written them down; then compare with last time.

How you know it worked: you can see how each person changes over time, not an average number. A drop for one person is a reason to talk, not a verdict.

Honestly: this kind of check-in, its schedule, and its scale haven’t been tested, let alone for networks. The closest thing is an online program for couples, after which the couples themselves reported their relationships had improved, but it was tested on heterosexual couples from the US, not on networks.

4. When it hits you, find the expectation

When: during a conversation about the form, your body switched on and you want to go quiet, argue, or leave.

What to do: note what’s happening in your body and what action you want to take first. If your body is silent, take the first action that came to mind. Then ask yourself: which expectation is being touched right now? Name it out loud. A strong emotion here is a signal, not an order to keep the relationship or to leave.

How you know it worked: you can name the expectation in words, not only through a reaction: silence, arguing, walking away.

5. Agree on what to tell and to whom

When: before talking to relatives, friends, coworkers — or when someone is already hiding things.

What to do: decide together whom you tell what about your form and what in this is safe for each of you. Whether to be open about it or not is a question of safety and preference, not a test of authenticity. Fear of judgment doesn’t mean a person is incapable of attachment.

How you know it worked: each of you can say what to tell and to whom, and everyone is safe with that.

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: biology has already decided: humans are monogamous by nature — or, on the contrary, they’re not, by nature.
In fact: according to family trees and DNA from families in the Netherlands and Belgium over several centuries, on average about one child in a hundred was born to someone other than the husband in the marriage4. This is about paternity, not about agreed non-monogamy, but it doesn’t support the slogan “everyone cheats by nature.” The opposite slogan is shaky too: in most societies from one cross-cultural list, polygyny was allowed, yet even there most marriages were with a single spouse5 (a summary of descriptions, poorly checked). People choose the form; biology doesn’t prescribe it.

People think: open relationships always fall apart, and monogamy is healthier.
In fact: the word “always” is refuted by relationships that last, and on average satisfaction is almost the same2. Whether people stay together is a separate question that can’t be derived from these estimates. One national survey in the US found lower satisfaction among “open” couples6: the picture depends on how you sort relationships into forms. These data don’t show that monogamy is “healthier” either. Neither “bound to fall apart” nor “bound to work out” has been measured.

People think: the people who go into non-monogamy are the ones afraid of closeness.
In fact: among monogamous and single people, avoidance of closeness is linked to willingness to consider such a form, but not to actually practicing it, and among those who already live this way, it turned out lower, not higher, in one of the samples7. This is preliminary. Instead of a diagnosis, it’s better to ask about reliability, consent, and responsiveness.

Talk it over together

  1. Which words do we use as if they meant the same thing — “open,” “faithfulness,” “relationship” — and what does each one mean to you?
  2. What have we agreed on out loud, and what has each of us filled in in our own heads?
  3. What will change for you if our form changes: what could you gain, what could you lose, what would be a relief, and where is the uncertainty?
  4. What do you notice in your body, and what do you want to do, when we talk about this? If your body is silent, what action comes to mind first?
  5. Which of the people close to us do we tell what about our form, and what in this is safe for each of us?
  6. By what signs will each of us know the agreement is working, and when will we come back to it?

When it is no longer about skills

A conversation about form stops being a conversation when it includes:

  • consent under pressure: out of fear, under threat, or under blackmail (how often this happens is unknown);
  • surveillance of a partner or of other participants in the relationship, threats, coercive control.

Then don’t do the exercises together, and don’t negotiate where it’s unsafe to speak freely. Safety comes first: reach out to specialized domestic violence support services (chapter 23). If repeated checking (“what if…”) seriously gets in the way of living, you need an assessment from a professional who looks specifically at obsessive symptoms.

25. Jealousy: taking it apart

In short

Jealousy is not one feeling but three layers: suspicions in your head, pain inside, and actions such as checking. They don’t always come together.

The body doesn’t say what it’s about. You fill in the meaning yourself: a loss of time, of your place, of trust, of self-confidence — or simply a broken agreement.

What to do: take the episode apart piece by piece, find out what exactly had been agreed on, and cool down before you talk. Don’t count checking and surveillance as love.

Ira and Liza have been together a long time, and their relationship is open. On Thursday Liza says she’s going on a date with Mei on Saturday — on exactly the day they had been saving “for the two of us.” “Of course, go,” Ira answers. In the kitchen she washes the same cup over and over: her palms are hot, her mouth is dry. Her hand reaches for the phone on its own — to see when Mei was last online. Liza hears the silence and asks from the doorway: “Are you okay?” — “Everything’s fine.” Both of them go to bed thinking the other one turned away first.

There was no fight. But the distance has already happened.

What is going on

This is a scheme for taking things apart, not a proven sequence in everyone who feels jealous. It all starts with a trigger: an unfamiliar name on the screen, a meeting time that got moved, a partner’s new date, a rule that changed without warning. Then come the body, the meaning, and the action.

The body. Science hasn’t found a bodily fingerprint of jealousy: no hormone, no sign in pulse or sweating, no pattern on a brain scan that would hold across different people and different agreements. But something next door to it has been studied — rejection. A couple of minutes is enough: in a computer game, strangers stop passing you the ball, and your sense of belonging, your self-esteem, and your sense of control drop sharply. This holds for people of any gender, age, and country1. And in one experiment after an unwanted breakup, a photo of the ex activated not only the “emotional” areas of the brain but also those that work when you burn your hand2. These are experiments on rejection, not on jealousy; carrying them over to jealousy is still a guess. On tears in a scene like this — chapter 5.

The meaning. Arousal doesn’t say what it’s about. The head fills it in: lost access to the partner, broken trust, inequality, a blow to self-esteem. In lab experiments where a partner, as part of the task, left for someone else, the main link was the blow to self-esteem, and jealousy led to aggression toward the rival. It’s possible that behind jealousy there is anger at a broken expectation — not being warned about a change of plans — rather than how attractive the third person is. This comes from looking at individual cases, not from a measured effect (chapter 16).

The action. Asking, checking the phone, leaving, responding coldly, threatening. For the other person, it’s a new trigger: a question sounds like distrust, checking like surveillance, silence like distance. The second person’s response starts the next turn. If there are several of you, the turn goes through each person. On listening to the body and finding words for feelings — chapters 2 and 3.

One word — several feelings. Within jealousy, researchers separate suspicions (thoughts), the emotional reaction, and behavior (checking, questioning). They don’t always come together: in one study, emotional jealousy was linked to love differently than suspiciousness was. And it isn’t envy: envy is about someone else’s desirable advantage, while jealousy is fear of losing an important relationship and anger at a rival. It hasn’t been shown that shame or anger is present in every case, so ask about each part separately: what happened, what your body did, what loss you’re filling in, what you do next.

Who gets more jealous. There is no single cause. Genes explain only part of the differences, about a quarter to a third. Attachment, self-esteem, and insecurity are likely companions, but this hasn’t been tested. An attractive female rival bothers women a little more than an attractive male rival bothers men; the idea that a “dominant” rival bothers men more wasn’t confirmed. But these are imagined scenes, not behavior in real life.

If there are more than two of you. A partner’s other date can mean less shared time, a shift in emotional priority, a broken agreement about health, a shift in the hierarchy. After that, the interpretation decides, not the mere fact of another person: a request for reassurance, revisiting the rules, and surveillance lead to different responses. According to surveys of polyamorous people themselves, “primary” and “secondary” partners have different experiences. People weren’t assigned to these roles at random, but it shows why a change in priority can hurt. Alongside jealousy, compersion can live there too — joy for a partner who is happy with someone else (chapter 26). Both feelings can occur at once, and it was the joy, not the jealousy, that was linked to satisfaction with the relationship.

Open relationships, swinging, and polyamory are set up differently. There are no data on solo polyamory, relationships without hierarchy, and relationship anarchy, and none on couples of women either (chapters 27 and 29).

How to tell it is happening

In yourself:

  • Your thoughts circle around one name, one time, or one changed plan.
  • Your body switched on before words did: heat, emptiness, a dry mouth, your hands busy with whatever is around.
  • You’re pulled to check: the phone, “last seen online,” location.
  • You say “everything’s fine,” but inside it isn’t.
  • If your body is quiet and your mind is fast, don’t try to squeeze a sensation out of your body: start with the event and the action — what was done, or what you wanted to do right away.

In your partner:

  • They ask, check, go quiet, leave — and you read it as distrust, surveillance, distance.
  • They ask for reassurance or respond coldly.
  • Each person’s response becomes a new trigger for the other, and the circle keeps going.

What to do

Honestly: what reduces jealousy best hasn’t been properly tested. There is a general online program for couples: it improved the relationship and the well-being of both partners, but it isn’t a treatment for jealousy, and there were no non-monogamous networks in the trial. There are no trials for jealousy with several partners, and no tested ranking of responses either. Below is a proposed mechanism: revisit the interpretation, ask calmly, notice the reaction, and don’t act right away.

1. Take the episode apart

When: after an episode, once it has subsided. Five to ten minutes, in writing.

What to do: write it down in order.

  1. The event: what happened — the way a camera would record it, without conclusions.
  2. The body: what changed — tension, heat, emptiness, breathing. If your body is silent, skip this item.
  3. The meaning: what loss or broken expectation got filled in — time, priority, trust, self-esteem, an agreement. Write it as a guess.
  4. The action: what happened next — a question, checking, silence, leaving.
  5. The other person’s reaction: what your partner did or said.
  6. Three ratings from 0 to 10: distress, suspicion, urge to check.

Repeat after each episode until a pattern shows through. If the notes turn into checking or surveillance, stop.

How you know it worked: fewer unnecessary checks, a clearer agreement. “Zero jealousy” isn’t the measure.

The technique hasn’t been tested: all that was studied is that jealousy has separate layers. It hasn’t been tested as a diary, let alone as a treatment.

2. A pause until the body cools down

When: you notice a signal in your body, and the conversation starts speeding up.

What to do:

  1. Say out loud: “I need a pause. I’ll come back to this conversation.”
  2. Do something calm that lowers arousal specifically for you, rather than revving it up (chapter 4).
  3. Name a time and come back. Discuss the expectation itself, not a verdict on the person.

How you know it worked: you came back to the conversation.

For anger in general, activities like these noticeably reduced anger and aggression3. This hasn’t been tested on jealousy in couples or in non-monogamy. Endless avoidance and keeping quiet about safety worries weren’t studied as an outcome: a pause is not a way to disappear.

3. Talk about the specific agreement

When: once it has subsided. One time, for about twenty to thirty minutes.

What to do:

  1. Each of you separately writes one sentence: “What, as I understand it, we agreed on.”
  2. Compare the sheets. A mismatch is a result, not an accusation.
  3. Take turns: what happened, what was expected, what changed.
  4. One specific request in a calm tone: “It would help me if…” — one action and a time frame.
  5. Before answering, retell what you heard and ask: “Is that right?”

How you know it worked: before and after the conversation, each of you independently describes the same agreement, and the topic comes back less often.

This script hasn’t been tested in non-monogamy or in intercultural couples. The closest support is a study of Chinese couples: where their views on relationships matched, the couples themselves rated their communication higher. If there are threats or coercive control, it isn’t safe to speak freely — then this isn’t the place for it.

4. Ask each person; don’t speak for them

When: there are more than two of you, or you come from different cultures.

What to do: if there are more than two of you, ask each person affected separately: in one study of male couples, a noticeable share of couples described their agreement differently. If you come from different cultures, ask each person what “relationship” and “exclusivity” mean, whether to tell family, where to live, in which language to argue. Retell your partner’s meaning in your own words: that way you can see whether the argument is about language, about duty to family, or about a broken promise. Don’t put a sharp reaction to changed expectations down to nationality — look at the agreement itself first (chapters 32 and 33).

How you know it worked: the argument stops being “about everything” and becomes about one named thing.

There are no data on couples where one partner is Russian-speaking or from Europe and the other is from mainland China; and there are no tested bilingual scripts for talking about non-monogamy either.

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: jealousy is proof of love.
In fact: partly. Emotional jealousy really is linked to what people report about love. But the strength of jealousy doesn’t measure love, and checking and control are already actions, not a feeling. Where intimate partner violence was studied (perpetrators men, victims women), jealousy together with control marked its most dangerous form4. “I’m jealous, so I love you” easily justifies control, although how often that happens hasn’t been measured (chapter 23).

People think: men are hurt by the sex, women by the feelings; that’s how nature made it.
In fact: partly. In a forced choice of “which hurts more,” heterosexual men more often name sexual infidelity, but among bisexual people, gay men, and lesbians there’s no difference between the sexes5. In a comparison of several societies, a sex difference in the forced choice turned up everywhere, but the strength of jealousy varied a lot, so this is an adjustable reaction, not a rigid automatic one. In one study, attachment style predicted the type of jealousy better than sex did. The debate goes on: some consider the difference stable, others think it exists only in a hypothetical choice.

People think: a hormone, a spray, or a brain scan will show how to treat jealousy.
In fact: for cycle hormones this has been refuted; the rest hasn’t been confirmed. In young women whose hormones were measured across their cycle, anxious jealousy — worry about a possible threat — didn’t track those hormones6. The idea that “ovulation pushes a woman to stray” found no support. A strong link did turn up with medications: in one study of people with parkinsonism who were taking dopamine drugs, delusional jealousy was several times more common7. Oxytocin isn’t a button: in one study, nasal drops may have reduced jealousy, but this is not a confirmed treatment.

People think: people in non-monogamous relationships are less jealous.
In fact: partly. These people reported less distress about some of their partner’s imagined relationships, but more frequent thoughts about their partner’s other relationships8. “Less” depends on which feeling you ask about. The groups were recruited differently and the scenes were imagined — neither “immunity” nor “the same jealousy” follows from this.

Talk it over together

  1. What exactly happened — and what did each of us consider our agreement before that?
  2. What was your body doing at that moment, and if it was silent, what was done or what did you want to do right away?
  3. What loss came up in your head first: of time, of your place, of trust, of self-confidence, of a promise given?
  4. Which of my actions — questions, checking, silence, jokes — became an alarm signal for you?
  5. What one specific change would help, and what part of it is doable?
  6. If there are more than two of us: what has changed for each person — time, disclosure, closeness, priority — and whom have we not asked?

When it is no longer about skills

Everything above is for ordinary jealousy, where both of you are generally safe. Three cases are different.

Jealousy together with control. Signs: demanding passwords, messages, and location; secretly checking the phone; tracking through apps (surveillance through technology is a separate form of intimate partner violence); banning contact with others and punishing it; threatening you, themselves, or others; violence has already happened. This combination marked the most dangerous form of violence; these are data on couples where the perpetrator is a man, and there are no pooled data for other couples. What to do: until you’re safe, don’t do joint exercises or “negotiations”; see a specialist in intimate partner violence and make a safety plan (chapters 23 and 35).

Certainty of infidelity despite the facts. Sign: a persistent conviction that no evidence changes, especially alongside a new medication (for example, in parkinsonism), dementia, psychosis, or a serious physical illness. People with this conviction were noticeably more often aggressive when admitted to a psychiatric clinic. In one study of people with dementia, it was often preceded by a serious physical illness, and after treatment it went away in most of them. What to do: get the person seen by a doctor, and don’t argue with the “facts.”

Obsessive doubts and checking. Sign: questions about your partner’s feelings or past come back after every answer, and checking and asking for confirmation noticeably get in the way of living. A question about an agreement that was actually broken has an answer; an obsessive one doesn’t go away when it gets an answer. What to do: get an assessment specifically for relationship-related obsessive conditions. How common this is among adults in general is unknown: the studies were done on non-clinical groups and students.

26. Compersion and new relationship energy

In short

Compersion is joy for your partner when they’ve formed a closeness with someone else. Researchers have learned to describe it, but the studies reviewed don’t show how common it is or whether it can be learned. Joy and jealousy can easily live in you at the same time.

Even less is known about new relationship energy — the lift at the start of a new connection: the closest study doesn’t even show how long it lasts.

What to do: write down joy and jealousy separately, write out how much time and how many commitments each person has, respond to your partner’s good news with genuine interest, and don’t demand joy as proof of consent.

These are reasonable techniques, not a proven treatment: try them and watch what changes in your loop.

Mara comes back from another evening with Inga and doesn’t try to hide how her eyes are shining. Lena listens, laughs in the right places, and really is glad: she hasn’t seen Mara come alive like this in a long time. At the same time something tightens under her ribs: on Tuesdays they always cooked together, and tonight Lena ate dinner in silence. “That’s great,” she says in a flat voice, and Mara hears the voice before the words. The story breaks off mid-sentence. Both feel the conversation has taken a wrong turn, and neither can explain where exactly. ### What is going on

First the body — and here there’s a gap. In the studies reviewed, compersion and new connections were studied with questionnaires, interviews and surveys: people were asked, not measured. Heart rate, breathing and hormones weren’t looked at in these studies. So stories about a “hormone of joy for your partner” or “the brain area responsible for it” have nothing behind them yet. Everything about the body here is a hypothesis you test on yourself: what’s going on in your chest, stomach, throat and breathing when your partner tells you about a date. Learning to notice this is chapter 02. Tears during a story like that are a signal too: read it together, without rushing to a conclusion (chapter 05).

Then meaning. The news gets read two ways at once: “my partner is happy” and “there’ll be less time for me.” The first feeds joy, the second feeds anxiety, and neither pushes the other out. If anger comes up, behind it there’s most often a broken expectation — “that’s not what we agreed” (chapter 16) — not a verdict on your partner.

Then action. Ask, stay quiet, make a joke, ask to move a date, be happy together. “That’s great” in a flat voice reads differently from “tell me what was funny.” Mara hears the flat voice and cuts her story short; Lena hears a short story and asks fewer questions. The loops wind each other up, and no ill will is needed for that.

If there are more than two of you, the chain is longer: each pair’s loops are linked, and one person’s reaction reaches the metamour through the partner (that’s what your partner’s partner is called). You can step in the same way: notice the body signal, name both feelings, see the expectation behind them, and choose an action instead of a reaction (chapters 03 and 04).

What compersion is. A warm feeling about your partner’s closeness with others. A philosophical analysis clarifies: it’s aimed at the flourishing of the person you love and can’t be reduced to pride, to pleasure lived vicariously in that person’s place, or to a masochistic enjoyment of someone else’s closeness. A scale was built for compersion, but it’s a way to measure it, not to count how often it happens. Interviews were done with people selected precisely for having this experience: that works for describing the experience, not for counting how often it occurs. These people named the conditions under which joy showed up: security in the relationship, good communication, warm feelings toward metamours. Hence the principle: mixed feelings are material for a conversation. No joy doesn’t mean consent has been broken. Neither does jealousy next to joy.

What new relationship energy is. A surge of infatuation at the start of a new connection; “energy” here is a label, not a physical quantity. Attention, time and openness flow toward the new person. A long-standing partner may read this as losing priority, or may be glad — and the answer changes the next loop for everyone. Neither outcome is predetermined. There’s little data: the closest study is a one-time survey of volunteers. It doesn’t show how long the lift lasts, what happens to long-standing partners over time, or whether scheduled check-ins about the state of the relationship help. On falling in love itself — chapter 10.

Can you steer this? In one survey people believed love in general can be turned up but not turned down. But beliefs aren’t skills. In an experiment that recorded brain activity, focusing on the bad sides of a loved one noticeably weakened feelings, while focusing on the good sides strengthened them only slightly, and even that was noticeable only for attachment. The samples are small and not about open relationships: there’s no direct data on whether new relationship energy can be steered.

How to tell it is happening

In yourself:

  • You’re happy, and at the same time something tightens: in your chest, stomach, throat.
  • You say “that’s great” in a flat voice, but inside it isn’t flat.
  • The news reads two ways at once: “my partner is happy” and “there’ll be less time for me.”
  • Suspicion shows up; you feel the pull to check their phone, messages, location.

In your partner:

  • Their story breaks off mid-sentence or gets shorter each time.
  • They answer in the same flat voice or ask fewer questions.
  • Later it turns out each of you understood the agreement your own way.

What to do

The first four techniques are reasonable but poorly tested. The fifth is covered separately.

1. Write down the event, the body, the meaning, the action

When: news about your partner stirred up mixed feelings.

What to do: one entry per event. What happened. What you notice in your body (if your body is silent, start with the event and the action, and add the sensations later). What meaning you’re filling in: what you’re afraid of or what you’re hoping for. What was done in response and how your partner responded. Separately, rate on your own scale your joy, heaviness, suspicion and urge to check. If anger is front and center, before reading it as jealousy, have each person name: what was agreed, what was assumed, what changed.

How you know it worked: over time, fewer unwanted checks and a clearer agreement. How many entries you need, and whether it helps at all, hasn’t been studied: splitting jealousy into parts was tested as a way of measuring, not as a treatment. The tips about a silent body and about anger are a reasonable adjustment, not something shown to work for people who have trouble hearing their body, or for anger over a broken expectation. If the journal turns into surveillance of yourself or your partner — stop.

2. Respond to good news with genuine interest

When: your partner shares something pleasant, including about another person.

What to do: ask for details, don’t turn the conversation to yourself, and don’t brush it off. Doing this in ordinary conversations is enough: how much is needed is unknown. Don’t demand joy: delight can’t be a condition of consent.

How you know it worked: each of you notes whether sharing felt warm.

What it rests on: research on how partners respond to good news in ordinary couples — not every warm response is equally good. The technique hasn’t been tested for joy about someone else’s romance. Joy at someone else’s good fortune depends more on closeness than sympathy for someone else’s misfortune does1. By analogy, joy for your partner may depend on closeness with the metamour, but that’s a guess.

3. Check your agreement and write out the time

When: before a new connection, during it, and when the agreement is about to change.

What to do: each of you separately and in writing records what happened, what was expected, and what it would take to change the agreement. Then you compare notes. While you’re at it, write out how much free time and how many commitments each person has.

How you know it worked: before and after the conversation, each of you independently retells the agreement, and the retellings match.

What it rests on: among married couples in northern China, similar expectations, communication and marital satisfaction went together2. The script itself hasn’t been tested in open or intercultural couples; writing out time is a practical check, not a treatment.

If you come from different cultures — say, one of you from mainland China and the other from Russia or Europe — the studies reviewed have no data either on compersion or on a tested bilingual script for negotiating. So ask each person: what the words “relationship” and “exclusivity” mean, what to tell family, where to live, which language to discuss hard things in. A “Chinese communication style” or an attitude toward non-monogamy doesn’t follow from nationality. If your partner is angry about a changed agreement, look for the cause in the agreement itself, not in nationality (chapters 33, 37 and 38).

4. If there are more than two of you, ask each person separately

When: in a network of three or more people, and especially when you’re tempted to call someone’s reaction jealousy.

What to do: ask each person separately about time and priorities. Before reading a reaction as jealousy, find out what was agreed and what was assumed (on agreements — chapter 27, on time and priorities in a network — chapter 29).

How you know it worked: after the separate conversations you can say what each person expects, and you’re not guessing from reactions.

Why separately: consent doesn’t make everyone’s position equal. In a large survey of volunteers in polyamory, commitment, satisfaction and investment differed between the primary and the secondary partner3, and a new connection can redistribute time and attention. The survey shows the differences, but not how they arose. Open relationships, swinging and polyamory are different forms; results from one don’t carry over to the others, and the primary partner’s assessment doesn’t stand in for everyone else’s. For forms without a “main” partner or a hierarchy, the studies reviewed have no comparisons between forms, no long-term outcomes, and no tested ways to work with jealousy; there’s no separate tested effect for same-sex couples either. And in these studies it’s more often one person answering, not everyone it concerns. When and how to open a relationship — chapter 28; on forms — chapter 24.

5. If you want a ready-made structure, try an online program for couples

When: you’re ready to take on one problem together.

What to do: together, pick one problem, go through the exercises for understanding and for change, and during the program have a few short calls with a coach. This is the only technique here that was compared with a waitlist group: in American mixed-sex couples, the relationship and each person’s well-being improved by their own reports4. But it was tested on couples who met the recruitment criteria — not on open relationships and not on a “China–Europe” couple. Its effect on jealousy in non-monogamy hasn’t been established.

How you know it worked: answer the same questions about the relationship again and see whether the chosen problem comes back. If threats or control make it hard to speak freely, the program isn’t a fit (see the section “When it is no longer about skills”).

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: joy for your partner and jealousy work like a scale: more of one, less of the other.
In fact: in surveys that’s often how it comes out: in one of them, people living by mutual agreement with several partners had higher joy for their partner and lower jealousy from fear of losing exclusivity than monogamous people did5. But a link within a sample isn’t a law. Reactions to situations like these can be warm too, not only jealous. On top of that, people in polyamory used “jealousy” as an umbrella for insecurity, possessiveness and unmet needs; as a rule, they told their partner about it to get acknowledgment and reassurance, not to push for changes in behavior6. So write down both feelings separately and don’t demand that one erase the other. On jealousy — chapter 25.

People think: falling in love switches your brain off.
In fact: in young people who had recently fallen in love, a task on suppressing interference didn’t show the expected link — the stronger the infatuation, the worse the control7. The authors allow that the effect may appear only when a person is actively thinking about the one they love. One measure, a small sample: an argument against the “mind switched off” image, but not proof that your head is always clear.

People think: if there’s no difference in satisfaction, everything else is the same too.
In fact: a pooled review of many studies does find almost zero difference in satisfaction between people living by mutual agreement with several partners and monogamous people8. But the estimate leaves room for small differences and for harm in particular groups, and the studies are observational: people chose their relationship form themselves. That commitment, security and stability of couples are the same isn’t confirmed by the data.

People think: compersion can be learned; you just need to train joy.
In fact: there’s no tested answer. Interviews name conditions but don’t show that anyone learned it; a link between mindfulness and compersion in a survey doesn’t prove that training works; the studies reviewed haven’t tried randomly assigning people to compersion training. In one study people with experience of polyamory named their strengths as handling emotions, communication and lessons from past relationships, but that’s self-description, not a test. Training joy for others added well-being, but not in couples and not regarding jealousy. A philosophical analysis suggests softening the tendency toward jealousy and training attention to the other person’s well-being. The possible harm of mandatory “training” is an assumption: someone who’s struggling gets shame on top, for not feeling joy.

Talk it over together

  1. What do you notice in your body when I talk about another person — before you’ve had time to name the feeling?
  2. What do you hear in my joy: “my partner is happy” or “there’ll be less time for me”? Is it ever both at once?
  3. What do you think we agreed on, and what did each of us fill in on our own? Let’s write it down separately and compare.
  4. How is it easier for you to get news from me — right away or later, briefly or in detail? What in my response feels like pressure?
  5. What part of our time and attention do you want to keep unchanged while the new connection is going on? How will we notice that it’s stopped working?
  6. What does each of us need from the metamour — getting to know them, distance, just information?

When it is no longer about skills

Threats, monitoring messages and whereabouts, coercive control — these are signs not of jealousy but of control. In that case the joint exercises in this chapter aren’t a fit: it isn’t safe to speak freely. Safety and specialized support come first (chapter 23).

Repeated checking — of the phone, messages, location, your partner’s past — that noticeably gets in the way of life, and intrusive thoughts you can’t shake: you need an assessment from a specialist who works specifically with obsessive symptoms.

27. Agreements and honesty: rules, boundaries, secrets

In short

An agreement in an open relationship more often breaks down not because there are too few rules, but because two people read the same words differently. The type of agreement, strict or loose, explains little. There are fewer breaches where there’s more trust and communication and where the two people understand the agreement the same way.

What to do: have each person write down the agreement separately and compare; separate three things — what you can keep to yourself, what’s worth telling, and one person’s right to end the other’s relationship, which affects someone who isn’t at the table; check mismatches by talking, not by reading faces.

A caveat: there’s little data — small surveys of people who volunteered, more often about couples of men. These are guidelines, not norms.

Ira and Mei recently opened their relationship and agreed in a few words: “No sleepovers.” On Friday a message comes in: “Staying at Liu’s, don’t wait up.” Ira reads it in the kitchen. Her heart is in her throat, her palms go cold, her breathing gets shallow. For Ira, “no sleepovers” meant “you don’t stay anywhere until morning.” For Mei it meant “you don’t bring anyone to our place.” Ira answers with one word: “ok.” Mei reads that as permission and writes: “Thank you, you’re the best.” Looking at her phone, Ira feels like she’s being laughed at.

Each of them has her own agreement in her head.

What is going on

The same loop, except the event is read through the agreement. A message or an invitation arrives, a new person appears — or there’s silence where you expect to hear how it went. The body responds before words do: your heart pounds, your palms go cold, your breathing is shallow. Your head fills in the meaning: “the agreement’s been broken” or “I can be replaced,” “it’s nothing” or “I’m being lied to.” Then action: confess, stay quiet, attack, write “ok.” For the other person this is a new event, and the two loops wind each other up. The complication: each of you may have your own agreement.

Little is known about the body here. Studies of agreements didn’t measure heart rate, breathing or hormones, so this chain is a working model, not a measured fact. There’s a hypothesis that rejection partly touches the same brain areas as physical pain, but the debate is open: it’s too early to explain “it hurt” by a single brain area.

The weak link is meaning. Anger here is more often a signal of a broken expectation than a verdict (chapter 16), and two people’s expectations can differ. So first compare which promise each of you thought had been made, and only then decide who broke what. Tears in a conversation like this — chapter 05.

An agreement is a conversation, not a piece of paper. It has to survive a breach, so it needs rules for revising it, not just prohibitions. A boundary is what a person does themselves (“I don’t stay over if…”). An agreement is a mutual promise. A rule is an instruction where you can see whether it’s followed (“not at home,” “always a barrier”). This makes talking easier, but it’s practitioners’ language: research hasn’t compared these distinctions.

What is known. In a study following couples of men, breaches of the agreement happened for a noticeable share of participants, less often where there was more trust, communication, commitment and support from the people around them1. Things were worse where the two had different strengths of feeling and different styles of fighting: there the agreement itself diverged more often too. The type of agreement explains little, and different surveys come to different results; but in one of the surveys satisfaction was higher among those whose actual arrangement matched the one they wanted. And in a small survey of young couples, most had at least one rule broken, and many kept rules that had never been said out loud.

Consent under pressure. An indirect hint: among college students, casual sex for unfree reasons (pressure on oneself, “it just happened”) went together with low self-esteem, anxiety and depression, while sex out of one’s own desire went with nothing bad2. There’s no direct research on consent to opening a relationship under pressure.

How to tell it is happening

In yourself:

  • Your heart pounds at a message, or at silence where you expect to hear how it went.
  • You’re completely sure one thing was promised and something else happened — and it’s hard to remember how exactly you agreed.
  • Thoughts about a secret, or “what if I get replaced,” keep coming back.
  • You want to tell from your partner’s face or voice whether they’re lying.

In your partner:

  • They ask for reassurance again and again — or they’re hiding something.
  • You argue not about what happened but about what was promised.
  • They don’t say that a rule doesn’t work for them: when you both act as if everything’s settled, admitting it is hard.

What to do

None of these techniques have been tested specifically on open relationships. Below it’s noted what’s taken from tested methods and what’s simply a reasonable format.

1. The agreement on one page

When: before a new experience, after any breach, and about once a month.

What to do:

  1. Each of you separately writes down the agreement in your own words: what’s okay, what isn’t, when it goes on pause (illness, a trip, a crisis), how it changes.
  2. Put the texts side by side. The places where the words differ are the main find. If anger flared up after a breach, tell apart what was actually promised from what was filled in.
  3. Answer the questions from technique 2 and write down the answers of everyone whose consent the agreement needs.
  4. Give the version a number and a date. Change it only with everyone’s consent.
  5. Before a new experience, name what you want and a stop signal; afterward, briefly ask how it went. Consent can be withdrawn at any moment without punishment.

Step 5 is borrowed from BDSM practices. There, no across-the-board worsening of mental health was found, and well-being was higher, but the survey included people who volunteered, so you can’t infer health benefits from it. A safeword doesn’t replace attention to pressure, to a weakened ability to consent, and to what happens afterward.

How you know it worked: comparing versions turns up fewer and fewer mismatches, and breaches get discussed rather than hidden. That the format reduces jealousy or the number of breakups hasn’t been shown.

If you come from different cultures. No data turned up on agreements in couples where one person speaks Russian and the other is from mainland China. Don’t derive a ready-made “cultural style of feelings” from nationality: ask about your partner’s own words, about the role of family, and about what exclusivity means for each of you. Check the words “we agreed,” “private,” “faithfulness” in each language separately (chapter 38; on the meanings of “yes” — chapter 37).

2. Three questions: what’s private, what to tell, whose decision it is

When: when you write the agreement and when it changes.

What to do: answer three questions separately.

  1. Can a person decline to hear details? Not wanting to hear the details isn’t the same as hiding the fact itself.
  2. Does your partner have to tell you about a new contact, a broken agreement, a changed health risk? A reasonable rule: anything that affects other people’s consent or health gets shared; thoughts and details stay private. There are no trials of disclosure rules in open relationships.
  3. Can one person end someone else’s relationship? That’s a veto, and it affects someone who isn’t at the table. If you do introduce a veto, write down what it covers and what say is left to the person whose relationship it affects. Harm from one-sided ending is plausible but hasn’t been measured.

The question for a couple isn’t “did I tell everything,” but “does what I’m keeping back change someone’s informed choice.”

How you know it worked: each question has a written answer, and it names everyone the decision affects.

Whether vetoes and “don’t ask, don’t tell” (an agreement not to discuss outside contacts) work hasn’t been tested: no one has compared them. Each can reduce uncertainty for one person but increase it, or take away the ability to have a say, for another.

If there are more than two of you. Research on agreements is almost all about couples. In a network, ask: whose decision changes if the rule or the information changes? If the answer names another person, for example your partner’s partner, that person needs the chance to agree or to revisit it. If it doesn’t — leave room for privacy. This is a suggestion for a conversation, not a tested protocol. Discuss separately housing, access in an emergency, privacy and the right to decide — with a primary, a secondary and an equal partner. On hierarchy (a “main” and a “second” partner), the data say neither that it does harm nor that going without it is more ethical: people living in a hierarchy were on average less satisfied with their relationships, but the low ratings came mainly from second and third partners, while primary partners didn’t differ from people without a hierarchy3. On time, children and how a network is set up — chapter 29; on maps of forms — chapter 24.

3. A health conversation before a new contact

When: before a new contact, with the people it affects.

What to do: discuss four things.

  1. Which acts, and with which partners, involve a barrier and which don’t, and who drew that line.
  2. When and for what each person was tested, and when the results will come in.
  3. Who learns about results and changes, and when.
  4. What to do if the plan is broken (a condom broke, “it just happened”).

How you know it worked: the measure of progress is conversations that actually happened and repeat tests, not a promise never to be jealous.

This list is homemade and poorly tested. The closest tested model is a trial with young couples of men: several video sessions about communication, HIV risk and the agreement versus one session with a test. We don’t give the size of the difference: there’s no reliable figure, and the result applies only to this group and to the outcomes compared there.

In surveys, people in open relationships used condoms, discussed testing and told their primary partner about outside contacts more often than people who cheated in secret4. Their past diagnoses, by their own account, were as many as those of monogamous people. But this is remembered behavior: it doesn’t measure transmission of infections and doesn’t prove equal risk, since the groups got tested differently. Clinics give a better picture: swingers came in for checkups more often than others, and they had as many new cases as other heterosexual visitors, only gonorrhea was more common5. Granted, that’s only the people who made it to the clinic.

“Fluid bonding” is a word from the community: dropping barriers with just one person. It isn’t a biological guarantee or a tested level of relationship: no one has measured the protective power of the label itself. Avoid both stigma and reassurance that isn’t backed by test results.

4. The outside view

When: after a fight over a broken expectation.

What to do: describe the fight in writing the way a neutral observer would see it. Pick out what was actually promised and what was filled in. In the study, the whole exercise took about twenty minutes in total.

How you know it worked: you remember the fight more calmly. Track how heavy the feelings are after fights and how you rate the relationship, not the number of partners.

Tested only on spouses in monogamous marriages: they kept up marriage quality longer than comparison couples. Not tested for open relationships or for cases of coercion.

5. A loop journal

When: when something got to you — a message, a silence, a piece of news.

What to do: write it down in columns: the event; what you notice in your body, or “can’t tell” (that’s an honest entry); what meaning you filled in; what you did; how the other person explains it; the version of the agreement; whether facts that matter for health were shared. Before a conversation about disclosure — a short private check-in: what’s in my body and how I explain it. It also works for people who don’t recognize feelings in the body (chapters 02 and 03).

How you know it worked: the entries show recurring places where expectation and agreement diverge. While both of you need the entries, look at them together. As soon as they start being used as evidence, stop. This is a way of keeping track, not an intervention: benefit and harm haven’t been measured.

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: clear rules protect against jealousy and keep the main couple safe.
In fact: partly. Rules clarify expectations, but there’s no evidence that they put out jealousy or keep the main couple safe: in a survey of couples of men, jealousy was even higher among those with a monogamous agreement6. And a false guarantee hides that someone is hurting: the person with less power in the couple finds it harder to say that a rule isn’t working.

People think: your partner can sense a lie.
In fact: lies are almost impossible to spot from behavior: people tell lies from truth only slightly better than a coin toss7. That’s the average across experiments, not the odds for a particular partner. Knowing someone closely helps in some cases, but doesn’t give general reliability. An important mismatch is better checked by talking: what happened, when, how it fits with what’s known.

People think: any secret from your partner is harmful.
In fact: worse well-being is linked not to privacy as such, but to a secret whose thoughts keep coming back: those thoughts predicted poor well-being more clearly than the need to conceal itself. The authors warned that this shouldn’t be read as “the secret made things worse”8. No one has tested “full openness” as a cure.

Talk it over together

  1. What words did we use to agree, and what does each of us see behind them? Let’s write it down separately and compare.
  2. What’s private for you, and what should others know about because it changes their choice?
  3. What do you need to know about right away, and what only if you ask?
  4. When does the agreement go on pause, and who decides?
  5. Whose decision will change if we change this rule, and is that person sitting at the table with us right now?
  6. What do you notice in your body when you hear “we need to talk,” and what could make it a little easier?

When it is no longer about skills

An agreement stops being an agreement when it’s enforced by threat or punishment. Red flags:

  • a veto used under pressure or as punishment;
  • a demand for full access to all messages and whereabouts as a condition of the relationship;
  • a journal and check-ins used as surveillance, evidence or revenge.

In that case, stop the formats from this chapter. On coercion and control — chapter 23.

28. Opening a relationship: when, how, and when not to

In short

The data don’t show that opening a relationship saves a couple that’s in a bad place. Among those who opened up, satisfaction with sex rose over a short follow-up — especially if they wanted to resolve a sexual mismatch. But the quality of the relationship with the primary partner showed no shift1.

The weak spot is understanding: partners often describe the same agreement differently, and a “yes” out of fear looks like consent.

What to do: before deciding, each of you separately and in writing answers what opening up should solve and what would count as a bad outcome; write down the agreement in detail; say promises about time and care out loud; talk about health in a separate conversation.

Vera says it over dinner, in passing: she’d like to try opening their relationship. Anya nods, answers “let’s talk about it,” and until late at night silently washes the same plate. Vera hears agreement in the nod. Anya knows her “let’s talk about it” is fear of saying “no.” In the morning Vera texts that on Friday she’s having dinner with someone new. Anya reads the message not as a plan but as “there’s no room for me anymore.”

What is going on

First the body, but there’s no ready-made clue. No characteristic bodily picture was found for the conversation “I want to open the relationship” — neither in general nor in couples where opinions differed. Other things were measured: self-rated satisfaction with sex, and tests for infections. So you catch the body’s signal by your own sensations (chapter 2) and find a word for it (chapter 3). If that doesn’t work — write exactly that: “can’t tell.” That’s a complete answer.

Then meaning. These are guesses, not measurements. One person hears the same sentence as an opportunity, the other as a loss. If you want to open up because of a recurring mismatch in sex, a new possibility may relieve exactly that frustration. If it’s because of fear, pressure, or because you made up poorly after fights, new partners may only add new negotiations.

Then action — and it becomes an event for the other person. “Let’s talk about it” out of fear is a different state from glad agreement. How often one or the other happens hasn’t been studied. A missed evening can be read two ways: “I have a calendar conflict” and “there’s no room for me anymore.” Behind anger at a point like this there’s most often a broken expectation (chapter 16), so first find out which promise was actually made and which was assumed. Tears are a separate signal and a request for help (chapter 5).

Who wants to open up, and what happens next. People who were thinking about opening up were followed for a while: some opened their relationships, others didn’t. Among those who opened up, satisfaction with sex rose, while life satisfaction and the quality of the relationship with the primary partner showed no shift1. But the period was short: this is a transition, not an end result. Who opened up and who didn’t was decided by the people themselves, not by a coin toss. How the partners saw it, who started the conversation, and whether the couples were in crisis isn’t described in the data. What happens to couples in deep crisis, and how often people are pressured into such a decision, can’t be seen either.

Interest in opening up in monogamous couples goes together most strongly with a sense that there are good options outside the couple2. By the authors’ calculations the chain looks like this: weaker attachment, more attractive outside options, more interest. They measured interest, not actions.

Desire comes in different kinds. Among people who have lived in open relationships for a long time, for some it’s strong and stable, and monogamy is hard for them; others, by the authors’ estimate, are comfortable in both formats. So the first question to ask about “let’s open up” is which of the two you’re dealing with.

One is for it, the other is against it. Almost nothing is known: the studies found didn’t follow such couples over time. The studies recruited people already living in polyamory (several romantic relationships with everyone’s knowledge), and can’t give a breakup rate for mixed couples. “It doesn’t work like that” has no support, and neither does the opposite. For same-sex couples, queer couples and “Chinese–Western partner” couples, no data were found at all. Not tested, but as a hypothesis: one person reads the other’s autonomy as a loss of safety. Then it matters more to agree on what each person actually chooses, including the right not to see other people yourself, than to even out the labels. According to gay men’s accounts, monogamy is often temporary: many couples start with it and open up later. These are accounts, not percentages.

The agreement: written down and heard. Almost everything known comes from one-time surveys, often online and without random selection, mostly about couples of men. An agreement of any type went together with greater satisfaction. The main finding is about matching. Among couples of men where both said “we have an agreement,” more than half agreed that they had discussed it explicitly, most named the same type, but both partners kept to it in only about half of the couples3. In another sample, younger partners broke the agreement more often. Men who saw an imbalance in the benefits (“I get less than my partner”) were less satisfied. Hence: spell it out in detail, and compare the benefits out loud (chapter 27).

Time and your partner’s partners. People in polyamory most often named time as the difficulty: scheduling and a feeling of being “stretched thin.” Next came stigma, jealousy and emotional load; time was named noticeably more often than jealousy4. Attachment can be shared differently from hours, but “unequal priority” is an assessment worth asking about, not a proven mechanism. A number of partners or hours that’s safe for everyone hasn’t been tested in the studies found.

Your partner’s partner (in the community, a metamour) brings comparison, curiosity, pleasure, anxiety, or all of these at once. For one person, meeting them replaces the unknown with the familiar; for another, it feels like an intrusion. In some online surveys, couples in polyamory and swinging were more satisfied, more passionate and more trusting than those who simply “opened” their relationship. But in other surveys the gap disappeared once they accounted for how the couple communicates, why they chose the format, belief in monogamy being “right,” and how often they’re in contact with their partners’ partners5. Surveys like these don’t show causes. Joy about your partner’s romance can be measured (chapter 26), but it hasn’t been proven that it’s required or that meeting the metamour in itself makes people more satisfied.

The body and health. The only bodily thing measured here was tests for infections. The most reliable test — with random assignment to groups — was done for couples of young men: a course of several video sessions (about communication, HIV risk and agreements) versus one testing consultation. A year later they looked at rectal chlamydia and gonorrhea and at anal sex without a condom. Whether the course helped, and by how much, isn’t given in the data; the result doesn’t carry over to other genders, older couples, coercive relationships or China; whether a “veto” works (the right to forbid your partner a specific connection) wasn’t tested. In another study following couples of men, better relationship dynamics went with less frequent unprotected sex outside the couple. That’s a link, not an answer to whether opening up makes people happier. An agreement of “no condoms, but only with each other” hasn’t been proven as protection: surveys measure what’s reported, not actual transmission.

If there are more than two of you. A useful question: whose choice changes if this information, this rule or this connection changes? If the answer names another person, that person needs the chance to agree or to revisit it. If it doesn’t, there’s room left for privacy. Disclosure protects informed choice, but doesn’t give a right to every thought someone else has. This is a proposed framework, not a tested program. Networks and children — chapter 29; formats — chapter 24.

Different cultures. No data were found on how couples with a partner from mainland China agree on opening up. In interviews with male couples in southern Africa, ideas about male power and hostility toward same-sex relationships got in the way of explicitly agreeing on sex outside the couple. The principle: norms about power and family decide whether an agreement is possible at all. Ask about your own situation: what language the conversation is in, whether family is involved, what each of you means by exclusivity. Whether the words “agreement,” “private,” “commitment” match across two languages hasn’t been tested (chapters 33, 37 and 38).

How to tell it is happening

In yourself:

  • You want to open up because of fear, pressure, or because you made up poorly after fights — not because of a specific mismatch.
  • “Yes” or “let’s talk about it” gets said out of fear of being left alone, not out of desire.
  • There seem to be good options outside the couple, and your attachment to your partner is weaker.
  • From one missed evening you’re ready to conclude “there’s no room for me anymore.”

In your partner:

  • They nod, say “let’s talk about it,” and go quiet.
  • They retell the agreement differently from how you remember it.
  • They say they get less than you do.
  • They put what you promised each other about time and care in different words.

What to do

Techniques 1–4 haven’t been tested: they’re proposed formats, and none of them has been compared with no technique for open relationships.

1. Two sheets before the conversation

When: before deciding, and at every revision of the agreement.

What to do: each of you, separately, in writing, without showing the other, answers: what opening up should solve; what would count as a bad outcome; which relationships outside the couple I accept; what I want to know about, and on what terms either of us can walk away; whether I want to see other people myself (each person keeps the right to say “no”). Then compare the sheets out loud. A mismatch is material, not a failure.

How you know it worked: the decision was made without pushing, the mismatches were named out loud, and each of you can retell the other’s sheet.

2. The agreement in writing, and a journal after a strong emotion

When: once you’ve agreed, and after every strong feeling.

What to do: write down in detail what’s acceptable, what gets shared and when, and what to do if the agreement is broken. Each of you has your own copy, with a date and a version number. After a strong emotion, each of you separately writes: what happened; what’s in your body (or “can’t tell”); what meaning you filled in; what you did; how the other person explains what happened; which version of the agreement was in force; whether what matters for health was shared.

How you know it worked: the entries show the difference between a promise that was made and one that was assumed. If the journal has turned into surveillance, control or revenge, stop (see the end of the chapter).

3. Time, care and contact — out loud

When: when you make the agreement, and once a month.

What to do: name your promises about time and care explicitly, including time alone together and time for the children.
