Ask each person what contact with other partners they want and what they need to know, and allow for revisions. Once a month, check whether broken promises are piling up around one partner. Being friends with your partner’s partner doesn’t prove maturity.

How you know it worked: “I have a calendar conflict” no longer sounds like “you don’t exist.”

4. Health — in a separate conversation

When: in a separate conversation, and regularly after that.

What to do: agree on what gets shared and when, and on repeat tests. Don’t count “no condoms, but only with each other” as protection: that hasn’t been proven.

How you know it worked: the conversations happened, the tests get repeated. That’s the measure here, not a promise never to be jealous.

5. The outside view

When: after an argument about opening up.

What to do: write about the argument as an outsider who wishes you both well. A small dose was tested — about twenty minutes of writing in total; a daily routine isn’t described in the data. It was tested on monogamous spouses, not on open couples; it wasn’t tested for coercive relationships.

How you know it worked: the tension around the conflict eases, and your rating of the relationship doesn’t sag. The number of partners doesn’t work as a measure.

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: opening a relationship is a way to fix the couple.
In fact: over a short follow-up, no shift was found in the quality of the relationship with the primary partner1. A review of common beliefs concludes: when tested, they turn out not to be what they seem, and often the opposite6.

People think: a new partner will give you everything that’s missing.
In fact: among people in polyamory, how well needs were met in one relationship barely explained satisfaction with others and didn’t predict commitment7. This is an online survey of people who volunteered, not a study over time, so the conclusion is cautious.

People think: hierarchical polyamory (where there’s a primary partner and the others are “secondary”) is worse and less ethical.
In fact: secondary and tertiary partners may be less satisfied, but primary partners and partners without a hierarchy barely differed8. The difference may be explained by length of relationship and roles, and satisfaction scores don’t measure ethics.

People think: once you’ve agreed, you’ve understood it the same way.
In fact: among couples of men where both said they had an agreement, both partners kept to it in only about half of the couples3.

Talk it over together

  1. What do you think opening up should solve, and what would be a signal for you that things have gotten worse?
  2. Which relationships outside the couple do you accept and which not, and is it okay for you to say “I personally don’t want to see other people”?
  3. When I say “yes” or “let’s talk about it,” how do you check that there’s desire behind it and not fear?
  4. What do you want to know about other partners, what would you rather keep private, and when do we need to say something about health?
  5. Whose choice changes if this rule or this information changes, and does that person have the chance to agree or to revisit it?
  6. Which promise about time did we actually make to each other, and which did each of us assume?

When it is no longer about skills

Red flags:

  • “yes” is said out of fear of being left alone or under pressure: that isn’t desire, even if it can look like consent;
  • the journal, check-ins and checking have turned into surveillance, coercive control or revenge for a breach.

What to do: stop the format (the journal, check-ins, new dates) and go back to separate written answers. If there’s control and threats, more on that in chapter 23.

29. Several partners: when there are more than two in the loop

In short

When people have several partners, the emotion loop from chapter 01 grows into a network: every action becomes a signal for everyone connected to that person — including people you didn’t choose and have never even seen.

A fair amount is known about the day-to-day life of such unions, less about outcomes: the surveys are mostly of volunteers, and often there’s no one to compare with.

What to do about it: see three layers (the pairs, the links between your partners’ partners, the outside world); before a new rule or secret, ask “whose choice does this change?”; with children, agree in advance who is responsible for what; and check the law where you live.

On Thursday Olya goes out on a date with David. Marina has known about it all week and said “of course” herself. But when Olya kisses her on the cheek at the door, it gets tight under her ribs and her breathing goes short. Marina’s daughter asks whether David will come to her birthday: after all, he read her a book. “I don’t know,” Marina answers, more sharply than she meant to, and the girl goes quiet. In the evening David’s plan with his partner Kai falls through. David texts Olya about it, and in the kitchen she reshuffles the calendar, and not for the first time.

No one here deceived anyone. But every action by one person became a signal for all the others.

What is going on

The loop becomes a network. In a couple there are two loops — body, meaning, action, an event for the other person — and they’re linked. In a network there are as many loops as there are connections, and they’re linked through shared people. Olya’s step is a signal for Marina. Marina’s sharp answer is a signal for the girl. David’s canceled plan is a signal for Kai, whom Marina has never once seen. Your partner’s partner takes part in your loop, even if you’ve never met.

The body. In people in such unions the body has hardly been measured — not heart rate, not breathing, not sleep — so there’s nothing to describe a “physiology of the network” with. What’s left is what you notice in yourself: tightness under the ribs, cold hands, a lump in the throat. Or “I can’t make sense of it” — that’s a normal first result (chapters 02 and 03).

Meaning. When a need gets met in another connection, two paths are possible. First: the pressure on the first connection drops — not everything rests on one person. Second: comparison and dissatisfaction show up. These are guesses, barely tested over time, and neither one proves that any partner is replaceable. On jealousy — chapter 25; on joy at your partner’s happiness with someone else — chapter 26.

Action. Texting, going quiet, reshuffling the schedule, asking — any action becomes a signal for the next person, and the chain reaches people who chose neither your conflict nor your breakup. Hence the question for the whole chapter: whose choice changes if this information, this rule or this connection changes? If there’s another person in the answer, that person needs the chance to agree or to revisit the agreement. If not — there’s room left for the private. This is a guideline, not a tested method.

A second partner doesn’t cancel the first. Connections with a “primary” and a “secondary” partner work differently, so the “one replaces the other” model is too simple. In one survey, satisfaction and tenderness were similar in people with one partner and with several, while there was more eroticism among those with one. And for everyone: the longer together, the less eroticism and the more tenderness1. This is a snapshot of one moment, not a couple’s history (chapter 18).

The network and breakups. A network isn’t a set of pairs: in a study of one city it had hubs and closed circles, and each connection had its own loop. In one survey, fear of losing a particular partner and a drive to keep one’s distance were linked to lower satisfaction with that particular connection (chapter 13).

When two people break up, a third may lose housing, contact or shared events without choosing that breakup. Loss, relief and split loyalty coexist in one person, and everyone sees the tears or the pointed indifference (chapters 05 and 22). This is logic, not measurement: only one analysis turned up — a triad of men in Taiwan — and it shows only that questions of loyalty stay inside the triangle. No data could be found on how such networks live over years (on opening a relationship — chapter 28).

Children. There are two different questions here: how the children are doing, and whether the law recognizes their family. A child can be affected by how predictable the adults are, by changes in who lives in the home, by the judgment of people around them, and by what can safely be explained. A parent’s number of partners as a cause of harm hasn’t been measured.

What there is are accounts. In Canadian interviews, most parents felt the upsides outweighed the difficulties: more adults means more help. In a small series of interviews, children valued their parents’ partners2. But these are words, not measurements.

The largest study comes from rural Tanzania, where polygyny is common: monogamous marriage wasn’t consistently linked to how children survive, grow and learn3. This weakens the argument that “the structure itself is harmful,” but only for those places, not for Western families.

The law is simpler: it’s described better than the outcomes. According to researchers’ descriptions, families with several parents fall outside legal protections, and discrimination happens in custody courts and at schools. Rules for living together without marriage fit several partners poorly4. Local registration of such families in some US cities and Canadian provinces isn’t marriage everywhere, isn’t custody rights, and isn’t recognition abroad.

Telling or keeping quiet. Telling family or a boss may bring support, or it may turn into the losses people fear in advance. Keeping quiet protects privacy in one place and cuts off support in another. Experienced judgment is linked to poor well-being, but you can’t tell the cause from that.

In one volunteer survey of a community of alternative practices and non-monogamy, most had had suicidal thoughts or attempts, and one in five had made an actual attempt; anxiety, depression and lower education were linked to this, and those whose relationships were monogamous had lower risk. You can’t judge non-monogamous people in general, or the causes, from one survey like this.

Decide separately for friends, children, relatives, your employer and your doctor: what each of them needs to know and what it might lead to. Your partner’s right to privacy and the child’s needs may pull in different directions — negotiate both (on secrets — chapter 27; on support outside the couple — chapter 34).

If you come from different cultures. No data could be found on non-monogamy in Chinese–Western couples. What there is, though, is the law: the Civil Code of the PRC enshrines monogamous marriage and prohibits bigamy and cohabitation by a married person with a third party5. But “any voluntary non-monogamy outside marriage is bigamy” would be an oversimplification: the consequences depend on the facts and on the specific provision. As for communication, a study of conversations in Western, Chinese and mixed couples isn’t about non-monogamy, and you can’t judge Chinese women or Western men as a type from it. Conclusions about children don’t carry over to mainland China.

How to tell it is happening

In yourself:

  • Your body kicked in (tightness, cold hands, a lump in the throat), even though out loud you said “of course.”
  • Your answer came out sharper than you wanted, and what you’re feeling is unclear.
  • From one change in the schedule you draw a conclusion about the whole connection: “I’m being replaced.”
  • You’re sure you know what was agreed, but it was never said out loud.

In your partner and around you:

  • News reaches the others not directly but through someone else’s retelling.
  • Reactions travel down the chain: one person gets upset, a second gets sharper, then a third.
  • People nobody asked — a partner’s partner, a child, relatives — start behaving differently.

What to do

These are reasonable guidelines, not tested programs: for a written agreement, a monthly check-in, dinner with your partner’s partner, vetoes, and aftercare following intense scenes, there are no comparative tests. Techniques 3 and 5 come closest to being tested, but they too were tested on other groups.

1. Ask: whose choice does this change

When: before a new rule, a secret or a change in a connection.

What to do: ask yourself the question. If the answer names another person, give that person the chance to agree or to revisit the agreement — before, not after. If there’s no one, there’s room left for the private.

How you know it worked: none of the people affected learn about a decision after the fact.

2. A loop journal

When: after an emotionally noticeable episode.

What to do: write on separate lines: what happened; what’s in your body (or “can’t tell” — that’s a complete entry); what meaning you filled in — a promise said out loud or your own conclusion; what you did; how your partner describes that meaning in their own words; which version of the agreement you’re relying on; whether facts that matter for health were passed on; whose choice changed and whether that person knows about it. For example, write after an intense episode and look it over together once a month; there’s no tested dose.

How you know it worked: tension after conflicts is lower, and your rating of each connection doesn’t drop. Stop if the journal turns into surveillance, coercion or revenge.

3. A conversation about health and agreements

When: in advance.

What to do: meet separately and talk only about health. Discuss who gets tested and how often, who gets health information and when, and what to do if something happens outside the agreement. Write down a version with a date.

How you know it worked: the conversations happened, the follow-up tests were done. The measure isn’t “never be jealous.”

What was properly tested is an HIV prevention program for young couples of men, including a conversation about monogamous and non-monogamous agreements: couples were randomly split into groups and compared with a single consultation. How much it helped isn’t stated in the descriptions. It wasn’t tested for other couples, coercive relationships or China; nor was the at-home version of the conversation.

4. Write it out in advance: children and breakups

When: while nothing has happened yet, and also when the set of adults in the home changes.

What to do: write out who can pick up the child, authorize medical treatment and be a source of support, and how to explain changes in the adults in an age-appropriate way. For a breakup — which promises outlast it: co-parenting, housing, health information, the other partner’s right to stay in contact. Check local law separately. On the family you grew up in — chapter 30; on raising children’s emotional skills — chapter 31.

How you know it worked: every “but who…” has an answer that all the adults know, not one made up in the middle of a crisis. These are questions, not a tested way to prevent a breakup.

5. Look at the conflict from the outside

When: after a recent conflict with one of your partners.

What to do: write about it the way a neutral third person would see it, someone with no stake in the outcome. A similar writing task preserved marriage quality in married couples; it hasn’t been tested on non-monogamous people.

How you know it worked: tension after conflicts is lower, and your rating of the relationship doesn’t drop.

If you come from different cultures, add these questions to any technique: what translated words mean to each person — “exclusivity,” “partner,” “private,” “family”; how each person understands faithfulness; whether family is involved. The answer can’t be derived from nationality (chapters 32, 33 and 37). These are questions, not a tested intercultural intervention.

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: a new partner will cover everything that was missing in the old relationship.
In fact: in a survey of people with two connections, what a person got in one barely explained how things were going in the other6. The data come from volunteers, and the cause can’t be determined. It’s more useful to ask: which need is changing, and what does each connection promise?

People think: relationship anarchy means relationships without commitments.
In fact: that’s the name for an approach that drops the “pyramid”: neither the couple nor sex gets the top spot in advance. It’s mostly theory: it questions imposed hierarchies, not chosen promises. In a small series of interviews with Swedes, consent, honesty and conversation stayed central, but how they were understood got revisited: for example, you can agree not to tell your partner everything. The question isn’t “are there commitments” but “which ones were chosen” (chapters 27 and 24).

People think: children in such families suffer and get confused. Or, the opposite, they’re definitely fine.
In fact: neither is confirmed. For Western families no proper comparison with other families could be found, and hand-picked accounts don’t show the cause7.

People think: research has shown that non-monogamous people are just as happy as monogamous ones.
In fact: many scales don’t measure the same thing in the two groups, and most earlier comparisons are too small to show anything8.

Talk it over together

  1. Which of our promises have been said out loud, and which did each of us fill in on our own?
  2. Which of the people around us does this rule affect, and did we ask them?
  3. What should stay just between you and me, and what does someone else need to know so their choice is an informed one?
  4. If one of us breaks up with someone, what of what we share remains: the home, the children, health information, contact with other partners?
  5. Who can pick up the child, authorize medical treatment, help with money, and what will we tell the child when the set of adults changes?
  6. Whom do we tell about how we’re set up — friends, relatives, bosses, a doctor — and why each of them?

When it is no longer about skills

It’s time to stop the format if:

  • the journal, the check-ins or “transparency” work as surveillance, coercion or revenge;
  • the entries are needed not to understand but to prove guilt, gain access to someone else’s private life, or punish;
  • control is growing and refusing is dangerous — then this is chapter 23, not exercises.

If, against a background of judgment, loneliness or a breakup in the network, thoughts of not wanting to live appear, reach out the same day to a doctor, a crisis line or someone close to you.

Part VI. Family and culture

30. The family you grew up in, inside your couple

In short

Childhood affects relationships, but on average the link is weak, and it isn’t a life sentence. Your mother-in-law’s voice, the smell of your mom’s kitchen, your father’s tone can set off old reactions in your body before any words, and your partner receives them as a new event.

Adult life changes a lot: according to observational studies, a warm partner softens things, while drawn-out conflicts with your partner’s parents wear down a marriage over the years.

What to do: look at today’s loop (the signal in your body, the meaning, the action, your partner’s response) rather than at your life story; recognize an old feeling about yourself like “no one sees me”; ask for one specific change; and agree together on what role each of your families plays.

Katya and Yun are driving to Katya’s mom’s place for the weekend. Closer to the house Katya stops responding to jokes: her jaw is clenched, her fingers grip the wheel harder, her breathing gets short. Yun sees only that the woman she loves has suddenly turned cold, and decides: it’s about me. She goes quiet too and buries herself in her phone. Katya hears that silence as confirmation: she’ll have to walk into her mom’s house alone. They pull up to the gate in silence, and each is waiting for the other to say one simple sentence: “I’m with you.”

What is going on

First the body. The signal can come from the past: a call from your mother-in-law, the smell of your mom’s kitchen, a raised voice behind the wall, the tone your partner uses with their father. The body responds before words do: the jaw clenches, breathing gets short, you want to pull away (how to notice signals like these — chapter 02).

Heart rate, breathing and hormones haven’t been measured directly on this topic: “the body” here is what people notice and report themselves. Indirectly: when a person withdraws in an argument, it may go together with growing anger inside1. And according to one recent hypothesis, the link between childhood abuse and sexual desire and sexual dissatisfaction in a couple may run through an aversion to touch: the partner’s touch feels unpleasant.

Next, meaning from home. If in childhood a fight ended in shouting, someone walking out, or a week of silence, the signal “the conversation has gotten tense” is easily read as “something bad is about to happen.” The body wants to protect itself: leave, lash out with words, freeze. A hard childhood can change your sense of safety and what support you expect, and your partner’s response can either lock that pattern in or revise it.

Protection is an event for the other person. Yun hears Katya’s coldness as “you’re not wanted here,” and Katya hears Yun’s silence as “I’m alone.” The two loops wind each other up. And anger, as this book sees it, is most often a signal of a broken expectation (chapter 16).

An old feeling about yourself. What comes from home may be not just a reaction but a ready-made feeling about yourself: “no one sees me,” “it’s all my fault again,” “everything’s on me,” “there’s no place for me here.” The triggers change, but the feeling is the same. The loop may look like this: a familiar scene, tension, the thought “they don’t want me again,” a reproach or a test, your partner getting defensive — and that seems to confirm the old feeling. But this could be a biased reading, or your partner really being cold, or both at once. The label “this is from childhood” doesn’t settle which of these is true, and arguing about the trigger itself does little until the feeling is named (technique 4).

This is a model, not a proven chain. It sounds plausible, but it hasn’t been tested link by link: usually the two ends are measured — childhood and the couple today — and what’s in between is harder to see.

What gets passed on: the climate at home. In a large study of parents and their adult married children, the parents’ fighting was linked to less harmony and more fighting in the children’s marriages. This came down neither to divorce, nor to income, nor to education, nor to mental health problems. About half of the link was explained by the children’s memories of their parents’ fights. Most strongly linked to the children’s troubled marriages were jealousy, domineering, a quick temper, criticism, moodiness and silence2. Warmth gets passed on too: a warm, involved atmosphere at home in adolescence predicted how positively a person behaved with their spouse decades later3. And how close parents were to each other predicted the quality of their children’s romantic relationships many years later — through a chain: how the parents raised the children, and then how those children got along with people and handled their feelings when young.

In the language of the loop: a child sees the parents fighting, anxiety or numbness stays in the body, the meaning is “this is what conflict looks like,” and in an adult fight the same set switches on by itself. This is an interpretation: memories and outcomes were measured, not the body. There are no data in these studies on same-sex couples or on unions of several people.

A hard childhood: there’s a link, not a fate. Abuse, neglect and violence in the family are linked to a greater chance that an adult will be a victim of violence from a partner, but the link is weak. It’s stronger among those who are dating than among married people, and about the same for men and women. One possible path: violence at home, then behavior problems in adolescence, then violence in one’s own couple. These are averages across large groups, not a forecast for a particular person.

What adult life changes. Your partner’s family isn’t background. For wives, conflicts with their mother-in-law and father-in-law predicted a lower rating of their own marriage later. The reverse — a good marriage predicting fewer conflicts with the in-laws — turned up only for husbands. As the authors concluded, drawn-out conflicts with a spouse’s relatives wear down the strength of a marriage over the years4.

Warmth nearby, according to observational studies, softens things instead. In a long study of three generations, among people with a warm partner who knows how to communicate, no link of the kind “I was treated harshly — and I treat my kids the same way” was found5. Harsh parents, meanwhile, slightly lowered the chance that a child would find such a partner. A partner who knows how to recover after fights softened the risk of an unstable relationship for those whose past experience had been insecure. A school and family program to prevent substance use improved problem-solving skills; that improved family life in adolescence, and family life in turn improved romantic relationships in young adulthood.

These are observations, not experiments: warm people may simply end up next to warm people more often. And the explanation through the loop — your partner responds differently to your usual defense, and the old meaning has nothing to hold on to — is so far only an interpretation. On repairing after fights — chapter 17.

Where your parents end and you begin. There’s an idea that maturity is the ability to stay connected while holding your own position. By self-report, this ability goes together with less anxiety and greater satisfaction with marriage. But this ability is hard to separate from overall well-being, and whether building it helps a couple hasn’t been tested, especially outside Western mixed-sex marriages. Closeness isn’t merging, and labels like “you haven’t separated from your mom” don’t follow from this (chapter 19).

If there are more than two of you. There are no data on how each person’s parents get woven into a union of three or more adults. There are no tested assessments for children in such families either, only the researcher’s own account. From what there is: among people with several partners, the sense of security depended on the particular partner, and openness to open unions and actual participation in them didn’t line up. The samples are volunteers and don’t represent all open unions. Your “how I attach” style is more a sliding scale than a breed, and it can differ from one relationship to another. A working question for a network: which exact agreement, which connection, which expectation and which order of consent are being discussed (chapter 29).

If you come from different cultures. There’s no research in this chapter’s sources on couples of “a Chinese woman and a Russian-speaking or European partner.” What’s known about China in general: among middle-aged and older adults, a hard childhood is linked to a greater likelihood of never marrying and to depression. Those who had a hard childhood also face a higher risk of violence from a partner, most often verbal. In a study not yet checked by independent reviewers, parents’ divorce is linked to a higher risk of divorce in the children, especially if it happened in the preschool and early school years and if only the father remarried afterward. Childhood in the first two studies is described from adults’ memories, and the risk everywhere is given only as “how many times higher,” without the baseline rates: you can’t tell whether that’s a lot or a little.

This is about a country, not about your couple. There’s no data that all Chinese women hold back their feelings and all Europeans demand openness. Instead of a stereotype, it’s better to ask each other — questions 4 and 5 below (chapters 32 and 33).

How to tell it is happening

In yourself:

  • Your body tenses up ahead of time, an hour before a visit to family (yours or your partner’s): jaw, shoulders, stomach, breathing.
  • The reaction is bigger than the trigger: a short remark about your parents brings on heat or an urge to shut down.
  • In a fight you recognize someone else’s intonation: your father’s, your mom’s.
  • You’ve got a conclusion ready: “no one’s standing up for me again,” “they’re deciding for me again.”
  • The fights are about different things, but the feeling about yourself in them is the same, and it’s familiar from home or from past relationships.

In your partner:

  • They turn cold or go quiet on the way to family or after a call from them.
  • They answer in the same tone: short and dry, on the defensive.
  • They pull away.
  • Decisions about family get made without you.
  • After a visit, each of you is sure it was the other who pulled away.

What to do

1. A gentle request

When: when you want to change something about how family takes part in your life.

What to do: start with where your partner already sees you: “This is where you really see me — I’d like the same when it comes to your parents.” This only works if it’s true, not as a technique: think ahead of time about what’s good about this person for you. Then name one specific behavior, not a character trait. Not “you always cave,” but “when weekend plans at your parents’ get discussed without me.” Ask for one change you can agree on: “let’s decide together first, then answer your mom.” Stop talking and give them time to answer. To start with — one such conversation a week.

How you know it worked: there’s less withdrawing and silence in the conversation. Have each of you separately rate on your own scale how well the conversation went, and watch both numbers over a month.

This is poorly tested: couples were only observed, and among those who spoke gently, the withdrawing partner withdrew less, and anger recurred less often1. Starting with something good wasn’t tested separately. For someone whose partner threatens them, this kind of openness isn’t a fit.

2. A “body — meaning — action” journal

When: right after something on the family theme got to you: a call, a visit, a remark.

What to do: write four short lines. Where in your body and what exactly: tightness, heat, emptiness, an urge to pull away. What meaning you filled in: “no one’s standing up for me,” “they’re deciding for me again.” What expectation was broken, and whether it had ever been said out loud. What you did, and what your partner ended up getting. Do this a few times a week for a couple of weeks.

How you know it worked: after two weeks you can see whether the same signal and the same meaning keep recurring, and whether the pause between signal and action has gotten longer.

The journal hasn’t been tested as a method. It rests on the idea that a feeling can be checked as the conversation goes along rather than guessed from a label, and the line about expectation rests on the book’s idea of anger as a signal of a broken expectation.

3. Support before a hard conversation about family

When: a minute before the conversation.

What to do: remember a time when someone reliably supported you: where it was, who was there, what resonates in your body. Then start the conversation. If what comes back isn’t support but heaviness, stop: choose a different memory or skip this technique.

How you know it worked: tension on your own scale is lower after than before, and rate how the conversation went separately. Immediate relief and lasting change are different things.

Short techniques aimed at a sense of safety noticeably change your immediate state and behavior, though there are doubts about the size of the effect. The technique hasn’t been tested at home, before a conversation with a partner, or over the long term.

4. Find the old feeling about yourself

When: the fights are about different things, but inside it’s the same feeling about yourself every time.

What to do: at a calm time, in writing, each of you for yourself.

  1. Finish the sentence: “In our fights I feel once again that…” (no one sees me, it’s all my fault, everything’s on me, there’s no place for me).
  2. Where, and since when, has this happened before: at home, in past relationships, at work?
  3. What role do you give your partner in those moments: the one who doesn’t notice, who blames, who leaves?
  4. Check against the journal from technique 2 or against your memory: what actually happened, and what did you fill in? If your partner’s coldness is real, that’s a conversation for the two of you (technique 1).
  5. Ask not “what did they do to me,” but “what did I lack back then”: support, recognition, belief in myself. And how to give yourself that today, not only through your partner: your own circle of people, asking for help with simple things, closeness with friends (chapter 34). If your life itself is shaky right now, set childhood aside: support for today comes first.

How you know it worked: you recognize the feeling the moment it switches on (“oh, this again”), and an argument about the dishes stays an argument about the dishes.

The technique hasn’t been tested. The closest tested one is “The view of a neutral third party” from chapter 17: in married couples who described their fight this way, the usual decline in marriage quality that year stopped6. Painful memories are better worked through with a specialist (chapter 35).

5. Your own place next to unkind family

When: relatives — yours or your partner’s — again and again needle you, belittle you or ignore you, and attempts to change their minds change nothing.

What to do: stop trying to get them to treat you differently: other people’s attitudes aren’t in your power. The question is a different one: at what distance and in what way can I be around them without losing my strength and dignity? How often to see them, when to leave, what not to respond to. Discuss together who takes on what (questions 3 and 4 below).

How you know it worked: after visits, less energy goes into recovering, and you expect less change from them.

The technique hasn’t been tested. If family members threaten or use force, this is no longer about distance.

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: children of divorced parents are doomed to divorce.
In fact: the risk is higher, but it’s one ingredient, not a script. When adopted people were studied, their divorces resembled those of their biological parents more than those of their adoptive parents, but the link was small, and the contribution of upbringing was even smaller7. How people lived before and after the divorce matters too: whether they fought in front of the children and how they raised them. Fatalism can do harm: the label “child of divorce” can stigmatize, though how much hasn’t been measured.

People think: a hard childhood predetermines that relationships will fail.
In fact: one partner’s hard childhood is only weakly linked to how satisfied the other partner is with the relationship8. Kinds of experience vary a great deal, and a link isn’t a cause: you can’t predict a couple’s fate from it. The more hard events have piled up, the higher the health risks — but that’s about health, not relationships. The supports available today are safe boundaries, supportive relationships and access to protection. There’s no need to erase your history.

People think: your partner was chosen to resemble your father or mother, and that explains everything.
In fact: in a study of adopted people, raters found a resemblance between adopted daughters’ husbands and their adoptive fathers9. But that can also be explained by social circle, by age, and by the fact that people generally choose others similar to themselves. It wasn’t tested for same-sex couples. “My partner is like my father” is a reason for curiosity, not an explanation of motives.

People think: if your parents are against it, love only grows stronger.
In fact: when this idea was rechecked with the same questions as in the original study, no growth in feelings from parental disapproval over time was found. And approval from people close to the couple went together with higher relationship quality10. That’s a link, not a cause.

Talk it over together

These questions also work for several partners: each person has their own family and their own answer.

  1. What do you notice in your body an hour before a visit to my parents, and to yours? Where, and what exactly?
  2. What phrase or tone from your family do you recognize in our fights? What does it set off in you?
  3. What have we agreed on out loud about family — calls, visits, money, holidays, children — and what did each of us take for granted?
  4. Which relatives take part in our decisions with both our consent, and which only as guests?
  5. In which language is it easier for you to tell me what you need? What do we lose in the other one?
  6. After tension with family, what do I do that makes it easier for you to cool down, and what do I do that makes it harder?
  7. What feeling about yourself switches on most often in our fights — and who do I become for you in those moments?

When it is no longer about skills

If there’s violence, tight control (deciding who you can see, checking your phone) or fear of your partner in the couple or in the family, the exercises in this chapter aren’t a fit. Asking the person who has been hurt to show “repair” or to open up to someone who threatens them adds risk, and that applies to both adults and children. Protection comes first: a safety plan for those who have been hurt, including children. Joint conversations and joint programs start once safety has been checked and everyone agrees. An old feeling about yourself doesn’t explain everything: if your partner really is controlling, threatening, monitoring or causing harm, that’s not a shared misunderstanding and not “your childhood stuff.” More on this in chapters 23 and 35.

31. Kids and the couple: emotions in a family where a child is growing up

In short

What matters for a child is not the parents’ argument itself but how it goes: hostility can do harm, while calmly resolving a disagreement is linked to the opposite.

After the first child, the relationship in a couple cools a little on average, and hoping the baby will bring you closer is risky: it’s better to agree on caregiving in advance and revisit those agreements later.

What to do: first let your body calm down, then talk; name the child’s feeling and set the limit on the action; decide in advance who takes over from a worn-out adult when the baby is crying.

The younger one, Nika, has been crying for a long time now, and Vera can feel the pounding in her temples. Ira comes out of the bedroom groggy: “You heard her wake up.” — “And you were asleep.” They speak in whispers, but the words cut. The older one, Tyoma, stands in the doorway of the kids’ room in his pajamas. He isn’t crying and doesn’t say anything; he just looks from one mom to the other and holds on to the doorframe.

What is going on

First, the adult’s body. Crying, a tantrum, a slammed door — and your heart speeds up, your breathing gets shorter, your temples pound, your muscles tighten. In that moment you hear worse: your attention narrows to whatever is pressing on you.

Then the meaning. Your body doesn’t tell you what happened. Your head fills in the meaning: “they’re doing it on purpose,” “I can’t cope,” “my partner is asleep again.” What you do depends on the meaning: brush it off, punish — or stop, name the child’s feeling, and set a limit. For example: “You’re angry that the game is over. You can’t hit your brother, but you can be angry.” The feeling is accepted, the behavior is limited.

This can be learned. But it is a skill of the adult, not a guarantee for a particular child. The idea rests on observations of families and early trials of programs for parents. How a parent rates themselves is one thing; how a child understands feelings is another; how the child handles feelings in the eyes of an outside observer is a third. Improvement in one doesn’t prove improvement in another.

Your response is an event for the child, and the loop closes. And the child has a loop of their own. A child may read hostility between adults as a threat: their body switches on anxiety, they want to step in or hide. If the child is also put between the people arguing, a second risk is added. This has been linked to anxiety and low mood, or acting out and aggression, showing up later. These are risk pathways, not a diagnosis from one overheard fight.

In one observation, for some of the measures, what mattered more was not how strongly the child’s body flared up but how quickly it calmed down afterward. For now this is a pointer for hypotheses, not a rule. And if a child has gone quiet, that doesn’t mean they noticed the making up.

A baby is the same loop in its purest form. Crying stirs up arousal in the adult, and that collides with sleep deprivation and the other adult’s irritation. If you put the baby down in a safe place and call for someone to take over, a dangerous buildup can be interrupted (technique 3).

The couple after the birth. Sleep changes, so does the amount of caregiving, so does personal space. Each of you has an unspoken list: who gets up at night, who is “by default” in charge of the doctor, how much time is left for the two of you, when the relatives come over. Until the list is said out loud, it isn’t agreed on, and every task left undone can read as “I’ve been abandoned” or “this isn’t fair.” Anger here is more often a signal of a broken expectation than a sign that love is over: it calls you to clarify the agreement (on anger — chapter 16, on workload — chapter 20, on calming each other down — chapter 04, on tears — chapter 05). On the body after childbirth, see “Alchemy Without Magic”; on desire over the years — chapter 18.

After divorce and in a new family. A child may feel the demands of a new adult as pressure, the divided attention of their own parent as a loss, and hostility between the exes as a demand to pick a side. This feeds resistance that adults mistake for the child’s fixed “position.” On the adult side of a breakup — chapter 22. It’s worth looking at the quality of the relationships in the family, not at the “stepfather” label. A caveat: the data are mostly about white American families with a mother and a stepfather; there are too few stepmothers to compare.

If you come from different cultures. There is no direct data on child-rearing and arguing in front of children in couples where a woman from mainland China is with a man from a Russian-speaking or European background. The closest thing is conflict conversations of student couples in the US: European American participants showed more positive emotional behavior than Chinese American ones. These are not mixed couples and not parents, and it doesn’t say who is more caring. Talking with your child about feelings in the language where that’s easier is a reasonable idea to test, not a proven outcome. Crying works differently in different countries, so ask each other: where is it okay to cry in front of the child, what words do you have for being upset, are touches needed, which relatives do you both agree to bring in (chapters 30, 32 and 33).

How to tell it is happening

In yourself:

  • Your body went off before the thought did: pounding temples, a clenched jaw, short breaths.
  • The meaning is ready in a second: “on purpose,” “I can’t cope,” “it’s all on me again.”
  • You get angry over something small, and behind it is an expectation you never agreed on out loud.
  • Your hand reaches to brush it off or punish before you have time to think.
  • You fight about the same thing again.

In your partner:

  • They answer sharply or get defensive, even in a whisper.
  • They may read a task left undone as a sign of neglect.
  • Their loop is the same, and what you do becomes the trigger.

In the child:

  • They freeze in the doorway, looking from one adult to the other.
  • They try to step in, hide, or become a messenger between the adults.

What to do

1. Expectations — out loud, before the birth and after

When: before the birth, a few weeks after, and regularly from then on. And when anger hits.

What to do: each of you separately writes down: who gets up at night, who is in charge of the doctor and the household, how much sleep and time for yourself you need, which relatives come over and when. If there are more than two adults, the list includes everyone who looks after the child. Next to each item, a note: “said and agreed” or “taken for granted.” Agree on one change at a time and ask for behavior, not for a change in character.

When anger hits: notice the signal in your body; name the broken expectation as a guess (“it seems the expectation was that…”); ask whether it had been agreed on; ask for a specific change; check how the other person understood the request. In a couple with different languages, each of you first restates the meaning of what the other said, and only then decides what it was: language, duty to family, or a broken agreement.

How you know it worked: each of you separately rates whether the workload has become fairer, and there are fewer fights about the same thing.

Honestly: this is a scheme built from ideas, not a tested protocol. Next to it there is a program of several sessions before and after the birth that was tested in a trial: couples who went through it did better on a number of measures of co-parenting, parent behavior and child adjustment1. Some of the effects depended on whether the parents lived together and on whether a son was born. One evening at home is something else. It is not proven that a short workshop will fix a relationship where there is coercion, or that it will work unchanged in non-monogamous families and in a couple from mainland China and Europe.

2. Body first, then words

When: your child is rebelling, and you feel yourself rising.

What to do: notice the rise in your body and let it settle: choose a calm activity, not “letting it out.” Such activities noticeably reduce anger and aggression2; there is no single dose, and this is about anger in general, not only a parent’s. For the technique itself with a child, the testing is weak: the basis is an early trial of a program for parents of preschoolers, and the available description doesn’t report the effect on the child. Then name the child’s feeling without approving of the act: “You’re upset that the game is over.” Set a limit on the action and offer a safe option.

How you know it worked: compare two measures: how you rate your own reaction, and what the child does in similar situations — ideally through the eyes of the other adult. And whether you come back to the conversation the same day.

For bilingual, non-monogamous and same-sex families, the effect has not been tested.

3. A plan for the baby’s crying

When: in advance, while everything is calm. And later — when things build up.

What to do: choose a safe place for the baby and who takes over. Each of you names your early signals of rising in the body. When the rise builds: put the baby in the safe place, step out, call for the person taking over, come back when your body has calmed down. Never shake the baby, under any circumstances.

How you know it worked: each adult can name the plan without prompting and uses it. If there are several adults, everyone takes part in the plan.

In a large trial, materials about infant crying increased knowledge, and more mothers said they had passed on to others the advice to step away when irritated. It did not show fewer cases of shaking.

4. Show the repair, and keep hostility away

When: the child saw or heard you arguing.

What to do: keep threats and contempt away from the child. If the argument ended calmly, don’t hide it: “We had an argument, it’s not because of you, we have a plan.” The phrase is reasonable, but it hasn’t been tested in an experiment.

How you know it worked: the child doesn’t try to make peace between you and doesn’t hide. Silence on its own isn’t an indicator.

5. In a new or large family — spell out the roles

When: after a breakup, when a new adult appears, and when more than two people look after the child.

What to do: agree on who has which role, who makes decisions for the child, and who to go to when things are hard. Don’t make the child a messenger between the adults, and don’t pull them into hostility between the exes.

How you know it worked: all the adults give the same answer to the question “who decides and who do we go to,” and the child doesn’t carry messages.

There is no tested rule for how quickly a child “should” bond with a new adult.

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: parents should never argue in front of the kids.
In fact: not all arguments are alike. In an observation of American families with young children, constructive and destructive conflict were linked to the child’s caring behavior in opposite directions3. Hiding every disagreement means hiding the repair too, while hostility can do harm. But this is an observation of families where two parents live together: it says nothing about separated, same-sex or non-monogamous families.

People think: a child brings a couple closer.
In fact: on average, after the first child, relationship satisfaction drops a little for both partners4. Satisfaction also dropped among childless newlyweds, so you can’t blame it all on the child. A long observation showed that the decline is sharper for new parents than for childless couples5. Both pictures can be true: the measure, the time span and the comparison group differ. This is an average shift, not the couple’s fate. But the hope that “a baby will fix the relationship” risks adding to the load; how often that happens hasn’t been measured.

People think: letting a baby cry it out means harming them.
In fact: that’s too broad. Two situations get mixed up here: an adult at their limit, who needs a safe place and someone to take over, and behavioral sleep methods for older babies who have trouble falling asleep. In trials, sleep improved, and no differences in attachment or in emotional problems were found later6, 7. But the groups were small, rare harm can’t be ruled out, and newborns weren’t included. That a baby’s crying leads to divorce is not proven.

People think: family makeup decides how a child turns out.
In fact: the makeup itself tells you little. In families where the parents belong to sexual minorities, children are on average slightly better adjusted than in families with heterosexual parents, and so are their relationships with their parents; on a number of other measures no difference was found, and the studies themselves varied8. The number of adults doesn’t determine either benefit or harm: it may come down to available care, consistent agreements, stigma, and how much conflict the child sees. The available descriptions of families with more than two adults have neither a representative sample of children nor a control comparison, and the review on families with sexual-minority parents doesn’t replace them. So it’s wrong to declare non-monogamy harmless, and it’s wrong to expect harm just because several consenting adults are doing the caring (chapter 29).

Talk it over together

  1. When the child cries or rebels, what do you notice first in your body — and what meaning do you fill in (“on purpose,” “I can’t cope”)?
  2. Which expectations about caregiving seem “taken for granted” to you, and which ones have we never agreed on out loud?
  3. In a hard moment, do you need someone close by, space, or touch more?
  4. What in our fights stays behind closed doors, and what are we ready to show — the calm resolution? What will we tell the child afterward?
  5. Which of the adults makes decisions for the child, who helps, who do we go to when things are hard for us — and has everyone agreed to this?
  6. In which language is it easier to tell the child “I’m sad” and “I’m angry,” and which relatives take part in this with our shared agreement?

When it is no longer about skills

Everything above is for ordinary fights where things are safe overall. If your fights include:

  • threats, coercion or violence, including in front of the child;
  • fear of your partner or control over you,

then arguing skills are not the answer. Joint sessions and talking about how you fight don’t fit here: safety comes first, and you need a safety plan (chapter 23).

Separately, about babies:

  • The baby is crying, and you feel you might cause harm. Put the baby in a safe place, step out, call another adult. Do not shake the baby.
  • A newborn is crying. First check for hunger, illness and safety. The sleep methods from the trials are for older babies who have trouble falling asleep.

32. Love and family in today’s China

In short

People from different cultures don’t so much feel differently as understand differently which feelings are the “right” ones. Quiet joy is not weak joy, and family is not a veto but a participant in the conversation. Figures on marriages and divorces count events across the country, not your couple’s odds.

What to do: ask instead of reading it off a face; talk about family and money separately; in an argument, look through the eyes of a well-meaning observer.

There is almost no data on couples made up of a Chinese woman and a European man, or on non-monogamous and same-sex unions on the mainland, so this chapter is a map of questions, not an instruction manual.

Wei tells Anton he got a promotion. Anton expects laughter and hugs. Wei just smiles, puts a hand on his shoulder and asks what to make for dinner. “Are you even happy?” Anton asks. Wei doesn’t know what to say: the joy is there, it’s just quiet. By evening Anton is disappointed: there was no celebration. And Wei feels he failed an exam in joy.

What is going on

First, the body. Pleasant states can be loud or quiet. Excitement is loud: there’s a lot of arousal. Calm is quiet: there’s little arousal. Which of the two counts as “real” joy differs on average between cultures: they differ not so much in what people experience as in which states they consider ideal1. These are group comparisons, and couples from the mainland aren’t among them. For example, people in Hong Kong valued calm joy more highly than European Americans. Who in a couple of a Chinese woman and a European man is drawn more to a “lively” partner, and who to a “calm” one, hasn’t been tested directly.

In the studies found, there are no measurements showing that Chinese partners feel less intensely: almost everywhere people were asked in questionnaires, and pulse and breathing weren’t measured. Words about a feeling and the body’s reaction are different channels: in an experiment with fear of spiders, putting the fear into words weakened the skin’s reaction a week later more than other techniques did, while the reported fear was the same for everyone. The experiment isn’t about couples, but it’s worth watching both channels (chapters 02 and 03).

What meaning the partner fills in. Wei has good news, and his body responds with an even, quiet joy. Anton fills in: “He doesn’t care.” And asks: “Be happier — louder.” For Wei this is a new trigger: now he has to be happy on demand, and his body tenses up. What decides it isn’t nationality but what each person puts into “loudness.” It sounds logical, but this chain hasn’t been tested directly in intercultural couples.

In an argument, the same loop. One wants to settle it right away, the other takes a pause so as not to inflame things. The pause and the pressure spur each other on. Silence by itself doesn’t tell you what’s behind it: agreement, doubt, avoiding the topic, or a pause to find words in a non-native language. How often Chinese couples go silent in an argument, and why, hasn’t been reliably measured. If restraint breaks through as tears, that’s a separate story (chapter 05).

Labels change the meaning. In the Chinese language of relationships there are words like 冷暴力 (leng baoli, “cold violence,” punishment by silence) and 安全感 (anquangan, a sense of security). “Pulls away” easily turns into “is using cold violence,” and the response changes. This is a possible mechanism, not a measured effect. The scale for 恋爱脑 (lian’ainao, “a head taken over by love”) was built on students: it doesn’t make everyone who gets called that a special type, and it doesn’t describe mainland youth as a whole. What the word means in your couple, you find out with a question: “What action do you mean?”

Words and deeds. In a family context, Chinese people say “I love you” less often than Americans and rely more on actions; how often the words are spoken between current romantic partners on the mainland hasn’t been measured. Belief in a “fated bond” (缘分, yuanfen) doesn’t stop people from investing in the relationship: in a small survey in Hong Kong it went together with satisfaction, but romantic love predicted both satisfaction and commitment better.

Family is a participant

A new partner is a trigger not only for the two of you but for the relatives too. A parent may see security in a job and housing, an adult child in care and attraction. Each person’s response becomes a new trigger for the others.

By the end of the seventies, marriages arranged by parents had become rare, and later almost disappeared: couples meet on their own or through family and friends2. These data are old; today’s apps aren’t in them. Interviews with urban app users show both new possibilities and old roles; how many couples started there, this study doesn’t say (chapter 08).

In a survey of parents and adult children, the parents rated status and contribution to the family higher, the children “good genes” and romantic love, but these are preferences, not who chose the spouse. Interviews with educated, working single women from Beijing describe pressure and different responses to it, but don’t estimate how often the parents have the last word. “Leftover women” is a pressure label, not a forecast, and age is not an expiration date.

Relationships with relatives are linked to how a marriage goes. Couples in the first years of marriage were surveyed several years in a row: the parents’ satisfaction with their child’s marriage affected the child’s own satisfaction through the child’s relationship with the in-laws: for a husband, his wife’s father and mother; for a wife, her husband’s father and mother. When the relationships with the father-in-law and the mother-in-law were counted together, only one path remained — for wives, through the mother-in-law (婆媳, poxi)3. Being drawn into “triangles” (when tension between two people is shifted onto a third, for example onto a parent) went together with marriage quality. This is a link, and the cause remains open.

Money

Bride price (彩礼, caili): money or gifts from the groom’s family to the bride’s family. Since 2024 a Supreme Court interpretation has been in effect: demanding property under the guise of marriage is not allowed; if the couple registered and lived together, the bride price is, as a rule, not returned in a divorce; with a short time living together and an excessive sum, the court may return part of it, taking into account, among other things, customs, pregnancy and the fault of the parties; for unregistered couples, the same factors are considered.

What people look for in a partner: in a national survey experiment, both women and men preferred an imagined spouse with a higher income and housing. The sense of security (安全感) wasn’t measured there, so “Chinese women look above all for security” is neither confirmed nor refuted, and the survey says nothing about what a particular woman wants (chapter 20).

What the numbers say about marriages

Marriage registrations have dropped by more than half in recent years4, but this is a count of events, not an assessment of whether couples handle feelings better or worse. Divorces fell noticeably after their peak (in the year the “cooling-off period” was introduced), then partly came back. One study links this period to fewer formalized divorces, but the observation was short, and it shows neither fewer harmful marriages nor better well-being for the spouses. Two reviews on marital satisfaction among Chinese couples disagree: one sees a decline, the other a rise (chapter 22).

If there are more than two of you

There is no data on multiple partners on the mainland: no representative survey distinguishing open relationships, swinging, polyamory and relationship anarchy has been found. So “attitudes toward them there are uniformly negative” hasn’t been tested, and neither has the opposite; conclusions about one form don’t carry over to others. Stories about 二奶 (ernai, “kept mistress”), concubinage and secret affairs are about secrecy, not about everyone’s consent. The status of a marriage, of an extramarital relationship and of a written agreement are three different legal questions: check the current law. Ask each person involved separately (chapters 24, 27, 28; on joy for your partner — 26).

If you come from different cultures

There is almost no data on couples where one person grew up in China and the other in Russia or Europe: similar studies were done on other groups (mixed marriages in the US, couples in Australia and Hong Kong). The traps of a non-native language: chapters 33, 37 and 38.

How to tell it is happening

In yourself:

  • You draw a conclusion from your partner’s face without asking: quiet joy reads as “my partner doesn’t care.”
  • You want to say “be happier, louder” or “answer now.”
  • A conversation about the relatives comes down to “will they allow it or not.”

In your partner:

  • They smile and change the subject when you expect a big reaction.
  • They take a pause so as not to inflame things, and you hear a refusal.
  • They answer with actions, not words.

What to do

1. Ask about joy instead of reading it off a face

When: your partner’s reaction is quieter than you expected, or in advance, before a celebration.

What to do: instead of “Does this even make you happy?” ask: “What kind of joy feels comfortable to you — noisy or quiet, in front of people or just the two of us? How can I tell that you’re feeling good when your face is calm?” If analyzing comes easier than feeling, ask about the body: “What’s in your body at a pleasant meeting — comfort or tension?” Words alone may not be enough to answer.

How you know it worked: you can tell a quiet “I’m feeling good” from “I don’t care,” and nobody has to act out joy.

2. The “event — body — meaning — wish” conversation

As a whole format it hasn’t been evaluated: there are no doses and no frequency. Of its parts, only putting a feeling into words has been tested, and that was in a different task. The question about a pause is a suggestion, not a result of an experiment.

When: after a misunderstanding, when you can both talk.

What to do:

  1. Ask: “Do you need a pause right now, support, or a conversation?” After the pause, check out loud what it meant.
  2. Before any explanations, name one sensation in your body, one possible feeling and one thing that’s unclear.
  3. Taking turns, in either language: what happened; what’s in your body (or “can’t make it out yet”); “I take it to mean…”; “I wanted…”; a question to the other: “What did my reaction come across as to you?”
  4. If there’s anger behind it, name three things: what action was expected, what happened, what you had actually agreed on (chapter 16).
  5. Switch roles.

How you know it worked: both descriptions are understood, and nobody pushed.

3. Family and money as a separate conversation, without the relatives

When: the relationship is getting serious, or the family is starting to influence decisions.

What to do: instead of “will the family allow it,” each of you answers three questions separately. What do you want to tell your family about your union? What obligations and support are there, including money? Who makes the final decision? About money, clarify: does anyone expect money to be handed over, how much, to whom, and is having your own home a wish or a hard condition. Don’t plug in a price from a story about another city — ask the family. The same questions work for same-sex and non-monogamous unions; how much more dangerous disclosure is for them, the survey of parents and children didn’t measure (on the family of origin in a couple — chapter 30, on parenthood — 31).

How you know it worked: you both have the same facts, not guesses.

4. The view of a well-meaning observer

When: after a recent falling-out, when you’re both calm.

What to do: describe it in writing through the eyes of a neutral person who wishes you both well. Write for about twenty minutes; reading it out loud is optional. How many times to repeat it is unknown: start with one falling-out.

How you know it worked: a week later, each of you separately writes down the date and answers: did you feel heard; were the agreements kept; did the argument end without fear. These are suggested measures, not a tested questionnaire.

In American married couples, this exercise preserved marriage quality over the observation period5. There were no Chinese–European, non-monogamous or same-sex couples there. There are also ready-made programs: OurRelationship (tested in the US) and a version for married couples in Beijing (the data are preliminary for now). These are optional, not a reason to insist if your partner doesn’t want to.

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: Chinese people are reserved, so they feel less.
In fact: what gets suppressed is the expression, not the feeling itself. Among American students, a habit of hiding feelings went together with a bad mood; among students from Hong Kong, it didn’t6. But the survey didn’t measure how strong the feelings were, and it doesn’t settle whether restraint is harmless for Chinese partners. A quiet person in trouble needs a question, not the conclusion “they don’t feel anything.”

People think: the bride price is always paid, and it’s huge.
In fact: in a survey of older people, the groom’s family paid in most cases, and a dowry was given in almost half7. “Always” doesn’t hold. The bride price was on average several times larger than the dowry, but the size depends on place and time, and the people surveyed married earlier, so the figures describe today’s grooms only in part.

People think: there are so many divorces and so many marriages, so that’s the chance of divorce.
In fact: these are events in one year among different couples: the people divorcing married in all sorts of different years. The chance is calculated from a group observed over years, and there’s no such calculation in the data8, 4.

Talk it over together

If there are more than two of you, ask the questions to everyone they concern.

  1. When I’m quiet or take a pause, what do you hear in it? What do you need more in an argument — a pause, support, or talking right away?
  2. What does caring through actions mean to you, and at what moments do you want to hear words?
  3. What from our life do you want to tell your family, and what do you want to keep between us? Who in your family makes decisions that affect us?
  4. Who does what at home, who earns money and who takes care of the parents — and when will we revisit this if the job or the country we live in changes?

When it is no longer about skills

Everything above is for ordinary misunderstandings where you’re both safe. If the relationship includes:

  • intimidation;
  • coercive control or surveillance;
  • violence;
  • real fear of your partner,

stop the joint exercises from this chapter: saying the truth out loud in that situation isn’t safe, and a conversation script doesn’t replace safety measures. The word 冷暴力 (“cold violence”) is a label, not proof: look at actions and control. What to do next — chapter 23.

33. The intercultural couple: feelings in a foreign language

In short

An intercultural couple is not doomed: research hasn’t found that such couples are, on average, less satisfied with their relationship. But a word for a feeling can mean different things to the two of you, and the weight of what’s said can depend on the language.

What to do about it: instead of arguing “how things are done where we’re from,” ask “what did this mean to you, and what do you think it meant to me.” Choose the language for tenderness, for fights and for important agreements separately, and translate agreements with consequences precisely and write them down.

Before putting a label on a feeling, find the sensation in your body. Talk through money, parents, and the right to live and work in a country before you move.

Katya and Mei have been together for more than a year. At home they speak English — a second language for both of them. After dinner at Mei’s parents’, Katya quietly says: “I’m upset.” Inside her, it sounds like the Russian “мне обидно” (mne obidno): in front of everyone, Mei had let slip a joke about her accent. Mei hears “sad.” She hugs Katya, puts the kettle on and asks her not to be sad. Katya pulls away: what she needed wasn’t tea but an acknowledgment that the joke had hurt. Mei feels the chill and doesn’t understand what she got wrong. By night, each of them is sure she said everything clearly enough.

Nobody wanted a fight. One word passed through two languages and lost the main thing along the way.

What is going on

An emotion runs along a loop: something happened → body → meaning → action. In a couple with two languages, part of the meaning can get lost at every link.

Body. Breathing, pulse, muscle tension — the first link of the loop. In a second language the response may be weaker: in one lab experiment with bilinguals (Turkish–English), reprimands and taboo words in the second language caused a weaker skin response than in the native one1. A word that seems mild to the person speaking it in their second language may sound like contempt to a partner who grew up with it. This is plausible, but it hasn’t been measured in couples. “In my language it sounds softer” explains the mismatch, while your partner’s hurt remains a topic for the conversation.

Meaning. The word chooses the interpretation. “I’m angry” can mean: I was treated unfairly, I didn’t get what I wanted, I’m not being respected. Most often behind anger is a broken expectation, and people from different countries may have different expectations (chapter 16). The boundaries of words don’t match either. The Russian “обидно” (obidno) puts in front the insult, the broken expectation and the person who broke it, and asks more for “acknowledge it” than for “comfort me.” This is an analysis of meaning, not a measured pattern: check it with a question.

Action. What was said or left unsaid becomes an event for the other person and gets translated into their own language: words into their own words, behavior into their own expectations. Silence can’t be decoded by nationality: there’s no reliable data on how often silence in Chinese romantic couples means disagreement rather than agreement. Better to ask for a clear, freely given answer (chapter 37). The skill here is to slow down the translation: notice the body’s signal, name it, ask what it meant.

Where arguments come from. Nationality is not a mechanism. People argue about specific things: language, parents’ demands, the right to live and work in a country, money. The outcome depends on how the couple reads and resolves them — this is a guess, not a measured chain. One person sees money sent to parents as loyalty, the other as leaving the couple (chapter 30). In couples from Australia and Hong Kong, those whose ideas about family duties matched were more satisfied with the relationship; this is a link, not a cause. Moving adds pressure: a visa deadline and work restrictions can turn an ordinary argument into a threat, and the one who keeps their job and has family nearby can say “no” more freely.

Closeness isn’t on every point. With a partner from another culture, you may not find closeness in your native language, favorite songs and books, jokes that are funny without explanation only to your own people. That’s not coldness yet, and not a failed relationship. Closeness is where it is: in shared tasks and plans, in conversations, in feelings. What’s missing you can look for among friends and acquaintances with the same native language and shared songs, rather than demanding it from your partner on every point. This is a working idea, not a tested rule. Where your closeness actually lies, you find out by asking, not by deducing it from a passport (see “What people usually think — and what is true”).

Feeling words that don’t translate

These are working translations — prompts for a conversation, not measured equivalents. For Russian–Chinese couples there’s no research on these words. If a word is in dispute, ask: what it means to this person, in what situation it comes up, and what response is expected behind it.

  • 委屈 (wěiqu) — “I was treated unfairly, and it’s hard to object”; not just sadness. Silent endurance is easy to mistake for agreement. Ask: “What was unfair, and what couldn’t you say?” If tears come — chapter 05.
  • 心疼 (xīnténg) — “my heart aches for you; I feel for you and want to take care of you.” It sounds like care: ask what kind of help is expected behind it.
  • 撒娇 (sājiāo) — to be sweetly capricious, to ask with cute pressure. What’s a game to one person may seem childish to the other: ask how it’s received. Its benefit for romantic couples hasn’t been tested.
  • 吃醋 (chīcù) — “to be jealous.” The word doesn’t say whether an agreement was broken: separate the feeling from the question “what did we agree on” (chapter 25).
  • 安全感 (ānquángǎn) — “a sense of safety and security.” A useful self-description, but not a proven “main need” of Chinese women. Ask what it’s made of for this person.
  • 冷暴力 (lěng bàolì) — “cold violence”: ignoring, silent punishment. An everyday label, not a diagnosis; an agreed pause is not coercion. If there’s control and fear — chapter 23.
  • 面子 (miànzi) — “face,” reputation in the eyes of others. A way into a conversation about family: whose opinion matters and what will preserve dignity (chapter 30).
  • тоска (toska) — an aching longing for what’s missing or out of reach. There’s no single English word, but it can be explained: translate the situation and the response you want, not the word.
  • обида (obida) — “I was hurt, an expectation or a boundary was broken.” Behind it there may be a request to acknowledge, not to comfort. Ask: “What was expected, did we agree on it, what would count as repair?”
  • нежность (nezhnost’) — affectionate softness. Russian speakers have no monopoly on it; ask what action conveys it to this particular person.

How to tell it is happening

In yourself:

  • You talk about a feeling in your second language, while inside a different word is sounding: “upset,” when you meant “обидно.”
  • It seems you said it clearly, but what you get back isn’t what you need: tea instead of acknowledgment.
  • You explain your partner’s silence by nationality: “that’s how they do it.”
  • You understood an agreement about money, documents or parents differently.
  • You expect closeness from your partner in everything that’s yours — language, songs, jokes — and read every “I don’t get it” as coldness.

In your partner:

  • They pull away after a word that seemed mild to you.
  • They’re silent, and it’s unclear: is it agreement or endurance.
  • The argument seems to be about one thing, but behind it are parents, money, the right to live and work in a country.

What to do

1. Choose the language for the task

There’s no tested exercise for bilingual couples; this is a reasonable recommendation. But the language of a decision isn’t neutral: in lab tasks in a foreign language, people were more willing to cause harm for a gain and less likely to avoid risk2.

When: at a calm moment, not during a fight.

What to do: each of you separately writes down in which language it’s easier to express tenderness, to fight, and to agree on money and documents. Compare your notes. Agreements with consequences — money, housing, visas, children — write them down and translate them precisely, reading them over together.

How you know it worked: after a few weeks, ask each other whether understanding has gotten easier. It’s working if nobody’s language is considered “worse” for serious topics.

2. A conversation in five steps

This format has never been evaluated in trials, so the dose and the success rate are unknown.

When: when the first wave after a fight has passed; one episode at a time.

What to do: taking turns, in any language: “The event that hooked me: … In my body: … (or ‘I can’t tell yet’). How I interpret it: … What I wanted: … How do you understand my reaction?” When you hear “my chest tightens,” ask what that means for the person saying it, rather than substituting “you’re sad?” If the anger is about a broken expectation, name three things: what was expected, what happened, and what you had actually agreed on.

How you know it worked: after each attempt, ask whether both descriptions were understood and whether there was any feeling of pressure.

3. The view of an outside observer

A short exercise of this kind was tested on married couples from the US: it preserved marriage quality over time3. The size of the effect isn’t confirmed, and the exercise hasn’t been tested in intercultural, same-sex or open couples.

When: after a recent argument, once you’ve cooled down.

What to do: each of you separately writes about the argument from the position of a neutral observer who wishes you both well. A safe frequency of repeats hasn’t been tested; there’s no schedule.

How you know it worked: a week later, each of you separately answers: did you feel heard; were the agreements kept; was the argument resolved without fear. This is a suggested set of questions, not a tested scale.

4. Body first, then the label

This combination hasn’t been studied in couples. In one experiment with English-speaking university adults, learning words for emotions did not significantly improve the differentiation of negative feelings or self-confidence, and did not reduce distress4; this doesn’t yet prove a zero effect.

When: when you can explain an emotion neatly but don’t notice your body.

What to do: before the label, check your breathing, muscle tension and the urge to act (chapters 02 and 03).

How you know it worked: the word came after the sensation, not instead of it.

5. A list of questions before moving

When: before moving, changing countries or visas.

What to do: go through the items: legal status, ability to earn money, healthcare, the language of agreements, children, visits to both families, money transfers, a safe exit plan (chapter 36). This is a list of questions about risks, not a tested protocol.

How you know it worked: every item has an answer, and both of you have it written down.

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: an intercultural couple means a higher risk of divorce.
In fact: research hasn’t found that such couples are, on average, less satisfied with their relationship5.
