Only part of it is true: in the Netherlands and Germany divorce is more common where the cultural distance is large6, while in the US, among interracial couples, no overall extra risk was found after accounting for the spouses’ characteristics (race isn’t the same as language and culture). These are links, not causes. Fatalism obscures specific questions about work, money and parents.

People think: feelings are weaker in a second language.
In fact: most multilingual people find “I love you” weightier in their native language, but some rate the languages as equal, or even find the one learned later stronger7. In a survey of people in intercultural couples, at first some lacked “emotional resonance” or felt they were speaking insincerely, but for most the barriers disappeared over months, and the partner’s language often became the “language of the heart.” These are answers in a survey, not declarations to real partners. A weak response to a phrase is not a measure of feeling.

People think: if a word doesn’t exist in another language, then the feeling doesn’t exist either.
In fact: the absence of a word doesn’t prove the feeling is absent: emotion words in many languages line up around shared axes — pleasantness and arousal — while the boundaries of meaning differ8. Lists of “untranslatable words” don’t measure culture: take them as a reason to ask a question, not as a portrait of your partner.

People think: Chinese partners show closeness through care and actions, and they don’t need words about feelings.
In fact: cultural differences exist, but research doesn’t confirm such a general rule. Among dating couples in Canada, people of Chinese descent disclosed less to their partner on average; this was partly explained by more traditional views on the roles of men and women, not by a tendency to put family and group above oneself9. Among couples living in Australia, most indirect ways of arguing were rare in all groups — Chinese, Western and mixed — and almost none of them were found to be linked to relationship satisfaction10. These are data on these groups, not on all Chinese partners and not on mainland China; they say nothing about a particular person. What words mean to your partner, and what actions mean, you find out by asking (see “Talk it over together”). The label is dangerous: it can easily be used to justify avoidance or indifference.

What we don’t know

Almost nothing has been measured about Russian–Chinese couples. Marriages with foreigners in China are few, and official statistics also include overseas Chinese and residents of Hong Kong, Macao and Taiwan. Studies of the border region and of marriage migration from the former USSR describe a different combination: a Russian wife and a Chinese husband. In the border region, as one researcher describes it, such marriages are often marriages of convenience, with mutual stereotypes and distrust. For a union of “a Russian-speaking man and a woman from mainland China,” there are no estimates.

The expectation “foreigner means high status” doesn’t hold everywhere. According to one study of the Chinese industry for dating Western men, the clients are mostly middle-aged women, often divorced, looking for a husband themselves — not young beauties. Such studies don’t give a probability that a Chinese woman ranks a foreigner higher. Instead of guessing at a “motive by passport,” look at the real power: who has the right to residence, whose income it is, who translates, who can leave a bad relationship. On love and family in today’s China — chapter 32.

The data in this chapter come from different groups (Australian and Hong Kong couples, Hong Kong youth, Chinese Americans and Canadians of Chinese descent, survey participants in China) and don’t substitute for one another. In a small survey of Hong Kong youth, belief in 缘 (yuán, “fate-encounter”) was linked to greater satisfaction, but romantic love predicted it better. About family, a shared language, and tenderness in public and in private, ask the partners themselves.

If there are more than two of you: there’s no data on the language of feelings in open unions and polyamory; everything described was obtained from couples and from multilingual people in general. Different kinds of open unions don’t carry over to one another. For jealousy, it helps to distinguish three phrases: “I’m jealous,” “I didn’t have enough information,” “an agreement was broken” — as options for a conversation, not diagnoses (chapters 25 and 27). “Compersion” (joy at your partner’s joy, chapter 26) has no tested Chinese translation. Ask each participant separately about consent, privacy and agreements.

Talk it over together

  1. In which language is it easier for you to say “I love you,” “it hurts” and “I’m angry”? Is it the same one in which you want to hear the answer?
  2. Which feeling words in your language are hard to translate? What response do you expect when you say them?
  3. What do pet names mean to you — “зайка” (zaika), 宝贝 (bǎobèi), 老公 / 老婆 (lǎogōng / lǎopó): a game, closeness, a commitment? Is it the same in private and in front of family?
  4. Which obligations to your family — money, visits, housing — go without saying for you?
  5. If one of us wants to leave: who has the right to stay and work, and what’s needed in advance so that each of us can leave safely?
  6. What did my last action in a fight mean to you — and what do you think it meant to me?
  7. Where do we already have closeness, and which things of your own — language, songs, jokes — is it easier for you to look for among friends? Which of my words and actions mean care, respect and desire to you?

When it is no longer about skills

The exercises in this chapter are meant for a couple where each person can speak without fear. Red flags: your partner intimidates you, controls you, monitors your messages and movements, threatens or uses violence, and you’re afraid for your own safety. Then openly talking about feelings and joint exercises around fights are unsafe: stop them. Talk about this away from your partner with someone you trust, and look for help designed for violence and control (chapter 23).

Part VII. Putting it all together

34. A support network: loneliness and “who else is there”

In short

Loneliness isn’t the number of people around you but the gap between the closeness you want and the closeness you have. That’s why you can be lonely even as a couple.

Strong ties with people go together with a better chance of living longer, and the idea that one partner should cover all your needs sounds beautiful but is poorly tested.

What to do: notice loneliness in your body, figure out what kind of support you need and from whom, and ask for it instead of waiting for someone to guess. Respond to someone else’s pain first by reflecting the feeling, then by asking whether they want advice.

Liza comes home from work and finds Yan in the kitchen: the tea has gone cold, the phone screen has gone dark. “How was your day?” — “Fine.” Liza hears a closed door in that “fine” and goes off to watch a show. Yan hears the footsteps behind the wall and thinks: she’s not interested. Before, at this hour Yan had coworkers from the old city and a friend she could call any time. After the move, there’s one listener left, and Yan protects her, doesn’t want to burden her. They’re both in the same apartment, and they’re both lonely.

What is going on

First, the body. In experiments, women were warned about an electric shock: when their husband sat next to them and held their hand, the brain reacted less strongly to the threat, and this depended on the quality of the marriage. Later the same was found with a friend. During pain, support lowered sweating (a sign of tension) in both women and men. No difference was found between holding a hand, stroking, and simply being there.

A hug before a stressful task lowered the release of the stress hormone in women, but not in men1. What this switched on in the body wasn’t shown, and the relationship afterward wasn’t looked at. In an experiment with a cold virus, hugs explained part of the benefit of support, but people weren’t randomly assigned to hugs, so “hug and you won’t get sick” doesn’t follow from this. These are lab and observational studies of different sizes: a pointer, not a norm. The hormone here isn’t a button: everything depends on who is with you.

Then your head fills in the meaning. A beautiful version, but not tested directly. Loneliness is a felt gap between the closeness you want and the closeness you have. It shifts your attention toward threat: you catch the places where you aren’t accepted. In response you pull away, and the gap grows. The other person reads your withdrawal as indifference and pulls away too. In long-term observations of couples this loop hasn’t been seen directly, so for now it’s an assumption. How to catch the body’s signal before it overwhelms you — chapters 02 and 04. Tears as a way of calling for help — chapter 05.

What’s important to understand. To choose an action, you need to understand what exactly is missing: people, or safety with them. And remember: the other person’s silence may be care, not refusal.

A gap, not a number of people

In everyday life, three things get mixed up: felt loneliness, isolation (few contacts) and living alone. They overlap but don’t coincide2.

What’s known most reliably is this: the stronger a person’s ties, the better their chances of being alive at the end of the observation period. Involvement in other people’s lives worked most strongly; simply “who you live with” worked most weakly, and there may have been no link there at all3. This doesn’t give an optimal number of friends. It’s an average across very different people, not one person’s fate, and it doesn’t prove that socializing will replace a doctor.

When loneliness is already there, what helps isn’t simply “more people.” In a review, programs where people learned to interpret social situations differently worked better than those that simply added contacts4. The question is whether you lack contacts, or safe and pleasant contacts.

Loneliness comes in different kinds, too. You may not have one close person you can come to with anything. You may not have a circle where you’re accepted. A loneliness questionnaire measures these separately: closeness in a couple, in the family and among friends5.

There’s also a working distinction — based on what’s inside. If inside it’s cold and it sounds like “something’s wrong with me, I’m not worth warmth,” a new person nearby may not warm you up: what’s more likely needed is gentleness toward yourself and checking your thoughts about others. If inside it’s warm, but it seems no one will ever fully live through your experience, it may help to share it with people going through something similar. The distinction itself hasn’t been tested. Programs against loneliness help on average in the short term, although the data are weak and mixed6; but no trials were found where help was matched to the kind of loneliness. And the advantage of programs that change interpretation rests on a small number of studies4.

Lonely as a couple

In a long-term observation of couples, married and not, those who were lonely at the start became less satisfied with the relationship over the years, and so did their partners, with a steeper decline7. Why this happens, the authors don’t know. The reverse is also possible: dissatisfaction with the relationship may itself intensify loneliness. In a survey of same-sex couples, stress and the ability to cope with it together were also linked to relationship satisfaction, but a single snapshot doesn’t show the cause.

So don’t count the hours together; look at four things: whether you feel understood, how things are with conflicts, whether you have company, and access to friends. Small bids for each other’s attention — chapter 14; the balance of closeness and autonomy — chapter 19.

One pillar or several

One event gives rise to different needs: someone close comforts you, a friend shares the experience, a third person knows how it gets solved in practice. The idea that a partner is obliged to cover everything hasn’t been confirmed by direct trials. There’s only a framework for thinking, not a test: according to one model, in American marriage expectations grow faster than the time and energy for it. These studies don’t support the conclusion “the more pillars, the better” either. Friends provide company, a reality check and resources outside the couple’s conflict loop, but the tested data have neither a threshold of “so many close friends” nor a proven effect of friendship as a buffer in fights. Instead of counting, ask yourself: is there a person with whom you feel supported, and for which situations is that kind of support missing?

There is a tension. If all your vulnerability goes outside, the couple’s agreement may suffer. If one person is the only listener, dependence may grow. So discuss what each connection can realistically give and who agrees to that role. For polyamorous people, several romantic partners don’t in themselves mean more support. For monogamous people, meaningful non-romantic connections aren’t cheating. There’s no comparative data on this (chapter 27).

Support from within: after your own mistake

When someone close to you is hurting because of you, harsh self-criticism can turn admitting a mistake into admitting that you’re good for nothing. Self-compassion — treating yourself as a person who also has bad times, without dropping responsibility — may help you look at what happened less defensively and move on to making it right. The difference matters: “I make mistakes and I’m responsible for the consequences” is not the same as “I forgive myself, so you should forgive me too.”

The data are limited: acceptance helps turn regret into improvement, but whether the partner received an effective apology and noticed a change hasn’t been established. It’s more reliable to ask the person who was hurt whether the behavior has changed (chapter 17).

Two kinds of guilt. Healthy guilt knows its limits: “my fault is in this.” It’s about your action, and it can be atoned for: it’s clear what to do to fix it. It doesn’t demand that you punish yourself. Corrosive guilt spreads over everything: “it’s all because of me.” It’s tied not to the action but to the other person’s state: as long as the other person feels bad, the guilt is on you, and no fix counts. This is a working distinction; it hasn’t been tested. How to forgive yourself step by step — technique 6.

If there are more than two of you

There’s little data, and the participants there selected themselves. Among people with several partners at once, attachment differed from relationship to relationship. The experiments with hand-holding and hugs were done on married or mixed-sex couples: they haven’t been tested for open unions, polyamory, “chosen family” or same-sex couples. That more partners protect against loneliness hasn’t been shown. The data make neither monogamy nor non-monogamy the default norm. A working move is a map: who agrees to which kind of support and what information is available to whom (chapters 27 and 29).

If you come from different cultures

There’s no direct data on loneliness and sources of support in “Russia or Europe — mainland China” couples. The data on ties and survival were collected mainly in North America and Europe. The closest study is about communication in Western, Chinese and mixed couples in Australia: the cultural makeup of the couple is linked both to behavior in conversation and to satisfaction. But this isn’t mainland China, and an average won’t predict a person. That a woman from mainland China prefers indirect communication, that her family will run the union, or that a Russian-speaking man will prefer directness — there’s no data. Instead of guessing, ask: what do a pause, consulting with family, a translated word about a feeling mean to you (chapters 33 and 37).

How to tell it is happening

In yourself:

  • An emptiness or heaviness in your chest, you want to curl up, any attempt to start a conversation irritates you.
  • There are people around, but no one to talk to about hard things. Or there’s one listener, and you protect that person.
  • You answer “fine” to “how was your day?” even though it isn’t.
  • You don’t count the care you’re given: you read advice or an offered dinner as “they want to get rid of me.”

In your partner:

  • They answer briefly and closed off, go into another room or into a screen.
  • They pull away, and you read it as indifference. And the other person may be reading yours the same way.
  • They stay silent out of care, so as not to burden you.

What to do

1. Notice and accept — for two weeks

When: loneliness or stress hits.

What to do:

  1. Find the sensation in your body: pressure in your chest, a lump in your throat, heaviness.
  2. Name it and the emotion precisely, without arguing with the fact that they’re there: “tightness in my chest, this is loneliness.” “Lonely” isn’t the same as “bored” or “hurt.”
  3. Choose the next action: write to a specific person, go out where there are people, or calmly finish what you’re doing.

If the sensation is hard to catch, start with the question “where is it tense right now?” This addition hasn’t been tested on its own.

Of the techniques here, this one has the strongest testing. In a randomized trial, the combination “notice and accept” reduced the body’s stress response more than “notice” alone or a control activity. Loneliness and contacts were measured before and after two weeks of sessions in an app, one a day, and the result went to the combination8. But it’s one study, and not on couples.

How you know it worked: two ratings every day: how lonely you feel (on your own scale) and how many live contacts you had, plus a note about the action you chose. If loneliness went down but contacts didn’t increase, that’s also a result. If there are more contacts but the loneliness is the same, look at the quality of the contacts.

Accepting doesn’t mean putting up with abuse. An app won’t create safe relationships where the main obstacle is access to people or control.

2. Say what support you need, and ask for it

When: you feel bad, and you’re about to answer “fine.”

What to do: ask yourself: “What do I want right now?” Comfort, shared experience, practical help, or just someone being there? Then say it in one sentence: “Right now I need someone to just listen, without advice” or “I need practical advice.” If your partner feels bad, don’t guess: “Do you need me more to listen, to hug you, to help solve it, or to leave you alone?”

How you know it worked: you get what you ask for, and your partner doesn’t have to guess from your face. This is a working hypothesis, not an experimental result: check it by asking your partner whether it worked.

3. A map of supports

Sounds reasonable, but it hasn’t been tested on couples.

What to do:

  1. Each of you separately writes down a few recurring hard situations: work, family, loneliness, a fight, fear.
  2. Next to each one, write who helps right now: your partner, a friend, family, a coworker, someone who knows the subject.
  3. Mark what you want: comfort, shared experience or practical knowledge.
  4. Together you discuss: what your partner is ready to give, what stays only between you, who else you could call on, and whether that person agrees.

When and how often: one conversation of about half an hour, then a review when things change: a move, a job, a child, one of your supports dropping away.

How you know it worked: every recurring situation has a named support, there’s no topic where the only listener is one person, and the agreements are kept.

4. Reflect the feeling, then ask about advice

When: the other person is sharing something hard.

What to do: restate the feeling and the context in your own words: “It got to you that you were interrupted at the meeting, and after that you just want to go home.” Ask: “Is that how it is for you?” Ask whether they want advice or whether it’s enough to just be there. Separate the feeling from agreeing with the facts: you can acknowledge “you’re hurt” without accepting the other person’s version of events.

This has only been tested on students, with written scenes of rejection: responses that reflected the feeling were compared with dismissive ones. It hasn’t been tested between partners.

How you know it worked: the person confirms or corrects your reflection, and the tension doesn’t grow after the conversation. “Not quite” isn’t a failure, it’s part of the exercise.

5. Count care in the form it’s given

When: you feel bad, and someone close or a friend responds differently than you wanted: with advice, tea, dinner, practical help instead of a conversation about feelings.

What to do: before concluding “they don’t see me,” ask yourself: maybe this is their way of caring, it’s just how this person knows how to do it? If so, accept it as care and say thank you. If the other person doesn’t notice your state, it isn’t necessarily indifference: they may have a lot of their own worries or not have enough energy. Then check what else is missing, and ask for it directly (technique 2).

How you know it worked: you notice more of the care around you, even if its form is the same, and there are fewer reproaches of “you don’t see me.”

This is a working technique; it hasn’t been tested. And it’s not about settling for less: if what you need never comes, that’s a topic for the map of supports (technique 3).

6. Forgive yourself step by step

When: guilt over something you did won’t let go, and the thought “something’s wrong with me” gets in the way of fixing it. Pick a calm day and a small story.

What to do (ideally in writing):

  1. The facts: what exactly happened and who was hurt by it.
  2. The moment of decision: what drove you then, honestly. It could be interest, need, an attempt to avoid something worse.
  3. Resources: did you have enough of them then to act differently.
  4. Regret: let it come. It’s a pity it turned out this way: you feel for the person who was hurt, and for yourself.
  5. A view from outside: look at yourself in that moment as you would at someone close to you. Do you want to help?
  6. Repair: what can be done now, for the other person and for yourself? Name one concrete step.

How you know it worked: instead of heaviness, resolve appears, the step is taken, and the person who was hurt notices a change. If shame overwhelms you, stop and take a smaller story.

These steps haven’t been tested as a program.

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: loneliness is as harmful as 15 cigarettes a day.
In fact: this is an illustration at the population level, not a measured “dose” for a particular person. In reviews, loneliness, isolation and living alone are separate exposures, measured by observing people, not by experiment. Cigarettes weren’t measured there. What is true is something else: strong ties go together with higher survival3, 2.

People think: people without a partner are unhappier than people in relationships.
In fact: one comparison isn’t enough to say that every single person is unhappy and that it’s about being in a couple. Status doesn’t replace connection: loneliness predicted a drop in relationship satisfaction both in marriages and outside them7.

People think: self-compassion makes you go soft, and you stop fixing your mistakes.
In fact: in experiments, after self-compassion people wanted to fix their weaknesses and make amends more than after self-esteem, distraction or no intervention, and they spent more time preparing for a task9. True, what was measured was desire and short-term behavior in the lab, not a change that a partner would confirm.

Talk it over together

The questions work for two people and for several partners. Ask them at a calm moment and leave room for “I don’t know.”

  1. Besides me, who is it easier for you to talk to about hard things, and what do you get there that you don’t get from me?
  2. What are we missing right now: more people around, or safer contact with the ones we have?
  3. When you feel lonely next to me, what do I usually do, and what could I do differently?
  4. When you feel bad, what do you want from me: to listen, to hug you, to help solve it, to leave you alone? How can I find out without guessing?
  5. If there are more than two of us: who can count on which kind of support, and whose consent is needed to bring someone else into the circle?
  6. Which of my care reaches you, and which gets lost along the way? In what form is it easier for you to accept it?

When it is no longer about skills

Everything above is for ordinary difficulties where you’re both safe overall. If the relationship includes:

  • a ban on seeing friends and family;
  • a demand to be the only listener;
  • control over who you talk to,

that’s isolation, not a question of balancing your supports. A hug or a hand helps only when it’s wanted and safe. Unwanted touch stops immediately. Signs and steps — chapter 23; who to turn to — chapter 35.

Separately, about guilt. If “it’s all because of me” comes up over anything at all and no fix takes the guilt away, it’s better to work through it with a professional (chapter 35).

35. A toolkit — and when to see a professional

In short

You pick a tool based on which link of the chain “body → meaning → action → the two of you locking together” you get stuck on.

Couples therapy helps on average, but the numbers are easy to misread: “on average” doesn’t mean “almost everyone” or “forever.”

What to do: start with the question of safety, take one protocol below, and look at what has changed for each of you separately.

See a professional when the chain keeps coming back despite the tools, or when a broken agreement can’t be fixed by the two of you. If there’s fear, threats or surveillance, what’s needed isn’t couples work but separate support.

Artyom and Dima are arguing again about whose turn it is to call his mother. The night before, Dima read a roundup of “techniques for couples” and lays out three at once: say thank you, say “I feel … when you…,” take a time-out. It comes out rehearsed and flat, and Artyom hears an accusation in it. His jaw is already clenched, but he doesn’t notice, and he answers with pressure. It isn’t about the call. They never agreed on whose duty it is, and each was sure they had. The techniques were picked at random — without understanding which link they work on.

What is going on

First, the body. When something gets to you, your heart speeds up, your breathing gets shorter, your muscles tighten, your vision narrows. In that moment you hear worse. Of the studies in this chapter, the body was measured in only one lab experiment: people were asked to imagine forgiving or holding a grudge, and their short bodily reactions were observed1. Everything else said about the body is a suggestion. Noticing that you’ve been overwhelmed before you accuse — this may give you time to understand more accurately what happened. Attention alone doesn’t create trust in your partner.

Then meaning and action. The body fills in the meaning: “they don’t respect me,” “they’re hiding something” — and prompts what to do. A drawn-out rift is a chain: accusation, anxiety, self-defense, counter-accusation. Each link sets off another. Schools of couples therapy explain their benefit by making the chain visible: once you can see it, you can interrupt it. But this is only a proposed explanation: what exactly produces lasting improvement hasn’t been proven.

The tools are tied to different links. Body: a pause and a time-out (protocols 1–2). Meaning: an expectations journal, a view from outside, taking jealousy apart (3–5). Action: an apology, an agreement, a “yes” with a next step (6–8). The two of you locking together: a checkup, an online program, a movie with a conversation, therapy (9–11 and the section on professionals).

Attention and nice phrases. Mindfulness practice teaches you to notice, but it doesn’t fix a broken agreement. An early mindfulness program for couples without serious distress produced improvement compared with those on a waiting list. But a later review showed: the participants were mostly white, educated, heterosexual, married or cohabiting people, and there are few strong comparisons with other treatment. The popular scheme “observation — feeling — need — request” as a treatment for couples hasn’t been confirmed by trials: what’s usually cited is a master’s thesis review, not a review of real trials. No separate trial was found for gratitude and for being happy about your partner’s good news either (chapter 14). A fluent script can hide a reproach, and demanding that a frightened person speak softly can make a power imbalance worse.

How to tell it is happening

Where you get stuck:

  • Body: you snap in the first second, your jaw is clenched, your breathing is shallow, and afterward you’re surprised at what came out.
  • Meaning: from one episode you have a ready conclusion about the person — “doesn’t respect me,” “is hiding something.”
  • Action: “my partner is supposed to,” even though you never agreed out loud whose duty it is. An apology comes with a “but.”
  • Locking together: the same fight goes around in circles — accusation, self-defense, counter-accusation.

Between you:

  • Each of you is sure you agreed, but you can’t restate the agreement the same way.
  • Being open costs you — during the conversation or after it. Then it isn’t about tools but about safety (the section “When it is no longer about skills”).

What to do

Before any protocol where you talk about something painful together — one question: can each of you tell the truth without paying for it? If not, start with the section “When it is no longer about skills” and chapter 23. Look at the result for each of you separately: before you start and in the months that follow. Where it says a technique hasn’t been tested, the doses and time frames are a suggestion for self-observation.

1. A pause based on body signals

When: you want to object, interrupt, answer right away.

What to do: stop and check four points: breathing, tension (jaw, shoulders, belly), heat (face, chest), the urge to interrupt. Name in one word which is strongest. Only then speak. Do this every time until the check becomes quick. Tears in a fight are a separate topic (chapter 05).

How you know it worked: before your first line there’s a pause of at least one breath, and there’s less accusation in your first sentence.

This hasn’t been tested in trials, including on people who have trouble sensing their body. More on this — chapters 02 and 03.

2. A time-out when it overwhelms you

When: the conversation is speeding up, and you can no longer hear.

What to do: on a calm day, agree on a stop word that either of you has the right to say. When it’s heard, the conversation stops, and the return is scheduled by the clock. During the pause, go back to checking in with your body instead of rehearsing accusations.

How you know it worked: after the pause the conversation starts less sharply, and the stop word isn’t used to win the argument.

No trials of time-outs for couples were found. If disagreeing means you’ll be punished, that’s a safety issue, not a time-out. More on this — chapters 02, 04, 15.

3. A broken-expectation journal

When: after every strong episode of anger, for several weeks in a row.

What to do: write down seven points: the event (what a camera would have seen); the body signal; what behavior you expect; whether that expectation was said out loud; the request; your partner’s response; the tension the next day.

How you know it worked: “my partner is supposed to” turns more and more often into “we agreed” or “we didn’t agree,” and the requests get more specific.

Such a journal hasn’t been tested for anger as a breach of expectations: it’s self-observation, not treatment. It doesn’t give you the right to go through someone else’s devices. If there are several people in the union, write down which partners the agreement concerns. More on this — chapter 16.

4. The view of a neutral third party

When: there’s a real argument you keep going back to in your mind.

What to do: recall it and describe it through the eyes of a well-meaning outsider who cares about both of you. Separate the event from its worst reading. Twenty-one minutes of writing in total — that’s how long people wrote in the trial.

How you know it worked: there’s less tension after arguments, and your rating of the relationship doesn’t sag in the months that follow.

This was tested in a trial: on average, among spouses who wrote such texts, marriage quality stopped declining, while in the comparison group the decline continued2. But it’s one study, American, it measured marriage quality, not divorces, and how large the effect is hasn’t been tested. Stop if the new reading erases a safety question: this isn’t a way to justify violence. More on this — chapters 15, 17.

5. Taking jealousy apart

When: jealousy has flared up.

What to do: go along the chain: what happened, without judgments; the body signal; what meaning is being filled in; what expectation or need is underneath it; whether this was agreed on with this particular person; a request that can be answered “yes” or “no.” If there are more than two of you, add: who the agreement concerns. In writing — after the episode; out loud — at a calm moment.

How you know it worked: the request is specific, and it can be fulfilled.

No separate trials for jealousy were found; the same chain skeleton is used. More on this — chapter 25.

6. An apology step by step

When: after a fight, when it’s clear your partner is hurting because of your words or actions.

What to do: name the action; take responsibility; describe how it affected the other person; offer a fix that can actually be done; no “but.” This is one conversation, and then a check over the following days and weeks: has the behavior changed.

How you know it worked: the person who was hurt says they see the responsibility taken, and what was promised gets done.

No one is obliged to accept an apology. An apology communicates responsibility, forgiveness depends on the hurt person’s motives, trust depends on the behavior afterward; one doesn’t replace another. The scheme has only been tested in lab experiments, on how convincing the parts of an apology are — it’s not a treatment for couples. More on this — chapter 17.

7. An agreement: who agreed to what

When: each of you understands an agreement in your own way, or there’s been a serious episode or changes.

What to do: write down: who agreed to which arrangement; what counts as a breach; what observable behavior will show it’s being kept; whose private information the conversation about repair will touch; who has the right to know what. One conversation is enough to draw it up; revisit it after a serious episode.

How you know it worked: each of you restates the agreement in your own words, and a breach is named by item, not as “you don’t respect me.”

There are no direct trials of such an agreement in unions of several people. More on this — chapters 27, 28, 29.

8. “Yes” — and the next step

When: once — for a conversation about meanings; then — for any “yes” on an important matter.

What to do: each of you says in your own words what an apology, a pause, an explicit promise and telling your family mean to you. From then on, add to any “yes” on an important matter: who, what, by what day, and how we’ll know it’s done.

How you know it worked: by the deadline the step is done or honestly renegotiated.

For couples who speak Russian and Chinese, there’s no data on repair outcomes. This is an arrangement tailored to you, not a tested method. More on this — chapters 33, 37, 38.

9. An annual checkup

When: once a year.

What to do: go through a structured relationship assessment with a professional, get feedback and discuss it together.

How you know it worked: look at each of you separately: stress, closeness, acceptance.

In a trial with married couples, such an annual assessment with feedback increased closeness and acceptance compared with those on a waiting list3. But it’s a professional procedure: a questionnaire from the internet isn’t what was tested.

At home, instead of an assessment — a conversation; these questions themselves haven’t been tested:

  • what each of you relies on in the other, and where you give more and where you take;
  • when specifically you felt close;
  • which feelings can’t be brought into this relationship;
  • what one trait describes the other as a person, not as a partner;
  • what you’re together for, and at what moment of the day it happens.

More on this — chapters 14, 19.

10. A guided online program

When: you need structured work on the relationship, and being open is safe.

What to do: go through a structured online program together and talk with a coach over several calls.

How you know it worked: each person’s satisfaction went up — measure it before, at the end and later.

In a trial with mixed-sex couples, such a program increased relationship satisfaction and confidence compared with those on a waiting list, but it was measured shortly after the program4. That the effect lasts for years, and that it’s safe to work through violence online, hasn’t been shown. More on this — chapter 15.

11. A movie about a couple — and a conversation

When: things are generally fine, and you want to take care of the relationship.

What to do: watch a movie about a couple together and discuss what the characters did and what of it resembles you.

How you know it worked: you understand your relationship better and try to change something, rather than simply staying together.

In an experiment with engaged couples and newlyweds, those who completed either a skills training or a session of discussing movies broke up roughly half as often in the following years as those without a program; these options didn’t differ from each other5. There’s no rigorous test of equivalence and no independent replication. This is prevention, not treatment for serious distress; no data were found for non-monogamous, same-sex or intercultural couples. More on this — chapters 03, 38.

If there are more than two of you

There are no tested trials of repair for non-monogamous unions. In a voluntary survey of people in consensually non-monogamous relationships, both harmful and helpful practices by therapists were found: bias does happen, but how often is unknown. The form of a union and the breach of a specific agreement are different things; what needs fixing is the agreement. If there are several partners, ask: whose trust was broken, who has to agree to disclosure. More on this — chapters 27 and 29.

If you come from different cultures

In an observation of Chinese newlyweds, both communication styles looked better than the fighting style — the warm, supportive one and the moderately restrained one: less demonstrative didn’t mean less satisfied. But how a particular person from mainland China will react doesn’t follow from this, and it doesn’t serve as a norm for Russian-speaking men. There’s no data on repair in a Chinese–European couple, on how an apology is understood in Chinese and in Russian, or on the role of relatives. So take the arrangement from protocol 8, not a “cultural method,” and don’t attribute “natural” directness or indirectness to anyone. More on this — chapters 32, 33, 37 and 38.

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: couples therapy helps most couples, and the result lasts.
In fact: among those who got therapy, satisfaction rose a lot; among those on a waiting list, it barely changed. But this is a “before and after” comparison within each group, not a proven advantage of treatment over a comparison group6. Average follow-up figures say the effect holds, but the data don’t say how many couples slid back. Years later, roughly half the couples were noticeably better, and roughly a quarter had split up; there was no untreated group at that point7. And these are couples with serious distress from one trial, not everyone who seeks help.

People think: there’s one most proven school of couples therapy, and that’s the one to look for.
In fact: the well-known schools have their own trials, but there’s nothing to build a ranking on. In a review of the school that teaches naming vulnerable feelings and asking directly, the numbers are very large, but the studies are too different to put them side by side with others. Another review of this school is a retelling of studies, not a new trial. An independent comparison for depression showed a benefit, but it was too small for general conclusions. Another popular school has a pilot after infidelity: fewer than half the couples made it to the second measurement, and the authors included people connected to the method. Its workshop for expectant parents reduced competitiveness in a family game, but didn’t test either divorce or therapy.

People think: a course for couples protects against divorce.
In fact: a large program for unmarried couples with a newborn didn’t improve either the relationship or co-parenting overall8. At one site, couples from the program stayed together less often than in the comparison group, and children less often lived with both parents — but that’s the result of one site, not an overall one. An army course for spouses also didn’t produce a lasting overall improvement. The outcome probably depends on whether the program actually reached people, whether financial hardship is weighing on them, and whether the course hits their real problem; but this explanation is plausible, not proven. Programs have different target audiences — early relationships, marital distress, the birth of a child — so one program’s result can’t be carried over to another. And to say a program prevents divorce, you have to measure the breakup itself, not just satisfaction with the relationship.

People think: if there’s violence in a couple, they need couples therapy.
In fact: what decides it isn’t whether there was violence once, but whether intimidation, control, fear and retaliation make free participation impossible. This is called coercive control; a clinical review doesn’t justify ordinary couples therapy when it’s present9. There’s no overall figure for the harm of joint therapy.

Talk it over together

If asking out loud isn’t safe — don’t ask; start with the section “When it is no longer about skills.”

  1. Which link of our recurring chain do we get stuck on — the body, the meaning, the action?
  2. What do an apology, a pause, a promise, telling family mean to each of us? How will we know that “yes” means “yes”?
  3. By what signs will each of us see that a tool is working?
  4. Is there a topic that’s frightening to talk about because of the consequences? Who can you tell about it separately?
  5. If we go to a professional, what’s essential for us — their attitude toward our form of union, assessing the result, separate one-on-one sessions?

When to see a professional

The tools above are for disagreements where both of you take part freely and nobody is afraid. A professional is needed in three cases:

  • fear, threats, surveillance, sexual coercion: this isn’t a topic for couples exercises (the section “When it is no longer about skills,” chapter 23);
  • the same chain keeps coming back, even though you’ve been using a tool for several weeks, and the tension isn’t going down for either of you. The data don’t give a waiting period before seeking help; “several weeks” is a working guideline; therapy, for its part, was tested on couples with marked distress;
  • an agreement has been broken, and the repair involves a third person or someone else’s privacy.

What to ask before the first meeting: how the professional assesses results — compared with people who didn’t get treatment, in terms of deterioration, and through follow-up after some time; whether the method fits the couple’s problem; whether they talk with each person separately about fear; whether they can work without treating an agreed-upon relationship structure as a symptom. Friends and a support circle are a separate topic, chapter 34.

What we don’t know yet. The main pooled analysis was done only on mixed-sex couples; for same-sex, non-monogamous and language-mixed couples there’s little or no data6. Which therapy works long-term, for whom and with what risk hasn’t been figured out: independent comparisons with follow-up years later are needed.

When it is no longer about skills

Red flags: fear of your partner; threats; surveillance and stalking; sexual coercion; escalating violence; paying for being open during or after a joint conversation.

If one partner can punish disagreement, being open together can become a pretext for threats after the conversation. And the conciliatory behavior of a frightened person can look like peace, while in fact it reflects an assessment of danger9. Feeling calm at the thought of forgiveness doesn’t prove it’s safe to go back: the lab experiment measured brief reactions to something imagined, not safety1. The difference between coercive control and situational violence holds for any gender, orientation, culture and relationship format.

What to do: don’t do couples exercises or joint conversations about the violence. Discuss the situation separately, in private, with an independent service focused on safety. Whether a joint format is acceptable in cases of situational violence can only be decided by a trained service — with prior screening and safety procedures. In a study of women who came to emergency care with intimate partner violence, coercive control went together with greater danger; it’s a selected group, so general risk figures can’t be drawn from it. More on this — chapter 23.

Part VIII. Bonus: between countries, languages, and meanings

36. Love at a distance: silence in the chat, everyday life, and moving

In short

Long-distance couples don’t break up more often than couples in the same city1. The trouble lies elsewhere: silence in the chat easily reads as rejection, the image of your partner you built while apart runs into everyday life, and moving can change who depends on whom.

What to do about it: agree not on the number of calls but on what silence means, who takes the inconvenient hours, and what can be kept of intimate material. Before moving, live through an ordinary working week together and write down each person’s fallback options.

Confiscated documents, threats of deportation, and surveillance are not a communication style but a safety issue (see the end of the chapter).

Katya in Tbilisi writes to Liu Yan in Shenzhen about a hard day and sees “read.” No reply. She opens the chat again and again, her chest tightens, her breathing goes shallow. Then she writes: “Are you even there?” By that hour Liu Yan is already asleep after a long shift, and she wakes up to a pile of messages. She reads them not as anxiety but as checking up on her, and answers briefly and dryly. Katya sees the dryness and decides they really are drifting apart.

Neither of them did anything wrong. Each responded to what she felt.

What is going on

The body first. When there’s no reply, the nervous system doesn’t receive a rejection — it receives uncertainty. The body responds with arousal: a tight chest, a fast pulse, shallow breathing (chapter 02; about tears when saying goodbye, chapter 05). Next to your partner there are signals that can help the body calm down — touch, a familiar presence. When you’re apart, some of them disappear. This is a working explanation, not a measured fact: pulse and sleep in couples living apart for long periods haven’t been measured separately.

Then meaning, then action. The same pause can be read as “I’m being ignored” or as “they have a workday.” What you do depends on how you read it: you recheck the chat, demand confirmation, write something cutting. For the other person this is a new event: a stream of messages feels like surveillance, the reply comes out cautious or sharp, and the first person reads that as distance. If your time zones don’t match, the inconvenient hours may fall to just one of you, and that burden feeds the same loop.

Anxiety is easy to mistake for evidence: they’re pulling away, there’s someone else. The familiar “protest at separation, seeking closeness” (chapter 13) is a handy language, not a proven explanation of jealousy; you can’t hang a label on a person based on it.

Anger takes the same path. While apart, each of you pictures an imagined everyday life: when to call, who writes first, what “I’ll be there soon” means. The other person’s real habit contradicts the picture, the body responds with arousal, and “you’re breaking our agreement” moves to the front. A useful question: was this said out loud, or filled in by you? This mechanism is a hypothesis: it hasn’t been tested in long-distance couples (chapters 16 and 04).

Closeness from afar. When time for talking is limited, conversation can drift toward the personal and important, and a caring reply feels like closeness. The other person doesn’t see ordinary life — boring, with arguments — and the empty space is easy to fill with agreement. Couples living apart were found to have a more idealized image of each other; the longer the gaps between visits and the stronger the idealization, the less stable the relationship was after reuniting2. This is a link, not proof that idealization breaks couples; it hasn’t been tested separately in non-monogamous couples, queer couples, or China–Russia couples.

When the couple is finally in one city, work schedules, money, sex in daily life, chores, and family obligations show up. If friction reads as a betrayal of the imagined relationship, protest and withdrawal wind each other up. So alongside the candid, show the ordinary too: routine, irritation, disagreement.

A body without a body. The closest anyone has come to this topic is studying short absences in long-term couples, not months between continents. In a lab experiment after acute stress, a partner’s hug reduced the release of the stress hormone in women, but not in men3. There were no vibrations or bracelets involved, so these studies didn’t test whether such devices replace touch. They also contain no data showing that constant video reliably improves sleep, that a “closeness hormone cures” separation, or that touch from other people in an open relationship cancels out its effect. Track your own: sleep, fatigue, unbidden wariness. A signal from a device is a sign of attention, not a substitute for a hug.

Channels and digital sex. Voice adds intonation, but it requires both of you to be awake. Text gives you time to think a phrase through or translate it, but it leaves the uncertainty of silence. Video shows behavior, but it requires a schedule. Time zones, unequal investment, and sex at a distance are studied less than satisfaction and communication, so what follows are reasonable agreements, not a tested protocol. For long-distance couples there’s no data on the benefits of sexting and video sex, on whether they protect against breakups, or on how safely intimate photos are kept. Sexual availability is not proof of trust, and a delayed reply is not proof of cheating. On the physiology of arousal, see our book “Alchemy Without Magic”; on jealousy, chapter 25.

Moving changes the bargaining. When one of the two moves to the other’s country, it’s not just the address that changes. Work connections, a language you speak freely, and the right to decide on your own where to live can disappear — and then a disagreement costs more to the one who moved. If the right to live in the country depends on your partner, an ordinary request can feel like a threat to your housing or status, and giving in or staying silent looks like agreement to the “sponsor.” This is how studies of Asian marriage migration describe it; it’s an analysis of context, not a measured effect. There are no comparisons for a Chinese woman moving to Europe or a European man moving to China, and the law, earnings, and language there can differ a lot. On a “yes” that isn’t always a “yes,” see chapter 37; on money and power, chapter 20.

If there are more than two of you. Distance can be combined with an agreement about other partners. Then a delayed reply gets read through two keys at once: the need for closeness and the vagueness of the agreement — “my partner has someone” or “this is what we agreed.” There’s no data on this combination: that a mostly monogamous relationship with agreed-upon exceptions is more stable at a distance and saves you from loneliness hasn’t been tested — nor has any other form of open relationship, from swinging to polyamory. Here’s what is known: according to a large review of studies, on average no noticeable difference in relationship or sexual satisfaction was found between monogamous and non-monogamous couples4, but some of the samples were collected online, and an average doesn’t mean every form works under every condition. What decides things is an explicit agreement that each person can revisit: who knows about which connections, what’s agreed about sexual health and privacy, what counts as a violation. Consent to an open relationship is not consent to surveillance or to forwarding intimate photos. An agreed-upon connection and a broken agreement are different things (chapters 27 and 29).

If you come from different cultures. In a survey of students, holding back feelings was linked to a sense of harmony with people among the Chinese participants, but not among European Americans. But these are students’ self-reports, not knowledge of what a specific person wants. Translation adds a layer: about half of the evaluated machine translations of emotionally charged Chinese posts into English lost the emotion5; that share says nothing about the accuracy of any single phrase. It’s better to clarify what family involvement, directness, and each phrase mean for these two particular people than to rely on an image of a “Chinese” or “Russian” manner (chapters 33, 37, and 39).

How to tell it is happening

In yourself:

  • Silence in the chat — your chest tightens, your breathing goes shallow, you open the chat again and again.
  • A conclusion is ready the moment there’s a pause — “I’m being ignored,” “there’s someone else” — and you want to demand confirmation or write something cutting.
  • You’re angry about a broken agreement that was never said out loud.
  • You show only the good and the candid, and hide boredom, irritation, and mess to look better.

In your partner:

  • They answer briefly, cautiously, or sharply: a stream of messages comes across as checking up or surveillance.
  • The inconvenient hours fall only to your partner.
  • After the move, they give in or stay silent — and that’s easy to mistake for agreement.

What to do

Everything below is a set of reasonable techniques, not tested programs: they haven’t been tried out separately on long-distance couples.

1. Five lines before replying

When: your body has reacted to silence or a message, and your hand is already reaching to reply.

What to do: before replying, write down five lines. What came in — the fact without judgment: “read, no reply.” What’s in your body: tightness, heat, emptiness; “not clear yet” is also an answer. What meaning you’ve filled in. What was said out loud, and what stayed as my own expectation. What action I’m choosing and how the other person will hear it. About five minutes; in the first month, at least once a week. Anger doesn’t always come from a broken expectation — the fourth line checks that.

How you know it worked: there’s more of a pause between the feeling and the sent message, and both of you can name your explicit agreements.

2. An agreement about silence, hours, and pictures

When: one conversation of about half an hour, then a review once a month.

What to do: decide what silence means — sleep, work, a break — and what word to use to signal it, for example “going to sleep.” Who takes the inconvenient hours; switch week by week. What mix of text, voice, and video is manageable for both of you (a call is not a loyalty check). Who initiates intimate material and whether it can be recorded, stored, and forwarded. How to clarify meaning if your messages go through translation.

How you know it worked: fewer repeated “are you there?” messages, and each of you can say “this doesn’t work for me” and revisit a point.

3. A working week and fallback options before moving

When: before the move; if the right to live in the country depends on your partner — before filing the documents.

What to do: live through an ordinary working week together, not a vacation; if that’s not possible, lay it out on paper hour by hour. Go through a list: chores, time alone, money, the right of residence. Each of you separately writes down your fallback options: where to live, on what money, what work, what language, how to get out of the situation. Get independent advice on the rules of the country you’re moving to. Compare your notes.

How you know it worked: each of you can name your fallback option out loud, and it isn’t taken as an insult.

4. Voice instead of a long message

When: after a pause or a tense exchange.

What to do: instead of a long text, call; to start, once a week. If one of you needs a written translation, or other people are within earshot, add a short written summary to the call: it can be translated, and the private parts can be written instead of said out loud in front of others. This was tested not on couples: in experiments, talking by voice gave more of a sense of connection than people expected, and the people talking were mostly old acquaintances or strangers6.

How you know it worked: the connection feels stronger than expected, and there are fewer misunderstandings.

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: long-distance couples fall apart more often than regular ones.
In fact: in a large survey, those who lived apart rated their relationships higher on a number of measures and expected a breakup less often, and at the follow-up survey they broke up just as often as those who lived close by1. Student samples showed a similar picture, but those were young Western participants: the similarity hasn’t been shown for other couples.

People think: after moving to the same city, couples quickly fall apart — supposedly about a third in the first few months.
In fact: these are the recollections of selected students, not a share for all couples7. The explanation “they were disappointed and broke up” wasn’t confirmed in this survey either: the gap between expectation and reality was noticed by those who stayed together too, even more strongly. Whether this period is more dangerous than others wasn’t compared in these studies.

People think: the more video calls, the closer the couple.
In fact: in a survey of students, how often they video-called wasn’t linked to satisfaction, and messages and voice were linked only weakly8. The data are modest: the frequency was reported by one of the two partners, and the sample was mostly Western. You can’t derive a “dose” of video or a ranking of channels from this; you can agree on a manageable mix of channels.

People think: the one who moves pays the most for the relationship.
In fact: these studies didn’t measure that. Here’s what is known: a dependent right of residence can shift special legal costs onto the one who moved. But the one who stays may also carry money, family obligations, caring for loved ones, and sponsorship obligations.

Talk it over together

Works for several partners too.

  1. What does silence in the chat mean for each of us: sleep, work, hurt, distance? How do we tell one from another?
  2. Whose hours are inconvenient right now, and how should we switch?
  3. What did we agree on out loud, and what did each of us keep in our head to ourselves?
  4. If one of us moves: what fallback options does each of us have — legally, financially, for work, for language?
  5. Which intimate messages and photos can be recorded, stored, and forwarded, and what stays only between us?
  6. What ordinary part of life — boredom, irritation, mess — do I not show because I want to look better?

When it is no longer about skills

These signs are a safety issue, not a difference in communication style:

  • confiscated or controlled documents, including a passport;
  • threats of deportation;
  • a ban on or restriction of contact with family and friends;
  • surveillance and stalking, including digital;
  • coercion into sex.

What to do: set this chapter’s exercises aside and don’t talk yourself into treating a threat as mutual misunderstanding. Get independent legal advice on the rules of the country where you live or where you’re going, separately from your partner. Make your own map of fallback options: where to live, on what money, who’s nearby. More in chapter 23.

37. A “yes” that isn’t a “yes”

In short

A short “yes” — 好, 可以, 嗯, 没问题 — can mean three different things: “I hear you,” a soft “no” that saves face, and agreement in words to take the pressure off. From the outside they look the same.

You can’t tell from the word or from nationality. But you can agree that a “yes” turns into a step (what, who, when), and that a “no” can be said at no cost.

This approach hasn’t been tested on couples. What has been tested is something else: if you ask a person, you learn more than if you guess on their behalf.

Lena suggests to Yue that they visit Lena’s parents in the summer. Yue answers: “可以.” Lena hears agreement and buys tickets that same evening. Later it turns out Yue has other plans for that time. When the tickets come up, her shoulders rise and her answers get short. Lena is angry: the “yes” has turned into a “no” after the fact. Yue feels pressured by someone else’s decision. Both are sure they acted honestly. There was one word, but two meanings.

What is going on

Little is known about the body here. What happens to pulse and breathing at the moment of agreeing or refusing has barely been measured. So the scheme below is a working one. All you can see is what’s going on with Yue: shoulders up, shorter answers.

You fill in the meaning from your own background. The word is familiar, but what it means each person decides by the rules they learned in their own surroundings: “noted” or “promised.” Then comes action: buy the tickets, put it off, say nothing, push. For the other person this is a new event, and the loops lock together. Lena responded to a “promise,” Yue to “pressure.” Each reacts to the previous step, not to what the other meant. When a “yes” turns out not to be a promise, a broken expectation kicks in, and anger grows out of it (chapter 16). If tears well up at this point, that’s also a body signal; what the other person hears in them is in chapter 05.

One “好,” three readings. You can’t see from the word how much agreement is in it. There are three readings. The fourth is the simplest: the person really did agree.

First: “I hear you.” An analysis of recorded conversations in Taiwanese Mandarin showed that 好 can accept what someone else said without promising action1. This is an analysis of recordings, not an experiment; how often this happens is unknown. Taiwan is not the mainland, so there’s no answer here for couples from mainland China. The mechanism is plausible: a request triggers a sense of obligation, and “uh-huh” keeps the conversation alive while the person decides whether to take on the commitment. One hears agreement and acts; the other feels rushed.

Second: a soft refusal that saves face. “Face” is your standing in other people’s eyes: capable, loyal, not letting the family down. An indirect refusal is a described technique, not a ban on saying “no.” If a request sounds like a statement about someone’s abilities, loyalty, or duty to their relatives, embarrassment and the wish not to lose face can change the answer. A “yes” saves face and postpones the explanation. The partner takes it as “I want to,” not as concern for one’s standing. This is a theory; it hasn’t been measured in couples. Pushing after a soft “no” breaks the very thing it was said to protect.

Third: strategic agreement. Agreeing in words to take the pressure off, then doing things your own way or squeezing out more on the details and deadlines. This hasn’t been tested for couples: there’s no data. There are descriptions of business negotiations: relationship first, then the deal, and haggling continues even after an agreement2. But this is expert opinion about business, without testing, and the rule “Chinese romantic agreement is only a starting point” doesn’t follow from it: a contract is not the same as a couple’s agreement, and a person from any culture can put a firm promise into a “yes.” There’s no data that this kind of agreement is a national trait.

What this does to the loop. If it was “I hear you” and the other person is already acting, the first one feels pressure. If it was a soft “no” and the other person builds a plan on a “yes,” the mismatch will surface later, along with the feeling “I wasn’t heard.” If it was a strategic “yes,” you find out through actions, and you’re left guessing which of the earlier “yeses” were real. These are plausible chains, not measured effects. The word doesn’t distinguish between the versions, so separate five things: the words that were heard, the intention that was filled in, voluntary agreement, a named future action, and the right to revisit it.

Three kinds of breakdown. Sometimes a word did the wrong job: “okay” meaning “go on” is heard as “I promise.” “I hear your suggestion” and “I promise to do it on Tuesday” get translated into the same short “yes,” but the commitments are different. And sometimes the two of you differ on what’s appropriate to ask for, refuse, or share about yourself in the first place. And sometimes the vocabulary itself is to blame: whose family counts as part of “us,” what an “open relationship” means. Which breakdown is more common in couples of Chinese and Europeans is unknown. So check not the translation of the word but the action each person inferred from it.

If there are more than two of you. Most studies of such relationships come from North America, and many participants were recruited by convenience. None of them shows that a written agreement reduces conflict. But the logic is clear: one person’s “yes” becomes an event for several people at once. Name whose consent is needed for which action, what gets communicated to whom, and how a rule gets changed (chapters 27 and 28).

If you come from different cultures. There’s no direct data on couples where one partner is Russian-speaking and the other is from mainland China; it also wasn’t possible to confirm same-sex and non-monogamous couples among those studied. The closest thing is an observation of Chinese, Western, and mixed couples in Australia: in all of them, satisfaction was linked to how the couple communicates, and the mixed couple didn’t turn out to be an averaged middle between two cultures3. Don’t assume that one partner will adopt the other’s habits on their own: compare your expectations about family and about showing affection.

A shared language can clear up ambiguity, or it can hide inequality if one person makes important decisions in a language that’s weak for them. This is plausible but not measured. For big decisions, let each person explain their side in the language they know best, then invite corrections in the shared one (chapters 33, 32, 39). This is a suggestion, not a tested technique.

How to tell it is happening

In yourself:

  • You say “okay,” but inside it stays “I’ll think about it” or “I don’t want to.”
  • Your body seems to disagree with the word: for example, your shoulders rise.
  • The “yes” slipped out on its own, because refusing is awkward or you’re afraid of letting your family down.
  • You want to agree in words and quietly do things your own way.
  • When you hear a “yes,” you immediately buy things and make arrangements.
  • You get angry when it turns out the “yes” wasn’t about action: “but you said ‘yes.’”

In your partner:

  • They answer with one word — 好, 可以, “okay” — with no date and no “who.”
  • There’s agreement, but nothing moves; after you take a step, your partner’s answers get shorter.
  • Later it turns out your partner has other plans that weren’t mentioned.
  • You catch yourself guessing which of your partner’s “yeses” are real.

What to do

Techniques 1–4 are put together from working guesses. They haven’t been tested in couples, including Chinese–Russian-speaking, same-sex, and non-monogamous ones: they sound reasonable but are poorly tested. Technique 5 is more reliable, but only the part about asking: paraphrasing has been tested weakly.

1. Turn a “yes” into a step: what, who, when

When: you hear a “yes” about something important — a trip, money, family, a purchase.

What to do: ask out loud: “Is this ‘I hear you’ or ‘let’s do it’? If we’re doing it — what exactly, who, and by what day?” Write it down as one line, both of you. For example: “I hear you, I need to ask my mom” — “Then we come back to this on Friday.” If it’s unclear whether it’s an idea or a decision, ask: “Are we exploring an idea, deciding now, or promising to come back to it before changing anything?” Three answers — three different commitments. This is needed because the two of you read earlier words differently: one as a condition that’s time to adjust, the other as a promise. If the decision is temporary, name right away what will be a reason to reopen it. Agreement to yesterday’s plan doesn’t renew itself — and that goes for rules in non-monogamous relationships too (chapter 27).

If you’re being asked for something important, take a few breaths and ask yourself: what’s happening with my breathing, shoulders, belly? “I can’t tell” is also an answer.

How you know it worked: “but you said ‘yes’” comes up less often, the number of named dates and actions grows, and mismatches surface earlier.

2. Make “no” safe

When: in advance, not in the middle of an argument.

What to do: say out loud that “no,” “I don’t know yet,” and “let’s do it differently” are normal answers, and there will be no cost for them. Offer a third option: “I’ll think about it until Thursday.” After a “no” about sex, money, or the boundaries of the relationship, don’t repeat the question: that “no” is the answer (chapters 11 and 23). A repeated “no” is not a reason to persuade.

How you know it worked: “no” is said more freely. A warning sign: “no” is heard less often, but there are more questions.

3. A week later, check what came of it

When: one short conversation a week and a check on every important “yes” (how often is really needed hasn’t been studied).

What to do: look at what got done, what didn’t, what changed. Without blame: a mismatch between “I hear you” and “I promise” says something about how the two of you handle words, not about who’s at fault.

How you know it worked: every step has an owner and a date, and the conversation doesn’t turn into a hunt for blame.

4. Separate face from substance

When: a request touches family, reputation, duty — or anger washes over you.

What to do: first ask “What here is about face — mine, yours, someone’s family’s?”, then “What’s the substance — money, a date, an action?”. Find out what can be discussed only between the two of you. If anger washes over you, write down two points: “What expectation did I have?” and “What was explicitly promised?”.

How you know it worked: it’s clear what’s been hurt and what exactly you’re deciding: the hurt and the decision haven’t stuck together.

5. Ask instead of guessing

When: you have a guess ready about what the person “really” meant.

What to do: say it carefully: “It seems like for you this was about…”. Ask what they think, and adjust. Then paraphrase: “Am I hearing correctly that…?” — and accept the correction. If the paraphrase sounds like “I know what you really mean,” it shuts down the correction. In a large series of experiments, asking a person gave information that imagination alone didn’t4. And the closer people are, the more confidently they read each other’s ambiguous messages, although their accuracy stays imperfect5. The experiments used tasks, not couples. How to carry clarification through to a shared decision is in chapter 38.

How you know it worked: write down your guess in advance and compare it with the answer. “Yes, that’s right” doesn’t count as accuracy: compare with what the person said in their own words.

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: Chinese people don’t say “no” directly.
In fact: in a comparison of refusals across several scenarios, Americans and students from Taiwan chose their answer based on who was asking — family or friends — and on status. Polite formulas, including apologies, were used by both groups6. An indirect refusal is a technique, and Chinese speakers can refuse directly too. Ritual refusal of an offer is written about, but the rule “refuse several times” is not confirmed. The sample was Taiwanese, and it wasn’t tested on couples.

People think: face matters only in public, not between partners.
In fact: in descriptions of face, it’s a matter of relationships and standing, not just a reaction to onlookers. The question “whose face is involved?” is useful, but it doesn’t prove that every argument is a maneuver to save face. In a survey of Chinese, Western, and mixed couples in Australia, duty to family, harmony, and face were part of relationship standards, and the groups differed; but this is a link, not a relative’s right of veto. Ask not “is our family Chinese,” but “are obligations to relatives involved, and can we settle this between the two of us.”

People think: nations divide into those who speak directly and those who speak in hints — this is a tested scale, and it shows what your partner means.
In fact: this is a metaphor that raises questions, not a tested classification of nations. A large review of publications on business communication found serious problems with attempts to predict the meaning of a phrase from a national “score”7. The label also primes you to look for hidden meanings before they show up, and the conclusion becomes a new grievance: “you never say what you think.” Look at other things: the specific request, who’s older, who has the money, whose family is closer, how well the person knows the language. The famous percentages about “words, voice, and face” don’t work either: they come from narrow lab experiments.

People think: a quiet Chinese couple is surely hiding problems.
In fact: in an observation of Chinese newlyweds filmed on video, both the restrained and the warmly supportive styles of communication were linked to greater satisfaction than the quarreling one8. This is a link, not a cause, but you don’t diagnose based on outward expressiveness. The formula “Chinese love is expressed in deeds, not words” is also too simple: in a survey about how people say “I love you,” some of the Chinese answers toned down both words and gestures.

Talk it over together

  1. What meanings can our “yes” have: “I hear you,” “I’ll think about it,” “it’s awkward to refuse,” “I’ll do it”? By what signs can we tell them apart?
  2. Which of my “yeses” let you down, and which one worked?
  3. How safe is it to say “no” in our relationship? What gets in the way: guilt, awkwardness, what our relatives think?
  4. Whose face — mine, yours, someone’s family’s — is involved in this decision? What can be discussed only between the two of us?
  5. How do we revisit an agreement: whose consent is needed for a change, and what serves as a reason to come back to the topic?
  6. Which action felt like care, and which was meant as care?

The same questions work for several partners: invite everyone the decision affects.

38. Understanding each other accurately and coming to an agreement

In short

Closeness doesn’t guarantee that you’ve been understood the way you meant: sometimes the confidence that you have grows faster than the understanding itself.

A guess about your partner is more reliably checked with a question than with imagination. “Yes” comes in different kinds — “I see,” “yes for now,” “I promise,” “I’ll do it” — and when they get mixed up, each of you feels the other broke their word.

What to do: say your guess out loud as a question, paraphrase and ask to be corrected, leave room for “no” and “I need time,” set a date to revisit, and instead of bargaining over concessions, look for an option both of you can say “yes” to. These techniques are assembled from tested pieces; as a whole they haven’t been tried on couples.

Katya and Alyona have lived together for a long time. Over dinner Katya says: “We need to decide something about vacation.” Alyona nods: “Yes, it’s about time.” For Katya this is an agreement: August, the sea, she’s handling the tickets. For Alyona it’s agreement that the topic is important and there’ll be a conversation soon. In July Katya shows the tickets she’s bought. Alyona’s throat tightens, and she goes quiet. Katya’s cheeks burn and her jaw sets: Alyona’s “yes” turned out to be empty. Each is sure the other broke a word she never gave. No one lied: the same “yes” meant different things.

What is going on

The body first, and a caveat right away. After an unclear exchange, there can be heat in the face, a lump in the throat, a set jaw. In that state it’s easier to slide from “let’s find out what was meant” to “I need to protect myself.” This is plausible but poorly tested: the studies this chapter relies on barely measured body signals at the moment of agreement and didn’t offer any sign by which you could guess your partner’s consent. The practical takeaway: “I feel uneasy” is a reason to ask a question, not a ready conclusion about what your partner did (chapters 02 and 04).

Meaning gets filled in from shared history. People build shared understanding exactly as far as they need for the task right now. The couple’s history fills in the gaps. That’s why a phrase that’s ambiguous to the listener seems clear to the speaker.

The listener acts on their own version, and for the other person this is a new event. From here there are two paths. Either someone stops and asks what exactly was said: in real conversations, people notice the glitch themselves and often let the speaker correct themselves. Or each defends their own version, and the two loops wind each other up. A conversation about the conversation itself can break the loop: the misunderstanding becomes a topic, not an accusation. The way in is simple: “Let’s talk for a minute about the conversation itself: it seems like we’re hearing different things.” It sounds reasonable, but it hasn’t been tested this way on couples: it rests on theory and descriptions of real conversations.

Four different “yeses.” “I understand what you’re asking for” is understanding. “Yes for now” is a choice. “I promise next month” is a commitment for the future. And to follow through, you also need to remember and handle the circumstances. A breakdown at any stage looks like bad faith to the other person. Between “yes, let’s decide it that way” and “yes, I’ll do it” there’s also readiness: “I need time” is a legitimate answer, not evasion.
