And an agreement lives longer than the conversation: revisiting it is part of it, not a violation. This breakdown is a working frame, not a result of testing.

Understanding, taking into account, and agreeing are different things. Understanding means knowing why it matters to the other person. Taking into account means acting so that what was said doesn’t have to be repeated. Respect is enough for that: you can carry out a request without fully understanding the reasons, or you can refuse outright and agree on limits together. Agreeing means accepting a specific decision. In a series of studies, arguments harmed relationship satisfaction less when people felt their partner understood them — and it wasn’t only that the dispute got resolved more often1. But understanding doesn’t replace an agreement: money, safety, consent to sex, and boundaries still need a separate “yes.” You can see your partner’s feeling accurately and still refuse what you’re being asked for. Whether “taking into account” without “understanding” is enough, and whether this holds for couples who speak two languages, hasn’t been tested separately.

Who gives way and how you argue. When you’re dividing time, money, housing, or who gets told what, both interests and anxiety about the relationship are at stake. One person hears a concession as a joint solution to the problem; the other hears surrender under pressure. The words are the same, the agreements are different. For one, disagreement is a solvable difference; for the other, it’s a threat to the bond, to the family’s reputation, or to safety. Then withdrawing triggers pursuit, and pursuit triggers new withdrawal (chapter 15). There’s no single “national style” here: family, friends, and a person’s position change the answer. And silence by itself is not a diagnosis: among Chinese newlyweds, the restrained style didn’t coincide with the quarreling style2.

A concession you put up with in silence. Your partner’s request runs against what you need: resistance or tiredness in your body, and in your head — “I’ll agree, just so we don’t fight.” On the outside you say “yes,” inside resentment builds. Your partner takes the concession for desire, and later the distance or the outburst seems to come without warning. The chain itself is a working scheme. What was measured is something else: when people hid their feelings while giving way to their partner, on average both their emotional state and their relationship were worse3. This is a link, not a proven cause. It doesn’t mean that every concession is harmful or that every feeling has to be laid out. A concession chosen freely (for example, because your partner’s calm is part of your own well-being) and surrender under pressure are different things. And honestly naming the cost of a concession is not the same as dumping all your irritation.

What couples’ courses give you. In one trial, an online program noticeably raised satisfaction among volunteer opposite-sex couples; in another, with army couples, no lasting overall benefit was found4, 5. Neither tested agreements as such, and which part works is unknown. There are no such trials for same-sex couples, non-monogamous couples, or “Russian-speaking — mainland China” couples.

If there are more than two of you. Research on consensual non-monogamy often relies on convenience samples, and interviews with gay couples about sexual agreements describe the arrangements but don’t compare communication techniques. It doesn’t follow from this that writing things down reduces conflict.

If you come from different cultures. The data come from different places — Beijing and Shanghai, Chinese newlyweds, Taiwanese Mandarin (where many studies of the word 好, “hao,” and of refusals come from) — and they don’t add up to a single “Chinese” assessment. For a couple of a Russian speaker and a woman from mainland China there’s no direct experiment; the closest observation, of Chinese–Western couples in Australia, doesn’t show what’s specific to Russian speakers. So distinguish “I hear you,” “I accept,” and “I promise, with a date”; ask about family expectations and what counts as care, without assuming either indirectness or the parents’ right to decide. What “好” means in your couple is in chapter 37; machine translation in your messages is in chapter 39.

How to tell it is happening

In yourself:

  • It feels like “but we agreed,” yet you can’t say on what exactly.
  • Someone else’s “yes” is taken as a promise, though it could have been “I hear you” or “the topic is important.”
  • The image of your partner in your head is so vivid and detailed that it seems accurate.
  • Your body sends a signal — heat in your face, a lump in your throat, a clenched jaw — and the conclusion about what your partner did is already ready.
  • Anger that your partner “didn’t follow through,” although this was never promised out loud (chapter 16).
  • You agree just so you won’t fight, and resentment builds inside.
  • You have to repeat the same request again and again: it’s heard but not taken into account.

In your partner:

  • They nod and say “yes,” and then it turns out they meant something else.
  • They go quiet when they find out you’re already acting on the “agreement.”
  • They’re puzzled or hurt by something that was never discussed: your actions came from a guess, not from what was said (chapter 06).
  • They don’t say “no” or “I need time” — check whether answering that way is even possible between you.
  • They agree easily, but later pull away or flare up seemingly for no reason: perhaps the “yes” was forced.

What to do

1. A guess out loud — as a question

When: before a high-stakes decision — money, housing, time, relatives, who gets told what. And when you notice a ready conclusion about your partner.

What to do: put your guess into one sentence as a question: “It seems to me you’re worried about your parents. Is that right?” Ask it and hear the answer out without correcting. Then adjust your guess and write the gap down in one line. If anger is behind the conversation, write next to it in two columns: “my expectation” and “what my partner promised out loud” (chapter 16).

How you know it worked: over time there are fewer gaps between your guess and the answer. But “my partner agrees” and “my guess is right” are different things. The principle itself, “ask, don’t imagine,” is well supported; it hasn’t been measured as an everyday habit in couples, and this check hasn’t been tested separately on anger.

2. Paraphrasing with the right to correct

When: in important conversations.

What to do: let them finish, without advice. Then paraphrase as a guess: “What I’m hearing is that… Is that it, closer to…, or something else?” Accept the correction and paraphrase again until you hear “yes, that’s it.” If it’s easier for you to sort out feelings with your head, add: “What do you notice in your body — if you notice anything?” — and treat “I don’t know” as a normal answer (this addition hasn’t been tested separately). If your language levels differ, let each of you paraphrase an important agreement in the language that’s comfortable for you; check the action, the deadline, and whether it’s safe to answer “no.” This procedure hasn’t been evaluated.

How you know it worked: rate separately “I feel heard” and “I confirm that the action was understood correctly.” If the paraphrase turns into “I know what you really mean,” the technique stops working.

3. A pause and a breakdown of the misunderstanding

When: it turns out you decided different things, or an argument is speeding up: one wants to leave, the other to chase.

What to do: stop. Each of you states the words as you remember them. Each separately writes down which action you considered agreed. Each makes one correction. Each answers: “yes,” “no,” or “I need time.” Then together say whether a decision has been made. Agree in advance on a pause rule: anyone can ask for one, must say whether the question stays open, and can come back to it at an agreed time. If tears came up in the conversation, that’s a separate body signal (chapter 05).

How you know it worked: the intended action matches what the listener wrote down before the correction; repeated misunderstandings come up less often; each of you could say “no.” Both the breakdown and the pause rule are assembled from parts; they haven’t been tested on couples. Don’t do this with a threatening or controlling partner (see the section “When it is no longer about skills”).

4. An agreement with a date to revisit

When: the decision will live longer than the conversation.

What to do: write in two columns: “what was heard” and “what I’m agreeing to.” State the action, from what point, whose explicit consent is needed, how to ask for a change, and that consent can be withdrawn — this is worth saying out loud. Set a day when you’ll come back to the point: research doesn’t say which interval is best, so go by how long the decision is in effect. The note is a reminder you can edit: it can clarify the terms, but it can also become an argument against revisiting and against withdrawn consent. If there are more than two of you, add: whose consent is needed for which action, what is disclosed to whom, how a rule gets changed. Ask everyone affected rather than imagining the position of someone who’s absent. Consent to how a relationship is set up is not consent to every disclosure or sexual act (chapters 27 and 24).

How you know it worked: what was said gets done, no one feels pressured, and revisiting doesn’t trigger accusations. Written agreements have been described in research, but they haven’t been compared with verbal ones; whether this kind of wording helps people follow through on what was said, no one has tested.

5. Ask each person separately before naming who decides

When: someone usually gives way, or you’re deciding who makes a decision for both of you.

What to do: ask each person separately: what outcome do you want, and which option can you turn down without consequences? Distinguish three situations: a welcome “you decide,” a freely chosen trade, and the constant surrender of the one with less power. Look at real power, not at nationality or gender: family pressure, someone’s interpretation being accepted by default, one of the partners in a union of several people having less access to housing and protection.

How you know it worked: concessions go both ways, and “yes” is said without pressure. This is a conclusion from analyzing the data, not a tested procedure.

6. Conditions for agreement instead of compromise

When: you want different things, and it seems the only way out is for someone to give way: where to live, where to go, whether to let a relative stay with you.

What to do:

  1. Each of you names not a complaint but what matters behind your position: “quiet in the evenings matters to me,” not “you’re loud.” The other paraphrases: “Quiet matters to you — right?” — and only then says: “My position for now is this: …”.
  2. Instead of the question “who will give way,” each of you answers: “On what conditions does this option work for me?” For example, if it’s about your partner’s mom staying with you: “Yes — if she has her own room and her own things to do, and we still have our evenings together.”
  3. Write out the options — not just “my way” and “your way” — and the cost of each for each of you.
  4. Choose the one both of you can say “yes” to without a hidden “but.” A limit can be named out loud: “I’ll pay for the housing until fall, not after that.” If something changes, the conversation continues (technique 4).
  5. An honest “no” is allowed. If there’s no option both of you agree to, the decision stays open, and whatever important thing you couldn’t agree on, each of you keeps in your life outside the couple (chapter 16). If there are more than two of you, go through this with everyone the decision affects.

How you know it worked: the decision isn’t remembered later as “I had to give in,” no resentment builds up over it, and each of you can name the conditions of your “yes.” Whether this kind of search is better than ordinary compromise wasn’t compared in the studies reviewed: it’s a working technique, not a tested method.

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: someone who loves you will understand without words.
In fact: closeness gives real shared knowledge, but sometimes it also strengthens the confidence that you’ve been understood. In experiments with ambiguous messages, friends and spouses sometimes overestimated how well they’d gotten their meaning across, more than people who barely knew each other6. These were lab tasks, not the whole life of a couple; this hasn’t been tested in mainland China or among non-monogamous couples. If you’ve long spoken each other’s language fluently, your vocabulary grows faster than your checking of common ground — but that’s a conclusion from theory, not from experiment.

People think: to understand another person, you have to imagine yourself in their place.
In fact: in a series of experiments, imagining someone else’s point of view didn’t consistently increase accuracy, but getting that point of view from the person themselves, by asking and talking, did7. Some of the experiments involved close people, many involved others; there’s no data on couples from different cultures. Imagination is good for preparing a gentle question, but only the person themselves gives the answer.

People think: if you’ve paraphrased your partner’s words, you’ve understood correctly.
In fact: paraphrasing strengthens the feeling of being heard more than advice or a simple “uh-huh” does, but compared with advice it doesn’t add liking for the other person8. These were students in first meetings, not couples, and accuracy of understanding wasn’t measured. “I was heard” and “I was understood correctly” are different things: a smooth but wrong paraphrase can mislead.

People think: couples who compromise are happier.
In fact: science hasn’t tested this. In an observation of couples from China, the West, and mixed couples, more constructive communication went together with greater satisfaction9, but the couples weren’t randomly assigned, and you can’t compare “compromise” with “one person deciding.” Following this blindly is risky: a concession under pressure looks like a compromise. Another path is to look for conditions on which both of you say “yes” (technique 6).

Talk it over together

  1. When were we sure we’d agreed, and then it turned out we understood different things? What told each of us back then that everything was clear?
  2. Which of our phrases mean “I hear you,” which mean “yes for now,” and which mean “I promise”? How can we tell them apart?
  3. What’s easier for you: saying “no,” “I need time,” or “I don’t know”? What usually happens next?
  4. Which decisions do we want to make together, and which are we willing to trust to one person? How do we check that it’s a free choice?
  5. When and how do we revisit an agreement? What shouldn’t happen when we do?
  6. Who else does our decision affect, and whose consent is needed?
  7. When did we agree just to avoid a fight? What was missing then for a real “yes”?
  8. Which requests have to be repeated? How can we take them into account even if the reasons aren’t fully clear?

When it is no longer about skills

Everything above assumes that each person can safely say “no.” This condition is broken if:

  • a refusal is followed by threats or punishment;
  • the same side constantly gives way under pressure;
  • there is coercive control.

Then a joint procedure is dangerous: a record of the conversation can expose the more vulnerable person, and no exercise replaces safety. Don’t go through such discussions as a pair. On violence and control, see chapter 23; on tools and when to see a professional, chapter 35.

39. The translator between us: translation, AI, and texting as a couple

In short

When you text through a translator, every message has a go-between — a machine. In translation, words survive more reliably than feeling, and tone in text reads worse than the writer thinks.

AI in a message can help or let you down: it all depends on how much it wrote for you and whether your partner knows.

What to do about it: confirm important things by meaning, not by words; move ambiguous and heated topics into voice or a pause; you can take words from AI, but apologize in your own name.

Vika in Kazan writes Mei a long message after yesterday’s fight. Mei reads it in a translator: even, slightly bureaucratic sentences, and the word Vika used to ask for forgiveness sounds like a letter from the bank. Mei answers briefly. Vika sees the reply in her own translator — drier than it may really have been. Both put their phones down and think the same thing: I’m not being heard.

What is going on

The body first. As in any texting: a message stings, your heart speeds up, your breathing gets shorter, your gaze narrows — and at that moment you hear worse. How exactly the body reacts to a translated message hasn’t been measured separately. How a body signal turns into meaning is in chapters 02 and 03.

The loop is longer than usual. You choose words by the politeness rules of your own language. The machine picks others — by its own rules. The reader understands the tone by a third set of rules, their own. Meaning takes shape from what’s read — “cold,” “rejection,” “no respect” — and the action depends on it: a short reply, silence, a reproach. That becomes a new message for the first partner and goes through the machine again. Each round passes through translation twice, there and back. This is a description of the mechanics, not a result of measurements.

That’s exactly what happened in the scene above. For Mei, the message wasn’t Vika’s words but their machine version: “the apology is formal” — a short reply. For Vika, that reply is a new trigger, again run through the machine. The loops wind each other up without anyone meaning harm. Anger here is most often a signal of a broken expectation (chapter 16), except that the expectation of politeness may have been broken not by your partner but by the translation; from the text alone you can’t tell whether the person meant to hurt you.

What gets lost in translation. One test turned up: Chinese–English machine translation of short emotional social media texts. The failure points named in the study are words with strong coloring and several meanings, negation, and abbreviations1. For a couple, these are three places where checking is especially appropriate. Caveats: they tested posts, not personal conversations; how things stand today and for other language pairs is unknown; there are no measurements for intimate texting in Chinese and Russian. For now, assume the accuracy is unknown, not that it’s high.

Tone in text. The writer hears the intended warmth in their head, while the reader sees only characters and fills in the rest from their own state. In experiments with emails, writers overestimated how accurately they’d conveyed tone, including sarcasm2. These were lab emails, not a chat; they weren’t compared with long-acquainted couples who check with each other. An emoji hints at tone, but it’s also read differently; how much so hasn’t been measured in long-distance couples. In translated texting it’s easy to confuse four different things: how many messages there are, what they’re about, how the tone was read, and what happened to the text in translation.

Voice. A voice carries timing, stress, and intonation; text doesn’t, and the listener may attribute more thought and feeling to the speaker. When people were asked to reconnect by voice, the connection felt stronger than they expected3. But this was tested on old friends and strangers, not on couples using translation, and what was measured was the impression, not accuracy of understanding: hearing is not mind reading. For a couple these are two tools, not a winner: text, which you can edit and reread, is probably more reliable for clarity of meaning, while voice gives more presence. This is a conclusion about the properties of channels, not a measurement. If a voice call brought you to tears, see chapter 05.

AI in a message. AI help by itself doesn’t make a message insincere. The mechanism works both ways. A smooth phrase helps the intention come through. But if it turns out that a difficult apology was generated, the judgment can shift from “I’m understood” to “responsibility was handed off.” What matters is how much the machine wrote and what the recipient knows. A light edit and a heavy draft are different things; in real life the couple draws the line themselves, and whether to mention AI help is also the couple’s decision. Whether an AI apology will repair a real couple, let alone through translation, isn’t shown in the studies found. On apology as repair, see chapter 17.

An AI companion. A chatbot is available any minute and can seem nonjudgmental, so confessing to it is easier at first. But that same conversation sends your words to a service and can change how much time and vulnerability are left for people. Users say they felt better, but these are reviews from those who chose to leave them, and there’s no comparison with what loneliness would have been like without the app: it’s support, not a proven cure. No reliable data on “AI cheating” turned up. What matters more is an agreed-upon boundary: sexual dialogue, secrecy, spending, disclosure mean different things to different partners. Users describe having almost no control over what exactly gets disclosed: mostly they decide whether to talk at all. Comfort in confessing and data protection are different things. A test question: does the app add to the live contact you want, or push it out? Chapters 34 and 27.

If there are more than two of you. On average, no noticeable difference in relationship or sexual satisfaction was found between people in relationships with several partners and people in monogamous ones4. But the samples were of those who were easiest to find, this says nothing about long-distance couples, and the studies found contain no direct data on translators and AI in such networks. Untested, but practical: talk through in advance who knows about which relationships, what agreements on privacy and health are in effect, what counts as a violation, and whether partners can be discussed in an AI chat or in a translator. Consent to several partners is not consent to digital control and not consent to sharing intimate images. Chapters 27 and 29.

If you come from different cultures. Among students, suppressing emotions was linked to a sense of harmony with people among the Chinese, but not among European Americans. But these are the students’ own answers about themselves, not proof that a specific person wants silence or that suppression helps that person’s marriage. Australian comparisons of Chinese, Western, and mixed couples (closeness, care, the role of family, harmony, “face” — that is, dignity in public) describe groups, not each person, and these aren’t Chinese–Russian couples at a distance. The question remains for the two of you: what family involvement, directness, and each phrase mean for you. Translation errors are a reason to check, not to lean on a stereotype. Chapters 33 and 37.

How to tell it is happening

In yourself:

  • A translated message seems dry or formal, and you immediately want to reply briefly.
  • A conclusion about rejection, coldness, or disrespect is ready before it’s clear what was meant.
  • You don’t know how your phrase looks in the other language, but you have no doubts about its tone.
  • You want to hand AI a message that should come from you, and not mention it.
  • You count how many messages went out and how many came in.

In your partner:

  • They answer your long message briefly.
  • The argument is about who meant what, not about the matter itself.
  • Both of you feel you’re not being heard.

What to do

1. Read, stop, clarify

When: a message stung, and a reply is already begging to be sent. For a refusal, agreement, commitment, apology, or fight; not needed for schedules.

What to do: don’t reply right away. Write down five short lines: what came in (word for word, with the original if there is one); what’s in your body (tension, heat, tightness — or “not clear yet,” which is also an answer); what conclusion got filled in; what you actually agreed on; what you want to do. If the message has a negation, a word with many meanings, an abbreviation, or strong feeling, translate it back into your language and compare it with what was intended. Then send a clarifying question with two options: “Do you mean X or Y?” — with the original phrase next to it. Ask them to retell in their own words what came across, and compare.

How you know it worked: arguments over how something was read repeat less often, and it’s easier for both of you to explain what was meant.

It sounds reasonable, but it hasn’t been tested on couples using translation. More on step-by-step clarification in chapter 38.

2. Voice for one ambiguity

When: a phrase is ambiguous or heats things up.

What to do: agree in advance that in such cases you switch to a voice call. If you need a written translation, privacy matters, or time zones get in the way — text remains. After the call, write down a short summary in both languages: what you decided. That way the clarity of text is kept, and the presence comes from voice. This hasn’t been tested on couples using translation.

How you know it worked: both of you have more of a sense of connection and fewer misunderstandings. Look at that, not at the minutes on the call.

3. Words from AI, the apology from you

When: you need to write something difficult — an apology, condolences, a reply after a fight.

What to do: ask AI for wording, but keep the meaning your own. Make the apology in your own name. Agree in advance whether AI help should be mentioned. Don’t hand off responsibility in secret: that’s an easy way to put trust at risk. This hasn’t been tested on real couples.

How you know it worked: your partner responds to the meaning, not to the smoothness.

4. A view from outside

When: after an argument, once emotions have cooled.

What to do: recall the conflict and describe it in writing as a neutral outsider who wants the best outcome for both of you. Write in whichever language is easier. In a trial with married couples, a few short writing tasks preserved marital quality over time, rather than raising it5. Limits: they tested established marriages of heterosexual couples, not distance, not several partners, and not translation; transfer to queer couples hasn’t been shown, nor how it works for arguments through translation. Don’t use it when there are threats or coercion (chapter 23).

Close to this is a structured online program: couples choose a meaningful problem, work through the material, and discuss it together. In a trial with heterosexual couples, compared with those waiting their turn, satisfaction and confidence in the relationship went up6. This is not the same as simply more video calls.

How you know it worked: satisfaction grows for both of you, and a repeat argument is easier to resolve.

5. Look at responsiveness, not at numbers

When: you argue about how much to write, or you worry that it’s too little or too much.

What to do: ask each other whether the contact feels responsive and voluntary. Don’t count messages and minutes of video. Which rhythm suits whom is an open question; it’s wiser to agree on it than to make it mandatory.

How you know it worked: both of you have the feeling that there’s a response, and no one feels checked up on. On small bids and responses in everyday life, see chapter 14; on closeness at a distance, chapter 36.

The rest is still an assumption for now: a mandatory scheduled video call, bracelets that transmit touch, a mandatory script for disclosing AI help. They haven’t been established as ways to help a couple.

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: machine translation is already good enough for talking about feelings.
In fact: for schedules and tickets it may be enough. For messages the relationship depends on — a refusal, agreement, commitment, apology — it hasn’t been shown to be enough: in the test mentioned above, emotion was distorted in about half the cases1 — but those were posts, not personal messages. Smooth text doesn’t mean correct. Confirm the intention in each person’s language before drawing conclusions (chapter 37).

People think: if your partner finds out a text was written by AI, it will always sound insincere.
In fact: in an experiment, an open label under a lightly rewritten apology slightly raised its rated authenticity, while under a heavily generated one it slightly lowered it7. There’s no blanket penalty for AI help: it all depends on what exactly the machine did. But the raters were recipients, not the senders’ partners, and what was measured was perceived sincerity, not repair of the relationship.

People think: lots of texting is a sign of a healthy relationship.
In fact: in a survey of students, the link between how often they texted and called and their satisfaction turned out to be weak, and for video calls no link was found8. These are the students’ own answers about themselves: a link, not a cause. The same number of messages can be welcome contact — or pressure and surveillance.

Talk it over together

  1. In which language is it easier for us to talk about a fight, and in which about tenderness, and when do we switch to the other?
  2. Which of my phrases sound harsher or drier in translation than I mean? Show me an example.
  3. Which topics do we take to voice instead of handing them to the translator?
  4. What do we think about AI help in messages: what’s fine, what’s worth mentioning, and what — like an apology — is better written ourselves?
  5. How many messages a day feel like care, and how many like being checked up on?
  6. What do we not send into a translator or an AI chat — intimate things, things about other people?

When it is no longer about skills

Everything above is for ordinary misunderstandings, where both of you are generally safe. But there are signs that mean the exercises won’t help:

  • A threat in a message is not a translation misunderstanding. Don’t try to “clarify” it down to a harmless meaning: save the original and the translation. What to do next is in chapter 23.
  • A demand to hand over your phone, passwords, or all your messages “to check the translation” is control, not clarifying meaning. More on surveillance and control in chapter 23.
  • Thoughts of suicide can’t be left to an app: an app is not crisis help. You need a living person nearby and the emergency service of your country.

Part IX. Bonus: a couple over time, and respect

40. A couple over time: normal crises and what changes over the years

In short

Love has no single schedule. “The first-year crisis,” “the seven-year itch,” and stages with deadlines are popular schemes, not a law: some couples stay satisfied for years, while for others satisfaction slowly melts away.

The guide is not the year number but the task the couple is facing right now. In the settling-in period, it’s showing who you are and agreeing on the limits. In the calm years, it’s noticing the good and not sliding into autopilot. In the rebuilding period, it’s letting go of what won’t happen and putting yourselves together again.

What to do: figure out where you are now by signs, not by the calendar; once a month, compare a few simple ratings; bring old questions out instead of waiting for them to dissolve on their own. If a hard period involves fear, humiliation, or control, it’s no longer about changes in the couple but about safety.

Vika and Timur have been together a long time. Sunday evening, both on the couch, each in their own phone. Vika remembers how, that first winter, they talked until morning, and her chest feels heavy and empty. The thought is ready at once: “We’ve become roommates.” She opens an article, “Everyone goes through the seven-year itch,” and can’t tell what she feels: relief — so it happens to everyone — or fear that this is the beginning of the end. Timur notices that Vika has gone quiet and decides: she’s bored with me. He says “I’m going to bed early” and leaves. Vika reads this as confirmation.

Both are waiting for “the crisis to pass on its own” or for the other to do something first. And no one has asked out loud what has changed in their life over the past year.

What is going on

There is no calendar. The familiar picture: every couple goes through the same steps — rapture, crisis, quiet years, a new crisis — all on schedule. A large review of long-term studies found no such common schedule. Changes in satisfaction were better explained by people’s age than by how many years they’d been together1. Children, health, money, and moves come to couples at different times, and each couple responds in its own way. When the same spouses were followed for many years, their paths diverged: for some, satisfaction stayed high; for others, it was lower or slowly declined2. Almost all of this data was collected on Western couples, mostly married ones; there are very few same-sex couples in it. The average line for a group is not a forecast for you.

Tasks instead of dates. It’s more useful to ask not “what year are we in” but “what task do we have right now.” Below is a working map, not a tested model. The periods don’t go strictly in order or by months: they repeat, overlap, and last for different lengths of time. A couple can find itself back in the settling-in period after a move, the birth of a child, or a new job.

  • The beginning. Lots of rapture and little knowledge of each other. The task is to enjoy it, take a closer look, and not lose your own life (chapters 10 and 12).
  • Settling in. Lives intertwine: shared household, money, schedule. One person’s action starts to affect the other’s plans, and the uncertainty of “what are we to each other” makes it hurt more; more on this in chapter 12. Weaknesses and fatigue show up. It’s easy to read this as deception: “we were pretending before.” There’s another reading: next to someone close, you stop trying so hard and don’t hide your inconvenient sides. The task is to show who you are: what matters to you and what you won’t give up. To name the limits for fights (chapter 12, technique 6). To find your own ways: who does what, how you fight and how you make up.
  • The calm years. Daily life is running smoothly, rituals have formed, much is done without words. The task is to notice and live through the good, to start shared things: plans, projects, things beyond chores — and to bring life from outside into the couple. The danger is autopilot: everything works, but it no longer nourishes you. Calm is not yet warmth (chapter 14).
  • Rebuilding. Life has changed: children, a move, work, illness, aging parents. The old way the couple was set up has become too tight. Tension builds up, and it becomes clear what, apparently, won’t happen in this relationship. Sometimes partners switch roles: the one who used to withdraw starts pursuing, and the one who pursued pulls into themselves. The task here is not to get to the bottom of everything but to let go of what can’t be, accept what is, and come to new agreements. How to say goodbye to an expectation is in chapter 16; how the thought of leaving differs from a decision is in chapter 22.

Sometimes, after rebuilding, warmth and desire come back, sometimes brighter than before. How often and why hasn’t been measured (on passion over the years, see chapters 10 and 18).

When things got worse: three different explanations. In a long-term observation of newlyweds, what the first years were like — how much love, tenderness, doubt, and feeling of being heard they held — was linked to how the couple lived afterward3. The researchers distinguished three processes. First: as novelty fades, the rapture of the first months quiets down. Second: what was in the couple from the very beginning doesn’t go anywhere. Third: a new trouble from new circumstances. These are different explanations, not steps on one ladder. American marriages were observed. This study didn’t show any common timeline in which everyone loses the rapture, and no recipe for restoring satisfaction after a decline follows from it. So not every fading of the first rapture is “the end of the honeymoon” or a sign of failure.

The old comes back. What went unsaid in the settling-in period can come up again in later hard periods. The longer you stay silent, the more each new small thing drags all the previous ones along with it. The good news: you can raise an old question at any time, not only at the beginning (technique 4). This is an observation by practitioners; it hasn’t been tested.

A hard year distorts your view. In the diaries of newlyweds under strong outside pressure, a single bad moment with the partner did more damage to their opinion of the whole relationship4 (on stress spillover, see chapter 15). And the memory of “how it used to be” is not a recording but a retelling, and what you remember can depend on your current mood5. So it’s better to judge all your years together by notes from several months, not by one heavy evening (technique 2).

If there are more than two of you. On average, satisfaction with relationships and sex didn’t differ noticeably between monogamous and non-monogamous people, although those surveyed were mostly people who volunteered6. But how such relationships change over the years wasn’t studied in the work found. Look at each connection separately: where it’s having difficulties right now, and where it’s at peace.

If you come from different cultures. There are few studies on China in the large review, and in them the mainland isn’t separated from Taiwan. There’s no direct data on couples of “a Russian-speaking or European partner and a partner from mainland China.” A move, parents, money for the family can mean something different to each person. Ask each other what the next change means to each of you, and whether language, duty to family, or the usual meaning of silence changes that meaning (chapters 33 and 37).

How to tell it is happening

The first list is about whether a period of change has started for you. The other two are about whether you’re coping or stuck.

Looks like you’re in a period of change:

  • Over the past year, something shifted in your life: a child, a move, work, illness, money, parents.
  • Irritation over small things has grown, though “nothing happened.”
  • You catch yourself comparing with the beginning: “we used to talk until morning.”
  • The old agreements pinch: who does what, how much time you spend together.
  • It’s as if you’ve switched roles: now the one pursuing is the one who used to withdraw.

It’s hard, but you’re coping:

  • Overall, there’s more good, and the unpleasant is bearable.
  • There’s sadness and misunderstanding, but both of you can handle it, and daily life — money, food, kids — keeps working.
  • Warm moments and closeness happen, even if less often.
  • After conversations, behavior changes, not just the mood for the evening.
  • Fights are about different things, and new moves show up in them.
  • Trust holds, even though things have cooled. You can think “what if we broke up” and not panic (chapter 22).

Looks like you’re stuck:

  • The same fight with the same feeling has gone on for years, and conversations change nothing (chapter 15).
  • The hard period ended not with warmth, but with each of you retreating into your own life.
  • A “love — hate” seesaw, attempts to end everything in one sudden move.
  • Less and less tenderness, more and more doubt.
  • No closeness at all; you can’t even agree on household matters.
  • You punish each other with silence or by withholding closeness.
  • Calm has become autopilot: you don’t joke, don’t touch, are together only for practical things, while “out of boredom” habits pile up (technique 3).
  • One or both of you have insomnia, low mood, panic attacks.
  • What mainly keeps you together is how much you’ve already invested.

These are working lists, not a tested test: a reason to talk, not a diagnosis.

What to do

1. Where we are now: by signs, not by the calendar

When: it feels like “something’s wrong,” or someone says: “It’s just the such-and-such-year crisis.”

What to do:

  1. Each of you separately writes down what has changed in your life over the past year: work, housing, children, health, money, parents.
  2. Figure out which of the three explanations is closest (more than one may fit): the rapture has quieted; an old question has come back; a new trouble has come from new circumstances.
  3. Choose the task for the period. In settling in — talking about what’s yours and coming to agreements. In the calm years — noticing the good and starting shared things. In rebuilding — letting go, accepting, and reworking agreements.
  4. Tell your partner your guess as a question: “It seems to me we’re in a time for this kind of task right now. How do you see it?”

How you know it worked: the argument isn’t about who’s to blame but about what’s needed now; both of you have a similar picture.

The map hasn’t been tested: it’s a way to agree on words, not a diagnosis.

2. A check-in instead of a curve

When: once a month, in a calm moment.

What to do: each of you separately makes brief notes:

  • how good it was together this month — on your own scale;
  • what you fought about — different things or the same thing;
  • how much pressure came from outside: work, money, health, relatives;
  • whether anything changed in behavior after conversations;
  • when, specifically, there was closeness.

Write it down with the date. Compare not with last evening but with several past months. If you want a view of a whole year, use the snapshot from chapter 22.

How you know it worked: you can see where the line is heading, and decisions rest on several notes rather than the mood of one evening.

Looking at your own repeated ratings instead of an average curve follows directly from how differently couples’ paths go. But the check-in itself hasn’t been tested, and a note is also one person’s view, not an objective measure.

3. The calm years: living calm or autopilot

When: everything has been even for a long time, with no major fights.

What to do: every few months, go through the list and honestly answer “yes” or “no”:

  • we joke, touch, flirt;
  • there’s time together that isn’t about practical things: a walk, a movie, a conversation about something interesting;
  • each of us has our own things and interests, and we bring them home;
  • there’s a shared project or plan;
  • we look after our health and don’t get stuck in “out of boredom” habits.

For each “no” — one small step for the coming week. Hints are in chapters 14 (lingering on the good), 18 (the slow ladder), and 19 (something new, chosen together).

How you know it worked: one or two “no”s have become “yes,” and the calm feels like rest, not boredom.

The list hasn’t been tested.

4. Bringing out an old question

When: the same dissatisfaction has been heard for years, and old complaints surface in a new hard period.

What to do:

  1. Write down what keeps repeating and when it was first said.
  2. Ask yourself: what of mine didn’t get a place back then? What did I have to silently give up?
  3. If it’s been building for a long time, first spend some time building up good things together — that gives you something to sort it out for.
  4. Raise one question. Start with what you value in this relationship, and talk about what you’re protecting, not about what’s wrong with your partner (chapter 16).
  5. After a few conversations, check: has behavior changed — or has it only gotten easier for the evening?

How you know it worked: the topic stops coming back in its old form — or you consciously decide to live with it (chapter 16, technique 5).

If the old question is about fear, humiliation, or someone taking things out on you, it doesn’t belong here: see chapters 23 and 41. This technique hasn’t been tested.

5. Rebuilding: don’t rush it and don’t dig to the bottom

When: life has changed, the old setup pinches, and there’s a background of sadness and mutual misunderstanding in the couple.

What to do:

  1. Keep daily life going: agree on who’s responsible for money, food, children, and parents during this time (chapter 20).
  2. Don’t try to end the period with one big conversation or a sharp decision. There’s no deadline by which everything has to pass: give it time.
  3. Notice if you’ve switched roles, and say it without reproach: “It looks like now I’m the one withdrawing, and you’re the one pursuing.”
  4. Protect the islands of warmth: an old ritual, a walk, breakfast together.
  5. Grieve what won’t happen here (chapter 16, technique 6). If the thought of leaving comes, look at chapter 22: a thought is not yet a decision.

How you know it worked: the sadness is bearable for both of you, daily life goes on, warm moments happen, and little by little new agreements take shape.

If insomnia, low mood, or panic have appeared, see the section “When it is no longer about skills.” The technique itself hasn’t been tested.

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: all couples go through the same stages on schedule: rapture for a few months, the first-year crisis, the seven-year itch.
In fact: long-term studies show no common schedule1, and spouses followed for many years went along different paths2. The average line for a large group can look like steps even when individual couples go very differently. The guide is what’s happening in your life, not the year number.

People think: marital satisfaction follows a horseshoe: it drops toward the middle, and when the kids grow up, it rises again.
In fact: such a horseshoe can appear when different couples — young, middle, long-standing — are compared at one point in time. When the same couples were surveyed many times, a common horseshoe wasn’t confirmed7. Waiting for it to “rise on its own later” is unreliable.

People think: over the years everything inevitably gets worse, and nothing can be done.
In fact: for some couples, satisfaction stays high for years2. And in one trial with married couples, marital quality on average declined for everyone over the first year. In the second year, one group wrote briefly several times about their fights through the eyes of a well-meaning outsider — and their decline stopped, while for the others it continued8. This is one short exercise and one year after it (chapter 17): decline isn’t inevitable, but the techniques in this chapter haven’t been tested this way.

People think: fights in the settling-in period are a sure sign the couple is getting stronger.
In fact: the data don’t show that fights are a normal sign of growing closeness. The scheme in which irritation grows along with getting closer is an explanation, not a law9; how it changes over the years hasn’t been measured yet (chapter 12). Fights prove neither growth nor the end. Look at whether anything changes after them.

Talk it over together

  1. What has changed in our life over the past year — and which of it has affected us as a couple?
  2. What time are we in now: settling in, the calm years, rebuilding? Why does it seem that way to you?
  3. What question has been with us from the very beginning? What of yours didn’t find a place back then?
  4. In which period are you less comfortable — in calm or in crisis?
  5. What do we do together that isn’t about practical things — and when was the last time?
  6. By what signs will we know it’s time to see a specialist?

When it is no longer about skills

A hard period is not a reason to put up with something dangerous. If the couple has:

  • violence, threats, surveillance, or control;
  • humiliation and intimidation, fear of saying “no”;
  • thoughts of suicide in one of you;
  • insomnia, low mood, or panic attacks that don’t go away,

then this is not an ordinary crisis, and first you need to take care of safety and health. If there’s a direct threat, call emergency services. In cases of violence and control — see a domestic violence specialist, and don’t do joint exercises (chapter 23). For low mood and panic — see a doctor or psychotherapist.

If there’s no fear, but jabs, contempt, and outbursts have become the norm, see chapter 41.

If there’s no danger but you’re stuck, you can go to a couples specialist or take a program for couples (chapter 35). Keep in mind: the trial of the online program that raised relationship satisfaction didn’t include couples with violence, suicidal thoughts, ongoing infidelity, or concrete plans to break up10. For such couples, its benefit hasn’t been shown.

41. Disrespect without violence: jabs, contempt, and years of putting up with it

In short

Disrespect isn’t one awkward joke but a pattern: jabs in front of other people, mockery of what you care about, a contemptuous tone, outbursts after which nothing changes. There’s no fear, so it seems you can just put up with it.

But silence doesn’t put out the jabs; it can make them the norm. And both people lose dignity: the one who jabs and the one who takes it for years.

What to do: check whether it’s an accident or a pattern; say “we’re not doing this” right away and calmly — and back up what you said with actions. For the one who jabs: understand what important thing stands behind the jab, and say it directly.

If fear, threats, or control have appeared, that’s chapter 23.

At dinner with friends, Roma tells the story of how they got ready for vacation: “Our Lyosha spent half a day picking out swim trunks — well, you know, that’s what matters most to him.” Everyone laughs. Lyosha smiles too, but his cheeks burn and something inside seems to clench. At home he says nothing: it’s a small thing, Roma doesn’t mean anything bad, it was awkward to object in front of people. It’s been like this for several years — about his cooking, his “clever books,” the way he drives. Roma sees this as lightness: he always jokes like this in company, and Lyosha laughs, after all. And then on Sunday Lyosha explodes over an unwashed mug: he shouts that he’s being treated like a servant and slams the door. Roma stands in the middle of the kitchen and doesn’t understand: “Over a mug?”

The mug has nothing to do with it. What burst out was what had gone unsaid for years. And now each of them has something to be hurt about.

What is going on

The body first. In the one being joked about: heat in the face, a clench inside, a strained smile, a wish to become less noticeable. That’s what to go by, not a list of “forbidden phrases”: the same phrase is a shared game in one couple and a jab in another. In the one who jabs, everything is faster: a short inner “oh, is that so,” the phrase flies out before any decision, and then sometimes comes “why did I do that?”. This is a working picture: the studies this chapter relies on didn’t measure the body.

Then meaning. The one who’s been hurt looks for a reason to stay silent: “it’s a small thing,” “they don’t mean anything bad,” “maybe I’m being too touchy.” The one who jabs sees a smile and silence and reads them as “everything’s fine.” That’s how one person’s silence becomes permission for the other. How fear of a fight piles up the unsaid is in chapter 15.

What stands behind a jab. Sarcasm looks like “just a joke,” but there’s anger in it. It seems a jab appears when something important is under threat, and there’s no energy or words to say it directly. Tiredness, a feeling that you’re not seen, that what you do has lost its meaning — and the joke passes this on to the other person: you feel it too. This is a frame for questions to yourself, not a tested fact. And an explanation isn’t an excuse: tiredness explains where the harshness comes from, but doesn’t give you the right to it.

Why years of putting up with it lock in the pattern. A jab that went unanswered becomes habitual for both. And a couple where harshness has become the norm starts dumping all of life’s tension there: at work a person holds back, at home they don’t. This is an observation, not a measurement.

The unsaid builds up and one day bursts out over a trifle, as with the mug. The one who’s put up with it for years starts to snap too, and the loop closes from the other side. The more you’ve invested, the more you want the years to “pay off” — and the harder it is both to speak up and to leave. What went unresolved from the first months can be raised at any moment; it just gets harder with each year (on setting limits at the start of a relationship, see chapter 12).

There are cautious data on forgiveness. Among newlyweds, forgiving someone who doesn’t make amends was linked to a drop in the forgiver’s self-respect1, and a tendency to forgive was linked to the partner’s psychological and physical aggression continuing2. These are couples of a man and a woman, and these are links, not proof that forgiveness breeds aggression. But forgiveness after which the other person changes nothing doesn’t stop the pattern by itself (more in chapter 17).

Both lose dignity. The one who jabs — when they’re carried away and no longer have a choice about how to speak. The one who takes it for years — when they let themselves be treated this way and stay silent about what matters to them. So the task isn’t for one to become more patient and the other more careful, but for neither of the two to attack and neither to take it. The norm is when no one in a conversation allows themselves to humiliate, not when the other has to calmly put up with it. You can understand someone else’s tiredness, but no one is obliged to serve as its trash can.

Where this chapter ends. Researchers break psychological abuse in couples into several sides: intimidation and domination, humiliation, restriction (whom you see, where you go), and hostile withdrawal into coldness3. Intimidation and restricting freedom are the subject of chapter 23. Humiliation and coldness also happen without fear — this chapter is about them. But the line shifts. Coercive control is not a stormy fight: its essence is that the other person’s freedom is steadily narrowed4. And among couples before the wedding, where no one had yet raised a hand, psychological aggression by either partner predicted the first physical violence in the early years of marriage5. This is a link and a risk, not a verdict, and the data are only on couples of a man and a woman.

The check is simple: can you say “we’re not doing this” without fear — and what happens afterward? If the jabs become fewer, this is a job for skills. If it’s scary to say, or the jabs get meaner in response, or punishment follows — see the section “When it is no longer about skills” and chapter 23.

If you come from different cultures or there are more than two of you. Almost everything this chapter relies on is couples of a man and a woman and surveys of women. For same-sex couples, open relationships, and couples where one partner is from Russia or Europe and the other from mainland China, there are no direct tests. A joke in a second language can come across harsher or softer than intended (chapter 33), so ask how it came across, and don’t put either the jab or the silence down to nationality. The measure is the same: what happens to you after the phrase, and what changes after a request.

How to tell it is happening

If you’re the one being hurt:

  • After a “joke” your cheeks burn, something clenches inside, you force a smile.
  • People laugh at what you care about: your work, a hobby, your body, your family. You no longer want to talk about it.
  • The jabs come in front of other people, and at home the conversation about them ends with “you have no sense of humor.”
  • Your partner is polite with others, but not with you.
  • You stay silent so as not to start something, and then explode over a small thing.
  • There are apologies, but no changes.
  • It’s hard to relax around them: as if you’re waiting for the next jab.

If you’re the one who jabs:

  • The phrase flies out before you have time to decide, followed by “it’s just a joke.”
  • You’re harsher with your partner than with colleagues and friends.
  • Afterward you think “why did I do that?” — and next time it all happens again.
  • The more tired you are, the more jabs there are.
  • When asked “don’t do that,” you answer that the other person has no sense of humor.

What to do

None of the techniques below has been tested in this form; where there are related data, that’s noted. Techniques 1–3 are for the one being hurt, 4 is for the one who jabs, 5 is for those who’ve been putting up with it for a long time. All of them are for couples where speaking up isn’t scary; if it’s scary, start with the section “When it is no longer about skills.”

1. Tell an accident from a pattern

When: you doubt whether you’re exaggerating, and it seems that “it’s just a small thing.”

What to do:

  1. For a couple of weeks, briefly write down each instance: what was said, in front of whom, what was in your body, how you feel afterward, what happened next.
  2. Don’t guess at intentions: what matters is not what was meant but what it does to you.
  3. Then reread and answer: does it repeat? Does it happen in front of people? Are they laughing at an action — or at you and what you care about? Does anything change when you ask? Is there fear?
  4. There’s repetition but no fear — move on to techniques 2 and 3. There’s fear — go straight to the section “When it is no longer about skills.”

How you know it worked: instead of a vague “something’s wrong,” you can name what exactly is happening, how often, and what it does to you afterward. The notes are your view on that day, not an objective record and not evidence for an argument.

2. Say “we’re not doing this” — right away and calmly

When: at the moment of the jab or as soon as both of you have cooled down — which is better hasn’t been compared. If the jab isn’t the first, there’s no point waiting to see “if it happens again” — it already has. If saying this is scary, this isn’t for you; go to the section “When it is no longer about skills.” Limits in the first months are in chapter 12; snapping after a hard day is in chapter 15.

What to do:

  1. Name the action, not the character: “When you joke about my work in front of friends…” or “When anger over small things gets taken out on me…”.
  2. Say what’s happening with you: “…it’s unpleasant and embarrassing for me.”
  3. Name what matters to you: “It matters to me that we’re on the same side in front of friends.” A boundary rests on what you’re protecting, not on reproaches to your partner.
  4. Say what you want: “We’re not doing this.”

In public, a short “I don’t like that” and a change of subject is enough; go into more detail at home, when both of you are calm. Don’t answer a jab with a jab, and don’t try to parent an adult. The goal isn’t to change their mind or win the argument, but to protect yourself and what you care about, and to leave the conversation with dignity. If your voice disappears or breaks into a shout, rehearse the phrase out loud in advance (chapter 16).

How you know it worked: it’s said without shouting and without swallowing it. You don’t need to expect acknowledgment or an apology right away: look at what happens next.

3. Hold to what you said

When: after “we’re not doing this” the jab happened again, but talking about it still isn’t scary.

What to do:

  1. Decide in advance what you’ll do this time — not as punishment, but so as not to take part: you’ll stop a conversation in that tone, leave the room, not laugh along in public and change the subject.
  2. Say it and name when you’ll come back: “I’m not continuing in this tone. Let’s come back to it after dinner.” Without a time frame, a pause can read as running away from the conversation (on pauses, see chapter 16).
  3. Don’t prove every time that it hurt “for real.” The other person doesn’t have to understand why it hurts you; they do have to take it into account, out of respect.
  4. Call the problem yours: “I have a problem with how I’m spoken to in front of people,” — and decide what you do, not what you expect from the other person (chapter 22).
  5. After a few weeks, check against your notes from technique 1.

How you know it worked: there are fewer jabs, talking about them isn’t scary, and there’s no payback for a “no.” If after your words the jabs got meaner, or the response was days of coldness, threats, prohibitions — it’s no longer about skills (the section “When it is no longer about skills”).

4. If you’re the one who jabs

When: the phrase is ready to go — or has already flown out.

What to do:

  1. Catch the moment before it: a short “oh, is that so,” tension, a wish for the other person to feel bad too.
  2. Ask yourself: what important thing am I losing right now? Energy? The feeling that I’m seen? Meaning in what I do? There’s no ready answer; each person has their own. There’s no need to shame yourself: protecting what’s important is normal; it’s the form that’s worth changing. A detailed breakdown for when you get carried away is in chapter 16.
  3. Say it directly, in one sentence instead of a joke. Instead of “Oh sure, our expert has figured it all out again” — “It hurts that I wasn’t asked.”
  4. If it has already flown out — apologize for the specific thing, without “it’s just a joke” and without “but”: “The joke about your work was humiliating. I’m sorry, I won’t do that again.” How to apologize part by part is in chapter 17.
  5. Look at where the harshness comes from. If you hold back with colleagues but not at home, outside tension is probably being dumped at home (chapter 15). If you put up with a lot in the rest of your life, there’s less energy left for calm words (chapter 20). And you can’t “let off steam” with a jab — why is explained in chapter 16.

How you know it worked: there are fewer jabs; your partner again tells you about important things without expecting mockery; you can name what really hurt you.

5. Years of silence: start with one thing

When: you’ve been putting up with it for a long time, a lot has gone unsaid, and it seems it’s already too late.

What to do:

  1. Write down only what you’ve silently accepted for years in how you’re treated (the general list “what I put up with” is in chapter 20). Next to it — what you told yourself each time and what it costs: the desire to share, ease around friends, self-respect, closeness.
  2. If your partner is polite with others but not with you, ask yourself: why do I put up with this? What keeps me here — hope that it’ll pass; the years invested; fear of a fight; children; housing? The answer isn’t a verdict on yourself but a map. If what keeps you is fear of your partner themselves, that’s the section “When it is no longer about skills.”
  3. Don’t lay it all out at once. Choose one thing, the most frequent, and start with technique 2: that way the conversation stays about a specific action.
  4. If you want to start with what you value in your partner — do, but only if it’s true, not a technique.
  5. Don’t file humiliation under what you “bear” by decision (on the difference, see chapter 20): disrespect is a reason for a boundary, not for patience.

How you know it worked: the first topic has been named out loud, and items you’ve already spoken about appear on the list.

What people usually think — and what is true

People think: if nobody’s hitting anyone, it’s not abuse, just that kind of humor.
In fact: in a large review, psychological abuse in couples was linked to worse mental health, up to and including trauma symptoms in women6. Almost all the studies there are snapshots at one point in time, mostly about women: it’s a link, and it doesn’t say that every jab does the same harm. But “it’s just humor” doesn’t make years of contempt harmless either.

People think: any jab is abuse, and you need to leave immediately.
In fact: one awkward joke is neither a pattern nor abuse. What matters is repetition, power, fear, control, and what happens to you. A label doesn’t decide how to live from here; it’s more useful to ask what this behavior does to your life (chapter 22).

People think: someone who puts up with it for years doesn’t notice or is to blame themselves.
In fact: among women in shelters for victims of violence, what they’d invested and having nowhere to go helped explain the decision to stay7. This is a special group: why people in general put up with disrespect for years hasn’t been established. Love, hope, fear, housing, children can all keep someone there. Don’t blame the one who stays — ask about safety and choice.

People think: staying silent means keeping the peace.
In fact: in the diaries of dating couples, concessions made to avoid something bad went along with worse following days, while concessions made for the sake of something good together went along with better ones8. This is a link, not a test of silence as a way of living. But giving way of your own free will and staying silent so things don’t get worse are different things, even if they look alike from the outside.

Talk it over together

For couples where it’s safe for both to speak honestly.

  1. Which of our jokes about each other do you enjoy, and which hurt? How can I notice the difference?
  2. What won’t happen in our conversations, even in anger?
  3. What that’s important to you do I make fun of? How can I respect it, even without sharing it?
  4. What word will you use to stop me if I cross the line — and what will I do then?
  5. What stands behind a jab for each of us: tiredness, hurt, a feeling of not being noticed?
  6. What has each of us not said for years so as not to spoil the evening? Where do we start?

When it is no longer about skills

This chapter is for couples where it’s safe for both to speak and to refuse. If there is:

  • fear: you choose every word so as not to trigger an outburst;
  • punishment for “we’re not doing this”: days of coldness, prohibitions, threats;
  • control: checking your phone, deciding whom you see, cut-off access to money;
  • shoving, thrown objects, blows, choking — even once;
  • humiliation that keeps getting meaner and more frequent;
  • threats of self-harm — yours or your partner’s,

then the techniques in this chapter don’t fit. Don’t have it out one-on-one with someone you’re afraid of, and don’t test the boundary to see if it holds. Tell someone you trust, contact a domestic violence help service, and if there’s a direct threat, call emergency services. More in chapter 23.

If the jabs and outbursts come from you, you can’t stop, and the other person is afraid of you, what you need is not a couple of tips but a program with accountability and an independent safety check (chapter 23).

Sources by chapter

Each number in a chapter’s text matches the same number in that chapter’s list here. The papers were checked against scientific databases: they exist and have not been retracted.
